On a January evening in the early seventies, when the snow in New York lay bruised and shiny as fish-skin under the lamps, Christine Nilsson was singing in *Faust* at the Academy of Music. There was already talk—always the talk, in drawing-rooms warmed by coal and confidence—of a new Opera House to be raised “above the Forties,” grand as any European temple. Yet fashion still returned, winter after winter, to the Academy’s worn red-and-gold boxes as to an inherited sideboard: nicked at the edges, inconvenient to open, but valued precisely because it kept the wrong hands from the silver.

Conservatives praised the place for being small and awkward; its narrow corridors and cramped tiers were a kind of social sieve, straining out the “new people” whom New York had begun to fear even as it leaned, hungry and curious, toward their glitter. The sentimental clung to its history; the musical to its sound. In an age when so much was built to impress the eye—marble, gilt, and gaslight—acoustics remained an older, rarer integrity, the one virtue you could not purchase by simply enlarging the room. Here, at least, a voice could travel cleanly, without being chewed up by echo or smothered by upholstery. The Academy held the human note the way a good kitchen holds aroma: not by trapping it, but by giving it the right walls to bloom against.

Madame Nilsson’s first appearance that winter had drawn what the papers liked to call “an exceptionally brilliant audience,” and the streets outside had become a slow, snorting procession of private broughams, family landaus, and the humbler but cleverer Brown coupés. To arrive by Brown was almost an honor in itself—one purchased by convenience rather than lineage—and leaving by Brown had the added advantage of letting you scramble into the first conveyance in line with a teasing nod to democratic principles, instead of waiting for your own coachman’s cold-and-gin congested nose to gleam under the portico. The livery-stableman who invented that system had understood something essential about Americans: they want to get away from pleasure even faster than they want to reach it.

When Newland Archer opened the door at the back of the club box, the curtain had just risen on the garden scene. He might easily have been earlier; he had dined at seven, alone with his mother and sister, and had lingered afterward with a cigar in the Gothic library—glazed black-walnut bookcases, finial-topped chairs—the only room where Mrs. Archer permitted smoke. The house, like the society it served, had its rules for air as strict as its rules for speech.

But New York was a metropolis now, and metropolises had their ritual delays. It was “not the thing” to arrive early at the opera; and what was or was not “the thing” governed Newland Archer’s world with a power as unquestioned as any ancient taboo. He told himself this was sophistication, not superstition; yet the result was the same: one moved according to signals one had been trained to hear and trained not to name.

There was, too, a more personal reason for his lateness. He had dawdled because he was, at heart, a dilettante; anticipating a pleasure often gave him a finer satisfaction than consuming it. Some men were greedy in the blunt way—grasping at women, money, applause—but Newland’s appetite was for the delicacy itself: the first taste, the first scent lifted from a covered dish, the sensation of knowing something exquisite was about to happen. And on this occasion, the moment he had been waiting for was so exact in its timing that, had he arranged his arrival with the prima donna’s stage-manager, he could not have entered more significantly than just as Nilsson was singing:

“He loves me—he loves me not—*he loves me!*”

She sang, of course, “*M’ama…*” and not “he loves me,” since an unalterable law of the musical world required that the German text of a French opera, sung by a Swedish artist, must be translated into Italian for the clearer understanding of English-speaking audiences. It seemed natural enough to Newland, as natural as the duties that shaped his own mornings: to part his hair with two silver-backed brushes bearing his monogram in blue enamel, and never to appear in society without a flower—preferably a gardenia—in his buttonhole. Convention, like a sauce repeated without question, came to be accepted as the only possible flavor.

“*M’ama… non m’ama…*” Nilsson sang, her notes falling as clean as dew shaken from a leaf, and then “*M’ama!*” with love triumphant as she pressed the ragged daisy to her lips and lifted her large eyes to the sophisticated countenance of the little brown Faust—Capoul—who, in tight purple velvet and a plumed cap, vainly tried to look as pure as his victim.

Leaning back against the wall at the rear of the box, Newland turned from the stage to scan the opposite side of the house. Directly facing him was the box of old Mrs. Manson Mingott, whose monstrous obesity had long since made it impossible for her to attend the opera in person, though she was always represented on nights that mattered by younger members of her clan, as a great estate is represented by its stewards. Tonight the front of the box was filled by her daughter-in-law, Mrs. Lovell Mingott, and her daughter, Mrs. Welland; and slightly withdrawn behind these brocaded matrons sat a young girl in white, her eyes held raptly on the stage-lovers.

As Nilsson’s “*M’ama!*” thrilled over the hushed house—because the boxes always stopped talking during the Daisy Song—a warm pink rose in the girl’s cheek, spread to her brow beneath the roots of her fair braids, and suffused the young slope of her breast down to the modest tulle tucker fastened with a single gardenia. She dropped her eyes to the immense bouquet of lilies-of-the-valley on her knee, and Newland saw her white-gloved fingertips brush the bells softly, as though testing their coolness through kid.

He drew a breath of satisfied vanity and turned his gaze back to the stage. The setting—no expense spared—was acknowledged to be beautiful even by those who spoke familiarly of Paris and Vienna. The foreground up to the footlights was covered with emerald-green cloth. In the middle distance, symmetrical mounds of woolly green moss, bounded by croquet hoops, formed the base of shrubs shaped like orange-trees but studded with large pink and red roses. Gigantic pansies, larger than the roses and resembling the floral pen-wipers made by female parishioners for fashionable clergymen, sprang from the moss beneath; and here and there a daisy grafted onto a rose-branch flowered with a luxuriance that seemed to promise Mr. Luther Burbank’s far-off prodigies.

In the center of this enchanted garden stood Nilsson in white cashmere slashed with pale blue satin, a reticule dangling from a blue girdle, and large yellow braids disposed on either side of her muslin chemisette. She listened with downcast eyes to Capoul’s impassioned wooing and affected a guileless incomprehension whenever, by word or glance, he indicated the ground-floor window of the neat brick villa projecting obliquely from the right wing.

“The darling,” Newland thought, his glance darting back to the young girl with the lilies-of-the-valley. “She doesn’t even guess what it’s all about.”

He contemplated her absorbed face with a thrill of possessorship in which pride in his own masculine initiation mingled with a tender reverence for her abysmal purity. “We’ll read *Faust* together… by the Italian lakes…” he thought, confusing—pleasantly, lazily—the future honeymoon with the masterpieces of literature it would be his privilege to unfold to his bride. Only that afternoon May Welland had let him guess that she “cared”—New York’s consecrated phrase of maiden avowal—and already his imagination had leaped ahead of the engagement ring, the betrothal kiss, and the bridal march from *Lohengrin*, placing her at his side in some scene of old European witchery.

He did not want the future Mrs. Newland Archer to be a simpleton. He meant her, thanks to his enlightening companionship, to develop a social tact and readiness of wit that would let her hold her own among the most popular married women of the “younger set,” in which it was the recognized custom to attract masculine homage while playfully discouraging it. If he had probed the bottom of his own vanity—as he sometimes nearly did—he would have found there the wish that his wife should be as worldly-wise and as eager to please as the married lady whose charms had held his fancy through two mildly agitated years, without, of course, any hint of the frailty that had so nearly marred that unhappy being’s life and had disarranged his own plans for a whole winter.

How such a miracle of fire and ice was to be created, and sustain itself in a harsh world, he had never taken the time to work out. It was enough to desire it, as one desires the perfect dish without troubling over the labor in the kitchen. Besides, he knew the wish was shared by the carefully brushed, white-waistcoated, buttonhole-flowered gentlemen who succeeded one another in the club box, exchanging greetings with him and then turning their opera-glasses on the circle of ladies produced by the system.

In matters intellectual and artistic, Newland felt himself distinctly superior to these chosen specimens of old New York gentility. He had probably read more, thought more, and even seen more of the world than any man among them. Alone, each betrayed his inferiority; together they represented “New York.” And the habit of masculine solidarity made him accept their doctrine on all issues called moral. He instinctively felt that, in this respect, it would be troublesome—and also rather bad form—to strike out for himself.

“Well—upon my soul!” exclaimed Lawrence Lefferts, turning his opera-glass abruptly away from the stage.

Lefferts was the foremost authority on “form” in New York. He had devoted more time than anyone to that intricate and fascinating subject, yet study alone could not explain his ease. From the slant of his bald forehead and the curve of his beautiful fair moustache down to the long patent-leather feet at the other end of his lean, elegant person, he wore correctness as if it were skin. A young admirer had once said: “If anybody can tell a fellow just when to wear a black tie with evening clothes and when not to, it’s Larry Lefferts.” On the question of pumps versus patent-leather Oxfords, his authority was law.

“My God!” he added, and silently handed his glass to old Sillerton Jackson.

Newland followed Lefferts’s stare and saw that the exclamation had been occasioned not by the stage but by the entry of a new figure into old Mrs. Mingott’s box. A slim young woman, a little less tall than May Welland, had appeared—brown hair growing in close curls about her temples and held by a narrow band of diamonds. The effect, giving her what was then called a “Josephine look,” was carried out in the cut of her dark blue velvet gown, theatrically caught up under the bosom by a girdle with a large old-fashioned clasp.

She seemed unconscious of the attention she drew. She stood a moment in the center of the box, speaking with Mrs. Welland about whether she ought to take the latter’s place in the front right-hand corner; then, yielding with a slight smile, she seated herself in line with Mrs. Welland’s sister-in-law, Mrs. Lovell Mingott, who occupied the opposite corner.

Mr. Sillerton Jackson returned the opera-glass to Lawrence Lefferts. The whole of the club box turned, instinctively waiting to hear what the old man would say, for Mr. Jackson was as great an authority on “family” as Lefferts was on “form.” He knew every ramification of New York cousinship; could untangle connections as dense as vines—how the Mingotts, through the Thorleys, were linked to the Dallases of South Carolina, and how the elder Philadelphia Thorleys related to the Albany Chiverses (never to be confused with the Manson Chiverses of University Place). He could also recite the hereditary traits of each tribe: the fabulous stinginess of the younger Leffertses of Long Island; the Rushworth tendency to make foolish matches; the insanity that appeared in every second generation of the Albany Chiverses, with whom their New York cousins had refused to intermarry—except, disastrously, in the case of poor Medora Manson, who, as everybody knew… but then her mother had been a Rushworth.

Beyond this forest of family trees, Mr. Jackson carried beneath his soft thatch of silver hair a register of scandals and mysteries that had smoldered for fifty years under the unruffled surface of society. So far did his information reach, and so retentive was his memory, that people believed he might be the only man who could tell who Julius Beaufort the banker really was, and what had become of handsome Bob Spicer, old Mrs. Manson Mingott’s father, who had disappeared mysteriously—with a large sum of trust money—less than a year after his marriage, on the very day a beautiful Spanish dancer, lately delighting audiences at the old Opera-house on the Battery, had taken ship for Cuba.

Yet these stories—and many others—were locked in Mr. Jackson’s breast. His sense of honor forbade him to repeat what was privately imparted, and he knew his reputation for discretion widened the channels through which secrets flowed to him. The club box waited, visibly suspended, while he considered.

For a moment he scrutinized the attentive group out of filmy blue eyes overhung by veined lids. Then he twisted his moustache thoughtfully and said, simply:

“I didn’t think the Mingotts would have tried it on.”

Newland Archer, in the space of a few minutes, found himself flushed with a kind of unwilling shame that crept up like heat from an over-crowded kitchen. It was irritating enough that the box on which the whole masculine attention of New York had converged should be the very one in which his betrothed sat—May Welland between her mother and her aunt—calm as a white dish set out for admiration; but for an instant he could not even make out the identity of the woman in the Empire dress, nor guess why her mere presence had quickened the house to that peculiar rustle—like the first crackle of kindling before a flame takes.

Then recognition came, and with it a sharp spurt of indignation, as sudden as a cut finger touched by salt.

No—surely the Mingotts had not meant to do this. No one would have thought the Mingotts would have tried it on. Yet they had, unmistakably; the low, clipped comments behind him—little hisses of scandal carefully kept under the cover of Mephistopheles’ phrases—left no room to doubt that the young woman was May Welland’s cousin, the cousin always spoken of at home with that air of dutiful pity New York liked to wear: “poor Ellen Olenska.”

He knew she had arrived from Europe only a day or two earlier; he had even heard from May—almost tenderly, and certainly without censure—that she had gone to see her cousin, who was staying with old Mrs. Mingott. Archer believed in family solidarity as in a creed, and one of the things he had long respected in the Mingotts was their obstinate championship of whatever black sheep their blameless flock produced. There was no smallness in him; he was glad May should be generous enough to be kind—privately—to an unhappy woman. But to receive Countess Olenska inside the family walls was one thing, like keeping a troubled relative fed and sheltered in the back rooms; to display her publicly at the Opera—at the Opera of all places, with May beside her, and May’s engagement to him to be announced within weeks—was another. That was bringing the whole household’s stewpot into the drawing-room and asking the guests to discuss the ingredients.

No: he found himself siding, for once, with old Sillerton Jackson’s offended certainty. He did not think the Mingotts would have tried it on.

Of course, he reflected, whatever man dared (within Fifth Avenue’s limits) old Mrs. Manson Mingott would dare. He had always admired the great, high-handed old lady—Catherine Spicer of Staten Island once, with a father mysteriously discredited, and neither money nor position enough to make people forget it—who had married into the wealthy Mingott line, married two of her daughters to “foreigners” (an Italian marquis and an English banker), and then capped her audacities by building a vast house of pale cream-coloured stone in a wilderness near Central Park, when brownstone was as obligatory as a frock-coat at four o’clock.

Her foreign daughters had become legend: they never returned to visit; and she—like many minds that rule by sheer will—had grown sedentary and enormous, and had stayed at home, reigning where she sat. But the cream-coloured house, said to be modelled on Parisian private hotels, stood like a public challenge. She throned there among pre-Revolutionary furniture and souvenirs of the Tuileries of Louis Napoleon—where she had shone in her middle age—as placidly as if it were the most natural thing in the world to live above Thirty-fourth Street, or to have French windows that opened like doors instead of sashes that pushed up.

Everyone agreed (including Mr. Jackson) that old Catherine had never been beautiful—beauty being, in New York’s eyes, the one sauce that could make any dish go down, and excuse a certain number of dubious flavours. Unkind people said that, like her Imperial namesake, she had won by sheer will, hardness, and a haughty effrontery that was somehow justified—perhaps even sterilized—by the extreme decency and dignity of her private life. Her husband had died when she was twenty-eight, after tying up the money with an extra caution born of the city’s distrust of the Spicers; but his young widow had gone on fearlessly: mixing with foreign society, marrying her daughters heaven knew where, hobnobbing with Dukes and Ambassadors, associating familiarly with Papists, entertaining Opera singers, and counting Mme. Taglioni among her intimates; and all the while (as Sillerton Jackson loved to insist) there had never been a breath on her reputation—the only respect, he always added, in which she differed from the earlier Catherine.

Mrs. Mingott had long since succeeded in untying her husband’s fortune and had lived in affluence for half a century; yet early privations had made her thrifty to the point of perversity. When she bought a dress or a piece of furniture she insisted on the best—sound fabric, honest workmanship, the sort of thing that lasted and could be handed down like a recipe written in a sure hand. But she could not bring herself to spend much on the transient pleasures of the table. So, for reasons wholly different, her food was as poor as Mrs. Archer’s, and her wines did nothing to save it. The clan felt that the penury of her dinners disgraced the Mingott name, so long associated with good living; yet people continued to crowd her drawing-room in spite of the made dishes and the flat champagne. And when her son Lovell—determined to restore the family credit like a chef hired to rescue a sinking house—imported the best cook in New York, she only laughed and said: “What’s the use of two good cooks in one family, now that I’ve married the girls and can’t eat sauces?”

Thinking of these things, Archer let his gaze drift back to the Mingott box. Mrs. Welland and her sister-in-law were facing their semicircle of critics with that Mingott _aplomb_ old Catherine had baked into all her tribe—so firm and smooth a crust that no knife of comment could break it. Only May betrayed, by a heightened colour, a sense of the gravity of the moment—perhaps because she knew he was watching her.

And the cause of the commotion—Ellen Olenska—sat in her corner with a composed grace, her eyes fixed on the stage. When she leaned forward she showed a little more shoulder and bosom than New York was accustomed to seeing—at least in a lady who had reasons for wishing to pass unnoticed. There was something in the naked line of it that disturbed him, as if an ingredient had been left out of a familiar dish and the absence threw the whole balance off. Few things seemed to Archer more awful than an offence against Taste—that far-off divinity of whom Form was the mere visible representative. Madame Olenska’s pale, serious face appealed to his fancy as suited to her unhappy situation; but the way her dress—without a tucker—sloped away from her thin shoulders both shocked and troubled him. He hated to imagine May exposed to the influence of a young woman so careless of the dictates that kept their world from turning rancid.

Behind him, as everyone chatted through the Mephistopheles-and-Martha scenes—those stretches of music New York treated like the clatter between courses—he heard one of the younger men begin: “After all, just _what_ happened?”

“Well—she left him; nobody attempts to deny that.”

“He’s an awful brute, isn’t he?” the youth went on, in the candid tone of one preparing to play the chivalric champion.

“The very worst; I knew him at Nice,” Lawrence Lefferts said, with the authority of a man who prided himself on knowing exactly how much to say and how little to risk. “A half-paralysed white sneering fellow—rather handsome head, but eyes with a lot of lashes. You know the kind: when he wasn’t with women he was collecting china. Paying any price for both, I understand.”

There was a general laugh—dry, well-bred, and somehow loud against the music, like a spoon tapping a glass too insistently.

“Well, then—?” the young champion pressed, and the question hung for a moment, anxious as a palate waiting for a verdict.

“Well, then; she bolted with his secretary.”

“Oh. I see.”

The youth’s face fell, as if a romantic sauce had curdled. Lefferts went on, more lightly: “It didn’t last long. A few months later I heard she was living alone in Venice. I believe Lovell Mingott went out to get her. He said she was desperately unhappy. That’s all right—but this parading her at the Opera’s another thing.”

“Perhaps,” the young man ventured—Thorley, Archer remembered; always earnest, always in danger of sincerity—“she’s too unhappy to be left at home.”

The remark was met with an irreverent laugh; the youth coloured, and tried to look as if he had meant one of those knowing innuendoes New York liked to brandish while pretending not to understand them.

“Well—it’s queer to have brought Miss Welland, anyhow,” someone murmured, with a sideways glance that found Archer without seeming to.

“Oh, that’s part of the campaign: Granny’s orders, no doubt,” Lefferts laughed. “When the old lady does a thing she does it thoroughly.”

The act was ending; the house shifted and stirred. Fans flickered like white wings; silk whispered; in the pit, voices rose and fell in that controlled murmur Archer always thought of as the city’s own dialect—an idiom of half-said things, of meanings carried in tone rather than words.

And suddenly he felt himself pushed—by pride, by protectiveness, by the odd desire to seize the sound of the room and change it—to decisive action. He wanted to be the first man to enter Mrs. Mingott’s box, to proclaim to the waiting world his engagement to May Welland, and to stand by her, publicly, through whatever difficulties her cousin’s anomalous situation might draw down. The impulse swept aside his scruples and sent him quickly through the red corridors to the farther side of the house, past doorways where the hum of talk thickened and thinned like the roar of a distant sea.

As he entered the box, May’s eyes met his. He saw at once that she understood his motive—understood without any need of explanation; for in their world the most important things were said in implication, and the closest alliances were made in silence. It gave him a sensation—almost like the sudden comfort of a familiar smell, bread or broth—that they were nearer in this wordless exchange than they could have been through any confession.

Her eyes said: “You see why Mamma brought me.”

His answered: “I would not for the world have had you stay away.”

“You know my niece Countess Olenska?” Mrs. Welland inquired, taking his hand with her smooth, sheltered smile.

Archer bowed, as was customary on being introduced to a lady, without offering his hand. Ellen Olenska bent her head slightly; her pale-gloved hands remained clasped on a huge fan of eagle feathers, its plumes spreading like some archaic trophy—something taken from another landscape, another set of sounds.

After greeting Mrs. Lovell Mingott—a large blonde lady in creaking satin—he sat down beside May and leaned toward her.

“I hope you’ve told Madame Olenska that we’re engaged?” he asked in a low tone. “I want everybody to know—I want you to let me announce it this evening at the ball.”

May’s face went rosy as dawn rising behind a white curtain; she looked at him with eyes that shone like a hearth newly kindled.

“If you can persuade Mamma,” she said; “but why should we change what is already settled?”

He answered only with his look; and, smiling more confidently, she added: “Tell my cousin yourself: I give you leave. She says she used to play with you when you were children.”

She pushed back her chair to make room, promptly and a little ostentatiously, with the innocent desire that the whole house should see what he was doing. Archer, feeling the weight of the watching—hearing it, too, in the faint change of the surrounding murmur—went and seated himself at Countess Olenska’s side.

“We _did_ use to play together, didn’t we?” she asked, turning her grave eyes on him.

“You were a horrid boy, and kissed me once behind a door; but it was your cousin Vandie Newland, who never looked at me, that I was in love with.” Her glance travelled over the horseshoe curve of boxes. “Ah—how this brings it all back to me—I see everybody here in knickerbockers and pantalettes,” she went on, with a slightly foreign trailing accent, and then looked back at him.

Her eyes were agreeable—too agreeable, Archer thought, in what they reflected. He was shocked that she could let them summon so unseemly a picture of the august tribunal before which, at that very moment, her case was being tried. Nothing could be in worse taste than misplaced flippancy. He stiffened his tone.

“Yes. You have been away a very long time.”

“Oh, centuries and centuries—so long,” she said, “that I’m sure I’m dead and buried, and this dear old place is heaven.”

For reasons he could not at once define, the words struck him as even more disrespectful than the first—an airy twist of language that made the whole of New York society sound like a joke told in another tongue. And all around them the house kept breathing—silk, voices, orchestral sound—like a great creature whose hearing was keenest when it pretended not to listen.

It invariably happened in the same way: on the night of her annual ball, Mrs. Julius Beaufort never failed to appear at the Opera, as punctually as a course in a well-ordered dinner. She chose an Opera night on purpose, as one chose a costly truffle or a rare early peach—not for hunger, but for the pleasure of showing that hunger had nothing to do with it. The ball could be set in motion without her; it was proof, offered to all New York, that household cares were for other women, and that in her house an invisible brigade could whip, roast, ice, light, polish, and arrange the world while she sat serene behind her fan.

The Beauforts’ brown-stone was one of the few that possessed a ball-room—a chamber built for a single lavish use and then left, for three-hundred-and-sixty-four days of the year, to a shuttered darkness that smelled faintly of beeswax, closed velvet, and stored gilt chairs stacked like unused serving-platters in a corner. In an age when it was beginning to be called “provincial” to stretch a crash over the drawing-room floor and send the furniture upstairs like so many kitchen stools before a feast, the mere fact of a permanent ball-room—kept for no daily purpose, like a family silver tureen too sacred for soup—was felt to compensate for whatever might be regrettable in the Beaufort past.

Mrs. Archer, who liked to coin her social philosophy into axioms the way certain cooks reduced sauces to their sharp essence, had once said: “We all have our pet common people—” The phrase was a daring seasoning, but its truth was secretly savoured in many an exclusive bosom. The Beauforts, however, were not exactly common; some whispered they were worse. Mrs. Beaufort had been Regina Dallas, of one of America’s most honoured families—a penniless beauty, introduced to New York by her cousin Medora Manson, who was forever doing the wrong thing from the right motive, as if she could not help adding nutmeg where propriety required only salt.

To be related to the Mansons and the Rushworths was to possess, as Mr. Sillerton Jackson—who had frequented the Tuileries—called it, a “_droit de cité_” in New York society. But did one not forfeit it by marrying Julius Beaufort? That question, like the origin of a suspiciously excellent pâté, was never asked aloud at table, though everyone turned it over privately.

Who was Beaufort? He passed for an Englishman; he was agreeable, handsome, ill-tempered, hospitable, witty. He had come to America with letters from old Mrs. Manson Mingott’s English son-in-law, the banker, and had quickly made himself important in affairs; yet his habits were dissipated, his tongue bitter, his antecedents mysterious. When Medora Manson announced Regina’s engagement, it was received as another of Medora’s imprudences—an unwise marriage being, in that world, as irreparable as a ruined consommé.

But folly is as often justified of her children as wisdom. Two years after the marriage it was admitted—through pursed lips—that young Mrs. Beaufort had the most distinguished house in New York. No one could quite say how the miracle was accomplished. She was indolent, passive; the caustic called her dull. Yet she dressed like an idol hung with pearls, grew younger and blonder and more beautiful with each season, and sat throned in Mr. Beaufort’s heavy palace drawing all the world there without lifting her jewelled little finger, as if mere presence were enough to summon candles into flame and musicians into tune.

The knowing people said it was Beaufort himself who did the work—trained the servants to move like a drilled kitchen staff; taught the chef new dishes (and made sure they were served hot, not sacrificed to delay); told the gardeners what hot-house flowers to force for the dinner-table and drawing-rooms; selected the guests; brewed the after-dinner punch; even dictated the little notes his wife wrote. If he did, these labours were performed in private, in the service corridors where the real life of a house went on, where one heard the rustle of skirts, the clink of glass, the muffled orders—an acoustic ecology as carefully managed as the menu. To the world he presented himself as a careless, hospitable millionaire who strolled into his own rooms with the detachment of an invited guest and said, lightly: “My wife’s gloxinias are a marvel, aren’t they? I believe she gets them out from Kew.”

That, people agreed, was his secret: the way he carried things off. It was all very well to whisper that he had been “helped” to leave England by the international banking-house in which he had been employed; he carried off that rumour as easily as he carried off everything. New York’s business conscience was as sensitive as its moral standard; yet he swept all resistance before him and all New York into his drawing-rooms. For over twenty years now people had said they were “going to the Beauforts’” with the same tone of security as if they had said they were going to Mrs. Manson Mingott’s—only with the added satisfaction of knowing they would get hot canvas-back ducks and vintage wines instead of tepid Veuve Clicquot without a year and warmed-up croquettes from Philadelphia.

So Mrs. Beaufort, as usual, appeared in her box just before the Jewel Song; and when, again as usual, she rose at the end of the third act, drew her opera cloak about her lovely shoulders, and vanished, New York understood, as clearly as it understood the passing of a dish from hand to hand, that in half an hour the ball would begin.

The Beaufort house was one New Yorkers were proud to show to foreigners, especially on this night. The Beauforts had been among the first to own their own red velvet carpet and have it rolled down the steps by their own footmen under their own awning, instead of hiring it with the supper and the ball-room chairs. They had also inaugurated the custom of letting ladies take their cloaks off in the hall, instead of shuffling up to the hostess’s bedroom to recurl their hair by the gas-burner, as if preparing oneself were a backstage affair unfit for public. Beaufort was understood to have remarked that he supposed all his wife’s friends had maids to see they were properly _coiffées_ before leaving home. Even this was part of his performance: the quiet assertion that in his house the right things happened without fuss, like bread rising in a warm kitchen.

Then there was the bold plan of the house itself: instead of squeezing through a narrow passage to the ball-room (as at the Chiverses’), one marched solemnly down a vista of enfiladed drawing-rooms—the sea-green, the crimson, the bouton d’or—each one a different note in a carefully composed progression. From far off one saw many-candled lustres reflected in polished parquetry; beyond, the depths of a conservatory, where camellias and tree-ferns arched their costly foliage over seats of black and gold bamboo. In those planted depths, among damp earth and waxy leaves, the air carried its own language—green, faintly metallic, and sweet—while from the ball-room came the softened thunder of feet and music, moderated by doors and heavy hangings the way a good host moderated noise with architecture.

Newland Archer, as became a young man of his position, strolled in somewhat late. He left his overcoat with the silk-stockinged footmen—silk stockings being one of Beaufort’s few fatuities, an affectation like putting gold leaf on a dish that was already rich enough—and dawdled in the library hung with Spanish leather and furnished with Buhl and malachite, where a few men chatted and tugged on their dancing-gloves. Their voices had the dry, confident cadence of men who had never needed to listen for anything but approval. At last he joined the line of guests whom Mrs. Beaufort received on the threshold of the crimson drawing-room, with the faint air of someone who had stepped away from a supper-table merely to prove she could.

Archer was distinctly nervous. He had not gone back to his club after the Opera, as the young bloods usually did; instead, the night being fine, he had walked some distance up Fifth Avenue before turning back toward the Beauforts’. The city’s sounds had seemed unusually present—the far rattle of wheels, the occasional shouted call, the thin metallic ring of modernity creeping in at the edges of the old arrangements. He found himself thinking how a harsh note could spoil a whole evening, as one wrong spice could spoil a sauce. He was afraid the Mingotts might be going too far: that they might, in fact, have Granny Mingott’s orders to bring the Countess Olenska to the ball.

From the tone in the club box he had understood how grave a mistake that would be. And though he was more than ever determined to see the thing through—determined, that is, to manage Ellen’s return to the family circle without catastrophe—he felt less chivalrously eager to champion his betrothed’s cousin than before their brief talk at the Opera. The sound of Ellen’s name in other mouths had been like a foreign word pronounced too loudly: it did not belong to the accustomed lexicon of New York, and therefore it threatened the whole fragile grammar of their lives.

Wandering on to the bouton d’or drawing-room—where Beaufort had had the audacity to hang “Love Victorious,” Bouguereau’s much-discussed nude—Archer found Mrs. Welland and her daughter standing near the ball-room door. Beyond them couples were already gliding over the floor: the wax-candlelight fell on revolving tulle skirts, on girlish heads wreathed with modest blossoms, on the dashing aigrettes and ornaments of young married women’s coiffures, and on the glitter of highly glazed shirt-fronts and fresh _glacé_ gloves. The room breathed a mingled perfume—hot wax, crushed flowers, the faint citrus bite of punch, and, from somewhere farther back, the savoury promise of supper: roast skin tightening under heat, sauces held at just the right trembling point before separation.

Miss Welland, evidently about to join the dancers, hovered on the threshold with lilies-of-the-valley in her hand—she carried no other bouquet. Her face was a little pale, her eyes burning with a candid excitement. Around her was a small crowd of young men and girls; there was hand-clasping, laughter, and pleasantry, while Mrs. Welland stood slightly apart, shining on them the beam of a qualified approval. It was evident May was in the act of announcing her engagement, while her mother affected the parental reluctance considered suitable—like a hostess protesting she has done nothing, while the table groans under dishes.

Archer paused. It had been at his express wish that the announcement should be made, and yet this was not how he would have chosen to have his happiness known. To proclaim it in the heat and noise of a crowded ball-room was to rob it of the fine bloom of privacy which should belong to things nearest the heart—like cutting into a delicate dessert before the perfume has had time to rise. His joy was so deep that this blurring of the surface left its essence untouched; but he would have liked to keep the surface pure too.

It was some satisfaction to find that May shared his feeling. Her eyes fled to his beseechingly, and their look said: Remember, we’re doing this because it’s right. No appeal could have found a more immediate response in Archer’s breast; only he wished the necessity had been dictated by some ideal reason, and not simply by poor Ellen Olenska, whose very presence threatened to change the vocabulary of their world.

The group made way for him with significant smiles; after taking his share of congratulations, he drew his betrothed into the middle of the ball-room and put his arm about her waist.

“Now we shan’t have to talk,” he said, smiling into her candid eyes, as they floated away on the soft waves of the “Blue Danube.”

She made no answer. Her lips trembled into a smile, but her eyes remained distant and serious, as if fixed on some ineffable vision beyond the chandeliers and the turning skirts.

“Dear,” Archer whispered, pressing her closer; and it was borne in on him that the first hours of being engaged, even when spent in a ball-room, had something grave and sacramental in them. What a new life it was going to be, with this whiteness, this radiance, this goodness at one’s side—like beginning a family recipe that one’s children and their children would repeat without ever knowing the first hand that had measured and stirred.

When the dance was over, the two, as became an affianced couple, wandered into the conservatory. There, behind a tall screen of tree-ferns and camellias, with moisture beading on leaves like clear broth on the rim of a bowl, Newland pressed her gloved hand to his lips.

“You see I did as you asked me to,” she said.

“Yes. I couldn’t wait,” he answered, smiling.

After a moment he added: “Only I wish it hadn’t had to be at a ball.”

“Yes, I know.” She met his glance comprehendingly. “But after all—even here we’re alone together, aren’t we?”

“Oh, dearest—always!” Archer cried.

Evidently she was always going to understand; always going to say the right thing in the right idiom of their class. The discovery made his happiness overflow, and he went on gaily: “The worst of it is that I want to kiss you and I can’t.”

As he spoke he glanced about the conservatory, assured himself of their momentary privacy, and, catching her to him, laid a fugitive pressure on her lips. The act was as swift as stealing a taste from a sauce before it was served—more daring for being so slight. To counteract the audacity, he led her to a bamboo sofa in a less secluded part of the conservatory. Sitting down beside her, he broke a sprig of lily-of-the-valley from her bouquet, crushing between his fingers that cool, shy fragrance that seemed made for secrecy.

She sat silent, and for a moment the world lay like a sunlit valley at their feet: the candlelight, the music muffled by foliage, the subdued roar of society moving as one body, and beneath it all the steady, disciplined labour of servants and cooks, the unseen hands keeping heat in the dishes and order in the rooms.

“Did you tell my cousin Ellen?” she asked at last, as if speaking through a dream.

He roused himself and remembered that he had not. Some invincible repugnance to speak of such things to that strange foreign woman had checked the words on his lips, as if his tongue refused the unfamiliar name in the presence of his own happiness.

“No—I hadn’t the chance after all,” he said, fibbing hastily.

“Ah.” She looked disappointed, but gently resolved on gaining her point. “You must, then, for I didn’t either; and I shouldn’t like her to think—”

“Of course not. But aren’t you, after all, the person to do it?”

She pondered. “If I’d done it at the right time, yes: but now that there’s been a delay I think you must explain that I’d asked you to tell her at the Opera, before our speaking about it to everybody here. Otherwise she might think I had forgotten her. You see, she’s one of the family, and she’s been away so long that she’s rather—sensitive.”

Archer looked at her glowingly. “Dear and great angel! Of course I’ll tell her.” He glanced, a trifle apprehensively, toward the crowded ball-room, where the music surged and broke and surged again like a tide. “But I haven’t seen her yet. Has she come?”

“No; at the last minute she decided not to.”

“At the last minute?” he echoed, betraying his surprise that she should ever have considered the alternative possible.

“Yes. She’s awfully fond of dancing,” May answered simply. “But suddenly she made up her mind that her dress wasn’t smart enough for a ball, though we thought it so lovely; and so my aunt had to take her home.”

“Oh, well—” said Archer, with happy indifference.

Nothing about his betrothed pleased him more than her resolute determination to carry to its utmost limit that ritual of ignoring the unpleasant in which they had both been brought up. She knew as well as he did the real reason Ellen had stayed away; yet May’s gentle words offered a different story, a safer dialect for the family to speak in public—a language that preserved the soundscape of their world, smoothing discord into silence.

“She knows,” he thought, “the real reason; but I shall never let her see, by the least sign, that I am conscious of there being a shadow of a shade on poor Ellen Olenska’s reputation.”

In the course of the next day the first of the usual betrothal visits were exchanged, as punctiliously as courses in a dinner whose order had been agreed upon generations ago and never altered without scandal. Newland Archer went first, as propriety required, with his mother and his sister to call on Mrs. Welland; and afterward he drove with Mrs. Welland and May out to old Mrs. Manson Mingott’s to receive that venerable benediction which, in New York, had the weight of a family deed and the permanence of a recipe copied in a grandmother’s hand.

A visit to Mrs. Manson Mingott always amused Archer, and not only because she was formidable. Her house itself had the air of an historical document that had been handled too much, thumbed and annotated, and yet preserved because no one dared throw it away. It was not as venerable, of course, as the pure 1830 interiors still lingering farther down—those solemn rooms whose grim harmony matched cabbage-rose garlands on carpets to the dark shine of rosewood consoles, to round-arched fire-places in black marble, to immense mahogany book-cases glazed like confectioners’ cases. Mrs. Mingott, who had built later, had swept out the massive furniture of her prime and mixed the Mingott heirlooms with the frivolities of the Second Empire, as if a sober old broth had been brightened—against all rules—by an unexpected dash of foreign spice.

She sat, as she always did, in the ground-floor window of her sitting-room, planted there like a great domestic landmark, calmly watching for life and fashion to flow northward to her solitary doors. The street outside had its own orchestra: the clatter of omnibuses bumping over cobble-stones, the iron-shod impatience of hoofs, the rasp of wheels, and, above it, the thin calls of vendors that rose and fell like birds over a field. There were hoardings and quarries, one-story saloons, wooden green-houses in ragged gardens, and rocks where goats stood and stared; but she seemed in no hurry to have them vanish. Her patience equalled her confidence. Some day, she was certain, all that raw, noisy frontier would be pushed back by residences as stately as her own—perhaps, in her impartiality, even statelier—and the rude percussion of the street would be softened under smooth asphalt, such as people reported having heard their footsteps on in Paris. Meanwhile, every one she cared to see came to her. She could fill her rooms as easily as the Beauforts and, without adding a single item to the menu of her suppers, make people feel they had been fed—if not by dishes, then by importance.

The immense accretion of flesh which had descended on her in middle life like lava on a doomed city had transformed her from a plump active little woman with a neatly turned foot and ankle into something as vast and august as a natural phenomenon. She had accepted this submergence with the same obstinate composure with which she accepted every trial; and now, in extreme old age, was rewarded by presenting to her mirror an almost unwrinkled expanse of firm pink-and-white flesh, in the centre of which the traces of a small face survived as if awaiting excavation. A flight of smooth double chins led down to the dizzy depths of a still snowy bosom veiled in snowy muslins, held in place by the miniature portrait of the late Mr. Mingott. Around and below, wave after wave of black silk surged away over the edges of a capacious armchair, while two tiny white hands, their wrists ringed with rolls of aged fat like ivory bracelets, poised above the billows with the lightness of gulls.

Long since, the burden of that flesh had made it impossible for her to go up and down stairs. With characteristic independence—and in flagrant violation of New York propriety—she had moved her reception rooms upstairs and established herself on the ground floor, so that, seated with her in the sitting-room window, you caught, through a door that was always open and a looped-back yellow damask portière, the unexpected vista of a bedroom: a huge low bed upholstered like a sofa, and a toilet-table dressed in frivolous lace flounces beneath a gilt-framed mirror. Visitors were startled and fascinated by the foreignness of the arrangement, which recalled the settings of French novels and those architectural incentives to immorality the simple American imagination had never dared to furnish. That, Archer told himself with secret amusement, was how women with lovers lived in wicked old societies: apartments with all the rooms on one floor, and all the indecent propinquities novels described. It entertained him to picture Mrs. Mingott’s blameless life led on a stage-set meant for adultery; but he admitted, with real admiration, that if a lover had been what she wanted, the intrepid woman would have had him too.

To the general relief the Countess Olenska was not present when the betrothed couple arrived. Mrs. Mingott said Ellen had gone out; which, on a day of glaring sunlight, at the shopping hour, seemed in itself a questionable thing for a compromised woman to do. But at least it spared them the embarrassment of her presence and the faint shadow that her unhappy past might seem to cast across their radiant future—like a sour note drifting in from the street just as a domestic harmony had been established.

The visit went off successfully, as every one had foreseen. Old Mrs. Mingott was delighted with the engagement; it had been long expected by watchful relatives and carefully passed upon in family council, like a proposed change to a household menu. And the engagement ring—a large thick sapphire set in invisible claws—met with her unqualified admiration.

“It’s the new setting; of course it shows the stone beautifully, but it looks a little bare to old-fashioned eyes,” Mrs. Welland explained, with a conciliatory side-glance at her future son-in-law.

“Old-fashioned eyes? I hope you don’t mean mine, my dear? I like all the novelties,” said the ancestress, lifting the stone to her small bright orbs, which no glasses had ever disfigured. “Very handsome,” she added, returning it. “Very liberal. In my time a cameo set in pearls was thought sufficient. But it’s the hand that sets off the ring, isn’t it, my dear Mr. Archer?” She waved one of her tiny hands, as if offering it for appraisal the way she might offer a pastry to see if it had been properly browned. “Mine was modelled in Rome by the great Ferrigiani. You should have May’s done: no doubt he’ll have it done, my child. Her hand is large—it’s these modern sports that spread the joints—but the skin is white. And when’s the wedding to be?” She broke off and fixed her eyes on Archer’s face with the directness of a cook demanding the date of a feast.

“Oh—” Mrs. Welland murmured; and Archer, smiling at May, answered: “As soon as ever it can, if only you’ll back me up, Mrs. Mingott.”

“We must give them time to get to know each other a little better, mamma,” Mrs. Welland interposed, with the proper affectation of reluctance.

“Know each other? Fiddlesticks! Everybody in New York has always known everybody,” the old lady returned. “Let the young man have his way, my dear; don’t wait till the bubble’s off the wine. Marry them before Lent; I may catch pneumonia any winter now, and I want to give the wedding-breakfast.”

The successive declarations were received with the correct measure of amusement, incredulity, gratitude. The air in the room had that faint domestic perfume of warmed textiles and old pot-pourri, and through it threaded the distant scrape of wheels and the intermittent clang of street life—a reminder that the city’s rougher music went on while families measured out their joys by ritual. The visit was breaking up in a vein of mild pleasantry when the door opened and the Countess Olenska entered in bonnet and mantle, followed by the unexpected figure of Julius Beaufort.

There was a cousinly murmur of pleasure among the ladies, and Mrs. Mingott held out Ferrigiani’s modelled hand to the banker.

“Ha! Beaufort, this is a rare favour!” She had an odd foreign way of addressing men by their surnames.

“Thanks. I wish it might happen oftener,” he said, in his easy arrogant way. “I’m generally so tied down; but I met the Countess Ellen in Madison Square, and she was good enough to let me walk home with her.”

“Ah—I hope the house will be gayer, now that Ellen’s here!” cried Mrs. Mingott with glorious effrontery. “Sit down—sit down, Beaufort: push up the yellow armchair; now I’ve got you I want a good gossip. I hear your ball was magnificent; and I understand you invited Mrs. Lemuel Struthers? Well—I’ve a curiosity to see the woman myself.”

She had forgotten her relatives, who were drifting out into the hall under Ellen Olenska’s guidance. Old Mrs. Mingott had always professed a great admiration for Julius Beaufort; there was a kind of kinship between them, in their cool domineering way and their talent for taking short-cuts through conventions. Now she was eagerly curious to know what had decided the Beauforts to invite, for the first time, Mrs. Lemuel Struthers—the widow of Struthers’s Shoe-polish—who had returned the previous year from a long initiatory sojourn in Europe to lay siege to New York’s tight little citadel.

“Of course if you and Regina invite her the thing is settled,” Mrs. Mingott declared, with a relish like that of someone tasting a new dish and deciding it will do. “Well, we need new blood and new money—and I hear she’s still very good-looking.”

In the hall, while Mrs. Welland and May drew on their furs, Archer saw the Countess Olenska looking at him with a faintly questioning smile, as though she were listening not just for words but for what lay underneath them—the private dialect that never translated well into public speech.

“Of course you know already—about May and me,” he said, answering her look with a shy laugh. “She scolded me for not giving you the news last night at the Opera: I had her orders to tell you that we were engaged—but I couldn’t, in that crowd.”

The smile passed from Ellen’s eyes to her lips. She looked younger suddenly, more like the bold brown Ellen Mingott of his boyhood, before Europe had added its complicated seasonings to her. “Of course I know; yes. And I’m so glad. But one doesn’t tell such things first in a crowd.”

The ladies were on the threshold. She held out her hand. “Good-bye; come and see me some day,” she said, still looking at Archer, as if asking him—without saying it—to remember that there were ways of speaking and being spoken to that did not belong to the loud public rooms of New York.

In the carriage, on the way down Fifth Avenue, they talked pointedly of Mrs. Mingott—of her age, her spirit, her wonderful attributes—as families will talk of a great table laid safely behind them, enumerating the dishes they have survived and praising the cook. No one alluded to Ellen Olenska; but Archer knew Mrs. Welland was thinking: It is a mistake for Ellen to be seen, the very day after her arrival, parading up Fifth Avenue at the crowded hour with Julius Beaufort. And Archer himself mentally added, with the complacence of a man who believed he lived in a quieter, better-ordered soundscape: She ought to know that a man who’s just engaged doesn’t spend his time calling on married women. But I daresay in the set she’s lived in they do—they never do anything else.

And, in spite of the cosmopolitan views on which he prided himself, he thanked heaven that he was a New Yorker, and about to ally himself with one of his own kind.

The next evening old Mr. Sillerton Jackson came to dine with the Archers.

Mrs. Archer, who had been a widow so long that the fact had the quiet air of a family custom, shrank from society even while she hungered to know its flavour; she liked information as other women liked preserves—sealed, labelled, and brought out at the right hour to be tasted in small spoonfuls. For this purpose she kept, among her few indulgences, an old friend who had turned gossip into a science. Mr. Sillerton Jackson applied to his acquaintances’ affairs the patience of a collector, the classification of a naturalist; and his sister, Miss Sophy Jackson, who lived with him and was entertained everywhere by those who could not secure the brother, brought home the lesser scraps—crusts and crumbs of hearsay—that helped complete his dish.

So, whenever anything happened that Mrs. Archer wished to have served up plainly to her and to Janey, she asked Mr. Jackson to dine. And because she honoured few people with her invitations, and because she and Janey made such an attentive audience—leaning forward, eyes bright, as if a name might carry a scent—Mr. Jackson usually came himself instead of sending Miss Sophy.

If he could have chosen all the conditions, he would have preferred the nights when Newland was out—not because the young man was uncongenial (at their club they went on capitally), but because Mr. Jackson felt, from Newland, a certain inconvenient habit of weighing evidence, of asking how a tale was cooked, while the ladies accepted it as it was set before them. Perfection, besides, would have required that Mrs. Archer’s dinners be a little better.

But New York, as far back as memory could reach, had been divided into two fundamental tribes. There were the Mingotts and the Mansons and their clan, who cared about eating, clothes, and money—who knew the season of a bird by the look of its breast, the age of a wine by the sound its cork made in leaving the bottle. And there were the Archer-Newland-van der Luyden people, who were devoted to travel, horticulture, and “the best fiction,” and looked down—gently, serenely—on the grosser forms of pleasure, as if appetite were something one ought to keep decorously in the pantry.

You could not have everything. If you dined with the Lovell Mingotts you got canvas-back and terrapin and vintage wines that spoke, on the tongue, of distant cellars and sea-voyages. At Adeline Archer’s you could talk about Alpine scenery and The Marble Faun; and luckily the Archer Madeira had indeed gone round the Cape and come home with a briny depth, as if it remembered the creak of rigging and the long breath of ocean.

Therefore when Mrs. Archer’s summons came, Mr. Jackson, true eclectic, would say to his sister: “I’ve been a little gouty since my last dinner at the Lovell Mingotts’—it will do me good to diet at Adeline’s.”

Mrs. Archer lived with her son and daughter in West Twenty-eighth Street. An upper floor was given over to Newland, and the two women squeezed themselves into narrower quarters below, as if the house were a loaf and the best slice had properly gone to the man. They passed their days in an unclouded harmony of tastes and interests: they cultivated ferns in Wardian cases, whose damp glass held the smell of dark soil and sprouting life; they made macramé lace and wool embroidery on linen, their fingers busy while their minds wandered through imagined landscapes; they collected American revolutionary glazed ware—plates that held, in their crazed surfaces, the faint echo of old kitchens; they subscribed to Good Words; they read Ouida for the Italian atmosphere, preferring the peasant novels because the scenery was ample and the sentiments pleasanter. They liked novels about society too, where motives and habits were more comprehensible; they spoke severely of Dickens, who “had never drawn a gentleman,” and thought Thackeray less at home in the great world than Bulwer—though Bulwer was beginning to be considered old-fashioned.

They were great lovers of scenery. It was what they chiefly sought abroad; architecture and painting they left to men, and especially to those learned persons who read Ruskin.

Mrs. Archer had been born a Newland; she and Janey, alike as sisters, were “true Newlands”—tall, pale, slightly round-shouldered, with long noses, sweet smiles, and a drooping distinction like that in certain faded Reynolds portraits. Their resemblance would have been complete had not an elderly embonpoint stretched Mrs. Archer’s black brocade, while Janey’s brown and purple poplins hung, year by year, more slackly on her unclaimed frame.

Mentally the likeness was less perfect than their identical turns of phrase suggested. The long habit of living together in mutually dependent intimacy had given them the same vocabulary, the same habit of beginning sentences with “Mother thinks” or “Janey thinks,” according as one or the other wished to float her own opinion as if it had been brewed in a common pot. Yet Mrs. Archer’s serene unimaginativeness rested easily in the accepted and familiar; Janey was subject to little starts and aberrations of fancy, romantic bubbles rising from springs she kept carefully covered.

They adored each other and revered their son and brother. Newland loved them with a tenderness made compunctious and uncritical by the sense of their exaggerated admiration, and by his secret satisfaction in it. After all, he thought it good for a man to have his authority respected in his own house, even if his sense of humour sometimes made him smile at the solemnity of his own decrees.

This evening he was sure Mr. Jackson would rather have had him dine out. But Newland had his own reasons for staying.

Of course old Jackson wanted to talk about Ellen Olenska. Of course Mrs. Archer and Janey wanted to hear what he could tell. All three would be a little embarrassed by Newland’s presence now that his prospective relation to the Mingott clan was known; and he waited, with amused curiosity, to see how they would work around the difficulty—how they would set the dish down without naming it.

They began obliquely, with Mrs. Lemuel Struthers.

“It’s a pity the Beauforts asked her,” Mrs. Archer said gently, as if she were regretting a spoiled custard. “But then Regina always does what he tells her; and Beaufort—”

“Certain nuances escape Beaufort,” Mr. Jackson observed, cautiously inspecting the broiled shad. He had the patient, faintly despairing look of a connoisseur confronted with earnest ignorance. For the thousandth time he wondered why Mrs. Archer’s cook always burnt the roe to a cinder—turning what should have been creamy, saline richness into a bitter charcoal that smelled faintly of over-hot iron. Newland, who had long shared his wonder, could always detect it in the older man’s expression of melancholy disapproval.

“Oh, necessarily; Beaufort is a vulgar man,” Mrs. Archer said. “My grandfather Newland always used to say to my mother: ‘Whatever you do, don’t let that fellow Beaufort be introduced to the girls.’ But at least he’s had the advantage of associating with gentlemen; in England too, they say. It’s all very mysterious—” She glanced at Janey and paused. She and Janey knew every fold of the Beaufort mystery, but in public Mrs. Archer continued to behave as though certain subjects, like certain foods, were not for the unmarried.

“But this Mrs. Struthers,” she resumed; “what did you say she was, Sillerton?”

“Out of a mine,” Mr. Jackson answered, “or rather out of the saloon at the head of the pit. Then with Living Wax-Works, touring New England. After the police broke that up, they say she lived—” He in his turn glanced at Janey, whose eyes began to bulge with avid curiosity from under her prominent lids. There were still gaps in Mrs. Struthers’s past where Janey’s imagination could wander like a child into a pantry.

“Then,” Mr. Jackson continued—and Newland saw him, even while speaking, note with inward irritation that the cucumbers had been sliced with a steel knife, giving them that faint metallic tang which no amount of vinegar can disguise—“then Lemuel Struthers came along. They say his advertiser used the girl’s head for the shoe-polish posters; her hair’s intensely black, you know—the Egyptian style. Anyhow, he—eventually—married her.”

There were volumes of innuendo in the spacing of that “eventually,” each syllable weighed as carefully as spice.

“Oh, well—at the pass we’ve come to nowadays, it doesn’t matter,” Mrs. Archer returned, with an indifference too deliberate not to betray that her mind was elsewhere.

The ladies were not interested in Mrs. Struthers. Ellen Olenska was too new, too exciting a flavour. Indeed, Mrs. Struthers had been introduced only that Mrs. Archer might soon say, with the least possible appearance of eagerness: “And Newland’s new cousin—Countess Olenska? Was she at the ball too?”

There was a faint touch of sarcasm in her reference to her son, and he knew it and had expected it. Even Mrs. Archer, who was seldom unduly pleased by human events, had been altogether glad of Newland’s engagement—especially after “that silly business with Mrs. Rushworth,” as she had once remarked to Janey, alluding to what had seemed to Newland, at the time, a tragedy that would scar him for life.

There was no better match in New York than May Welland, whichever way one looked at it. Such a marriage was only what Newland was entitled to; but young men were foolish and incalculable, and some women ensnaring and unscrupulous. To see one’s only son safe past the Siren Isle and in the haven of blameless domesticity seemed almost miraculous.

Newland knew his mother felt all this; he knew also that she had been perturbed by the premature announcement of the engagement, or rather by its cause. And because on the whole he was a tender and indulgent master, he had stayed at home tonight.

“It’s not that I don’t approve of the Mingotts’ esprit de corps,” Mrs. Archer had grumbled to Janey in private, “but why Newland’s engagement should be mixed up with that Olenska woman’s comings and goings I don’t see.” She had behaved beautifully—beautiful behaviour was her element—during the call on Mrs. Welland; but Newland knew (and May likely guessed) that all through the visit his mother and sister had been nervously on watch for Madame Olenska’s possible intrusion. And when they left the Wellands she had permitted herself: “I’m thankful that Augusta Welland received us alone.”

The inward disturbance moved Newland the more because he, too, felt the Mingotts had gone a little too far. Yet it was against every rule of their code that mother and son should ever touch what lay uppermost in their thoughts. So he had merely answered: “There’s always a phase of family parties to be gone through when one gets engaged, and the sooner it’s over the better.”

His mother had pursed her lips under the lace veil that hung from her grey velvet bonnet trimmed with frosted grapes.

Now he sensed her lawful revenge: she would draw Mr. Jackson out on Countess Olenska before him; and having publicly done his duty as a future member of the Mingott clan, he had no objection to hearing the lady discussed in private—except that the subject was already beginning to weary him with its predictable vibrations.

Mr. Jackson helped himself to a slice of the tepid filet the mournful butler offered with a sceptical look, and rejected the mushroom sauce after a scarcely perceptible sniff. He looked baffled and hungry, as though he would have to finish his meal on scandal.

He leaned back, glanced up at the candlelit faces of Archers, Newlands and van der Luydens in their dark frames on the dark walls—ancestral mouths set in permanent discretion, as if every portrait were listening and disapproving.

“Ah, how your grandfather Archer loved a good dinner, my dear Newland!” he remarked, his eyes resting on the plump, full-chested young man in a stock and blue coat, with a white-columned country-house behind him. “Well—well—well… I wonder what he would have said to all these foreign marriages!”

Mrs. Archer ignored the allusion to ancestral appetite. Mr. Jackson, with deliberate satisfaction in prolonging what his audience had come for, continued: “No, she was not at the ball.”

“Ah—” Mrs. Archer murmured, in a tone meaning: She had that decency.

“Perhaps the Beauforts don’t know her,” Janey suggested, with an artless malice that was almost a spice.

Mr. Jackson gave a faint sip, as though tasting invisible Madeira, letting its imagined warmth travel down his throat. “Mrs. Beaufort may not—but Beaufort certainly does, for she was seen walking up Fifth Avenue this afternoon with him by the whole of New York.”

“Mercy—” moaned Mrs. Archer, as if the sound alone of Fifth Avenue had become louder, harsher, more exposed with that image: carriages rattling, voices rising, the city’s public ear pressed to their private acts.

“I wonder if she wears a round hat or a bonnet in the afternoon,” Janey speculated. “At the Opera I know she had on dark blue velvet, perfectly plain and flat—like a night-gown.”

“Janey!” her mother warned; and Janey blushed, then tried to look audacious.

“It was, at any rate, in better taste not to go to the ball,” Mrs. Archer went on.

A spirit of perversity stirred Newland. “I don’t think it was a question of taste with her. May said she meant to go, and then decided the dress in question wasn’t smart enough.”

Mrs. Archer smiled, satisfied to have her inference confirmed. “Poor Ellen,” she said simply; then, compassionately: “We must always bear in mind what an eccentric bringing-up Medora Manson gave her. What can you expect of a girl who was allowed to wear black satin at her coming-out ball?”

“Ah—don’t I remember her in it!” Mr. Jackson said, and added “Poor girl!” in the tone of one who had enjoyed the omen even as he recognized it.

“It’s odd,” Janey ventured, “that she should have kept such an ugly name as Ellen. I should have changed it to Elaine.” She glanced about the table to see what effect this small poetic rebellion might have.

Newland laughed. “Why Elaine?”

“I don’t know; it sounds more—more Polish,” Janey said, blushing at her own daring.

“It sounds more conspicuous,” Mrs. Archer replied distantly; “and that can hardly be what she wishes.”

“Why not?” Newland broke in, suddenly argumentative, as though a word—Ellen, Elaine—were not a trifle but a kind of chain. “Why shouldn’t she be conspicuous if she chooses? Why should she slink about as if it were she who had disgraced herself? She’s ‘poor Ellen’ certainly, because she had the bad luck to make a wretched marriage; but I don’t see that that’s a reason for hiding her head as if she were the culprit.”

“That, I suppose,” Mr. Jackson said speculatively, “is the line the Mingotts mean to take.”

Newland reddened. “I didn’t have to wait for their cue, if that’s what you mean, sir. Madame Olenska has had an unhappy life: that doesn’t make her an outcast.”

“There are rumours,” Mr. Jackson began, glancing at Janey.

“Oh, I know: the secretary,” Newland cut in. “Nonsense, mother; Janey’s grown-up. They say, don’t they, that the secretary helped her to get away from her brute of a husband, who kept her practically a prisoner? Well—what if he did? I hope there isn’t a man among us who wouldn’t have done the same in such a case.”

Mr. Jackson glanced over his shoulder to the sad butler and said, with an air of concession that was almost humorous: “Perhaps… that sauce… just a little, after all—” He helped himself, as if resigning to what was offered rather than desiring it, and then returned calmly to his point. “I’m told she’s looking for a house. She means to live here.”

“I hear she means to get a divorce,” Janey announced boldly, her voice a little too clear in the restrained room, as if a window had been opened on a street of louder sounds.

“I hope she will!” Newland exclaimed.

The word fell like a bombshell into the pure, tranquil atmosphere of the Archer dining-room. It seemed to ring, to have an echo of its own, as though even the heavy curtains could not fully muffle it. Mrs. Archer’s delicate eyebrows rose in the particular curve that signified: The butler—

And Newland, suddenly aware of the indecency of discussing such matters in public, branched off hastily into an account of his visit to old Mrs. Mingott, as if changing the subject were a napkin thrown over a stain.

After dinner, according to immemorial custom, Mrs. Archer and Janey trailed their long silk draperies up to the drawing-room. There, while the gentlemen smoked below stairs, they sat beside a Carcel lamp with an engraved globe, facing each other across a rosewood work-table with a green silk bag under it, and stitched at two ends of a tapestry band of field-flowers meant to adorn an “occasional” chair in the drawing-room of young Mrs. Newland Archer. Their needles made small, steady sounds; the lamp made a faint, confidential hiss; and in that soft domestic acoustics their hands translated colour into pattern—petals and stems that would outlast the day’s talk even if no one could ever name what, exactly, had been felt while making them.

Downstairs, in the Gothic library, Newland settled Mr. Jackson in an armchair near the fire and handed him a cigar. Mr. Jackson sank back with satisfaction, lit it with perfect confidence—Newland bought them—and stretched his thin old ankles toward the coals.

“You say the secretary merely helped her to get away, my dear fellow?” he asked. “Well, he was still helping her a year later, then; for somebody met ’em living at Lausanne together.”

Newland reddened. “Living together? Well, why not? Who had the right to make her life over if she hadn’t? I’m sick of the hypocrisy that would bury alive a woman of her age if her husband prefers to live with harlots.” He stopped, turned away angrily to light his cigar, as if the flame could cauterize his impatience. “Women ought to be free—as free as we are,” he declared, making a discovery whose consequences he was too irritated to measure.

Mr. Sillerton Jackson edged his ankles nearer the coals and emitted a sardonic whistle—thin, sharp, a little like the winter wind finding a crack in a window-frame.

“Well,” he said at length, “apparently Count Olenski takes your view; for I never heard of his having lifted a finger to get his wife back.”

That evening, when Mr. Sillerton Jackson had at last gathered up his comments as neatly as his gloves and taken himself away, and the ladies had withdrawn behind the chintz curtains of the bedroom—where a maid’s low voice, the click of a hairbrush, and the soft fall of silk upon silk made a little domestic music—Newland Archer went upstairs to his study.

Someone vigilant had kept the place as carefully tended as a kitchen range banked for morning: the coals nestled under their white ash, the lamp-wick trimmed to a steady, honey-coloured flame. The room received him with its familiar smells—leather bindings warmed by the fire, a faint peppery dust from old paper, the clean tang of oiled bronze on the mantel where “The Fencers” held their eternal pose. It was, in its way, as reassuring as the well-run back of a house: everything in readiness, everything in its place.

He sank into his chair by the hearth, and his eyes went, as they always went now, to the large photograph of May Welland. In the first days of his courtship she had given it to him with that straightforward air she had of offering what she had, without flourish and without reserve; and it had displaced every other portrait on the table, as a single, dominant flavour overwhelms the lesser notes of a dish.

Tonight he looked at it with a new, startled attention, as though he were tasting something he had believed he knew and suddenly found altered by an ingredient he had not noticed. The frank forehead, the serious eyes, the gay innocent mouth—features as familiar as the rooms of his mother’s house—seemed to look back at him from behind a veil. There she was, and yet there was also, superimposed upon her, the young creature his world had manufactured with such patient, ancestral skill: a girl who knew nothing and expected everything, not from vanity but from training; a soul kept, like a rare preserve, sealed from air and bruising touch.

And it came over him, with a chill like stepping out of a warm dining-room into the raw street, that marriage was not the snug anchorage he had always heard it called, but a voyage with reefs unmarked on any chart. The Countess Olenska—Ellen, with her foreign ways and her inconvenient candour—had stirred settled convictions from their resting place, as a spoon stirs sediment from the bottom of a cup, clouding the whole draught.

His own words came back to him, spoken in heat and with that easy confidence men had when they argued about women: Women should be free—as free as we are. It struck now like a knife pushed too far into a joint, cutting to the root of what everyone in his circle agreed not to name. “Nice” women, however wronged, would never claim such freedom; and men—generous-minded men like himself—were therefore lavish, in debate, in conceding it. Yet he saw, with the unpleasant clarity of a food historian recognizing adulteration in a familiar staple, how such verbal generosity masked the hard, binding conventions underneath. One spoke of liberty while tightening the knot.

Here was the bitter absurdity: he was pledged to defend, for May’s cousin, a line of conduct which, if it were his own wife’s, would justify him—by all the thunders of Church and State—in crushing her beneath condemnation. Of course, the predicament was “hypothetical.” He wasn’t a blackguard Polish nobleman; it was ridiculous to speculate on what his wife’s rights would be if he were. Yet he was too imaginative not to feel how the tie might gall for subtler reasons than brutality and betrayal. What could he and May truly know of each other, when it was his duty, as a decent fellow, to conceal his past, and hers, as a marriageable girl, to have no past to conceal?

He saw, as clearly as if he had been seated at the head of a long table tasting one marriage after another, how most unions around him were composed: the same dishes served in different houses, the same sauces disguising the same boiled facts. Suppose, without any gross scandal, they tired of one another; suppose they misunderstood, irritated, bruised. He reviewed the marriages of his friends—the “happy” ones—and found none that resembled the ardent, tender comradeship he had always pictured as his own fate with May. That picture, he began to understand, presupposed in her the very things she had been denied: experience, versatility, freedom of judgment. She had been trained, like a delicate fruit, to ripen without sun and wind; and with a foreboding shiver he saw his marriage becoming what so many others were: a dull association of material and social interests, held together by ignorance on one side and hypocrisy on the other.

Lawrence Lefferts floated up in his mind as the husband who had most completely achieved the social ideal. As high-priest of form, Larry had shaped his wife to his convenience as surely as a cook shapes a paste—pressing, smoothing, sealing out any air that might make it rise unexpectedly. In the most conspicuous moments of his frequent affairs with other men’s wives, Mrs. Lefferts moved about in smiling unconsciousness, saying that Lawrence was “so frightfully strict”; and she had been known to blush and turn away, as if offended by a coarse smell, when someone alluded to Julius Beaufort’s “other establishment”—a vulgarity that, in New York, was always attributed to foreigners as taints are attributed to imported meats.

Archer tried to console himself: he was not such an ass as Larry Lefferts, nor May such a simpleton as poor Gertrude. But the difference, he admitted, was one of intelligence, not of standards. In truth they all lived in a hieroglyphic world, where the real thing was never said or done or even thought, but only represented by agreed-upon signs—as when Mrs. Welland, who knew perfectly why Archer had pressed to have the engagement announced at the Beaufort ball (and had expected him to do no less), still felt obliged to simulate reluctance and the air of having her hand forced, like those “savage” brides described in the new books on Primitive Man—dragged shrieking from their parents’ tent to prove the value of the bargain.

The result was that the very girl at the centre of this elaborate system of mystification remained inscrutable, not in spite of her frankness but because of it. May was frank, poor darling, because she had nothing to conceal; assured because she knew of nothing to be on guard against. And with no better preparation than this, she was to be plunged overnight into what people, with the prudence of those who name nothing sharply, called “the facts of life”—as if they were a coarse cut of meat to be covered with sauce and served without mention of its origin.

He was sincerely, placidly in love.
