He delighted in her radiant good looks, her health, her horsemanship—the clean, outdoor scent that seemed to cling to her after a ride—her grace and quickness at games, and the shy interest in books and ideas she was beginning to develop under his guidance. She had advanced far enough to join him in ridiculing the Idyls of the King, but not to feel the strange beauty of Ulysses and the Lotus Eaters. She was straightforward, loyal, brave; and her sense of humour—chiefly proved by laughing at his jokes—made him feel, with an indulgent tenderness, that there were depths of feeling in her that it would be a joy to waken.

Yet each time he made this little inventory he came back discouraged, as if he had praised the sweetness of a fruit only to remember it had been ripened in a hothouse. This frankness and innocence were an artificial product. Untrained human nature was not frank and innocent; it was full of twists, defences, instinctive guile. He felt oppressed by this factitious purity, so cunningly manufactured by a conspiracy of mothers and aunts and grandmothers and long-dead ancestresses—manufactured because it was supposed to be what he wanted, what he had a right to, so that he might exercise his lordly pleasure in smashing it like an image made of snow.

There was, he knew, a certain triteness in such reflections; they were those habitual to young men nearing their wedding day. But they were usually seasoned with compunction and self-abasement, and of that Archer felt no trace. He could not deplore—like Thackeray’s heroes, who always exasperated him—that he had not a blank page to offer his bride in exchange for her unblemished one. He could not escape the knowledge that if he had been brought up as she had, they would both have been as helpless as babes in the wood; nor could he, for all his anxious turning-over, find any honest reason—any reason not tied to his own momentary pleasure and the male vanity of possession—why his bride should not have been allowed the same freedom of experience as himself.

Such thoughts, at such an hour, were bound to drift through a man’s mind; but Archer was aware that their uncomfortable persistence and their sharpness of outline came from the inopportune arrival of the Countess Olenska. Here he was, at the very moment of betrothal—a moment meant for pure thoughts and cloudless hopes—pitchforked into a coil of scandal that brought up all the special problems he would rather have let lie, like bones and gristle that spoil an appetite.

“Hang Ellen Olenska!” he muttered, as he covered the fire and began to undress.

He could not honestly see why her fate should have the least bearing on his; and yet, somewhere in him, he felt that he had only begun to measure the risks of the championship his engagement had forced upon him.

A few days later the bolt fell.

The Lovell Mingotts sent out cards for what was known as a formal dinner—three extra footmen, two dishes for each course, and a Roman punch in the middle, that frosty, citrus-laced interlude that arrived like a bell struck between movements—and they headed the invitations with the words: To meet the Countess Olenska. It was the hospitable American fashion, treating strangers as if they were royalties, or at least their ambassadors; as though to offer a new arrival one’s most elaborate table were the same as offering her a treaty.

The guest list had been chosen with a boldness and discrimination in which the initiated recognised the firm hand of old Catherine—Catherine the Great, as people called her behind fans and in carriage corners. Alongside immemorial standbys like the Selfridge Merrys, who were asked everywhere because they always had been, were the Beauforts, for whom there was a claim of relationship, and Mr. Sillerton Jackson and his sister Sophy, who went wherever her brother told her to go. There were also some of the most fashionable and yet most irreproachable of the “young married” set: the Lawrence Leffertses; Mrs. Lefferts Rushworth, the lovely widow; the Harry Thorleys; the Reggie Chiverses; young Morris Dagonet and his wife, who was a van der Luyden. It was, in short, a perfectly assorted company—people who, through the long season, moved together daily and nightly with undiminished zest, like a well-trained chorus that never missed an entrance.

And then, forty-eight hours later, the unbelievable happened.

Everyone refused the Mingotts’ invitation except the Beauforts and old Mr. Jackson and his sister.

The intended slight was sharpened by the fact that even the Reggie Chiverses—of the Mingott clan—were among those inflicting it; and by the uniform wording of the notes: every one “regretted that they were unable to accept,” without even the decent varnish of a “previous engagement.” In a society so small, so tightly laced together, and so scantly supplied with evenings and entertainments, everyone—including livery-stable keepers, butlers, and cooks—knew exactly when people were free. The refusals were not merely refusals; they were a proclamation, sounded without mercy through every pantry and kitchen in town, that the writers were determined not to meet the Countess Olenska.

New York’s snubs travelled not only on paper but on sound. The whisper at the fishmonger’s, the suppressed laugh in a servant’s hall, the rattle of a delivery cart pausing just long enough for the man on the box to trade news with a footman at an area door—these were the city’s true messengers. And for all its elegance, the place had an undertone, a constant clink and murmur, in which reputations were carried like scents on air.

The blow was unexpected; but the Mingotts, as their way was, met it gallantly. Mrs. Lovell Mingott confided the case to Mrs. Welland; Mrs. Welland confided it to Newland Archer; and Archer, aflame at the outrage, appealed passionately and authoritatively to his mother.

Mrs. Archer resisted inwardly, temporised outwardly, and then—because she always succumbed to his instances—gave way at last. Once she had yielded, she embraced his cause with an energy redoubled by her earlier hesitation. She put on her grey velvet bonnet, as solemnly as one might tie on an apron before undertaking a task that must be done properly, and said: “I’ll go and see Louisa van der Luyden.”

The New York of Archer’s day was a small, slippery pyramid, still uncracked, in which footholds were hard won and quickly lost. At its base stood the firm foundation of what Mrs. Archer called “plain people”—an honourable but obscure majority of respectable families who had been raised a little above their level by marriage into a ruling clan. People, Mrs. Archer always said, were not as particular as they used to be; and with old Catherine Spicer ruling one end of Fifth Avenue and Julius Beaufort the other, one couldn’t expect the old traditions to last much longer.

Narrowing upward from this wealthy but inconspicuous substratum was the compact, dominant group the Mingotts, Newlands, Chiverses, and Mansons so actively represented. Most people imagined them to be the apex; but those of Mrs. Archer’s generation knew that, in the eyes of professional genealogy, only a still smaller number could claim that height.

“Don’t tell me,” Mrs. Archer would say, “all this modern newspaper rubbish about a New York aristocracy. If there is one, neither the Mingotts nor the Mansons belong to it; no, nor the Newlands or the Chiverses either. Our grandfathers and great-grandfathers were respectable English or Dutch merchants who came to the colonies to make their fortune and stayed because they did so well. One of your great-grandfathers signed the Declaration, another was a general on Washington’s staff and received General Burgoyne’s sword after Saratoga. These are things to be proud of; but they have nothing to do with rank or class. New York has always been a commercial community, and there are not more than three families in it who can claim aristocratic origin in the real sense of the word.”

Mrs. Archer and her children knew, as everyone knew, who those privileged beings were: the Dagonets of Washington Square, descended from an old English county family allied with the Pitts and Foxes; the Lannings, who had intermarried with descendants of Count de Grasse; and the van der Luydens, direct descendants of the first Dutch governor of Manhattan and connected by pre-Revolutionary marriages with French and British aristocracy.

The Lannings survived only in the persons of two very old but lively Miss Lannings, who lived cheerfully among family portraits and Chippendale, feeding on memory as on a well-stocked pantry. The Dagonets were a considerable clan, allied to the best names in Baltimore and Philadelphia. But the van der Luydens, who stood above all of them, had faded into a kind of super-terrestrial twilight, from which only two figures emerged with impressive distinctness: Mr. and Mrs. Henry van der Luyden.

Mrs. Henry van der Luyden had been Louisa Dagonet. Her mother had been the granddaughter of Colonel du Lac—of an old Channel Island family—who had fought under Cornwallis and, after the war, settled in Maryland with his bride, Lady Angelica Trevenna, fifth daughter of the Earl of St. Austrey. The ties between the Dagonets, the du Lacs, and their Cornish kinsfolk the Trevennas had remained close and cordial, like an old recipe handed down in a household where the original tongue was still spoken over the kneading-board.

Mr. and Mrs. van der Luyden had more than once paid long visits to the present head of the house, the Duke of St. Austrey, at his seat in Cornwall and at St. Austrey in Gloucestershire; and his Grace had often announced his intention of returning their visit—without the Duchess, who feared the Atlantic.

They divided their time between Trevenna, their place in Maryland, and Skuytercliff, the great estate on the Hudson granted in colonial days to the famous first Governor, and of which Mr. van der Luyden was still “Patroon.” Their large, solemn house on Madison Avenue was seldom opened; and when they came to town they received there only their most intimate friends. Their silence had the authority of a held note; when they chose to speak, the whole chorus adjusted pitch.

“I wish you would go with me, Newland,” his mother said, pausing at the door of the brown coupé. In the street below, wheels hissed over winter grit, a hawker called, a far-off hammer rang—modern noises always pushing in at the edges of the old quiet. “Louisa is fond of you; and of course it’s on account of dear May that I’m taking this step—and also because, if we don’t all stand together, there’ll be no such thing as Society left.”

Mrs. Henry van der Luyden heard her cousin’s account as she heard everything: without haste, without interruption, as though words were a broth that must be left to settle so the fat could rise and be skimmed. Mrs. Archer, who had rehearsed her story on the drive up, told it again in that high, white Madison Avenue drawing-room where even sound seemed domesticated—muffled by gauze-draped ormolu and shrouded frames, softened as if the house itself feared the least clatter. The air held a faint, stale sweetness, like linen long laid away with sachets; and beneath the monumental tick of the clock on the marble mantel, Mrs. Archer’s indignation seemed to fall, drop by drop, into a silence that did not answer back.

It was all very well to remind oneself, in advance, that Mrs. van der Luyden was always silent, and that her silence—trained into her, like a manner of serving—could be a kind of kindness. Yet the chill came all the same. The room was too lofty, too pale, too prepared: brocaded armchairs uncovered for the occasion as carefully as a family silver tureen uncovered for guests; the great painted faces—the Gainsborough ancestress and Huntington’s “perfect likeness”—watching with that unblinking authority which had outlasted generations of dinners, engagements, scandals, and reconciliations.

Newland Archer, sitting beside his mother, watched Mrs. van der Luyden beneath her own portrait and thought how little time did to her. She still wore black velvet and Venetian point when she admitted society into her rooms—since she never dined out, she received as one performs a rite. Her fair hair had faded without turning grey; her pale blue eyes looked, not coldly, but as if preserved—life held in suspension, like fruit in ice. He esteemed her, as he had been taught to; but her sweetness—bent, compliant, and always deferring—was less approachable than the blunt refusals of the fierce old spinsters in his family, who said “No” on principle and only afterward asked what had been proposed.

Mrs. van der Luyden’s face, on the other hand, said neither yes nor no. It leaned toward mercy until her thin lips wavered into the shadow of a smile and the customary words appeared, laid on the conversation like a lid: “I shall first have to talk this over with my husband.”

Archer had often wondered—watching the two of them, so exactly matched—how, after forty years of living in one another’s thoughts, two such merged identities ever separated enough for anything as controversial as a “talking-over.” Yet it was the consecrated formula; and Mrs. Archer, having set forth the case, waited resignedly for it.

But Mrs. van der Luyden did something unaccustomed. Her long hand went out toward the bell-rope.

“I think,” she said, “I should like Henry to hear what you have told me.”

A footman appeared, soundless as a shadow. Mrs. van der Luyden addressed him gravely: “If Mr. van der Luyden has finished reading the newspaper, please ask him to be kind enough to come.”

She said “reading the newspaper” as another woman might have said “presiding at a Cabinet meeting”—not from vanity, but because her whole life, and the posture of her world toward her husband, had taught her that his smallest acts carried weight. Then, as if to keep her promptness from being mistaken for commitment, she added, with the gentlest look: “Henry always enjoys seeing you, dear Adeline; and he will wish to congratulate Newland.”

The double doors reopened with the same solemnity as a church’s, and Mr. Henry van der Luyden appeared between them: tall, spare, frock-coated, his faded fair hair brushed back, his straight nose dividing pale grey eyes that held the same frozen gentleness as his wife’s. He offered Mrs. Archer cousinly affability, gave Newland low-voiced congratulations in nearly the same words Mrs. van der Luyden had used, and seated himself in a brocaded armchair with the unstudied simplicity of a man born to be deferred to.

“I had just finished reading the Times,” he said, laying his long fingertips together. “In town my mornings are so much occupied that I find it more convenient to read the newspapers after luncheon.”

Mrs. Archer, ever ready with the right response, answered as one hands across the table the dish expected of her. “Ah, there’s a great deal to be said for that plan—indeed I think my uncle Egmont used to say he found it less agitating not to read the morning papers till after dinner.”

“Yes: my good father abhorred hurry. But now we live in a constant rush,” said Mr. van der Luyden, looking about him with a measured benevolence, as though the shrouded room were an emblem of the world he regretted.

“But I hope you had finished your reading, Henry?” his wife interposed quickly.

“Quite—quite,” he assured her.

“Then I should like Adeline to tell you—”

“Oh, it’s really Newland’s story,” Mrs. Archer smiled; and as if beginning again at the beginning were as necessary as the careful repeating of a recipe, she rehearsed once more the monstrous tale of the affront to Mrs. Lovell Mingott: the dinner planned, the invitations sent, the Leffertses’ refusal, and the whispered implication that the Countess Olenska was too questionable to be met.

“Of course,” she ended, “Augusta Welland and Mary Mingott both felt that, especially in view of Newland’s engagement, you and Henry ought to know.”

“Ah—” said Mr. van der Luyden, and drew a deep breath.

The silence that followed made the clock’s tick swell until it seemed to pound like a signal gun. Newland looked at the two slender, faded figures sitting side by side in viceregal rigidity, mouthpieces of that remote ancestral authority which the city still obeyed even while it pretended to outgrow it. In his mind—because the van der Luydens always roused in him the image of sheltered routine—he saw them far away at Skuytercliff, in the hush of clipped lawns where the loudest thing might be a distant mower or a sparrow’s quarrel, bending over their invisible weeds and playing Patience in the evenings. Their lives were arranged like a carefully planned household menu: simple, repetitive, and so exact that any new flavor—any foreign spice—was treated as a danger to digestion.

Mr. van der Luyden spoke first. “You really think this is due to some—some intentional interference of Lawrence Lefferts’s?” he asked, turning to Newland.

“I’m certain of it, sir,” Archer said. He felt again, as he spoke, the ugly relish of Lefferts’s maneuver—the way a man could salt his own sins by loudly condemning another’s supposed impropriety. “Larry has been going it rather harder than usual lately—if cousin Louisa won’t mind my mentioning it—having rather a stiff affair with the postmaster’s wife in their village, or some one of that sort; and whenever poor Gertrude Lefferts begins to suspect anything, and he’s afraid of trouble, he gets up a fuss of this kind, to show how awfully moral he is, and talks at the top of his voice about the impertinence of inviting his wife to meet people he doesn’t wish her to know. He’s simply using Madame Olenska as a lightning-rod; I’ve seen him try the same thing often before.”

“The Leffertses!” said Mrs. van der Luyden, and in her faint emphasis there was horror, as if someone had suggested serving fish with a sweet sauce.

“The Leffertses!” echoed Mrs. Archer. “What would uncle Egmont have said of Lawrence Lefferts’s pronouncing on anybody’s social position? It shows what Society has come to.”

“We’ll hope it has not quite come to that,” Mr. van der Luyden returned firmly.

“Ah, if only you and Louisa went out more!” Mrs. Archer sighed—and instantly felt the mistake in her own ears, as one hears a dish crack in the kitchen and knows at once it cannot be repaired.

The van der Luydens were morbidly sensitive to criticism of their seclusion. They bore, like a hereditary burden, the city’s expectation that they should sit as judges; yet they were shy, retiring people, with no taste for public power. They lived as much as possible away from New York’s roar, pleading Mrs. van der Luyden’s health when invitations came, and protecting their quiet as farmers protect seed-corn. Newland, hearing his mother’s stumble, stepped in at once.

“Everybody in New York knows what you and cousin Louisa represent,” he said. “That’s why Mrs. Mingott felt she ought not to allow this slight on Countess Olenska to pass without consulting you.”

Mrs. van der Luyden glanced at her husband; he glanced back. Their pale eyes held together in that long, inward conference which always preceded a decision, as if they were translating the city’s noisy gossip into the older, more cautious dialect of their own conscience.

“It is the principle that I dislike,” Mr. van der Luyden said at last. “As long as a member of a well-known family is backed up by that family it should be considered—final.”

“It seems so to me,” Mrs. van der Luyden added, with the air of offering a new thought rather than repeating an inherited rule.

“I had no idea,” Mr. van der Luyden continued, “that things had come to such a pass.”

He paused, then looked again at his wife. “It occurs to me, my dear, that the Countess Olenska is already a sort of relation—through Medora Manson’s first husband. At any rate, she will be when Newland marries.” He turned to Archer. “Have you read this morning’s Times, Newland?”

“Why, yes, sir,” Archer answered. He had read it over his coffee, as he read half a dozen papers—New York feeding itself on print as steadily as it fed itself on oysters and roast.

Husband and wife exchanged another look. In that gaze, a plan formed with the quiet certainty of a household decision: what was to be served, to whom, and when—because in their world an invitation could be as decisive as a verdict. A faint smile fluttered over Mrs. van der Luyden’s face; she had evidently guessed and approved.

Mr. van der Luyden turned to Mrs. Archer. “If Louisa’s health allowed her to dine out—I wish you would say to Mrs. Lovell Mingott—she and I would have been happy to—er—fill the places of the Lawrence Leffertses at her dinner.”

He left a pause, letting the irony sink in as naturally as a spoon left standing in a thick custard.

“As you know, this is impossible,” he added.

Mrs. Archer gave the sympathetic assent required.

“But Newland tells me he has read this morning’s Times; therefore he has probably seen that Louisa’s relative, the Duke of St. Austrey, arrives next week on the Russia. He is coming to enter his new sloop, the Guinevere, in next summer’s International Cup Race; and also to have a little canvasback shooting at Trevenna.”

Mr. van der Luyden’s measured voice went on, growing almost kindly with the prospect of action. “Before taking him down to Maryland we are inviting a few friends to meet him here—only a little dinner—with a reception afterward. I am sure Louisa will be as glad as I am if Countess Olenska will let us include her among our guests.”

He rose, bending his long body with stiff friendliness toward his cousin and her son. “I think I have Louisa’s authority for saying that she will herself leave the invitation to dine when she drives out presently: with our cards—of course with our cards.”

Mrs. Archer, who understood at once that the famous seventeen-hand chestnuts would not be kept waiting, rose with hurried murmurs of gratitude. Mrs. van der Luyden beamed on her with a look like a gentle intercession; but Mr. van der Luyden lifted a protesting hand.

“There is nothing to thank me for, dear Adeline; nothing whatever. This kind of thing must not happen in New York; it shall not, as long as I can help it,” he pronounced with sovereign gentleness, steering them toward the door as though conducting them from a private audience.

Outside, the city’s noise resumed—wheels, calls, the restless scrape of modern life against old stone—but the van der Luydens’ decision moved through it like a calm current.

Two hours later, everyone knew that the great C-spring barouche in which Mrs. van der Luyden took the air at all seasons had been seen at old Mrs. Mingott’s door, where a large square envelope had been handed in. It was discussed the way menus are discussed in kitchens and drawing-rooms: not only for what it contained, but for what it signified—what it would permit, what it would forbid, what it would make impossible to say aloud thereafter.

That evening at the Opera, Mr. Sillerton Jackson was able to state, with the pleased precision of a man citing a document, that the envelope had contained a card inviting the Countess Olenska to the dinner the van der Luydens were giving the following week for their cousin, the Duke of St. Austrey.

Some of the younger men in the club box exchanged a smile at the announcement and looked sideways at Lawrence Lefferts. He sat carelessly in the front, pulling his long fair moustache, hearing—if he heard at all—the layered soundscape of the house: the soprano’s suspended note, the soft stir of silk, the whisper that traveled faster than music. Then, as the singer paused, he remarked with authority, as if nothing else mattered and his taste were the only law worth naming:

“No one but Patti ought to attempt the Sonnambula.”

It was generally agreed in New York that the Countess Olenska had lost her looks; and New York said it with the same finality with which it judged a sauce spoiled, a roast overdone, a family gone a shade too far toward the unknown. People remembered her first appearance as if it had been a course served long ago at a celebrated table: a brilliantly pretty child of nine or ten, all bright color and tight curls, a little thing whom one “ought to paint,” as though she were fruit arranged on a silver dish.

Her parents had been continental wanderers—those restless souls who live out of trunks and timetables, who change cities the way a cook changes pans—until death took them both and the child was handed over to her aunt, Medora Manson. Poor Medora, repeatedly widowed, was always returning to New York to “settle down,” each time in a smaller house, as if the city were a farm that would take her back after every failed season. She would come with a new husband or an adopted child, and within a few months she would part from the husband or quarrel with the ward, sell the house at a loss, and set off again—packing up her rooms the way a careless hostess clears a table, sweeping away what she cannot keep.

New York, having long since resigned itself to Medora’s oddities—she had the Rushworth blood, and had been linked by an unhappy marriage to one of the crazy Chiverses—watched her with indulgence. But when she returned with the orphaned niece, people thought it a pity that such a pretty child should be in such hands. Everyone meant to be kind to little Ellen Mingott, though her dusky red cheeks and gaiety seemed improper for a child who, by the unalterable rules of American mourning, ought still to have been steeped in black, like tea left too long to draw.

Medora flouted those rules the way a foreign cook flouts a New York receipt. When she stepped from the steamer, the family were scandalized to see that the crape veil she wore for her own brother was inches shorter than those of her sisters-in-law, while little Ellen appeared in crimson merino with amber beads—warm, glowing, and audacious, like a gipsy foundling brought into a parlor that smelled of starch and solemnity. Yet New York had known Medora too long to be truly shocked; only a few old ladies shook their heads, while the rest of the relations fell, in spite of themselves, under the child’s charm.

Ellen was fearless and familiar, with disconcerting questions and precocious comments. She had outlandish arts too—dancing a Spanish shawl dance, singing Neapolitan love-songs to a guitar—things that set the drawing-room air quivering with unfamiliar rhythms, like spices stirred into a bland broth. Under her aunt—whose real name was Mrs. Thorley Chivers, but who, having received a Papal title, had resumed her first husband’s patronymic and called herself the Marchioness Manson because in Italy she could turn it into Manzoni—Ellen received an expensive, incoherent education: “drawing from the model,” which New York regarded as an indecent ingredient, and playing the piano in quintets with professional musicians, as if a respectable girl were meant to be served up in public.

Of course no good could come of it, New York said, with the satisfied sound of a maxim laid down like a carving-knife. When poor Chivers finally died in a madhouse, his widow—draped in strange weeds—pulled up stakes again and departed with Ellen, now grown into a tall, bony girl with conspicuous eyes. For a time no more was heard of them; then news came of Ellen’s marriage to an immensely rich Polish nobleman of legendary fame, met at a ball at the Tuileries—one of those glittering occasions that New York imagined as a banquet it had not been invited to, with candles and music and air too thick with perfume. He was said to have princely establishments in Paris, Nice, and Florence, a yacht at Cowes, and many square miles of shooting in Transylvania. Ellen vanished into that tale like a flame swallowed by smoke: a kind of sulphurous apotheosis.

A few years later Medora came back yet again—subdued, impoverished, mourning a third husband, seeking a still smaller house—and people wondered that her rich niece had not been able to do something for her. Then came the heavier news: Ellen’s own marriage had ended in disaster, and she was returning home to seek rest and oblivion among her kin.

These things went through Newland Archer’s mind a week later as he watched the Countess Olenska enter the van der Luyden drawing-room on the evening of the momentous dinner. The occasion had the hush and solemnity of a ceremony; even the silences seemed arranged and served at the proper intervals, as carefully as courses. Archer, who could smile at his old New York while cherishing it, felt a nervous curiosity as to how she would carry herself in the presence of that most chosen company, assembled with an awful correctness.

She came rather late, one hand still ungloved and fastening a bracelet at her wrist, and yet she entered without haste or embarrassment, as if time were an element she could season to her own taste. In the middle of the room she paused, looking about her with a grave mouth and smiling eyes; and in that instant Archer rejected the verdict that she had “lost her looks.”

It was true her early radiance had gone. The high red cheeks had paled. She was thin, worn, and a little older-looking than her age—nearly thirty, Archer guessed. But there was about her the mysterious authority of beauty: a sure carriage of the head, an exact movement of the eyes, trained without being theatrical, conscious without being loud. She was simpler in manner than most of the women present, and many were disappointed afterward, Janey would report, that she was not more “stylish”—for stylishness was what New York valued most, as if it were the last gloss on a finished dish. Perhaps it was because Ellen’s early vivacity had disappeared; because she was quiet—quiet in her movements, her voice, and the low pitch of it. New York, expecting something more resonant from such a history, found itself disconcerted by her restraint.

Dinner with the van der Luydens was at best no light matter; with a Duke who was their cousin, it became almost a religious solemnity. Archer, who understood his city’s palate, enjoyed the thought that only an old New Yorker could taste the difference—so immense to New York—between being merely a Duke and being the van der Luydens’ Duke. Stray noblemen were taken calmly, even with a certain distrustful hauteur, except in the Struthers set; but noblemen who arrived with such credentials were received with an old-fashioned cordiality they would have been mistaken to attribute solely to Debrett.

The van der Luydens had emphasized the importance of the occasion with the mute eloquence of porcelain and plate. The du Lac Sèvres and the Trevenna George II silver were out; so was the van der Luyden “Lowestoft” and the Dagonet Crown Derby. The table gleamed with that cold, ancestral brilliance that seemed to chill the very clink of spoons. Mrs. van der Luyden looked more than ever like a Cabanel, and Mrs. Archer, wearing her grandmother’s seed-pearls and emeralds, reminded her son of an Isabey miniature. All the ladies wore their handsomest jewels, yet the house and the occasion insisted, characteristically, on old-fashioned settings—heavy, inherited, unquestioned. Old Miss Lanning, persuaded to come, actually wore her mother’s cameos and a Spanish blonde shawl, the lace lying on her shoulders like a relic.

The Countess Olenska was the only young woman at the table; and as Archer scanned the smooth, plump elderly faces rising from diamond necklaces and towering ostrich feathers, they struck him as curiously immature beside hers. That maturity—etched in fine lines, in the steadiness of her gaze—frightened him. He thought, with a kind of inward recoil, of what must have gone to the making of her eyes.

The Duke of St. Austrey, seated at his hostess’s right, was nominally the chief figure; yet if the Countess was less conspicuous than people had hoped, the Duke was almost invisible. He had not committed the vulgarity of arriving in a shooting-jacket, but his evening clothes were shabby and baggy, worn with an air of being as homely as a workman’s; and with his stooping way of sitting, and the vast beard spreading over his shirt-front, he scarcely looked dressed for dinner at all. He was short, round-shouldered, sunburnt, with a thick nose, small eyes, and a sociable smile; but he seldom spoke, and when he did, it was in tones so low that—despite the frequent expectant silences about the table—his remarks were lost to all but his neighbors. Even his voice seemed to withdraw from the room’s careful acoustics, as if the Duke refused to contribute to the soundscape that New York so strictly managed: the rustle of silk, the measured laugh, the discreet murmur that must never rise into anything like clamor.

When the men joined the ladies after dinner, the Duke went straight up to the Countess Olenska; they sat down in a corner and plunged into animated talk. Neither seemed aware that etiquette required the Duke first to pay respects to Mrs. Lovell Mingott and Mrs. Headly Chivers, nor the Countess to converse with the amiable hypochondriac Mr. Urban Dagonet of Washington Square—who, for the pleasure of meeting her, had broken through his fixed rule of not dining out between January and April. The two talked for nearly twenty minutes, their voices making a little private weather in the corner: a warmer current amid the room’s controlled air.

Then the Countess rose and walked alone across the wide drawing-room and sat down at Archer’s side.

It was not the custom in New York drawing-rooms for a lady to get up and leave one gentleman in order to seek another. A woman was expected to remain—immovable as an idol—while the men who wished to speak to her succeeded one another. But the Countess seemed unaware she had broken any rule. She settled herself at ease in the corner of the sofa beside Archer, and looked at him with the kindest eyes.

“I want you to talk to me about May,” she said.

Instead of answering, he asked: “You knew the Duke before?”

“Oh, yes—we used to see him every winter at Nice. He’s very fond of gambling—he used to come to the house a great deal.”

She said it as simply as if she had remarked that he was fond of wild-flowers; and then, with candid addendum, she added: “I think he’s the dullest man I ever met.”

The freedom of it pleased Archer so much that he forgot the slight shock of her mentioning the Duke’s visits. It was undeniably exciting to meet a woman who could find the van der Luydens’ Duke dull—and dare to say so. Archer longed to question her, to draw out more of that other life whose mere scraps of speech gave off such a pungent scent, like a jar opened briefly in a pantry where foreign herbs were kept. But he feared to touch distressing memories; and before he could find a safe question, she drifted back, as she had meant to from the first, to May.

“May is a darling; I’ve seen no young girl in New York so handsome and so intelligent. Are you very much in love with her?”

Archer reddened and laughed. “As much as a man can be.”

She considered him thoughtfully, as though she were tasting his words to detect their true flavor. “Do you think, then, there is a limit?”

“To being in love? If there is, I haven’t found it!”

She glowed with sympathy. “Ah—it’s really and truly a romance?”

“The most romantic of romances!”

“How delightful! And you found it all out for yourselves—it was not in the least arranged for you?”

Archer looked at her incredulously. “Have you forgotten,” he asked with a smile, “that in our country we don’t allow our marriages to be arranged for us?”

A dusky blush rose to her cheek, and he regretted his words at once, as if he had brought a harsh metallic taste into a delicate dish.

“Yes,” she answered, “I’d forgotten. You must forgive me if I sometimes make these mistakes. I don’t always remember that everything here is good that was—that was bad where I’ve come from.”

She looked down at her Viennese fan of eagle feathers, and he saw her lips tremble. The room around them kept up its proper murmurs—soft talk laid over softer talk, laughter carefully leashed—yet in that tremor Archer heard another sound, a cracked note from a different world, and he understood how a life could be altered by the voices one must listen to, the words one must answer in, the very dialect of intimacy imposed by power.

“I’m so sorry,” he said impulsively; “but you are among friends here, you know.”

“Yes—I know. Wherever I go I have that feeling. That’s why I came home. I want to forget everything else, to become a complete American again, like the Mingotts and Wellands, and you and your delightful mother, and all the other good people here tonight.” She glanced toward the door and added, with a quietness that held him: “Ah, here’s May arriving, and you will want to hurry away to her.”

She did not move; her eyes turned back from the doorway to rest on Archer’s face.

The drawing-rooms were filling now with after-dinner guests, their entrances marked by a soft increase of sound—steps on thick carpet, greetings exchanged in the approved key, the low swell of many voices kept from becoming noise. Following Madame Olenska’s glance, Archer saw May Welland entering with her mother. In white and silver, a wreath of silver blossoms in her hair, the tall girl looked like a Diana just alight from the chase—fresh as winter air, bright as iced fruit.

“Oh,” Archer said, “I have so many rivals; you see she’s already surrounded. There’s the Duke being introduced.”

“Then stay with me a little longer,” Madame Olenska said in a low tone, and just touched his knee with her plumed fan.

It was the lightest touch; and yet it thrilled him like a caress, like the brush of fingers while passing a warm cup—an intimacy too small to name and too vivid to mistake.

“Yes, let me stay,” he answered in the same tone, scarcely knowing what he said.

But at that moment Mr. van der Luyden came up, followed by old Mr. Urban Dagonet. The Countess greeted them with her grave smile, and Archer—feeling his host’s admonitory glance like a hand at his shoulder—rose and surrendered his seat.

Madame Olenska held out her hand as if to bid him goodbye. “Tomorrow, then, after five—I shall expect you,” she said; and then turned back to make room for Mr. Dagonet.

“Tomorrow—” Archer heard himself repeating. There had been no formal engagement; she had given no hint during their talk that she wished to see him again. Yet the words, once spoken, seemed to bind like a ribbon knotted too quickly to be untied without notice.

As he moved away he saw Lawrence Lefferts, tall and resplendent, leading his wife up to be introduced; and heard Gertrude Lefferts beam on the Countess with her large unperceiving smile. “But I think we used to go to dancing-school together when we were children—.”

Behind her, waiting their turn to name themselves, Archer noticed a number of the recalcitrant couples who had declined to meet the Countess at Mrs. Lovell Mingott’s. The lesson had been served, and now they came to swallow it politely. As Mrs. Archer had remarked: when the van der Luydens chose, they knew how to give a lesson. The wonder was that they chose so seldom.

Archer felt a touch on his arm and turned to see Mrs. van der Luyden looking down on him from the pure eminence of black velvet and family diamonds, as if she were the high altar itself.

“It was good of you, dear Newland, to devote yourself so unselfishly to Madame Olenska,” she said. “I told your cousin Henry he must really come to the rescue.”

He smiled at her vaguely, and she added, as if condescending to his natural shyness: “I’ve never seen May looking lovelier. The Duke thinks her the handsomest girl in the room.”

The Countess Olenska had said “after five”; and at half after the hour Newland Archer rang the bell of the peeling stucco house with the giant wisteria throttling its feeble cast-iron balcony—an old plant with a patient, agrarian grip, as if it had been trained there by some stern hand long dead, and had never been told that the neighborhood had changed. The door’s iron gave back a tired little click; above it the street breathed its winter breath, and the thin soundscape of West Twenty-third—hoofbeats, a distant vendor calling, the soft scratch of a broom somewhere—made a kind of music precisely because it was not the grand metallic roar of the newer avenues.

It was certainly a strange quarter to have settled in. Small dressmakers, bird-stuffers, and “people who wrote” were her nearest neighbors; and farther down the dishevelled street Archer recognized a dilapidated wooden house at the end of a paved path—Winsett’s, the journalist’s, who never invited anyone in. Archer remembered a nocturnal walk when Winsett had pointed it out with a brief gesture, as if ashamed of the narrow porch and the poor light behind the window; and Archer had asked himself, with that nervous shiver one feels when a familiar word suddenly sounds foreign in the mouth, if the humanities were so meanly housed in other capitals too.

Madame Olenska’s own dwelling was redeemed from the same look only by a little more paint about the window-frames; and as Archer measured its modest front he told himself that the Polish Count must have robbed her of her fortune as well as of her illusions.

He had spent an unsatisfactory day. He had lunched with the Wellands, hoping afterward to carry off May for a walk in the Park—hoping, too, for one of those private pauses when words could be seasoned to taste, when a man could say softly, without witnesses and without the starch of ceremony, how enchanting his betrothed had looked the night before, and how proud he was of her, and press her—gently, ardently—to hasten their marriage. But Mrs. Welland had kept the afternoon carefully arranged, like a table spread for the family’s appetite: visits laid out in courses, each one to be tasted, praised, and swallowed in turn.

When he hinted at advancing the date, she had lifted her reproachful eyebrows and sighed out, as if counting jars in a pantry: “Twelve dozen of everything—hand-embroidered—”

Packed in the family landau they rolled from one tribal doorstep to another; and when the afternoon’s round was over, Archer parted from May with the unsettled feeling of having been shown off like some rare creature—caught, displayed, and carried about for approval. He supposed his readings in anthropology made him coarse, made him hear the ritual’s faint rattle as if it were a chain; but when he remembered that the Wellands did not expect the wedding to take place till the following autumn, and pictured the long months between—months of correctly paid calls and correctly repeated phrases—his spirit seemed to dampen like linen left too long in a cold scullery.

“Tomorrow,” Mrs. Welland called after him, “we’ll do the Chiverses and the Dallases”; and he perceived that she was going through their two families alphabetically, and that they were only in the first quarter of the alphabet.

He had meant to tell May of the Countess Olenska’s request—her command, rather—that he should call that afternoon; but in the brief moments when they were alone he had had more pressing things to say. Besides, it struck him as a little absurd to allude to it. He knew May most particularly wanted him to be kind to her cousin; was it not that wish which had hastened the announcement of their engagement? It gave him an odd sensation to reflect that, but for the Countess’s arrival, he might have been—if not still free—at least less irrevocably pledged. But May had willed it so; and he felt himself somehow relieved of further responsibility—and therefore at liberty, if he chose, to call on her cousin without telling her.

Now, on Madame Olenska’s threshold, curiosity was his uppermost feeling. He was puzzled by the tone in which she had summoned him; he concluded she was less simple than she seemed. The door was opened by a swarthy foreign-looking maid with a prominent bosom under a gay neckerchief—Sicilian, he vaguely fancied, because her glance seemed full of sun and salt. She welcomed him with all her white teeth, and, answering his enquiries by a head-shake of incomprehension, led him through the narrow hall into a low, firelit drawing-room.

The room was empty, and she left him—for an appreciable time—to wonder whether she had gone to find her mistress, or whether she had not understood what he was there for and thought it might be to wind the clock—which, he perceived, the only visible specimen had stopped. He knew the southern races communicated with each other in the language of pantomime, and was mortified to find her shrugs and smiles so unintelligible; their meanings slipped away like steam, carrying scent but no sense.

At length she returned with a lamp; and Archer, having meanwhile put together a phrase out of Dante and Petrarch, evoked the answer: “La signora e fuori; ma verra subito”—which he took to mean: She’s out, but you’ll soon see.

What he saw, meanwhile, with the help of the lamp, was the faded, shadowy charm of a room unlike any room he had known. He knew Countess Olenska had brought some of her possessions with her—bits of wreckage, she called them—and these, he supposed, were represented by some small, slender tables of dark wood, a delicate little Greek bronze on the chimney-piece, and a stretch of red damask nailed on the discolored wallpaper behind a couple of Italian-looking pictures in old frames. The effect was of a meal made from scraps, not mean but intentional: the kind of supper an old grandmother on a farm might contrive out of what had been saved and dried and put by, with a cunning hand that knew how memory itself could be eaten.

Newland Archer prided himself on his knowledge of Italian art. His boyhood had been saturated with Ruskin; he had read the latest books—Symonds, Vernon Lee’s “Euphorion,” Hamerton’s essays, and that new volume, “The Renaissance,” by Walter Pater. He talked easily of Botticelli, and spoke of Fra Angelico with a faint condescension. But these pictures bewildered him, for they were like nothing he was accustomed to look at (and therefore able to see) when he travelled in Italy; and perhaps, too, his powers of observation were impaired by the oddness of finding himself in this strange, empty house where apparently no one expected him.

He was sorry he had not told May Welland of Countess Olenska’s request, and a little disturbed by the thought that his betrothed might come in to see her cousin. What would she think if she found him sitting there with the air of intimacy implied by waiting alone in the dusk at a lady’s fireside? But since he had come he meant to wait; and he sank into a chair and stretched his feet toward the logs.

It was odd to have summoned him in that way and then forgotten him; but Archer felt more curious than mortified. The atmosphere of the room was so different from any he had ever breathed that self-consciousness vanished in the sense of adventure. He had been in drawing-rooms hung with red damask, with pictures “of the Italian school”; what struck him was the way Medora Manson’s shabby hired house—blighted, no doubt, with pampas grass and Rogers statuettes—had, by a turn of the hand and the skilful use of a few properties, been transformed into something intimate, “foreign,” subtly suggestive of old romantic scenes and sentiments.

He tried to analyze the trick—to find a clue in the way the chairs and tables were grouped, in the fact that only two Jacqueminot roses (of which nobody ever bought less than a dozen) had been placed in the slender vase at his elbow, and in the vague pervading perfume that was not what one put on handkerchiefs, but rather like the scent of some far-off bazaar: a smell compounded, as if by a patient cook of the senses, of Turkish coffee, ambergris, and dried roses. Even in the stillness the room seemed to taste of something—dark, resinous, faintly sweet—like a spoon dipped into a jar whose label was in a language you could not read, yet whose contents you somehow recognized in the mouth.

His mind wandered away to the question of what May’s drawing-room would look like. He knew Mr. Welland—who was behaving “very handsomely”—already had his eye on a newly built house in East Thirty-ninth Street. The neighborhood was thought remote, and the house was built in a ghastly greenish-yellow stone that the younger architects were beginning to employ as a protest against the brownstone that coated New York like cold chocolate sauce; but the plumbing was perfect. Archer would have liked to travel, to put off the housing question; but though the Wellands approved of an extended European honeymoon (perhaps even a winter in Egypt), they were firm as to the need of a house for the returning couple.

He felt his fate sealed: for the rest of his life he would go up every evening between the cast-iron railings of that greenish-yellow doorstep, and pass through a Pompeian vestibule into a hall with a wainscoting of varnished yellow wood. Beyond that his imagination could not travel. He knew the drawing-room above had a bay window, but he could not fancy how May would deal with it. She submitted cheerfully to the purple satin and yellow tuftings of the Welland drawing-room, to its sham Buhl tables and gilt vitrines full of modern Saxe. He saw no reason to suppose she would want anything different in her own house; and his only comfort was to reflect that she would probably let him arrange his library as he pleased—which would be, of course, with “sincere” Eastlake furniture, and plain new bookcases without glass doors.

The round-bosomed maid came in, drew the curtains, pushed back a log, and said consolingly: “Verra—verra.” When she had gone Archer stood up and began to wander about. Should he wait any longer? His position was becoming rather foolish. Perhaps he had misunderstood Madame Olenska—perhaps she had not invited him after all.

Down the cobblestones of the quiet street came the ring of a stepper’s hoofs; they stopped before the house, and he caught the opening of a carriage door. Parting the curtains he looked out into the early dusk. A street-lamp faced him, and in its circle of light he saw Julius Beaufort’s compact English brougham, drawn by a big roan, and the banker descending from it and helping out Madame Olenska.

Beaufort stood, hat in hand, saying something which his companion seemed to negative; then they shook hands, and he jumped into his carriage while she mounted the steps.

When she entered the room she showed no surprise at seeing Archer there; surprise seemed the emotion she was least addicted to.

“How do you like my funny house?” she asked. “To me it’s like heaven.”

As she spoke she untied her little velvet bonnet and, tossing it away with her long cloak, stood looking at him with meditative eyes. The fire threw up a soft crackle, a domestic, ancient sound; the lamp hummed faintly; outside, the street’s hoofbeats and wheels fell away, leaving her voice unusually audible, as if the room itself had been designed to let speech be heard.

“You’ve arranged it delightfully,” he rejoined—alive to the flatness of the words, but imprisoned in the conventional by his consuming desire to be simple and striking.

“Oh, it’s a poor little place. My relations despise it. But at any rate it’s less gloomy than the van der Luydens’.”

The words gave him an electric shock, for few were the rebellious spirits who would have dared to call the stately home of the van der Luydens gloomy. Those privileged to enter it shivered there, and spoke of it as “handsome.” Yet suddenly he was glad she had given voice to the general shiver, as if she had translated—without knowing it—what everyone said in the dominant lexicon of approval into the private dialect of sensation.

“It’s delicious—what you’ve done here,” he repeated.

“I like the little house,” she admitted; “but I suppose what I like is the blessedness of its being here, in my own country and my own town; and then, of being alone in it.”

She spoke so low he hardly heard the last phrase; but in his awkwardness he took it up. “You like so much to be alone?”

“Yes; as long as my friends keep me from feeling lonely.”

She sat down near the fire, said: “Nastasia will bring the tea presently,” and signed to him to return to his armchair, adding: “I see you’ve already chosen your corner.” Leaning back, she folded her arms behind her head and looked at the fire under drooping lids.

“This is the hour I like best—don’t you?”

A proper sense of his dignity caused him to answer: “I was afraid you’d forgotten the hour. Beaufort must have been very engrossing.”

She looked amused. “Why—have you waited long? Mr. Beaufort took me to see a number of houses—since it seems I’m not to be allowed to stay in this one.”

She appeared to dismiss both Beaufort and himself from her mind, and went on: “I’ve never been in a city where there seems to be such a feeling against living in des quartiers excentriques. What does it matter where one lives? I’m told this street is respectable.”

“It’s not fashionable.”

“Fashionable! Do you all think so much of that? Why not make one’s own fashions? But I suppose I’ve lived too independently; at any rate, I want to do what you all do—I want to feel cared for and safe.”

He was touched, as he had been the evening before when she spoke of her need of guidance. “That’s what your friends want you to feel. New York’s an awfully safe place,” he added, with a flash of sarcasm.

“Yes, isn’t it? One feels that,” she cried, missing the mockery. “Being here is like—like—being taken on a holiday when one has been a good little girl and done all one’s lessons.”

The analogy was well meant, but did not altogether please him. He did not mind being flippant about New York, but disliked to hear anyone else take the same tone. He wondered if she did not begin to see what a powerful engine it was, and how nearly it had crushed her. The Lovell Mingotts’ dinner—patched up in extremis out of social odds and ends—ought to have taught her the narrowness of her escape; but either she had been all along unaware of having skirted disaster, or else she had lost sight of it in the triumph of the van der Luyden evening. Archer inclined to the former theory; he fancied her New York was still undifferentiated, and the conjecture nettled him.

“Last night,” he said, “New York laid itself out for you. The van der Luydens do nothing by halves.”

“No: how kind they are! It was such a nice party. Every one seems to have such an esteem for them.”

The terms were hardly adequate; she might have spoken in that way of a tea-party at the dear old Miss Lannings’.

“The van der Luydens,” said Archer, feeling himself pompous as he spoke, “are the most powerful influence in New York society. Unfortunately—owing to her health—they receive very seldom.”

She unclasped her hands from behind her head and looked at him meditatively.

“Isn’t that perhaps the reason?”

“The reason—?”

“For their great influence; that they make themselves so rare.”

He coloured a little, stared at her—and suddenly felt the penetration of the remark. At a stroke she had pricked the van der Luydens and they collapsed. He laughed, and sacrificed them, as if relieved to lay down a heavy ceremonial dish and admit it had never nourished him.

Nastasia brought the tea, with handleless Japanese cups and little covered dishes, placing the tray on a low table. The kettle sang as she poured—an intimate, domestic sound; the steam smelled faintly vegetal, and the cups warmed the palms at once, as if the hands were being taught another language. The world outside held its winter hush; within, there was the soft percussion of porcelain lids and the small crackle of the fire, the kind of carefully tended quiet in which a mind can begin to hear itself.

“But you’ll explain these things to me—you’ll tell me all I ought to know,” Madame Olenska continued, leaning forward to hand him his cup.

“It’s you who are telling me; opening my eyes to things I’d looked at so long that I’d ceased to see them.”

She detached a small gold cigarette-case from one of her bracelets, held it out to him, and took a cigarette herself. On the chimney were long spills for lighting them.

“Ah, then we can both help each other. But I want help so much more. You must tell me just what to do.”

It was on the tip of his tongue to reply: Don’t be seen driving about the streets with Beaufort—; but he was being too deeply drawn into the atmosphere of the room, which was her atmosphere, and to give advice of that sort would have been like telling someone bargaining for attar-of-roses in Samarkand that one should always be provided with arctics for a New York winter. New York seemed much farther off than Samarkand, and if they were indeed to help each other, she was rendering what might prove the first of their mutual services by making him look at his native city objectively. Viewed thus, as through the wrong end of a telescope, it looked disconcertingly small and distant; but then, from Samarkand it would.

A flame darted from the logs and she bent over the fire, stretching her thin hands so close that a faint halo shone about the oval nails. The light touched to russet the rings of dark hair escaping from her braids, and made her pale face paler.

“There are plenty of people to tell you what to do,” Archer rejoined, obscurely envious of them.

“Oh—all my aunts? And my dear old Granny?” She considered the idea impartially. “They’re all a little vexed with me for setting up for myself—poor Granny especially. She wanted to keep me with her; but I had to be free—”

He was impressed by this light way of speaking of formidable Catherine, and moved by the thought of what must have given Madame Olenska this thirst for even the loneliest kind of freedom. But the idea of Beaufort gnawed him, like a too-rich sauce that keeps rising to the throat.

“I think I understand how you feel,” he said. “Still, your family can advise you; explain differences; show you the way.”

She lifted her thin black eyebrows. “Is New York such a labyrinth? I thought it so straight up and down—like Fifth Avenue. And with all the cross streets numbered!”

She seemed to guess his faint disapproval, and added—with the rare smile that enchanted her whole face: “If you knew how I like it for just that—the straight-up-and-downness, and the big honest labels on everything!”

He saw his chance. “Everything may be labelled—but everybody is not.”

“Perhaps. I may simplify too much—but you’ll warn me if I do.” She turned from the fire to look at him. “There are only two people here who make me feel as if they understood what I mean and could explain things to me: you and Mr. Beaufort.”

Archer winced at the joining of the names, and then, with a quick readjustment, understood, sympathised, and pitied. So close to the powers of evil she must have lived that she still breathed more freely in their air. But since she felt he understood her also, his business would be to make her see Beaufort as he really was, with all he represented—and abhor it.

He answered gently: “I understand. But just at first don’t let go of your old friends’ hands: I mean the older women—your Granny Mingott, Mrs. Welland, Mrs. van der Luyden. They like and admire you—they want to help you.”

She shook her head and sighed. “Oh, I know—I know! But on condition that they don’t hear anything unpleasant. Aunt Welland put it in those very words when I tried….” Her voice faltered, as though she had reached for the right term and found only the approved ones—those safe, dominant words that flatten what they touch. “Does no one want to know the truth here, Mr. Archer? The real loneliness is living among all these kind people who only ask one to pretend!”

She lifted her hands to her face, and he saw her thin shoulders shaken by a sob.

“Madame Olenska!—Oh, don’t, Ellen,” he cried, starting up and bending over her.

He drew down one of her hands, clasping and chafing it like a child’s while he murmured reassuring words; her fingers were cold with the kind of chill no fire quite reaches. But in a moment she freed herself and looked up at him with wet lashes.

“Does no one cry here, either? I suppose there’s no need to, in heaven,” she said, straightening her loosened braids with a laugh, and bending over the tea-kettle as if the simple act of pouring—measuring, warming, giving—could steady the shaken part of her.

It was burnt into his consciousness that he had called her “Ellen”—called her so twice; and that she had not noticed it. Far down the inverted telescope he saw the faint white figure of May Welland—in New York—remote as a statue in a public square, immaculate and soundless.

Suddenly Nastasia put her head in to say something in her rich Italian. Madame Olenska, again with a hand at her hair, uttered an exclamation of assent—a flashing “Gia—gia”—and the Duke of St. Austrey entered, piloting a tremendous black-wigged and red-plumed lady in overflowing furs.

“My dear Countess, I’ve brought an old friend of mine to see you—Mrs. Struthers. She wasn’t asked to the party last night, and she wants to know you.”

The Duke beamed on the group, and Madame Olenska advanced with a murmur of welcome toward the queer couple. She seemed to have no idea how oddly matched they were, nor what a liberty the Duke had taken in bringing his companion; and, to do him justice, as Archer perceived, the Duke seemed as unaware of it himself.

“Of course I want to know you, my dear,” cried Mrs. Struthers in a round rolling voice that matched her bold feathers and her brazen wig. The room’s careful quiet filled at once with her sound—too loud, too upholstered—as if a brass band had turned a corner into a small kitchen. “I want to know everybody who’s young and interesting and charming. And the Duke tells me you like music—didn’t you, Duke? You’re a pianist yourself, I believe? Well, do you want to hear Sarasate play tomorrow evening at my house? You know I’ve something going on every Sunday evening—it’s the day when New York doesn’t know what to do with itself, and so I say to it: ‘Come and be amused.’ And the Duke thought you’d be tempted by Sarasate. You’ll find a number of your friends.”

Madame Olenska’s face grew brilliant with pleasure. “How kind! How good of the Duke to think of me!” She pushed a chair up to the tea-table and Mrs. Struthers sank into it delectably, like someone settling into a banquet.

“Of course I shall be too happy to come.”

“That’s all right, my dear. And bring your young gentleman with you.” Mrs. Struthers extended a hail-fellow hand to Archer. “I can’t put a name to you—but I’m sure I’ve met you—I’ve met everybody, here, or in Paris or London. Aren’t you in diplomacy? All the diplomatists come to me. You like music too? Duke, you must be sure to bring him.”

The Duke said “Rather” from the depths of his beard, and Archer withdrew with a stiffly circular bow that made him feel as full of spine as a self-conscious schoolboy among careless, unnoticing elders.

He was not sorry for the denouement of his visit; he only wished it had come sooner, and spared him a certain waste of emotion—those sudden, rich, unguarded feelings that rose in him here as scents rise from a kettle and then are dispersed by a draughty door.

As he went out into the wintry night, New York again became vast and imminent, its noises thickening as he moved—wheels on frozen ruts, a shout, the hard metallic clink of a distant streetcar bell like an argument repeated. And May Welland became again the loveliest woman in it.

He turned into his florist’s to send her the daily box of lilies-of-the-valley which, to his confusion, he found he had forgotten that morning. The shop was an embowered warmth, wet green leaves breathing into the air; the smell of cut stems and damp moss was like a clean broth set to simmer, a domestic promise. As he wrote a word on his card and waited for an envelope he glanced about the crowded shelves and buckets, and his eye lit on a cluster of yellow roses.

He had never seen any as sun-golden before. Their color was not the pale yellow of propriety but something deeper, as if the flower had concentrated summer into a single, slightly dangerous hue. His first impulse was to send them to May instead of the lilies. But they did not look like her—there was something too rich, too strong in their fiery beauty, like a spice that would overwhelm a delicate dish.

In a sudden revulsion of mood, and almost without knowing what he did, he signed to the florist to lay the roses in another long box, and slipped his card into a second envelope on which he wrote the name of the Countess Olenska; then, just as he was turning away, he drew the card out again and left the empty envelope on the box—an offering without the official word that would translate it, reduce it, fix its meaning.

“They’ll go at once?” he enquired, pointing to the roses.

The florist assured him that they would.

The next day he persuaded May to slip away for a walk in the Park after luncheon.

Sunday afternoons, in their old Episcopalian corner of New York, belonged by right to solemn pew-cushions and measured hymns; yet Mrs. Welland, who could be indulgent when indulgence served the calendar of propriety, had that very morning won May to the wisdom of a long engagement—time, she said, for the hand-embroidered trousseau to grow stitch by stitch into its ordained dozens, as orderly as loaves rising in identical tins. May had listened as a daughter listens, with that bright pliancy Newland both adored and mistrusted: she could be guided like a well-tempered batter, taking the shape of whatever bowl was held out to her.

Outside, the air tasted of cold iron and sunlight. The bare trees along the Mall lifted their dark ribs into a sky so clear it looked enamelled—lapis above, snow below, the world between them crisp as spun sugar snapped underfoot. The Park had its own music in winter: the far clip of hooves on packed paths, the intermittent swish of sleigh-runners, a child’s call skimming and dying into the blue; and, threading through it all, the thin rustle of dry branches that seemed to answer one another like old women gossiping in an unforgotten dialect. Archer noticed how the city’s noise pressed at the edges, a dull mechanical murmur beyond the trees—as if modern life, impatient and iron-wheeled, were always trying to grind down the softer sounds. Here, at least, May’s laughter could still be heard cleanly.

The weather brought out her radiance as a flame shows truest against frost. She moved beside him with the lightness of a young creature bred for open air, her cheek fresh and firm as a winter apple. Archer felt pride in the glances that turned after her—pride, and the sudden simple comfort of possession, like a hand closing over something warm in a cold room. For a few minutes it swept away the restlessness that had lately lived under his ribs.

“It’s so delicious,” she said, drawing in her breath as if she could taste the words, “waking every morning to smell lilies-of-the-valley in one’s room. Yesterday they came late. I hadn’t time in the morning—”

“But your remembering each day to send them makes me love them so much more,” he answered, “than if you’d given a standing order and they arrived like a lesson—punctual as a music-teacher. As I know Gertrude Lefferts’s did, for instance, when she and Lawrence were engaged.”

“Ah—they would!” May laughed, quick to seize the implied satire. Her delight in the small recognitions of social minutiae always amused him: she could discriminate between one family’s habits and another’s as delicately as some careful palate tells wines apart. He looked at her, at the clear curve of her jaw under the fur edge of her muff, and felt rich and safe enough to add, lightly:

“When I sent your lilies yesterday afternoon, I saw some rather gorgeous yellow roses and packed them off to Madame Olenska. Was that right?”

May’s eyes warmed. “How dear of you. Anything of that kind delights her. It’s odd she didn’t mention it: she lunched with us today, and spoke of Mr. Beaufort’s having sent her wonderful orchids, and cousin Henry van der Luyden a whole hamper of carnations from Skuytercliff. She seems so surprised to receive flowers. Don’t people send them in Europe? She thinks it such a pretty custom.”

“Oh—well, no wonder mine were overshadowed by Beaufort’s,” Archer said, more sharply than he intended.

At once he remembered he had put no card with the roses, and a petty vexation pricked him. Why had he spoken of them at all? It made the act seem to him suddenly less graceful, more like a signal, and he disliked the faint air of secrecy that began to gather about anything connected with Ellen. He wanted to say: I called on your cousin yesterday—but hesitated. If she had not spoken of his visit, to mention it now might sound like an explanation offered without being asked for. Yet not to speak of it made the silence seem charged, like a dish served without naming its ingredients.

To shake off the question, he began to talk of their own future, their plans, and Mrs. Welland’s insistence on a long engagement.

“If you call it long!” May protested. “Isabel Chivers and Reggie were engaged for two years. Grace and Thorley for nearly a year and a half. Why aren’t we very well off as we are?”

The traditional maidenly interrogation fell from her as if she had been taught to set it down, like a proper spoon placed beside a plate. Archer felt a sudden shame at his own impatience; and yet, as he watched her speak, he wondered at what age “nice” women began to season their thoughts with anything of their own. She was nearing her twenty-second birthday, and still her phrases seemed chosen from a family larder.

Never, if we won’t let them, he thought; and his mind flashed back to his own hot words to Sillerton Jackson—Women ought to be as free as we are. It would soon be his task, he told himself, to take the bandage from this young woman’s eyes and bid her look out on the world. But how many generations of women had gone to her making—women who had learned to keep their seeing for embroidery, their hearing for approving murmurs, their speech for the sanctioned vocabulary? How many had gone down into the family vault with their senses swathed, as if life’s full taste and sound were improper?

He shivered a little, remembering some scientific book he had been reading and the oft-cited Kentucky cave-fish which had ceased to develop eyes because it had no use for them. What if, when he told May to open hers, they could only look out blankly at blankness?

“We might be much better off,” he said, pushing the thought aside by giving it a different shape. “We might be altogether together—we might travel.”

Her face lit at once, as if he had set a candle behind it. “That would be lovely,” she admitted. She would love to travel. But her mother would not understand their wanting to do things so differently.

“As if the mere ‘differently’ didn’t account for it!” he insisted, hungry for resistance, for proof that she wanted something with teeth and savour in it.

“Newland! You’re so original!” she exclaimed.

His heart sank. He heard himself, with a sick clarity, making the approved passionate protests of engaged men; and he heard her answering with the sweet, trained replies that instinct and tradition had put in her mouth—even to the point of calling him original, as if originality were a garnish pinned on.

“Original! We’re all as like each other as those dolls cut out of the same folded paper,” he burst out. “We’re like patterns stencilled on a wall. Can’t you and I strike out for ourselves, May?”

He had stopped and faced her; his breath smoked a little in the cold as he spoke. Her eyes rested on him with bright, unclouded admiration—an admiration so complete it was almost without curiosity.

“Mercy—shall we elope?” she laughed.

“If you would—”

“You do love me, Newland! I’m so happy.”

“But then—why not be happier?”

“We can’t behave like people in novels, though, can we?”

“Why not—why not—why not?”

She looked faintly bored by his insistence. She knew very well that they could not; but it was troublesome to have to dredge up the reason. Her tone suggested the weariness of being asked to translate something that had never been spoken in her own language.

“I’m not clever enough to argue with you. But that kind of thing is rather—vulgar, isn’t it?” she offered at last, relieved, as if she had found the one word that would close the door.

“Are you so much afraid, then, of being vulgar?”

She was staggered. “Of course I should hate it—so would you,” she rejoined, a little irritably.

He stood silent, beating his stick against his boot-top, listening to the small, neat sounds of their own footsteps and the bigger breathing of the Park around them. Somewhere a carriage wheel bumped over frozen ruts, a hard, modern clatter; farther off a sleigh-bell chimed thinly, like a memory. The city’s roar beyond the trees seemed to swell, as if the word vulgar had called it up—an invisible force of judgment, a dominant lexicon that could end an argument with a single label.

Feeling, no doubt, that she had indeed used the right word, May went on light-heartedly, as if she had wiped the subject away with a clean cloth.

“Oh—did I tell you I showed Ellen my ring? She thinks it the most beautiful setting she ever saw. There’s nothing like it in the rue de la Paix, she said. I do love you, Newland, for being so artistic!”

The next afternoon Archer, before dinner, sat smoking sullenly in his study. He had not stopped at his club on the way up from the office where he practised law in the leisurely manner customary among well-to-do New Yorkers. He was out of spirits and slightly out of temper; and a haunting horror of doing the same thing at the same hour every day besieged him.

Sameness—sameness! The word ran through his head like a tune he could not shut out. He had passed the familiar tall-hatted figures lounging behind the plate-glass and, because he usually dropped in at that hour, had gone home instead, as if turning aside could alter the melody. He knew, with the miserable exactness of someone who has eaten a dish too often, not only what they would be talking about but which man would say which thing, and in what tone—each phrase pre-measured, each opinion salted to the same degree.

The Duke would of course be their principal theme; though the appearance in Fifth Avenue of a golden-haired lady in a small canary-coloured brougham with a pair of black cobs—Mr. Beaufort was generally thought responsible—would also be discussed with relish. Such “women,” as they were called, were few in New York; those driving their own carriages fewer still. The very sound of her wheels in the fashionable hour seemed to have jarred society’s nerves: people spoke of it as if a discordant note had been struck in a church anthem. Only the day before, her carriage had passed Mrs. Lovell Mingott’s, and Mrs. Mingott had instantly rung the little bell at her elbow and ordered the coachman to drive her home.

“What if it had happened to Mrs. van der Luyden?” people asked each other with a shudder, as if the mere proximity of that rolling, rattling modernity could contaminate the pure air of tradition.

Archer, in his study, could almost hear Lawrence Lefferts holding forth at that moment on the disintegration of society—Lefferts’s voice, round and complacent, like a well-fed drum.

He raised his head irritably when his sister Janey entered, then bent quickly over his book—Swinburne’s Chastelard, just out—as if he had not seen her. Janey glanced at the writing-table heaped with books, opened a volume of the Contes Drolatiques, made a wry face over the archaic French, and sighed:

“What learned things you read!”

“Well—?” he asked, as she hovered before him with Cassandra’s air of bringing disaster nobody wished to hear.

“Mother’s very angry.”

“Angry? With whom? About what?”

“Miss Sophy Jackson has just been here. She brought word that her brother would come in after dinner. She couldn’t say very much, because he forbade her to: he wishes to give all the details himself. He’s with cousin Louisa van der Luyden now.”

“For heaven’s sake, my dear girl, try a fresh start,” Archer said. “It would take an omniscient Deity to know what you’re talking about.”

“It’s not a time to be profane, Newland… Mother feels badly enough about your not going to church—”

With a groan he plunged back into his book.

“Newland! Do listen. Your friend Madame Olenska was at Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s party last night: she went there with the Duke and Mr. Beaufort.”

At the last clause a senseless anger swelled in him. He could not have told whether it was the Duke’s name, or Beaufort’s, or Ellen’s, or the idea of her among that particular crowd—but it rose, hot and thick, like a sauce turning in the pan when one is not watching. To smother it he laughed.

“Well—what of it? I knew she meant to.”

Janey paled and her eyes began to project with indignation.

“You knew she meant to—and you didn’t try to stop her? To warn her?”

“Stop her? Warn her?” He laughed again, the sound harsher. “I’m not engaged to be married to the Countess Olenska!”

The words had a fantastic sound even to himself, like a line overheard from the wrong play.

“You’re marrying into her family.”

“Oh, family—family!” he jeered.

“Newland—don’t you care about Family?”

“Not a brass farthing.”

“Nor about what cousin Louisa van der Luyden will think?”

“Not the half of one—if she thinks such old maid’s rubbish.”

“Mother is not an old maid,” said his virgin sister with pinched lips.

He felt like shouting back that yes, she was—and so were the van der Luydens, and so were they all, whenever reality brushed them with a wing-tip. But he saw Janey’s long gentle face puckering into tears and felt ashamed of the useless pain he was inflicting.

“Hang Countess Olenska! Don’t be a goose, Janey—I’m not her keeper.”

“No; but you did ask the Wellands to announce your engagement sooner so that we might all back her up; and if it hadn’t been for that cousin Louisa would never have invited her to the dinner for the Duke.”

“Well—what harm was there in inviting her?” Archer retorted. “She was the best-looking woman in the room. She made the dinner a little less funereal than the usual van der Luyden banquet.”

“You know cousin Henry asked her to please you: he persuaded cousin Louisa. And now they’re so upset that they’re going back to Skuytercliff tomorrow. I think, Newland, you’d better come down. You don’t seem to understand how mother feels.”

In the drawing-room he found his mother. She raised a troubled brow from her needlework.

“Has Janey told you?”

“Yes.” He tried to keep his tone as measured as hers. “But I can’t take it very seriously.”

“Not the fact of having offended cousin Louisa and cousin Henry?”

“The fact that they can be offended by such a trifle as Countess Olenska’s going to the house of a woman they consider common.”

“Consider—!”

“Well, who is,” he persisted, “but who has good music, and amuses people on Sunday evenings, when the whole of New York is dying of inanition.”

“Good music?” Mrs. Archer’s voice tightened, as if the word itself had an indecent tang. “All I know is, there was a woman who got up on a table and sang the things they sing at the places you go to in Paris. There was smoking and champagne.”

“Well—that kind of thing happens in other places, and the world still goes on.”

“I don’t suppose, dear, you’re really defending the French Sunday?”

“I’ve heard you often enough, mother, grumble at the English Sunday when we’ve been in London.”
