“New York is neither Paris nor London.”

“Oh, no, it’s not!” her son groaned, and the groan carried with it all the muffled city noise he had been trying to escape—the rumble of commerce, the clank of opinion, the grinding repetition.

“You mean, I suppose,” she continued, misunderstanding him with the perfect sincerity of those who live within the approved meanings, “that society here is not as brilliant? You’re right, I daresay; but we belong here, and people should respect our ways when they come among us. Ellen Olenska especially: she came back to get away from the kind of life people lead in brilliant societies.”

Newland made no answer; and after a moment his mother ventured, with a careful softness:

“I was going to put on my bonnet and ask you to take me to see cousin Louisa for a moment before dinner.”

He frowned, and she went on: “I thought you might explain to her what you’ve just said: that society abroad is different… that people are not as particular, and that Madame Olenska may not have realised how we feel about such things. It would be, you know, dear,” she added with an innocent adroitness, “in Madame Olenska’s interest if you did.”

“Dearest mother, I really don’t see how we’re concerned in the matter,” he answered. “The Duke took Madame Olenska to Mrs. Struthers’s—in fact he brought Mrs. Struthers to call on her. I was there when they came. If the van der Luydens want to quarrel with anybody, the real culprit is under their own roof.”

“Quarrel? Newland, did you ever know of cousin Henry’s quarrelling? Besides, the Duke’s his guest—and a stranger too. Strangers don’t discriminate: how should they? Countess Olenska is a New Yorker, and should have respected the feelings of New York.”

“Well, then, if they must have a victim, you have my leave to throw Madame Olenska to them,” he cried, exasperated. “I don’t see myself—or you either—offering ourselves up to expiate her crimes.”

“Oh, of course you see only the Mingott side,” his mother said, in the sensitive tone that was her nearest approach to anger.

At that moment the sad butler drew back the drawing-room portières and announced: “Mr. Henry van der Luyden.”

Mrs. Archer dropped her needle and pushed her chair back with an agitated hand. “Another lamp,” she called to the retreating servant, while Janey bent to straighten her mother’s cap.

Mr. van der Luyden’s tall figure loomed on the threshold, and Archer went forward to greet his cousin.

“We were just talking about you, sir,” he said.

Mr. van der Luyden seemed overwhelmed by the announcement. He drew off his glove to shake hands with the ladies, smoothed his tall hat shyly, while Janey pushed an arm-chair forward; and Archer, with an impulse that was half defiance, added:

“And the Countess Olenska.”

Mrs. Archer paled.

“Ah—a charming woman. I have just been to see her,” said Mr. van der Luyden, and complacency returned to his brow. He sank into the chair, laid his hat and gloves on the floor beside him in the old-fashioned way, and continued, with an unusual flow of speech:

“She has a real gift for arranging flowers. I had sent her a few carnations from Skuytercliff, and I was astonished. Instead of massing them in big bunches as our head-gardener does, she had scattered them about loosely, here and there… I can’t say how. The Duke had told me—he said: ‘Go and see how cleverly she’s arranged her drawing-room.’ And she has. I should really like to take Louisa to see her, if the neighbourhood were not so—unpleasant.”

A dead silence met this. Mrs. Archer drew her embroidery out of the basket into which she had nervously tumbled it. Newland, leaning against the chimney-place, twisted a humming-bird-feather screen in his hand; he watched Janey’s gaping face brighten as the servant returned with the second lamp, and the room filled with a thicker, steadier light—lamplight that made everything seem already preserved, already museum-like.

“The fact is,” Mr. van der Luyden went on, stroking his long grey leg with a bloodless hand weighted by the Patroon’s great signet-ring, “I dropped in to thank her for the very pretty note she wrote me about my flowers; and also—but this is between ourselves, of course—to give her a friendly warning about allowing the Duke to carry her off to parties with him. I don’t know if you’ve heard—”

Mrs. Archer produced an indulgent smile. “Has the Duke been carrying her off to parties?”

“You know what these English grandees are. They’re all alike. Louisa and I are very fond of our cousin—but it’s hopeless to expect people who are accustomed to the European courts to trouble themselves about our little republican distinctions. The Duke goes where he’s amused.”

He paused; no one spoke.

“Yes—it seems he took her with him last night to Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s. Sillerton Jackson has just been to us with the foolish story, and Louisa was rather troubled. So I thought the shortest way was to go straight to Countess Olenska and explain—by the merest hint, you know—how we feel in New York about certain things. I felt I might, without indelicacy, because the evening she dined with us she rather suggested… rather let me see that she would be grateful for guidance. And she was.”

He looked about the room with a mild benevolence that, on another face, might have been self-satisfaction. Mrs. Archer’s countenance dutifully reflected it.

“How kind you both are, dear Henry—always!” she said. “Newland will particularly appreciate what you have done because of dear May and his new relations.”

She shot an admonitory glance at her son. Archer answered:

“Immensely, sir. But I was sure you’d like Madame Olenska.”

Mr. van der Luyden looked at him with extreme gentleness.

“I never ask to my house, my dear Newland,” he said, “any one whom I do not like. And so I have just told Sillerton Jackson.”

With a glance at the clock he rose. “But Louisa will be waiting. We are dining early, to take the Duke to the Opera.”

After the portières had solemnly closed behind their visitor, silence fell upon the Archer family, as if the room were holding its breath in the approved manner.

“Gracious—how romantic!” Janey burst out at last.

No one knew exactly what her elliptical comment referred to; and long ago her relations had ceased trying to interpret her private language, so full of sudden exclamations and borrowed phrases.

Mrs. Archer shook her head with a sigh. “Provided it all turns out for the best,” she said, in the tone of one who knows how surely it will not.

“Newland, you must stay and see Sillerton Jackson when he comes this evening: I really shan’t know what to say to him.”

“Poor mother!” Archer laughed, bending to kiss away her frown. “But he won’t come—”

Two weeks later, when the city had settled again into its winter habits—coal fires banked early, sleigh-bells sparing their bright little clatter for the Park, and the rest of the town muffled in the soft pact of snow—Newland Archer sat in his private compartment at Letterblair, Lamson and Low, affecting to read while his thoughts wandered where they were least welcome. The office had its own soundscape: the steady scratch of pens through partitions, the dry cough of paper shuffled into order, a clerk’s steps measured by long training on the carpet runner. It was a quiet engineered for old families, a hush meant to keep trouble from echoing.

A messenger came, discreet as a footman at dessert, to say Mr. Letterblair wished to see him.

Old Mr. Letterblair—who advised New York as a family physician advises a lineage—sat enthroned behind mahogany burnished by generations of anxious hands. He stroked his close-clipped white whiskers, then raked fingers through the rumpled gray above his jutting brows with the air of a diagnostician offended by symptoms that would not fit a polite category.

“My dear sir—” he began; he always called Archer “sir,” as if the title itself were a splint for youthful impetuosity. “I’ve sent for you to go into a little matter; a matter which, for the moment, I prefer not to mention either to Mr. Skipworth or Mr. Redwood.”

Archer knew the names on the letterhead were, in the New York manner, more enduring than the men; the partners commemorated there had long since become portrait and precedent, and Mr. Letterblair, professionally, was his own grandson.

“For family reasons,” Mr. Letterblair went on, letting the words fall as if they should explain everything.

Archer’s eyes lifted.

“The Mingott family,” said Mr. Letterblair, and his mouth softened into the faint bow he reserved for established power. “Mrs. Manson Mingott sent for me yesterday. Her grand-daughter—the Countess Olenska—wishes to sue her husband for divorce. Certain papers have been placed in my hands.”

The blood moved sharply in Archer’s temples. He had seen Ellen Olenska only once since his visit to her—at the Opera, in the Mingott box—and in the interval she had dimmed, unwillingly receding as May Welland resumed her place like a steady lamp in the center of his life. Since Janey’s first airy mention, he had heard no more of divorce and had filed it away as gossip. Divorce, in theory, offended him almost as it did his mother; and he resented, too, the obvious plan—no doubt old Catherine Mingott’s—to drag him into the mess as if he were already pledged body and mind to the clan.

Mr. Letterblair unlocked a drawer and drew out a packet.

“If you will run your eye over these papers—”

Archer did not take them at once. “I beg your pardon, sir; but just because of the prospective relationship, I should prefer your consulting Mr. Skipworth or Mr. Redwood.”

Surprise, and a touch of rebuke, crossed the old man’s face. Openings were not refused in their world; they were accepted, digested, and made useful.

“I respect your scruple, sir; but in this case I believe true delicacy requires you to do as I ask. Indeed, the suggestion is not mine but Mrs. Manson Mingott’s and her son’s. I have seen Lovell Mingott; and also Mr. Welland. They all named you.”

His temper rose as if a band had tightened around his chest. For a fortnight he had let himself drift—letting May’s radiance smooth away the Mingotts’ demands, letting the pleasant domestic current bear him along. But now the clan’s assumption of rights in advance of marriage made him feel the collar.

“Her uncles ought to deal with this,” he said.

“They have. The matter has been gone into by the family. They are opposed to the Countess’s idea; but she is firm, and insists on a legal opinion.”

Archer took the packet at last and did not open it. “Does she want to marry again?”

“It is suggested,” Mr. Letterblair admitted, drumming the desk with a thick forefinger; “but she denies it.”

“Then—”

“Will you oblige me, Mr. Archer, by first looking through these papers? Afterward, when we have talked the case over, I will give you my opinion.”

Archer withdrew with the envelope as if it held something that might leak. He had, without fully meaning to, collaborated with events in laying down the burden of Ellen Olenska. That hour alone with her in the firelight had drawn them into a sudden intimacy; then the Duke of St. Austrey’s intrusion—bringing Mrs. Lemuel Struthers with him, as if even indiscretion must be escorted—had providentially snapped the thread; and Ellen’s quick, glad greeting of them had made Archer feel, sharply, that she was not made of the stuff May was made of, that her warmth went out too freely, and therefore too easily. Two days later he had watched the comedy of her reinstatement in the van der Luydens’ favor and thought, with a taste of bitterness, that a woman who could thank all-powerful elderly gentlemen so deftly for a bunch of flowers did not need his private consolations or his public championship.

It had been an oddly comforting thought, and it had burnished May’s domestic virtues until they shone. He could not imagine May—whatever emergency arose—hawking her troubles about, spending her confidences on strangers. She had never seemed finer than in the week that followed, when he had even yielded to her wish for a long engagement because she had answered his plea for haste with the one thing that disarmed him.

“You know, when it comes to the point, your parents have always let you have your way ever since you were a little girl,” he had argued.

“Yes,” May had said, looking at him with that clear directness that seemed like a native dialect of their class, “and that’s what makes it so hard to refuse the very last thing they’ll ever ask of me as a little girl.”

That note—pure, obedient, perfectly phrased—had moved him more deeply than he cared to admit. In that language, so crystalline it almost cut, the New York air seemed clean, and anything less exact began to feel suffocating.

He opened the packet.

The papers told him little of facts and much of atmosphere—an exchange of letters between Count Olenski’s solicitors and a French legal firm to whom the Countess had applied about money. Their phrases were precise, their courtesies edged like knives; each sentence seemed to translate something raw into terms that would pass inspection, and in doing so to kill it a little. There was also a short letter from the Count to his wife.

Archer read that once; then, as if his fingers had touched a contaminated object, he rose, thrust the papers back into the envelope, and went straight to Mr. Letterblair.

“Here are the letters, sir. If you wish, I’ll see Madame Olenska,” he said, and heard how strained his own voice sounded.

“Thank you—thank you, Mr. Archer. Come and dine with me tonight if you’re free, and we’ll go into the matter afterward: in case you wish to call on our client tomorrow.”

That afternoon Archer walked home through a winter evening of transparent clearness. The young moon hung above the rooflines like a newly polished platter; the snow underfoot gave back a faint squeak at each step, and from a distance a street-organ tried out a tune with a wheezing persistence that made him think of how modern noise forced itself into every crevice of life, turning even sorrow into a public performance. He wanted to fill his lungs with cold radiance and not exchange a word until he and Mr. Letterblair were closeted after dinner.

It was impossible to decide otherwise than he had: he must see Ellen himself rather than let her story be turned over by indifferent hands. Compassion—stronger than his annoyance—washed away his recent indifference. She rose before him as exposed, pitiful, in danger of wounding herself further by plunging at the bars of fate. He remembered what she had told him of Mrs. Welland’s request to be spared whatever was “unpleasant,” and he winced, suspecting that this very averted mind was the filter that kept their air so “pure.”

“Are we only Pharisees after all?” he wondered; and the thought, instead of being abstract, sat in him like smoke in a closed room—irritating, inescapable. He saw, for the first time, how elementary his own principles had been. People thought him daring; there had been that affair with poor silly Mrs. Thorley Rushworth—never secret enough to prevent its investing him with a decorative air of adventure. But Mrs. Rushworth was “that kind of woman”: foolish, vain, clandestine by nature, and more enamored of the spice of danger than of any charm he possessed. When he had understood that, it had nearly broken his heart; now, oddly, it seemed the one redeeming feature. The affair had been precisely the sort most young men went through and emerged from with calm consciences and an undisturbed faith in the deep distinction between women one loved and respected and women one enjoyed—and pitied.

Their mothers and aunts helped them keep that lexicon intact. In their vocabulary, when “such things happened” it was always foolish in a man and somehow criminal in a woman. Any woman who loved imprudently was assumed to be scheming; the man, simple-minded, helpless in her clutches. The remedy was to marry him quickly to a nice girl and trust to her to manage him like a well-run pantry.

But in older European societies, Archer began to guess, love might not submit so easily to their categories. Rich, idle, ornamental worlds must breed stranger tangles; there might even be a case in which a woman naturally sensitive and aloof could, through sheer loneliness and defencelessness, be drawn into a tie no New York phrasebook could excuse.

At home he wrote a line to Ellen, asking at what hour the next day she could receive him, and sent it by a messenger-boy. The boy returned with word that she was going to Skuytercliff in the morning to stay over Sunday with the van der Luydens, but that he would find her alone that evening after dinner.

Her note was on an untidy half-sheet, without date or address; the handwriting was firm and free. The carelessness of form struck him like a foreign intonation in a familiar street. He smiled at the idea of her “week-ending” in the stately solitude of Skuytercliff, then felt at once that there, of all places, she would most keenly feel the chill of minds rigorously turned away from the “unpleasant.” Even a house could have a way of listening, of withholding sound, of making every human movement seem too loud.

He was at Mr. Letterblair’s punctually at seven, grateful for the pretext of leaving soon after dinner. He had formed his own opinion from the papers and did not care to have it shaped by his senior partner’s.

Mr. Letterblair was a widower, and they dined alone, copiously and slowly, in a dark shabby room hung with yellowing prints of “The Death of Chatham” and “The Coronation of Napoleon.” The room smelled of coal, old leather, and the sweet, heavy breath of long-uncorked wine—an odor as conservative as the firm itself. Between fluted Sheraton knife-cases stood a decanter of Haut Brion and another of old Lanning port, a client’s gift—wine whose story Archer knew: Tom Lanning had sold off his cellar before his mysterious, discreditable death in San Francisco, and the family had found that death less publicly humiliating than the sale.

Dinner moved with ceremonious weight, like a ritual meant to keep disorder at bay. There was velvety oyster soup—thickened to that perfect point where it coated the spoon and carried the sea in a softened whisper—then shad with cucumbers, the fish handled with the reverence due to a seasonal tradition, its flesh flaking under the fork in clean white petals. After came a young broiled turkey with corn fritters, the fritters crisp at the edges and steaming with a sweet grain-scent when broken; then a canvas-back with currant jelly, its iron richness sharpened by the tart gloss, and a celery mayonnaise whose cool perfume seemed to rinse the palate back into propriety.

Mr. Letterblair, who lunched on a sandwich and tea, dined as if dinner were his one daily argument with mortality, and he insisted Archer do the same. The only loudness in the room was culinary—the faint crackle as skin yielded, the soft tap of knife against plate, the steady glug of wine into glass. Outside, the city’s noises were kept at a respectful distance, as if even sound needed an invitation.

When the closing rites had been accomplished—the cloth removed, cigars lit, the port sent westward—Mr. Letterblair leaned back, spreading his shoulders to the coal fire behind him.

“The whole family are against a divorce,” he said. “And I think rightly.”

At once Archer felt himself pushed to the other side, not by conviction but by the old man’s tone—well-fed, self-protective, serenely indifferent.

“But why, sir? If there ever was a case—”

“Well—what’s the use? She’s here—he’s there; the Atlantic’s between them. She’ll never get back a dollar more of her money than what he’s voluntarily returned to her: their damned heathen marriage settlements take precious good care of that. As things go over there, Olenski’s acted generously: he might have turned her out without a penny.”

Archer knew it and held his tongue.

“I understand, though,” Mr. Letterblair continued, “that she attaches no importance to the money. Therefore, as the family say, why not let well enough alone?”

An hour earlier Archer might have agreed. Now, hearing the argument in this voice, it tasted like a dish prepared without salt for fear of offending an invalid—blandness elevated to virtue.

“I think that’s for her to decide.”

“H’m—have you considered the consequences if she decides for divorce?”

“You mean the threat in her husband’s letter? What weight would that carry? It’s no more than the vague charge of an angry blackguard.”

“Yes; but it might make some unpleasant talk if he really defends the suit.”

“Unpleasant—!” Archer’s exclamation came out like a sound he could not help making, and he hated the way it broke the room’s careful quiet.

Mr. Letterblair looked at him from under inquiring brows. Archer, feeling the futility of explaining what was pounding in him, bowed slightly, and the old man went on as if restoring a disturbed arrangement.

“Divorce is always unpleasant.”

“You agree with me?” Mr. Letterblair asked after a pause, his manner assuming consent as naturally as a host refills a glass.

“Naturally,” Archer said.

“Well, then, I may count on you—the Mingotts may count on you—to use your influence against the idea?”

Archer hesitated, hearing, as if from a distance, the city’s winter hush, and within it the sharp, small noises by which reputations were made and broken: a whispered word at a dinner-table, a laugh held back too late, the click of a fan closing like a verdict.

“I can’t pledge myself till I’ve seen the Countess Olenska,” he said at last.

“Mr. Archer, I don’t understand you. Do you want to marry into a family with a scandalous divorce-suit hanging over it?”

“I don’t think that has anything to do with the case.”

Mr. Letterblair set down his port and fixed Archer with a cautious, apprehensive gaze. Archer understood that his mandate might be withdrawn, and for some obscure reason he disliked the prospect. Now that the task had been pushed onto him, he would not relinquish it; and he saw he must reassure the unimaginative conscience of the Mingotts.

“You may be sure, sir, that I shan’t commit myself till I’ve reported to you; what I meant was that I’d rather not give an opinion till I’ve heard what Madame Olenska has to say.”

Mr. Letterblair nodded, approving this excess of caution as if it were seasoning properly measured. Archer glanced at his watch, pleaded an engagement, and took leave, the sounds of the quiet house closing behind him like a door carefully latched against whatever the night might bring.

Old-fashioned New York ate at seven, as regularly as the chiming of Trinity’s bells, and afterward it dispersed itself—pair by pair, carriage by carriage—into that dutiful round of evening calls which Archer’s own circle affected to mock and yet could not quite abandon. When he left Waverley Place and walked up Fifth Avenue, the city had the hushed, well-fed quiet of a dining-room after the servants have cleared away and the last fragrance of roast and wine still lingers in the curtains.

The avenue was mostly empty, save for a cluster of carriages waiting before the Reggie Chiverses’, where there was a dinner for the Duke, and for the occasional elderly gentleman wrapped like a parcel against the cold, mounting a brownstone stoop and vanishing into a gas-lit hall. Crossing Washington Square, Archer noticed old Mr. du Lac calling on the Dagonets; at West Tenth Street he saw Skipworth from his own firm, unmistakably bound for the Miss Lannings. These little movements had the soothing order of a household routine—the social body digesting, then making its measured rounds as if the very air required it.

Farther up, Beaufort appeared at his own door, his broad figure cut out against a blaze of light, and went down to his private brougham with the air of a man leaving the table for some stronger, more private course. It was not an Opera night and no one was giving a party; Beaufort’s excursion could only mean one of those dinners not spoken of, served in a smaller house with newer paint. Archer, with the unromantic exactness of his class, instantly supplied the likely address: a little place beyond Lexington Avenue where beribboned curtains had lately appeared in the windows, where flower-boxes looked unnaturally bright against winter brick, and where Miss Fanny Ring’s canary-coloured brougham was often seen waiting as patiently as a hired appetite.

Beyond the neat, slippery pyramid that composed Mrs. Archer’s world lay an almost unmapped quarter inhabited by artists, musicians, and “people who wrote.” The phrase itself carried, in New York mouths, a faintly medicinal caution—as if such creatures were to be handled with gloves, not because they were dangerous, but because one did not know what they might leave on one’s fingers. They had never shown any desire to be melted into society’s mould; they lived apart, and even when respectable—and it was always added, as a kind of reluctant seasoning, that most of them were—they preferred their own tables and their own talk.

Medora Manson, in her prosperous days, had tried to set up a “literary salon,” but it had died quickly, starved by the very people it hoped to nourish: the literary were shy of being displayed. Others had tried again; there was the Blenkers’ household—an intense, voluble mother and three blowsy daughters who copied her—where one might be handed Edwin Booth or Patti as one would pass a dish, and where magazine editors and musical critics circulated like curiosities on a tray. Mrs. Archer and her friends felt a timid reverence for such persons, and the uneasiness one feels before a language spoken too fast to catch. Art and literature were respected in Archer’s set—deeply, solemnly respected, as one respects distant monuments—yet the artists themselves, with their uncertain origins, their hair, their intimacy with stage doors and cheap suppers after midnight, did not fit the old criteria by which one “placed” people.

“When I was a girl,” Mrs. Archer used to say, “we knew everybody between the Battery and Canal Street; and only the people one knew had carriages. It was perfectly easy to place any one then; now one can’t tell, and I prefer not to try.”

Old Catherine Mingott, with her magnificent indifference to moral fineness and her almost parvenu bravery, might have bridged the abyss; but she had never opened a book or looked long at a picture, and cared for music chiefly because it recalled gala nights at the Italiens, in the days when she had shone at the Tuileries. Beaufort, bold enough to attempt anything, might have forced a fusion; yet his grand house with its silk-stockinged footmen was itself an obstacle to any informal mingling, and he was as illiterate as Mrs. Mingott, believing that “fellows who wrote” were merely paid to season rich men’s leisure—spices to be bought, not voices to be heard.

Archer had known all this as long as he could remember and had accepted it as the structure of his universe. He had read of societies where poets and painters and men of science were pursued as eagerly as Dukes; he had pictured drawing-rooms dominated by the talk of Mérimée—whose letters he carried about like a private relish—by Thackeray, Browning, William Morris. Yet in New York such a mixture remained unthinkable, and therefore faintly disquieting. He knew many of the “fellows who wrote,” the musicians and painters; he met them at the Century, or at the little clubs where music and theatre were beginning to make modest nests. He liked them there and was bored with them at the Blenkers’, where they were passed around between fervid, dowdy women, as if New York were trying to swallow an unfamiliar dish without tasting it.

Even after his best talks with Ned Winsett he would come away with the sense that if his own world was small, so was theirs; and that the only way to enlarge either was to reach a stage of manners where they might merge without being consciously served up to one another.

It was impossible not to think of all this when he tried to imagine the society in which the Countess Olenska had lived and suffered—and perhaps tasted joys that were both mysterious and, in New York terms, unnameable. He remembered her amusement at the objections raised by her grandmother and the Wellands to her living in a “Bohemian” quarter given over to “people who wrote.” It was not the peril but the poverty her family disliked; but that shade had escaped her, and she supposed they found literature compromising. She herself had no fear of it. The books scattered about her drawing-room—books in a part of the house where books were usually considered as out of place as kitchen smells in the front parlour—had sharpened Archer’s curiosity with unfamiliar names: Paul Bourget, Huysmans, the Goncourts. They were like foreign seasonings sprinkled on a table set with the same old dishes.

Turning these thoughts over as he approached her door, he felt again that curious reversal she produced in him—how she made him doubt the values he had swallowed whole, how she forced him to imagine conditions so unlike his own that his imagination stumbled, as if the language for them did not exist in his native dialect.

Nastasia opened the door, smiling with a secrecy that, in another household, might have meant a surprise course kept warm. But in the hall Archer’s eye fell at once on what lay on the bench: a sable-lined overcoat, a folded opera hat of dull silk with a gold J. B. on the lining, and a white silk muffler. Costly things, certain things. There was no mistaking Julius Beaufort’s presence.

Anger rose in Archer so quickly it made him light-headed. For an instant he was on the point of scribbling a word on his card and going away. Then he remembered his own discretion—how, in writing to Madame Olenska, he had never plainly said he wished to see her alone. He had left her a door to interpretation; and he had no right to be outraged if she had opened her door to someone else.

He went in, grimly determined to make Beaufort feel himself in the way—and to outstay him.

Beaufort was leaning against the mantelshelf draped with an old embroidery held down by brass candelabra containing church candles of yellowish wax. He thrust his chest out, propped his shoulders on the mantel, and rested his weight on a large patent-leather foot, as if he were a man posing in his own portrait. He was smiling down at his hostess.

Madame Olenska sat on a sofa set at right angles to the chimney; behind it a table banked with flowers formed a screen, and against orchids and azaleas—tributes Archer recognised as having come from Beaufort’s hot-houses—she lay half-reclined, her head propped on her hand, the wide sleeve falling back to leave her arm bare to the elbow.

New York ladies receiving in the evening wore what were called “simple dinner dresses”: close-fitting armour of whale-boned silk, a cautious opening at the neck with lace ruffles filling the crack, sleeves tight until a flounce uncovered just enough wrist for an Etruscan bracelet or a velvet band. Madame Olenska, heedless of that domestic grammar, wore a long robe of red velvet edged at the chin and down the front with glossy black fur.

Archer remembered having seen, in Paris, a Carolus Duran portrait in which a woman wore such a sheath-like robe, her chin nestled in fur with an air both indolent and defiant. Fur in the evening, in a heated drawing-room, was a perversity—like serving a winter dish in summer, heavy and intoxicating, while baring the arms as if for a warmer season. The contrast pleased the eye; it also unsettled, the way an unfamiliar chord unsettles the ear.

“Lord love us—three whole days at Skuytercliff!” Beaufort was saying in his loud, sneering voice as Archer entered. “You’d better take all your furs, and a hot-water-bottle.”

“Why? Is the house so cold?” she asked, holding out her left hand to Archer in a manner that oddly suggested she expected him to kiss it.

“No; but the missus is,” Beaufort said, nodding carelessly toward Archer. “But I thought her so kind. She came herself to invite me. Granny says I must certainly go.”

“Granny would, of course. And I say it’s a shame you’re going to miss the little oyster supper I’d planned for you at Delmonico’s next Sunday, with Campanini and Scalchi and a lot of jolly people.”

She looked doubtfully from the banker to Archer. “Ah—that does tempt me! Except the other evening at Mrs. Struthers’s I’ve not met a single artist since I’ve been here.”

“What kind of artists? I know one or two painters, very good fellows, that I could bring to see you if you’d allow me,” Archer said, too boldly perhaps, but with a sudden wish to oppose Beaufort’s heavy proprietorship with something finer.

“Painters? Are there painters in New York?” Beaufort asked, as if the existence of painters depended on whether he bought their pictures.

Madame Olenska turned to Archer with her grave smile. “That would be charming. But I was really thinking of dramatic artists, singers, actors, musicians. My husband’s house was always full of them.”

She said “my husband” without apparent bitterness, almost with a breath of nostalgia, as if she were recalling not the man but the life that had been served around him—the glittering suppers, the talk, the music drifting through rooms like perfume. Archer stared, perplexed at the ease with which she touched that past at the very moment she was risking her name to escape it. Was it lightness, or some deeper kind of dissimulation—an instinct to keep experience untranslatable, locked in its own tongue?

“I do think,” she went on, “that the imprévu adds to one’s enjoyment. It’s perhaps a mistake to see the same people every day.”

“It’s confoundedly dull, anyhow; New York is dying of dullness,” Beaufort grumbled. “And when I try to liven it up for you, you go back on me. Come—think better of it! Sunday is your last chance, for Campanini leaves next week for Baltimore and Philadelphia; and I’ve a private room, and a Steinway, and they’ll sing all night for me.”

“How delicious! May I think it over, and write to you tomorrow morning?” Her voice was amiable, yet it carried the faintest edge of dismissal.

Beaufort felt it. He stood staring, the stubborn line between his eyes deepening.

“Why not now?”

“It’s too serious a question to decide at this late hour.”

“Do you call it late?”

She met his glance coolly. “Yes; because I have still to talk business with Mr. Archer for a little while.”

“Ah,” Beaufort snapped. There was no appeal from her tone. With a slight shrug he recovered his composure, took her hand and kissed it with practised ease, and from the threshold called out: “I say, Newland, if you can persuade the Countess to stop in town of course you’re included in the supper,” and went out with his heavy, important step.

For a moment Archer thought Letterblair must have warned her he was coming; but her next words undeceived him.

“You know painters, then? You live in their milieu?” she asked, her eyes alight with genuine curiosity.

“Oh—not exactly. I don’t know that the arts have a milieu here, any of them; they’re more like a very thinly settled outskirt.”

“But you care for such things?”

“Immensely. When I’m in Paris or London I never miss an exhibition. I try to keep up.”

She glanced down at the tip of the little satin boot peeping from the hem of her draperies. “I used to care immensely too: my life was full of such things. But now I want to try not to.”

“You want to try not to?”

“Yes: I want to cast off all my old life, to become just like everybody else here.”

Archer felt heat rise under his collar. “You’ll never be like everybody else,” he said.

Her straight eyebrows lifted. “Ah, don’t say that. If you knew how I hate to be different!” Her face had the sombre stillness of a tragic mask. She leaned forward, clasping her knee in thin hands, and stared past him as if looking into a country whose names she could not pronounce. “I want to get away from it all,” she insisted.

He waited, then cleared his throat. “I know. Mr. Letterblair has told me.”

“Ah?” She turned quickly. “That’s the reason you’ve come. He asked you to—you see I’m in the firm.”

She looked surprised, then relieved; her eyes brightened as if a lamp had been trimmed. “You mean you can manage it for me? I can talk to you instead of Mr. Letterblair? Oh, that will be so much easier!”

Her trust warmed him, and with it came that self-satisfied confidence he was ashamed of and could not resist. He saw now that her “business” had been a pretext to get rid of Beaufort; and to have routed Beaufort felt, absurdly, like a triumph.

“I am here to talk about it,” he repeated.

She did not at once answer. Her head rested again against her hand; the rich red of her dress made her face look paler, as if the colour had been drawn out of her skin and poured into the velvet. She struck him suddenly as not merely unusual but pathetic—almost pitiful—in her isolation.

Now, he thought, they were coming to hard facts; and he felt in himself the same instinctive recoil he had so often criticised in his mother and her friends. He had so little practice in the difficult. Even the vocabulary of what they were about to discuss seemed foreign, belonging to novels, to theatre programmes—words one heard, perhaps, but did not use at one’s own table.

After a pause she broke out with unexpected vehemence. “I want to be free; I want to wipe out all the past.”

“I understand that.”

Her cheeks warmed faintly. “Then you’ll help me?”

“First—” He hesitated. “Perhaps I ought to know a little more.”

She looked surprised. “You know about my husband—my life with him?” He assented. “Well—then—what more is there? In this country are such things tolerated? I’m a Protestant—our church does not forbid divorce in such cases.”

“Certainly not.”

Silence returned, and in it Archer felt again the hideous presence of Count Olenski’s letter—half a page of vague accusations in a blackguard’s hand. He had called it, to Letterblair, the vague charge of an angry brute; yet vagueness could be more poisonous than detail, because it let each hearer supply his own flavour of scandal.

“I’ve looked through the papers you gave to Mr. Letterblair,” Archer said at last.

“Well—can there be anything more abominable?”

“No.” She shifted slightly, lifting her hand to screen her eyes.

“Of course you know,” Archer continued, “that if your husband chooses to fight the case—as he threatens to—”

“Yes—?”

“He can say things—things that might be unpl—might be disagreeable to you: say them publicly, so that they would get about, and harm you even if—”

“If—?” she echoed.

“I mean: no matter how unfounded they were.”

She was silent for so long that, to avoid staring at her shaded face, Archer found himself looking with strange attention at her other hand on her knee—its exact shape, the small knuckles, the three rings on the fourth and fifth fingers. Among them he noticed there was no wedding ring. The detail pierced him with an intimacy he had not asked for.

At length she said, very quietly: “What harm could such accusations, even if he made them publicly, do me here?”

“My poor child—far more harm than anywhere else!” rushed to his lips. But he answered in a voice that sounded, even to himself, like Mr. Letterblair’s.

“New York society is a very small world compared with the one you’ve lived in. And it’s ruled, in spite of appearances, by a few people with—well, rather old-fashioned ideas.”

She said nothing, and he went on, carefully, like a man cutting bread too thin because the loaf is costly.

“Our ideas about marriage and divorce are particularly old-fashioned. Our legislation favours divorce—our social customs don’t.”

“Never?”

“Well—not if the woman, however injured, however irreproachable, has appearances in the least degree against her, has exposed herself by any unconventional action to—to offensive insinuations—”

Her head drooped a little lower. Archer waited, hoping—almost praying—for a flash of anger, for a crisp denial, for some clean word that would rinse the air. None came.

A little travelling clock ticked purringly at her elbow, like a small animal asleep and breathing. In the grate a log broke and sent up a shower of sparks; the sound was sharp in the hush, as if the room itself were listening.

“Yes,” she murmured at last, “that’s what my family tell me.”

He winced. “It’s not unnatural—”

“Our family,” she corrected softly; and Archer felt the blood climb into his face.

“For you’ll be my cousin soon,” she added gently.

“I hope so.”

“And you take their view?”

He stood up, suddenly restless. He wandered across the room, stared without seeing at one of the pictures against the old red damask, then came back to her side. How could he say: Yes—if what your husband hints is true, or if you have no way of disproving it? The very thought seemed indecent in speech, yet her silence had invited it.

“Sincerely—” she began, as if to stop him.

He looked down into the fire. “Sincerely, then—what should you gain that would compensate for the possibility—the certainty—of a lot of beastly talk?”

“But my freedom—is that nothing?”

In that instant the charge in Olenski’s letter flashed before him as if it had been written in flame: that it was true, and that she meant to marry the partner of her guilt. He saw, too, with cold clarity, the brutal mechanism of law: the State’s inexorable opposition to certain kinds of freedom. The suspicion made him feel harsh and impatient, as if she were asking him to season poison and call it a feast.

“But aren’t you as free as air as it is?” he said. “Who can touch you? Mr. Letterblair tells me the financial question has been settled—”

“Oh, yes,” she answered indifferently, as if money were merely a plate cleared away.

“Well, then: is it worth while to risk what may be infinitely disagreeable and painful? Think of the newspapers—their vileness! It’s all stupid and narrow and unjust—but one can’t make over society.”

“No,” she said; and the word was so faint, so desolate, that remorse flooded him for his own hard imaginings.

“The individual, in such cases, is nearly always sacrificed to what is supposed to be the collective interest,” he went on, hearing himself recite the familiar phrases as if he were repeating a lesson in a language not quite his own. “People cling to any convention that keeps the family together—protects the children, if there are any.” He spoke on, urgently, piling up stock arguments like dishes set between them to prevent any naked truth from being passed hand to hand.

Since she would not—or could not—say the single word that might have cleared the air, he wanted only to keep them on the surface, in that prudent New York way that preferred silence to confession, propriety to speech, the safe and tasteless to the dangerous and vivid.

“It’s my business,” he added, “to help you to see these things as the people who are fondest of you see them. The Mingotts, the Wellands, the van der Luydens, all your friends and relations: if I didn’t show you honestly how they judge such questions, it wouldn’t be fair of me, would it?”

He heard his own voice grow insistent, almost pleading, as if he were trying to drown out the meaning of her silence with the very sound of accepted words.

She said slowly: “No; it wouldn’t be fair.”

The fire had crumbled into greyness. One of the lamps made a gurgling appeal for attention, as if it, too, were choking on the air of the room. Madame Olenska rose, wound it up with a quick practised motion, and came back toward the hearth without sitting down again. Her remaining on her feet told him, plainly, that there was nothing left for either of them to say.

Archer rose also.

“Very well; I will do what you wish,” she said abruptly.

The suddenness of her surrender took his breath. Blood rushed to his forehead. He caught both her hands awkwardly in his, as if he could warm them by force.

“I—I do want to help you,” he stammered.

“You do help me. Good night, my cousin.”

He bent and pressed his lips to her hands. They were cold, lifeless, as though all her heat had been spent earlier, in that one fierce declaration: I want to be free. She drew her hands away.

In the hall, under the faint gas-light, he found his coat and hat. The sable-lined overcoat and the opera hat with the gold initials lay where they had been, silent evidence of another man’s ease in her rooms. Archer went out into the winter night, where the city’s muted sounds—the distant wheels, the thin crackle of frost underfoot—seemed gentler than the talk he had helped to silence, and he carried with him, bursting too late into shape, all the belated eloquence of the inarticulate.

Wallack’s was packed to the cornice, the whole house breathing together as if the velvet, the gaslight, and the press of bodies had made one great animal of it. “The Shaughraun” was in full tide—Boucicault nimble as ever in the title role, the English company at that sweet crest of popularity where even the stale turns of a familiar scene could be made to taste new. From the galleries came the hearty, unembarrassed roar of delight; below, in stalls and boxes, the applause wore gloves—people smiled at the old contrivances and yet surrendered to them, because the crowd itself, with its layered murmur and sudden storms of clapping, made surrender easy.

There was one passage that always stilled the house into a kind of listening silence, a hush so exact that Newland Archer could hear the faint creak of dress-fabrics and the soft percussion of a dropped programme as distinctly as a drum in a small room. It was the parting: Montague, after a brief, dry exchange with Ada Dyas—words pared down to something like bone—turning to go while she stood by the mantel, her face angled toward the fire as if warmth were a language she understood better than speech.

She wore a grey cashmere gown, unlooped, untrimmed, falling in long clean lines that seemed to make her stillness more eloquent. At her throat, a narrow velvet ribbon—black, and so simple it looked almost severe—its ends trailing down her back. When he turned from her, she braced her arms on the mantel-shelf and hid her face in her hands. He reached the threshold, paused, then stole back with the caution of a man entering a sanctuary; he lifted one end of the velvet ribbon and pressed his lips to it—no more than a touch, like tasting a remembered thing—and slipped away while she remained motionless, unalerted. The curtain dropped upon that dumb surrender, and the audience, caught in the net of it, held its breath a moment longer than the stage required.

It was for that scene that Archer came, again and again. He had seen great actors in Paris and London spend themselves in torrents of speech and gesture; yet this reticence—the refusal to translate grief into display—moved him more deeply than any famous outpouring. Tonight it struck him with a particular ache, as if the theatre’s hush had made room for another silence entirely: his own leave-taking from Madame Olenska after their talk a week or ten days earlier.

He could not have explained the likeness, and if one had set the two tableaux side by side, they would have seemed to belong to different worlds. He was no romantic young actor with a handsome anguish; and Ada Dyas, tall and red-haired, had a monumental plainness, her pale face pleasantly ugly in a way that was the very opposite of Ellen Olenska’s vividness. Nor had he and Madame Olenska been lovers parting in broken-hearted quiet: they had been client and lawyer separating after a consultation that had left the lawyer with the worst possible sense of the client’s case.

And yet his heart beat with a retrospective excitement, a faint, dangerous pleasure in remembering. The resemblance lay, perhaps, in Ellen Olenska’s mysterious capacity to suggest a life beyond the daily run of things—tragic and moving possibilities that hovered at the edge of common rooms like music heard through a closed door. She hardly ever said anything to produce that effect; it was simply part of her, as if her outlandish past clung to her like an incommunicable scent, or as if something dramatic, passionate, unusual, lived quietly in her very nerves.

Archer had always inclined to think that chance and circumstance mattered less than the inward tendency some people had for events—for collisions, for upheavals—to seek them out. In Madame Olenska he had felt this from the first. She seemed to him precisely the kind of woman to whom things were bound to happen, no matter how she shrank from them. The singular point was that she had lived in an atmosphere so thick with drama that her own propensity to provoke it had apparently passed unnoticed. The odd absence of surprise in her—her way of taking certain facts for granted—was what gave him the sense of her having been plucked out of a maelstrom. What she accepted without comment measured what she had had to rebel against.

He had left her that evening with the conviction—painful, reluctant—that Count Olenski’s accusation was not unfounded. The shadowy figure in her past, “the secretary,” had probably not gone unrewarded for his part in her escape. The conditions she had fled were intolerable—past speaking of, past believing. She was young, frightened, desperate. What more natural than gratitude to a rescuer? And what more cruel than that gratitude’s power, in the law’s eyes and the world’s, to level her with the husband she abhorred?

He had made her understand this, because he was bound to; and he had made her understand something else, harder still: that simple-hearted, kindly New York—on whose broader charity she had counted—was precisely the place where she could least hope for indulgence. To have to tell her that, and to watch her accept it with a quiet resignation, had hurt him in a way he had not expected to feel. It drew him to her with obscure currents of jealousy and pity, as if her dumbly confessed error had placed her—humbled, and therefore dear—in his hands. He was glad it was to him she had spoken, and not to the cold professional scrutiny of Mr. Letterblair, nor under the embarrassed gaze of her family.

And, as was inevitable in that world, he had at once made it his business to reassure the others: to assure them she had abandoned her idea of divorce, basing the decision on her having understood its uselessness. With infinite relief they had all turned their eyes away from the “unpleasantness” she had spared them.

“I was sure Newland would manage it,” Mrs. Welland had said proudly of her future son-in-law; and old Mrs. Mingott, having summoned him for a confidential interview, had congratulated him on his cleverness and then snapped out impatiently: “Silly goose! I told her myself what nonsense it was. Wanting to pass herself off as Ellen Mingott and an old maid, when she has the luck to be a married woman and a Countess!”

Those voices—cheerful, complacent, incurious—ran now like an under-tune beneath the theatre’s applause. When the curtain fell on the stage-parting, Archer’s eyes filled unexpectedly, and he stood to go. In rising he turned toward the boxes behind him—and there, as if the evening had been arranged by some director with an ironical taste for symmetry, he saw the woman he had been thinking of.

Madame Olenska was seated in a box with the Beauforts. Lawrence Lefferts was there, and one or two other men, and the whole little group seemed framed in gilt and plush as neatly as the play itself. Archer had not spoken with Ellen alone since their evening together; indeed he had tried to avoid being with her in company. But their eyes met; at the same moment Mrs. Beaufort recognized him and made her languid gesture of invitation—an airy motion that managed to be both a welcome and a sign that she need not exert herself further. He could not, without awkwardness, refuse. He went in.

Beaufort and Lefferts shifted, giving him space, and after a few words to Mrs. Beaufort—who always preferred to look beautiful rather than to talk—Archer seated himself behind Madame Olenska. The only other person in the box was Mr. Sillerton Jackson, bent toward Mrs. Beaufort and confiding, in that carefully modulated undertone which suggested both secrecy and the pleasure of being overheard, details of Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s last Sunday reception—where, some people reported, there had actually been dancing.

Under cover of this narrative, to which Mrs. Beaufort listened with her perfect smile and her head held at just the angle that gave the stalls her most flattering profile, Madame Olenska turned slightly and spoke to Archer in a low voice.

“Do you think,” she asked, glancing toward the stage, “he will send her a bunch of yellow roses tomorrow morning?”

The question struck him like the sudden lifting of a lid from something he had kept covered. He reddened; his heart gave a leap of surprised delight. He had called twice on Madame Olenska, and each time had sent her a box of yellow roses—each time without a card. She had never alluded to them; he had assumed she had never connected them with him. Now, with that simple, almost playful sentence, she showed that she had known; and by linking the roses to the tenderness on the stage she gave his small anonymous act a meaning it had not dared to claim for itself.

“I was thinking of that too,” he said, his voice a little unsteady. “I was going to leave the theatre in order to take the picture away with me.”

To his surprise, colour rose in her face—reluctantly, duskily, as if it came from some deeper source than mere amusement. She lowered her eyes to the mother-of-pearl opera-glass in her smoothly gloved hands and, after a pause, said quietly: “What do you do while May is away?”

“I stick to my work,” he answered, and felt, faintly, an annoyance at the question—as though it pried at the careful partitions he had been building.

The Wellands, in obedience to a habit as established as a religion, had left the previous week for St. Augustine. Mr. Welland’s bronchial tubes—so often discussed and so seldom visibly afflicted—required, it was believed, the southern air for the latter part of the winter. Mr. Welland was a mild, silent man with no opinions and many habits, and his habits were treated as laws of nature. One of them demanded that his wife and daughter should accompany him on his annual migration. An unbroken domesticity was essential to his peace of mind; he would not have known where his hair-brushes were, nor how to obtain stamps for his letters, without Mrs. Welland there to tell him. Since all the members of the family adored one another, and since he was the central object of their idolatry, it never occurred to his wife and May to let him go alone. The sons—both in the law, and therefore winter-bound to New York—would join for Easter and travel back with him.

Archer could not decently question the necessity of May’s accompanying her father. The family physician’s reputation rested largely on the pneumonia Mr. Welland had never had; and Mr. Welland’s insistence on St. Augustine was inflexible. Originally, May’s engagement to Archer had been meant to remain unannounced until her return from Florida. The fact that it had been made known sooner was not expected to disturb Mr. Welland’s plans.

Archer would have liked to go south—sunshine, boating, May’s clear presence made brighter by open air. But custom and convention held him as firmly as any legal obligation. Little arduous as his professional duties were, he would have been convicted of frivolity by the whole Mingott clan if he had asked for a holiday in mid-winter. So he accepted May’s departure with the resignation he was beginning to recognize as one of the principal ingredients of married life.

He was conscious, now, that Madame Olenska was watching him from under lowered lids.

“I have done what you wished—what you advised,” she said abruptly.

“Ah—I’m glad,” he returned, embarrassed that she should broach it here, with Beaufort’s broad back near and Sillerton Jackson’s murmuring gossip filling the air like a thin, persistent instrument.

“I understand—that you were right,” she went on, a little breathlessly; “but sometimes life is difficult… perplexing…”

“I know.”

“And I wanted to tell you that I do feel you were right; and that I’m grateful to you,” she ended; and as she spoke she lifted her opera-glass quickly to her eyes, because the door of the box opened and Beaufort’s resonant voice broke in on them with the noise of a larger world reclaiming its place.

Archer stood up and left the box and the theatre. The sounds of Wallack’s—applause, voices, the stirring of the crowd—followed him out into the corridor and down the stairs, thinning as he went, until the night air received him with its own harsher notes: wheels on paving-stones, a distant call, the city’s perpetual unrest.

Only the day before he had received a letter from May Welland. With her characteristic candour she had asked him to “be kind to Ellen” during their absence.

“She likes you and admires you so much—and you know, though she doesn’t show it, she’s still very lonely and unhappy. I don’t think Granny understands her, or uncle Lovell Mingott either; they really think she’s much worldlier and fonder of society than she is. And I can quite see that New York must seem dull to her, though the family won’t admit it. I think she’s been used to lots of things we haven’t got; wonderful music, and picture shows, and celebrities—artists and authors and all the clever people you admire. Granny can’t understand her wanting anything but lots of dinners and clothes—but I can see that you’re almost the only person in New York who can talk to her about what she really cares for.”

His wise May—how he had loved her for that letter, for the generous confidence in it, for the way her innocence could sometimes strike straight to a truth. Yet he had not meant to act on her request. He was too busy, for one thing; and he did not care, as an engaged man, to play too conspicuously the part of Madame Olenska’s champion. Besides, he had an idea that Ellen knew how to take care of herself far better than May imagined. Beaufort was visibly at her feet; Mr. van der Luyden hovered above her like a protecting deity; and any number of candidates—Lawrence Lefferts among them—waited their opportunity in the middle distance.

And yet, as he walked away into the city’s clamour, he could not deny what he felt each time he saw her, each time her voice slid through the din and found him: May’s ingenuousness, after all, came close to divination. Ellen Olenska was lonely, and she was unhappy.

As he came out into the lobby, the last muffled bars of the orchestra still clinging to the plush and gilt, Archer ran into Ned Winsett. Winsett stood a little apart from the well-fed ripple of the crowd, his shoulders rounded under a coat that had known too many weathers, and his thin face turned, not toward the departing ladies, but back toward the Beaufort box, as if a single glance could keep what he had seen from slipping away into the general churn of Fifth Avenue talk.

They shook hands; Winsett’s palm was dry and ink-stained, the hand of a man who lived by words and yet could never afford to waste them. He nodded toward the street with the air of proposing something both consoling and cheap.

“Bock?” he suggested. “Little German place round the corner. They draw it properly—cold as a cellar stone, with that clean yeast-breath on it.”

Archer, who had the taste of theatre dust in his throat and an impatience for Winsett’s corner of the world just then, declined on the plea that he had work to do at home. Winsett’s mouth twisted in a half-smile.

“Oh, well—so have I, for that matter. I’ll be the Industrious Apprentice too.”

They went out together. Broadway sounded raw after the house: wheels on paving-stones, a newsboy’s cry, the clack of hoofs, the thin metallic laughter of a street-organ struggling against the traffic. Archer noticed, as he always did, how the city’s noise changed a man’s thoughts—how it pressed them flatter, made them harder to hold. Winsett walked as if he were used to being jostled by sound and circumstance, and presently said, as if the matter had been brewing in him all evening:

“Look here—what I’m really after is the name of the dark lady in that swell box of yours—with the Beauforts, wasn’t she? The one your friend Lefferts seems so smitten by.”

Archer could not have said why, but annoyance pricked him—quick and irrational. What did Ned Winsett want with Ellen Olenska’s name? And why, above all, drag Julius Beaufort’s box and Lawrence Lefferts’s glib admiration into it? Winsett was not usually curious in that way; then Archer remembered: journalist. The habit of needing names, needing labels, needing the proper spelling for a thing that, once named, could be circulated till it lost its savor.

“It’s not for an interview, I hope?” Archer laughed.

“Well—not for the press; just for myself,” Winsett returned. “The fact is, she’s a neighbour of mine—queer quarter for such a beauty to settle in—and she’s been awfully kind to my little boy.”

Archer’s annoyance loosened, and something warmer dilated in him—an intimate glow, as if a shutter had been lifted and a small domestic scene allowed him, suddenly, into her life.

“He fell down her area chasing his kitten,” Winsett went on, “and gave himself a nasty cut. She rushed in bareheaded, carrying him in her arms, with his knee all beautifully bandaged—clean work, mind you; she’d have made a decent nurse in a campaign—so sympathetic and beautiful that my wife was too dazzled to ask her name.”

There was nothing extraordinary in the tale; any woman could have tied up a child’s knee. But Archer saw it as if he had been there: Ellen without hat or cloak, hair loosened by haste, hands steady with that particular competence which came not from training but from pity that did not pause to consider itself. He could almost smell the sharp clean bite of spirits, the faint sweet of old linen, the sting of winter air brought into a narrow staircase.

“That is the Countess Olenska,” he said. “A granddaughter of old Mrs. Mingott’s.”

“Whew—a Countess!” Winsett whistled. “Well, I didn’t know Countesses were so neighbourly. Mingotts ain’t.”

“They would be, if you’d let them,” Archer answered—knowing even as he spoke that it was their old, worn argument: the refusal of the “clever people” to frequent the fashionable; the fashionable’s conviction that cleverness was merely a contrivance for being shabby with a good conscience. They had crossed that ground too many times to find anything new in it.

Winsett broke off, his eyes narrowing, that sudden flicker of penetration lighting his tired face.

“I wonder how a Countess happens to live in our slum?”

“Because she doesn’t care a hang about where she lives,” Archer said, and heard in his own tone a secret pride in her freedom, “or about any of our little social sign-posts.”

“H’m—been in bigger places, I suppose,” Winsett commented, and then nodded toward his corner. “Well—here’s my turning.”

He slouched away across Broadway, swallowed by the jangle of vehicles and the shout of men. Archer stood and watched him go, thinking of the way Winsett’s mind flashed—brief illuminations over a landscape he seemed content to tramp through in twilight. Those flashes were the most interesting thing about him, and always made Archer wonder why they had allowed him, at an age when most men were still struggling, to accept failure so stolidly.

Archer had known that Winsett had a wife and child, but he had never seen them. He met Winsett at the Century, or in the haunts where journalists and theatrical people ate and drank cheaply among loud voices and perpetual smoke. The German restaurant Winsett had mentioned came back to him with sensory exactness: the sour-sweet smell of cabbage boiled too long, the honest savor of sausage split open to the heat, the beer’s cold foam leaving rings on thick glass, and above all the clamor—chairs scraped, plates slapped down, a piano ground out of tune—sound used like fuel to keep a room from thinking.

Winsett had once given Archer to understand that his wife was an invalid; it might be true, or might merely mean she lacked the social gifts, the evening clothes, or both. Winsett himself had a savage abhorrence of social observances. Archer, who dressed in the evening because he thought it cleaner and more comfortable to do so, had never paused to reckon that cleanliness and comfort were among the costliest items in a modest budget. He had always taken Winsett’s attitude for part of the dull “Bohemian” posture that amused and irritated people who changed their clothes without remarking on it and did not harangue about servants and simplicity. Yet Winsett always stimulated him; whenever Archer caught sight of the lean bearded face and melancholy eyes, he found himself routing the man out and carrying him off for long talk.

Winsett was not a journalist by choice. He was a pure man of letters, untimely born in a world that had no need of letters—no patience for the slow reduction of life into exact words. After publishing one volume of brief, exquisite literary appreciations—of which one hundred and twenty copies were sold, thirty given away, and the rest destroyed by the publishers (as per contract) to make room for more marketable matter—he had abandoned his real calling and taken a sub-editorial job on a women’s weekly. Fashion-plates and paper patterns alternated with New England love-stories and advertisements of temperance drinks. On the subject of “Hearth-fires” (as the paper was called) he was inexhaustibly entertaining; but under his jokes lay the sterile bitterness of a still-young man who had tried and given up.

His talk always made Archer take the measure of his own life and feel how little it contained; but Winsett’s, after all, contained still less. Their common stock of intellectual curiosity made their conversations exhilarating, and yet their exchange of views usually remained within the limits of a pensive dilettantism—as if they were forever tasting dishes they could not afford to order.

“The fact is, life isn’t much a fit for either of us,” Winsett had once said. “I’m down and out; nothing to be done about it. I’ve got only one ware to produce, and there’s no market for it here, and won’t be in my time. But you—you’re free and you’re well-off. Why don’t you get into touch? There’s only one way to do it: go into politics.”

Archer had thrown his head back and laughed. In that laugh was the whole unbridgeable difference between men like Winsett and men like Archer. Every one in polite circles knew that, in America, a gentleman couldn’t go into politics. But since he could hardly put it in those words, he had answered evasively:

“Look at the career of the honest man in American politics! They don’t want us.”

“Who’s ‘they’? Why don’t you all get together and be ‘they’ yourselves?”

Archer’s smile had lingered—slightly condescending. It was useless to prolong it. Everybody knew the fate of the few gentlemen who had risked their clean linen in municipal or state politics in New York. The day was past when it was possible; the country was in possession of bosses and the emigrant, and decent people had to fall back on sport or culture.

“Culture!” Winsett had cried. “Yes—if we had it. But there are just a few little local patches, dying out here and there for lack of—well, hoeing and cross-fertilising: the last remnants of the old European tradition that your forebears brought with them. But you’re in a pitiful little minority: you’ve got no centre, no competition, no audience. You’re like the pictures on the walls of a deserted house: ‘The Portrait of a Gentleman.’ You’ll never amount to anything—any of you—till you roll up your sleeves and get right down into the muck. That, or emigrate… God! If I could emigrate…”

Archer, mentally shrugging, had always turned the talk back to books—where Winsett, even when uncertain, was interesting. Emigrate! As if a gentleman could abandon his country. One could no more do that than roll up one’s sleeves and go down into the muck. A gentleman stayed at home and abstained. But you couldn’t make a man like Winsett hear the unspoken rules; perhaps because rules that were never spoken were like dialect words—understood only inside the little circle that used them, untranslatable outside it without losing their force.

And it was this, Archer thought, that made the New York of literary clubs and exotic restaurants—though at first shake it looked like a kaleidoscope—turn out, in the end, to be a smaller box with a more monotonous pattern than the assembled atoms of Fifth Avenue. The same phrases, the same approvals and condemnations, clattered through every room like the ceaseless street-noise outside: a din that drowned the finer registers of feeling before they could become articulate.

The next morning Archer scoured the town in vain for more yellow roses. He went from florist to florist with a hunger that had, absurdly, the keenness of appetite: as if the right shade of gold, the right clustered scent—honeyed and green, with that faint peppery undernote in the petals—could be found and carried, like a message, to the one person who would understand what it meant. The shops were bright with winter flowers forced into bloom, their moisture and leaf-smell rising under glass; but nowhere did he see that particular warm yellow he wanted—yellow like a small domestic sun held in the hand.

In consequence of this search he arrived late at the office, perceived at once that his lateness made no difference whatever to anyone, and was filled with sudden exasperation at the elaborate futility of his life. Why should he not, at that moment, be on the sands of St. Augustine with May Welland? No one was deceived by his pretense of professional activity.

In old-fashioned legal firms like that of which Mr. Letterblair was the head—firms mainly engaged in the management of large estates and “conservative” investments—there were always two or three young men, fairly well-off and without professional ambition, who for a certain number of hours each day sat at their desks accomplishing trivial tasks or simply reading newspapers. It was supposed proper that they should have an occupation; the crude fact of money-making was still regarded as derogatory; and the law, being a profession, was accounted a more gentlemanly pursuit than business. But none of these young men had much hope of advancing, or any earnest desire to do so. Over many of them the green mould of the perfunctory was already perceptibly spreading.

It made Archer shiver to think that it might be spreading over him too.

He had, to be sure, other tastes and interests: he spent his vacations in European travel, cultivated the “clever people” May spoke of, and generally tried to “keep up,” as he had somewhat wistfully put it to Madame Olenska. But once he was married, what would become of this narrow margin of life in which his real experiences were lived? He had seen enough of other young men who had dreamed his dream—perhaps less ardently—who had gradually sunk into the placid, luxurious routine of their elders. Their days became like dinners repeated with slight variations: the same courses, the same sauces, the same talk, until even pleasure lost its flavor from being served too often and too predictably.

From the office he sent a note by messenger to Madame Olenska, asking if he might call that afternoon, and begging her to let him find a reply at his club. At the club he found nothing; nor did he receive any letter the following day.

This unexpected silence mortified him beyond reason. It was not only the thwarting of a plan; it was as though the one voice he had begun to distinguish from the general city-din had suddenly fallen silent, leaving him with nothing but the loud, familiar hum of his own world. And though the next morning he saw, behind a florist’s window-pane, a glorious cluster of yellow roses—tight-hearted buds and a few full-blown flowers exhaling their rich, almost buttery perfume into the heated shop—he left it there, turning away as if the sight of them could bruise him.

Only on the third morning did he receive a line by post from the Countess Olenska. To his surprise it was dated from Skuytercliff, whither the van der Luydens had promptly retreated after putting the Duke on board his steamer.

“I ran away,” the writer began abruptly, without the usual preliminaries, “the day after I saw you at the play, and these kind friends have taken me in. I wanted to be quiet, and think things over. You were right in telling me how kind they were; I feel myself so safe here. I wish that you were with us.”

She ended with a conventional “Yours sincerely,” and without any allusion to the date of her return.

The tone surprised Archer. What was Madame Olenska running away from, and why did she need to be safe? His first thought, as it always was with her, went to some dark menace from abroad—an invisible hand reaching across the ocean. Then he reflected that he did not know her epistolary style, and that it might run to picturesque exaggeration. Women always exaggerated; and moreover she was not wholly at her ease in English, which she often spoke as if she were translating from the French. He could hear, behind her words, the cadence of another tongue—the way meaning shifted when poured from one language into another, losing one flavor to gain a different sharpness.

“Je me suis évadée…” Put in that way, the opening sentence immediately suggested that she might merely have wanted to escape a boring round of engagements; which was very likely true, for he judged her to be capricious and easily wearied of the pleasure of the moment.

It amused him to think of the van der Luydens’ having carried her off to Skuytercliff on a second visit—and this time for an indefinite period. The doors of Skuytercliff were rarely and grudgingly opened to visitors; a chilly week-end was the most ever offered to the few thus privileged. But Archer had seen, on his last visit to Paris, the delicious play of Labiche, “Le Voyage de M. Perrichon,” and he remembered M. Perrichon’s dogged, undiscouraged attachment to the young man he had pulled out of the glacier. The van der Luydens had rescued Madame Olenska from a doom almost as icy; and though there were many other reasons for being attracted to her, Archer knew that beneath them all lay his own gentle and obstinate determination to go on rescuing her.

He felt a distinct disappointment on learning she was away; and almost immediately remembered that only the day before he had refused an invitation to spend the following Sunday with the Reggie Chiverses at their house on the Hudson, a few miles below Skuytercliff. He had had his fill long ago of the noisy friendly parties at Highbank—coasting, ice-boating, sleighing, long tramps in the snow, and a general flavour of mild flirting and milder practical jokes. He had just received a box of new books from his London bookseller—fresh paper, crisp cut pages, the faint dusty sweetness of cloth bindings—and had preferred the prospect of a quiet Sunday at home with his spoils.

Now he went into the club writing-room, wrote a hurried telegram, and told the servant to send it immediately. He knew that Mrs. Reggie did not object to her visitors suddenly changing their minds, and that there was always a room to spare in her elastic house.

Newland Archer reached the Chiverses’ place on Friday evening, and on Saturday submitted, as if to a liturgy, to every rite a country week-end at Highbank demanded.

In the morning he skimmed the ice in a little boat that hissed like a knife along a plate, the wind singing in the rigging and the runners ringing on the hard surface; there was a clean, mineral smell where the lake had frozen, and the distant cry of a crow stitched itself into the white air. His hostess laughed too loudly, as people did when the cold made them feel alive and watched at once, and Newland smiled when he ought, answering in the accepted phrases.

In the afternoon Reggie Chivers took him “over the farm,” past stalls as polished as drawing-rooms, where the sweet ammoniac warmth of horses and the sharp bite of leather rose and fell with every breath; the clink of a currycomb against a buckle had the exactness of music, and Reggie’s voice, droning on about bloodlines and feed and the virtues of a particular bit, seemed almost part of the stable soundscape, as necessary as the stamping and the patient, heavy exhalations. Archer listened, nodded, asked the proper questions, and felt the hours packed around him like wool.

After tea—tea that tasted of over-steeped leaves and propriety—he talked in a corner of the hall, firelight making a false dusk on gilded cornices, with a young lady who had once announced herself broken-hearted at his engagement and now, with the zeal of the newly consoled, wanted him to be moved by her own matrimonial prospects. The house itself was never quiet. Doors opened and shut, feet crossed corridors, a servant appeared with coals, another with the tray, and the constant, careful rustle of comfort made its own kind of noise—an upholstered, well-fed noise that left no room for a private thought.

At midnight the week-end’s spirit of regulated disorder took possession of the guests. Archer helped put a gold-fish into one visitor’s bed; he assisted in dressing up a burglar in the bath-room of a nervous aunt; and, toward dawn, he was drawn into a pillow-fight that ranged from nurseries to basement, the shrieks and thuds and flurried steps ricocheting through stairwells and passages until the whole place sounded like a holiday market hastily shut in under a roof.

But Sunday after luncheon he borrowed a cutter and drove over to Skuytercliff.

People had always been told the house there was an Italian villa. Those who had never been to Italy believed it; so did some who had. It had been built by Mr. van der Luyden on his return from the grand tour, in anticipation of his marriage to Miss Louisa Dagonet: a large square wooden structure painted pale green and white, with a Corinthian portico and fluted pilasters between the windows—Italy translated into the stiff grammar of New York carpentry, a dialect that tried for melody and achieved only correctness.

From the high ground a series of terraces, bordered by balustrades and urns, descended in the steel-engraving style to a small irregular lake edged with asphalt and overhung by rare weeping conifers. Right and left the famous weedless lawns, studded with specimen trees—each a different variety, each labelled by its mere existence—rolled away to long ranges of grass crested with elaborate cast-iron ornaments. Below, tucked into a hollow like an older sentence surviving in a new language, lay the four-roomed stone house the first Patroon had built on the land granted him in 1612.

Against the uniform sheet of snow and the greyish winter sky the villa loomed up grimly; even in summer it kept its distance, and the boldest coleus bed had never ventured within thirty feet of its awful front. Now, when Archer rang, the long tinkle seemed to run through the place as through a mausoleum. It took time for the sound to be answered. When at length the butler appeared, his surprise was such as if he had been called back from final sleep.

Happily Archer was of the family; irregular though his arrival was, he was entitled to information.
