“The Countess Olenska is out, sir,” the butler said, with the grave relief of a man delivering a message to which his whole house was subordinate. “She drove to afternoon service with Mrs. van der Luyden exactly three quarters of an hour ago. Mr. van der Luyden is in, sir; but my impression is that he is either finishing his nap or else reading yesterday’s Evening Post. I heard him say, sir, on his return from church, that he intended to look through the Evening Post after luncheon; if you like, sir, I might go to the library door and listen—”

Archer thanked him and said he would go and meet the ladies. The butler, obviously relieved that he was not to be sent as a spy to a library door, closed the great door on him with majesty. A groom took the cutter away, runners crunching on packed snow like bones underfoot, and Archer struck through the park toward the high-road.

Skuytercliff village was only a mile and a half away; but Mrs. van der Luyden never walked, and he knew he must keep to the road to meet the carriage. Presently, however, coming down a footpath that crossed the highway, he caught sight of a slight figure in a red cloak, with a big dog running ahead.

He hurried forward. Madame Olenska stopped short and smiled as if he were the answer to something she had asked of the air.

“Ah, you’ve come!” she said, drawing her hand from her muff.

The red cloak made her look gay and vivid, like the Ellen Mingott of old days—something bright and alive moving across the disciplined whiteness. He laughed as he took her hand.

“I came to see what you were running away from.”

Her face clouded, but she answered: “Ah—well. You will see, presently.”

It puzzled him. “Why—do you mean you’ve been overtaken?”

She shrugged, with a little movement that reminded him of Nastasia, and at once lightened her tone. “Shall we walk on? I’m so cold after the sermon. And what does it matter, now you’re here to protect me?”

The word protect set his blood beating like a drum. He caught a fold of her cloak, as if to hold her where she stood.

“Ellen—what is it? You must tell me.”

“Oh, presently—let’s run a race first: my feet are freezing to the ground,” she cried; and, gathering her cloak, she fled across the snow, the dog leaping about her with challenging barks.

For a moment Archer stood watching, his gaze delighted by the flash of that red meteor against the white. The snow itself seemed to throw sound back at him—the quick tap of her boots, the dog’s excited bark, the faint creak of branches—so that her laughter, when it came, landed on his nerves like warmth. Then he started after her, and they met, panting and laughing, at a wicket that led into the park.

She looked up at him. “I knew you’d come!”

“That shows you wanted me to,” he said, and took an almost shameful joy in their foolishness.

The white glitter of the trees filled the air with its own strange brightness, and as they walked on the ground seemed to sing under their feet—snow protesting softly, a clean, crisp sound that made their steps feel like the only human thing in an immense quiet.

“Where did you come from?” she asked.

He told her, and added: “It was because I got your note.”

After a pause she said, with a chill barely perceptible: “May asked you to take care of me.”

“I didn’t need any asking.”

“You mean—I’m so evidently helpless and defenceless? What a poor thing you must all think me! But women here seem not—seem never to feel the need: any more than the blessed in heaven.”

He lowered his voice. “What sort of a need?”

“Ah, don’t ask me! I don’t speak your language,” she retorted, petulant as a child who has been corrected.

The sentence struck him with the bluntness of a blow. He stopped in the path and looked down at her, and the cold air seemed suddenly to have no fragrance, only a thin, metallic taste.

“What did I come for, if I don’t speak yours?”

“Oh, my friend—!” She laid her hand lightly on his arm, and it was as if she were trying to soften the very words she had thrown.

He pleaded, the need in him louder than any discretion: “Ellen—why won’t you tell me what’s happened?”

She shrugged again. “Does anything ever happen in heaven?”

They walked on a few yards without speaking. Then, as if the house itself had answered her with its perpetual, muffled vigilance, she broke out:

“I will tell you—but where, where, where? One can’t be alone for a minute in that great seminary of a house, with all the doors wide open, and always a servant bringing tea, or a log for the fire, or the newspaper! Is there nowhere in an American house where one may be by one’s self? You’re so shy, and yet you’re so public. I always feel as if I were in the convent again—or on the stage, before a dreadfully polite audience that never applauds.”

“Ah, you don’t like us!” Archer exclaimed.

They were passing the old Patroon’s house—squat walls, small square windows grouped around a central chimney. The shutters stood wide, and through one of the newly washed panes Archer caught the glow of a fire. That little place seemed to breathe differently from the big villa: the crackle of burning wood, the quiet that held it, the sense that the sound belonged to the room and not to an audience.

“Why—the house is open!” he said.

She stopped. “No; only for today, at least. I wanted to see it, and Mr. van der Luyden had the fire lit and the windows opened, so that we might stop there on the way back from church this morning.” She ran up the steps and tried the door. “It’s still unlocked—what luck! Come in and we can have a quiet talk. Mrs. van der Luyden has driven over to see her old aunts at Rhinebeck and we shan’t be missed at the house for another hour.”

He followed her into the narrow passage.

His spirits, which had dropped at her words about “your language,” rose with an irrational leap. The homely little house stood there, its panels and brasses shining in firelight as if it had been waiting through centuries to shelter just such an hour. A bed of embers still glowed in the kitchen chimney, under an iron pot hung from an ancient crane. The faint smell of woodsmoke and iron and old stone—something like the taste of rye bread baked close to a hearth—wrapped itself around him with an intimacy the great villa could never permit. Rush-bottomed chairs faced each other across the tiled hearth; rows of Delft plates stood on shelves against the walls, blue designs repeating themselves like a family story told in the same words from generation to generation.

Archer stooped, chose a log, and set it on the embers. The fire answered with a little crack, a soft settling sigh, and then the quicker, brighter speech of flame. Madame Olenska dropped her cloak and sat down. He leaned against the chimney and looked at her.

“You’re laughing now; but when you wrote me you were unhappy.”

“Yes.” She paused. “But I can’t feel unhappy when you’re here.”

“I shan’t be here long,” he said, his mouth stiff with the effort to offer no more than the fact.

“No; I know. But I’m improvident: I live in the moment when I’m happy.”

The words moved through him like temptation given a voice. He stepped away from the hearth and stood at the window, gazing out at black tree-boles scored against the snow. But it was as if she had shifted with him; between him and the trees he still saw her, bent toward the fire, smiling in that indolent way that seemed to hold a whole history he could not translate.

His heart beat out of step with his reason. What if she had been running from him—what if she had made him come for that? What if she had waited until they were alone in this secret, modest room, with its honest fire and its quiet?

“Ellen, if I’m really a help to you—if you really wanted me to come—tell me what’s wrong, tell me what it is you’re running away from,” he insisted.

He spoke without turning. If the thing was to happen—whatever it was—let it happen with the width of the room between them, his eyes fixed on the snow, as if he could keep himself from it by refusing to look.

For a long moment she was silent. In that moment he imagined—almost heard—her stealing up behind him, those light arms coming around his neck, warmth against the cold glass of his restraint.

While he waited, soul and body throbbing, his eyes mechanically took in the figure of a heavily coated man advancing along the path, fur collar turned up. The man was Julius Beaufort.

“Ah—!” Archer cried, bursting into a laugh that hurt him as it came out.

Madame Olenska had sprung up and moved to his side, slipping her hand into his; but after a glance through the window her face went pale and she shrank back, as if the air itself had changed key.

“So that was it?” Archer said, with a derision that was meant for his own folly as much as for anything else.

“I didn’t know he was here,” she murmured.

Her hand still clung to his; but he drew away, went out into the passage, and flung open the door.

“Hallo, Beaufort—this way! Madame Olenska was expecting you,” he called, and his voice sounded too loud in the little house, as if the old walls resented being made a stage.

On the way back to New York next morning Archer relived, with exhausting vividness, his last moments at Skuytercliff.

Beaufort, clearly annoyed at finding him there, had carried off the situation as he always did—high-handedly, by behaving as though whatever inconvenienced him scarcely existed. His talent for making the inconvenient feel invisible had a brutal efficiency, and Archer, sensitive enough to feel it, suffered from that strange sense of nonexistence. Yet the humiliation gave him the ghostly advantage of watching without being watched.

Beaufort had entered the little house with easy assurance, but he could not smile away the vertical line between his eyes. It was clear Madame Olenska had not known he was coming, though her talk to Archer had hinted at some looming pursuit; at any rate she had not told Beaufort where she was going when she left New York, and her unexplained departure had exasperated him. The ostensible reason of his appearance was the discovery, the night before, of a “perfect little house”—not in the market—which was exactly the thing for her and would be snapped up instantly if she didn’t take it. He was loud in mock reproaches for the dance she had led him, running away just as he’d found it.

“If only this new dodge for talking along a wire had been a little nearer perfection I might have told you all this from town,” he grumbled, “and been toasting my toes before the club fire at this minute instead of tramping after you through the snow.”

Madame Olenska, taking the opening as if it were a rope thrown across a chasm, twisted the talk away to the fantastic possibility that people might one day converse from street to street—or, incredible dream, from one town to another. The notion drew from all three the inevitable allusions to Edgar Poe and Jules Verne, and the platitudes that rise when one is pressed for time and faced with an invention one is afraid of seeming too naïve to believe. So the telephone—this new instrument that would carry voices while leaving bodies behind—brought them safely back to the big house, where sound was always managed and supervised, and nothing human was ever allowed to ring too nakedly.

Mrs. van der Luyden had not yet returned. Archer took his leave and walked off to fetch the cutter, while Beaufort followed the Countess indoors. Little as the van der Luydens encouraged unannounced visits, Beaufort could count on being asked to dine and then sent back to the station to catch the nine o’clock train; but more than that he would not get. It would never occur to his hosts that a gentleman travelling without luggage might wish to spend the night; and it would be distasteful to them to propose such a thing to a person for whom their cordiality was so carefully limited. Beaufort knew all this, must have foreseen it. His willingness to travel so far for so small a reward measured his impatience.

He was undeniably in pursuit of Madame Olenska; and Beaufort had only one object in view when he pursued pretty women.

His dull, childless home had long ago palled on him; besides more permanent consolations he was always in quest of amorous adventure in his own set. This was the man from whom Ellen was, by her own admission, flying. The question was: had she fled because his importunities disgusted her, or because she did not wholly trust herself to resist them? Or—most disturbing possibility—had her talk of flight been only a blind, her departure a manoeuvre?

Archer did not really believe that. Little as he had actually seen of her, he was beginning to think he could read her face, and if not her face, her voice. Both had betrayed annoyance, even dismay, at Beaufort’s sudden appearance.

And yet—if she truly feared him—was that not worse than if she had gone to meet him? If she had chosen him knowingly, she would simply have “classed” herself, irretrievably, among the vulgarest of dissemblers; she would cease to be mysterious, and therefore to be—he hated himself for the word—interesting. But it was worse a thousand times if, judging Beaufort and likely despising him, she was still drawn by what made him different from the other men around her: his habit of two continents and two societies, his easy familiarity with artists and actors and people in the world’s eye, his careless contempt for local prejudices.

Beaufort was vulgar, uneducated, purse-proud; yet circumstances and a certain native shrewdness made him, in talk, more various than many men morally and socially his betters, whose horizon was bounded by the Battery and Central Park. How could any one who had known a wider world not feel the difference, and feel, in spite of herself, its pull? Ellen had flung at Archer, in irritation, that they did not speak the same language; and he knew that in some respects it was true. But Beaufort understood every turn of her dialect and spoke it fluently: his view of life, his tone, his posture toward experience, were a coarser reflection of what Count Olenski’s letter had revealed. It might seem a disadvantage with Olenski’s wife; but Archer was too intelligent to imagine that a young woman would recoil from everything that reminded her of her past. She might revolt against it; yet what had once charmed her could still charm her, even against her will—like a taste remembered, impossible to explain to someone who had never eaten the dish, impossible to forget once it had melted on the tongue.

Thus, with painful impartiality, he made out the case for Beaufort, and for Beaufort’s possible victim. He longed to enlighten her; there were moments when he imagined all she asked was to be enlightened. Yet what did “enlighten” mean, when she had said he did not speak her language? Perhaps the very words he would use were part of the trap: the accepted terms, the dominant lexicon of his world, the phrases that could classify and condemn without ever understanding.

That evening he unpacked his books from London. The box was full of things he had waited for impatiently: a new volume of Herbert Spencer, another collection of Alphonse Daudet’s brilliant tales, and a novel called Middlemarch, of which the reviews had lately been saying interesting things. He had declined three dinner invitations in favour of this feast, but though he turned the pages with the sensuous joy of a book-lover, he did not know what he was reading. One book after another slipped from his hand. Even the smell of paper and cloth and fresh-cut pages—usually as bracing as a draught of wine—could not bring his mind to heel.

Suddenly, among them, he lit on a small volume of verse he had ordered because the name had attracted him: The House of Life.

He took it up and found himself plunged into an atmosphere unlike any he had breathed in books: warm, rich, and yet ineffably tender, giving a new, haunting beauty to the most elementary human passions. All night he pursued through those enchanted pages the vision of a woman with Ellen Olenska’s face—her quick laughter, her sudden chill, the untranslatable accent of her pain.

But in the morning, when he woke and looked out at the brownstone houses across the street, and thought of his desk in Mr. Letterblair’s office and the family pew in Grace Church, his hour in the park at Skuytercliff became as far outside probability as the visions of the night. The city’s daily roar—the wheels, the shouted calls, the muffled thunder of ceaseless commerce—closed around him like a lid, flattening mystery into routine.

“Mercy, how pale you look, Newland!” Janey commented over the coffee-cups at breakfast.

His mother added: “Newland, dear, I’ve noticed lately that you’ve been coughing; I do hope you’re not letting yourself be overworked?”

For both ladies were convinced that, under the iron despotism of his senior partners, his life was spent in exhausting professional labours—and he had never thought it necessary to undeceive them.

The next two or three days dragged heavily. The taste of the usual was like cinders in his mouth; at moments he felt as if he were being buried alive under his future. He heard nothing of the Countess Olenska, nothing of the perfect little house. When he met Beaufort at the club they merely nodded across the whist-tables, as if what had happened in that small stone house belonged to a language neither of them would use aloud.

It was not till the fourth evening that he found a note awaiting him on his return home.

“Come late tomorrow: I must explain to you. Ellen.”

Those were the only words.

He was dining out. He thrust the note into his pocket, smiling a little at the Frenchness of the “to you,” at the faint foreign tilt of it—like a spice one could taste without naming. After dinner he went to a play; it was not until he returned home after midnight that he drew the missive out again and reread it slowly, a number of times, as if repetition could make its meaning safer.

There were several ways of answering it. He weighed each one through the watches of an agitated night. And when morning came, the decision he finally made was to pitch some clothes into a portmanteau and jump aboard a boat leaving that very afternoon for St. Augustine.

When Archer went down the sandy main street of St. Augustine toward the house the townspeople had pointed out as Mr. Welland’s, the morning seemed to rinse the mind as cleanly as river-water poured from a clay jar. The street had the pale, sifted look of flour shaken through a sieve; the heat already sat on it, and yet the air was full of living, layered sound—the faint scrape of palmetto fronds, the creak of a distant oarlock, a mule’s slow hoof in sand, the soft argument of sparrows in the eaves. It was a quieter world than New York’s iron din, and in that quiet he felt his own pulse become audible, like a kettle just beginning to murmur.

May Welland stood under a magnolia with the sun caught in her hair as if it were a pale net thrown over her head. At the sight of her he wondered, with a stab of self-reproach, why he had delayed so long. Here was what he had been trained to call truth—here was reality, the appointed life; and he, who liked to think himself contemptuous of arbitrary restraints, had been afraid to break away from his desk for fear of what people would say about his stealing a holiday. The thought seemed absurd in this place where time moved as slowly as sap.

Her first words were not the intuitive, romantic ones he had half imagined in the long, hot hours of his journey.

“Newland—has anything happened?”

It came to him, with the faintest chill, that it would have been more “feminine” if she had read his reason in his eyes. But when he answered, “Yes—I found I had to see you,” her surprise melted into a happy blush that warmed the air between them. He saw, with a rush of relief that tasted almost like sweetness on the tongue, how easily he would be forgiven; how quickly Mr. Letterblair’s mild disapproval would be smiled away by a tolerant family as one pours cream into too-strong coffee until it becomes harmless.

The main street, even this early, offered no shelter for tenderness. A wagon groaned past; someone called to someone else in a drawling, half-sung dialect he could not place and could not have repeated if asked; the syllables slid away like fish in shallow water. Archer longed to be alone with May, to pour out what pressed behind his ribs—impatience, hunger, a kind of desperate gratitude.

It still lacked an hour to the late Welland breakfast-time, and instead of drawing him into the house she proposed, with the brisk innocence that was her own form of decision, that they walk out to an old orange-garden beyond the town. She had just been rowing on the river; the sun that netted the small waves with gold seemed to have caught her too. Across the warm brown of her cheek her blown hair glittered like silver wire, and her eyes looked lighter—almost pale—in their youthful limpidity.

They walked side by side, her long, swinging gait as sure as a young creature’s, her face wearing the vacant serenity of a marble athlete. To Archer’s strained nerves she was like cool water set beside a flame: soothing, steadying, almost anesthetic.

In the orange-garden the trees stood with that patient domestic abundance that belongs to old orchards. The leaves were thick and glossy; the air carried a clean, oily bitterness where a twig had been snapped, and beneath it the faint ferment of fallen fruit warming in the grass. Bees worked the blossoms with a low, monkish hum; somewhere a bird kept striking the same note as if testing the day’s acoustics.

They sat on a bench under the trees. Archer slipped an arm about her and kissed her. The sensation was like drinking at a cold spring with the sun on it—shockingly pure, almost too simple for what he felt. But his pressure may have been more vehement than he intended, for the blood rose to her face and she drew back as if he had startled her.

“What is it?” he asked, smiling.

She looked at him with surprise. “Nothing.”

A slight embarrassment fell between them, like a thin cloth dropped over a dish still steaming; her hand slid from his. It was only the second time he had kissed her on the lips—the first had been that fugitive embrace in the Beaufort conservatory—and he saw that he had shaken her out of her cool, boyish composure.

“Tell me what you do all day,” he said, leaning back, crossing his arms under his head, pushing his hat forward to shield his eyes from the sun-dazzle.

To have her speak of familiar, simple things was the easiest way to keep his own thoughts moving in their separate current. Her days unrolled with the innocent rhythm of a holiday: swimming, sailing, riding; the occasional dance at the primitive inn when a man-of-war came in and made the place briefly aware of the wider world. A few pleasant people from Philadelphia and Baltimore were picknicking at the inn, and the Selfridge Merrys had come down for three weeks because Kate Merry had had bronchitis.

They were planning to lay out a lawn-tennis court on the sands; but no one except Kate and May had racquets, and most of the people had not even heard of the game. It all kept May “very busy,” as she put it, with the earnestness of youth making work out of play. She had barely had time to do more than look at the little vellum book Archer had sent her the week before—Sonnets from the Portuguese—yet she was learning by heart “How they brought the Good News from Ghent to Aix,” because it was one of the first things he had ever read to her. It amused her to be able to tell him that Kate Merry had never even heard of a poet called Robert Browning.

As she spoke, Archer noticed the way the orchard sounded: not the metallic, trampling noise of the city, but a woven fabric of small noises, each with room around it. He thought, absurdly, that a person’s soul could breathe in such a place. In New York, sound came like pressure; here it came like permission. And yet the very gentleness of it made him more aware of his own hidden unrest—like a sauce tasted in a clean spoon that suddenly reveals too much salt.

Presently May started up, exclaiming that they would be late for breakfast. They hurried back to the tumble-down house with its pointless porch and unpruned hedge of plumbago and pink geraniums, where the Wellands were installed for the winter.

Mr. Welland’s sensitive domesticity shrank from the discomforts of the slovenly southern hotel, and at immense expense—and against almost insuperable difficulties—Mrs. Welland was obliged, year after year, to improvise an establishment partly made up of discontented New York servants and partly drawn from the local African supply. She explained, as she always did, that the doctors wanted her husband to feel he was “in his own home,” otherwise he would be so wretched the climate would do him no good. And in truth there was something touching and faintly comic in the way this “camping”—as Mr. Welland called it—arrived at the Welland table.

Breakfast, miraculously, was abundant. The table smelled of coffee and hot starch and fruit. Straw-coloured griddle-cakes lay in a mound like small sunlit sponges, and golden syrup poured over them in slow ribbons. There were oranges, too—actual Florida oranges—cut and ready, their skins thick and aromatic, their sections shining as if varnished.

Mr. Welland beamed across the spread at Archer. “You see, my dear fellow, we camp—we literally camp. I tell my wife and May that I want to teach them how to rough it.”

Mr. and Mrs. Welland had been as surprised as their daughter by Archer’s sudden arrival; but Archer had thought to explain that he had felt himself on the verge of a nasty cold, and to Mr. Welland this was reason enough for abandoning any duty.

“You can’t be too careful, especially toward spring,” Mr. Welland said, heaping his plate with griddle-cakes and drowning them in syrup as if sweetness were a remedy. “If I’d only been as prudent at your age May would have been dancing at the Assemblies now, instead of spending her winters in a wilderness with an old invalid.”

“Oh, but I love it here, Papa; you know I do. If only Newland could stay I should like it a thousand times better than New York.”

“Newland must stay till he has quite thrown off his cold,” said Mrs. Welland indulgently, with that air of practical benevolence that smoothed discomfort as she smoothed tablecloths.

Archer laughed and said he supposed there was such a thing as one’s profession. But after an exchange of telegrams with the firm, he managed to make his cold last a week. It shed an ironic light on his situation to know that Mr. Letterblair’s indulgence was partly due to the satisfactory way his brilliant young junior partner had settled the troublesome matter of the Olenski divorce. Mr. Letterblair had let Mrs. Welland know that Mr. Archer had “rendered an invaluable service” to the whole family, and that old Mrs. Manson Mingott had been particularly pleased.

The days slid by with a soft repetitiveness: river air in the morning, the faintly medicinal smell of sun-warmed magnolia leaves at noon, a kind of quietness at night so complete that a single voice outdoors seemed an intrusion. Archer found himself listening, against his will, for the New York clatter in his own head—the ceaseless naming and judging that made the city feel like a machine that ran on words.

One afternoon, when May had gone driving with her father in the only vehicle the place produced, Mrs. Welland chose to touch on a subject she always avoided in her daughter’s presence. She spoke as she might have spoken of a stain that had been removed just in time from a table-linen—a thing unpleasant because it existed at all.

“I’m afraid Ellen’s ideas are not at all like ours. She was barely eighteen when Medora Manson took her back to Europe—you remember the excitement when she appeared in black at her coming-out ball? Another of Medora’s fads—really this time it was almost prophetic! That must have been at least twelve years ago; and since then Ellen has never been to America. No wonder she is completely Europeanised.”

“But European society is not given to divorce,” Archer said; and at the sound of the name—Countess Olenska—his face grew warm in spite of himself, as if some forbidden spice had been shaken into a dish meant to be bland. “Countess Olenska thought she would be conforming to American ideas in asking for her freedom.”

It was the first time he had pronounced her name since he had left Skuytercliff, and the syllables felt heavy and intimate in the quiet St. Augustine air.

Mrs. Welland smiled compassionately, as at a child who has been misled by a story. “That is just like the extraordinary things that foreigners invent about us. They think we dine at two o’clock and countenance divorce! That is why it seems to me so foolish to entertain them when they come to New York. They accept our hospitality, and then they go home and repeat the same stupid stories.”

She went on, and Archer, hearing her, felt again that peculiar oppression the family’s vocabulary produced—the way certain words were allowed and others banished, as if the mere naming of a thing could poison the household. There were realities one could taste but not speak of, as if the language itself had been trimmed and pruned until it could no longer hold the full flavor of life.

“But we do most thoroughly appreciate your persuading Ellen to give up the idea,” Mrs. Welland continued. “Her grandmother and her uncle Lovell could do nothing with her; both of them have written that her changing her mind was entirely due to your influence—in fact she said so to her grandmother. She has an unbounded admiration for you. Poor Ellen—she was always a wayward child. I wonder what her fate will be?”

What we’ve all contrived to make it, Archer thought. If you’d all rather she should be Beaufort’s mistress than some decent fellow’s wife you’ve certainly gone the right way about it. He did not say the words. He pictured, with a swift clarity, the sudden discomposure of Mrs. Welland’s firm placid features if he had. A lifelong mastery over trifles had given her an air of factitious authority; yet traces lingered on her face of the fresh beauty that May now had. He asked himself if May’s face was doomed to thicken into that same middle-aged image of invincible innocence.

He did not want May to have that kind of innocence—the innocence that seals the mind against imagination and the heart against experience. He wanted… he did not know what he wanted, except that the life he was being served, course by course, had the look of perfection and the taste of something missing.

“I verily believe,” Mrs. Welland went on, “that if the horrible business had come out in the newspapers it would have been my husband’s death-blow. I don’t know any of the details; I only ask not to, as I told poor Ellen when she tried to talk to me about it. Having an invalid to care for, I have to keep my mind bright and happy. But Mr. Welland was terribly upset; he had a slight temperature every morning while we were waiting to hear what had been decided. It was the horror of his girl’s learning that such things were possible—but of course, dear Newland, you felt that too. We all knew that you were thinking of May.”

“I’m always thinking of May,” Archer said, rising to cut the conversation short.

He had meant to seize this private moment to urge Mrs. Welland to advance the date of his marriage. But he could think of no argument that would move her—there was no leverage in that smooth, guarded mind—and with a sense of relief he saw Mr. Welland and May driving up to the door.

His only hope was to plead with May. On the day before his departure he walked with her to the ruinous garden of the Spanish Mission. The broken walls held heat; the stones smelled of sun and age, and in the fissures little herbs had taken root, releasing a faint sharpness underfoot when they were brushed. The place had its own acoustic—the hush of thick masonry, the small echo of a footfall, a distant bell that did not ring so much as hover in the air. It made him think of Europe, of old ceremonies and older languages, of words that carried histories inside them.

May, under a wide-brimmed hat that cast a shadow of mystery over her too-clear eyes, looked her loveliest; the hat made her seem less like a figure cut in bright daylight and more like someone with secrets. The Mission background lent itself to allusions to European scenes, and she kindled into eagerness as he spoke of Granada and the Alhambra.

“We might be seeing it all this spring—even the Easter ceremonies at Seville,” he urged, exaggerating his demands in the hope of winning a larger concession.

“Easter in Seville?” she laughed. “And it will be Lent next week!”

“Why shouldn’t we be married in Lent?” he rejoined, too quickly.

She looked so shocked that he saw his mistake at once, as one sees the instant a sauce has broken.

“Of course I didn’t mean that, dearest; but soon after Easter—so that we could sail at the end of April. I know I could arrange it at the office.”

She smiled dreamily at the possibility; but he perceived, with a disappointment that sank slowly, that to dream of it sufficed her. It was like hearing him read aloud from his poetry books: the beautiful things that could not possibly happen in real life.

“Oh, do go on, Newland; I do love your descriptions.”

“But why should they be only descriptions? Why shouldn’t we make them real?”

“We shall, dearest—of course; next year.” Her voice lingered on the words as if they were a promise and a lullaby together.

“Don’t you want them to be real sooner? Can’t I persuade you to break away now?” He tightened his arm about her waist. “Why should we dream away another year? Look at me, dear! Don’t you understand how I want you for my wife?”

For a moment she remained motionless; then she raised her eyes to him—eyes of such despairing clearness that he half released her, startled, as though he had felt the edge of something sharp.

But suddenly her look changed and deepened into something he could not read.

“I’m not sure if I do understand,” she said. “Is it—is it because you’re not certain of continuing to care for me?”

He sprang up as if struck. “My God—perhaps—I don’t know,” he broke out angrily, the words escaping him like steam from a pot whose lid has been forced.

May rose too. As they faced each other she seemed to grow in womanly stature and dignity; the girl who had listened to his descriptions now stood with an uprightness that surprised him. They were silent for a moment, dismayed by the unforeseen turn of their words.

Then she said in a low voice: “If that is it—is there some one else?”

“Some one else—between you and me?” He repeated the words slowly, as though they were only half intelligible and he needed to hear them again to understand what they meant.

She seemed to catch the uncertainty in his voice and went on, her tone deepening: “Let us talk frankly, Newland. Sometimes I’ve felt a difference in you; especially since our engagement has been announced.”

“Dear—what madness!” he recovered himself to exclaim.

She met his protest with a faint smile. “If it is, it won’t hurt us to talk about it.” Then, lifting her head with one of her noble movements, she added: “Or even if it’s true: why shouldn’t we speak of it? You might so easily have made a mistake.”

He lowered his head, staring at the black leaf-pattern the sun made on the path at their feet. “Mistakes are always easy to make; but if I had made one of the kind you suggest, is it likely that I should be imploring you to hasten our marriage?”

She looked down too, disturbing the pattern with the point of her sunshade while she struggled for expression. “Yes,” she said at length. “You might want—once for all—to settle the question: it’s one way.”

Her quiet lucidity startled him. Under her hat-brim he saw the pallor of her profile and the slight tremor of the nostril above her resolutely steadied lips. He could not think her insensible; the very steadiness looked like effort.

“Well—?” he asked, sitting down again and looking up at her with a frown he tried to make playful.

She dropped back to her seat. “You mustn’t think that a girl knows as little as her parents imagine. One hears and one notices—one has one’s feelings and ideas. And of course, long before you told me that you cared for me, I’d known that there was some one else you were interested in; every one was talking about it two years ago at Newport. And once I saw you sitting together on the verandah at a dance—and when she came back into the house her face was sad, and I felt sorry for her; I remembered it afterward, when we were engaged.”

Her voice sank almost to a whisper. Her hands clasped and unclasped around the handle of the sunshade as though it were an anchor. Archer laid his hand over hers with a gentle pressure. In that instant his heart dilated with an inexpressible relief—so strong it was almost shameful. All this terror of hers had fastened, not on Ellen, not on the present fever of his life, but on an old, remote episode: his love-affair with Mrs. Thorley Rushworth.

“My dear child—was that it?” he said. “If you only knew the truth!”

She raised her head quickly. “Then there is a truth I don’t know?”

“I meant—the truth about the old story you speak of.” He kept his hand on hers, as if holding her there might hold the conversation in safety too.

“But that’s what I want to know, Newland—what I ought to know. I couldn’t have my happiness made out of a wrong—an unfairness—to somebody else. And I want to believe that it would be the same with you. What sort of a life could we build on such foundations?”

Her face had taken on a look of such tragic courage that he felt like bowing at her feet. There was a grandeur in it that seemed to come from some deeper spring than the Welland drawing-room.

“I’ve wanted to say this for a long time,” she went on. “I’ve wanted to tell you that, when two people really love each other, I understand that there may be situations which make it right that they should—should go against public opinion. And if you feel yourself in any way pledged… pledged to the person we’ve spoken of… and if there is any way… any way in which you can fulfill your pledge… even by her getting a divorce… Newland, don’t give her up because of me!”

His astonishment at her generosity—her reckless, almost unorthodox magnanimity—made him giddy. If other problems had not pressed on him he would have been lost in wonder at the prodigy of the Wellands’ daughter urging him to marry his former mistress. For a moment he could not speak. The whole scene seemed to quiver with the untranslatable: how could one put into the sanctioned language of their world the actual flavors of the heart—the acrid, the sweet, the burning, the aftertaste that lingered?

At last he said: “There is no pledge—no obligation whatever—of the kind you think. Such cases don’t always… present themselves quite as simply as…” He broke off, then went on, trying to steady his voice with a smile. “But that’s no matter. I love your generosity, because I feel as you do about those things. I feel that each case must be judged individually, on its own merits—irrespective of stupid conventionalities. I mean—each woman’s right to her liberty—”

He pulled himself up, startled by where his mind had leapt, as if a forbidden word had risen to his tongue and made itself heard. Quickly he turned back to what he had come here to win.

“Since you understand so many things, dearest,” he continued, “can’t you go a little farther, and understand the uselessness of our submitting to another form of the same foolish conventionalities? If there’s no one and nothing between us, isn’t that an argument for marrying quickly, rather than for more delay?”

She flushed with joy and lifted her face to his; as he bent to kiss her he saw her eyes brim with happy tears. But almost at once she seemed to slip down from that momentary height of womanly courage to helpless, timorous girlhood, as if she had stepped too far out over deep water and hastened back to the shore. Archer understood then that her initiative was all for others, and that she had none for herself.

The effort of speaking had cost her more than her studied composure had shown; at his first reassurance she fell back into the usual safety, as a too-adventurous child takes refuge in its mother’s arms. Archer had no heart to go on pleading. He was too much disappointed at the vanishing of the new being who had looked at him for one instant with such clear, fearless eyes.

May seemed aware of his disappointment without knowing how to remedy it; and they stood up and walked home in silence, through the Mission garden where the stones held their heat and the air held its hush, and the small sounds of the place—footsteps, a bird’s call, the soft brush of her skirt—fell one by one, like words in a dialect that could name nothing that mattered most.

“Your cousin the Countess called on mother while you were away, Janey,” Janey Archer announced as soon as her brother had taken his seat at the small evening table.

Newland had expected, after his hurried flight back from Florida, the soft safety of familiar rooms: the measured scrape of cutlery, the reliable hush of old carpets, the mild crackle of a coal fire. Even the house’s sounds seemed trained to good behavior. Yet at Janey’s words he looked up sharply, and the knife in his hand paused above the roast as if it had forgotten its office.

Across from him Mrs. Archer’s eyes remained lowered to her plate. She did not approve of astonishment in her dining-room; surprise was a kind of noise.

“She had on a black velvet polonaise with jet buttons,” Janey went on, quickened by the importance of being first with a novelty, “and a tiny green monkey muff; I never saw her so stylishly dressed. She came alone, early on Sunday afternoon—luckily the fire was lit in the drawing-room. She had one of those new card-cases. She said she wanted to know us because you’d been so good to her.”

Newland laughed, and the laugh sounded too loud against the thick damask quiet. “Madame Olenska always takes that tone about her friends. She’s very happy at being among her own people again.”

“Yes, so she told us,” said Mrs. Archer. There was a faint emphasis on “told,” as if gratitude, like gravy, could be poured too freely. “I must say she seems thankful to be here.”

“I hope you liked her, mother.”

Mrs. Archer drew her lips together. “She certainly lays herself out to please, even when she is calling on an old lady.”

“Mother doesn’t think her simple,” Janey interposed, her eyes screwed upon her brother’s face with the exacting curiosity of one who has never missed a syllable at table.

“It’s just my old-fashioned feeling; dear May is my ideal,” Mrs. Archer said, cutting her meat with the conscientious neatness that was her manner of opinion.

“Ah,” Newland said, with the helpless honesty of a man who could still taste Florida oranges on his tongue, “they’re not alike.”

He had come back from St. Augustine with messages for old Mrs. Mingott, and within a day or two he went up to her house. The city’s winter streets met him with their iron-clad clatter—hooves, wheels, shouted names, the thin grind of sleigh-runners where the snow had not yet turned to soot. Yet Catherine Mingott’s drawing-room absorbed noise the way a great kitchen absorbs steam: thick curtains, heavy upholstery, and the soft, continuous rustle of servants moving as silently as well-trained hands at a sideboard.

The old lady received him with unusual warmth. She was grateful—practically jubilant—that he had persuaded Ellen Olenska to give up the idea of divorce; and when he confessed, with a shame that still carried a private sweetness, that he had deserted the office without leave and rushed down to St. Augustine simply because he wanted to see May, she gave an adipose chuckle and patted his knee with her puff-ball hand.

“Ah, ah—so you kicked over the traces, did you? And I suppose Augusta and Welland pulled long faces, and behaved as if the end of the world had come? But little May—she knew better, I’ll be bound?”

“I hoped she did; but after all she wouldn’t agree to what I’d gone down to ask for.”

“Wouldn’t she indeed? And what was that?”

“I wanted to get her to promise that we should be married in April. What’s the use of our wasting another year?”

Mrs. Manson Mingott screwed up her little mouth into a grimace of mimic prudery and twinkled at him through malicious lids. “‘Ask Mamma,’ I suppose—the usual story. Ah, these Mingotts—all alike! Born in a rut, and you can’t root ’em out of it. When I built this house you’d have thought I was moving to California! Nobody ever had built above Fortieth Street—no, says I, nor above the Battery either, before Christopher Columbus discovered America. No, no; not one of them wants to be different; they’re as scared of it as the small-pox. Ah, my dear Mr. Archer, I thank my stars I’m nothing but a vulgar Spicer; but there’s not one of my own children that takes after me but my little Ellen.”

She broke off, still twinkling, and asked with the casual irrelevance of old age, as if tasting a thought and finding it oddly pleasant: “Now, why in the world didn’t you marry my little Ellen?”

Newland laughed. “For one thing, she wasn’t there to be married.”

“No—to be sure; more’s the pity. And now it’s too late; her life is finished.”

She spoke with the cold-blooded complacency of the aged throwing earth into the grave of young hopes. A chill passed through him, sudden as stepping from sun into shade, and he hurried on: “Can’t I persuade you to use your influence with the Wellands, Mrs. Mingott? I wasn’t made for long engagements.”

Old Catherine beamed on him approvingly. “No; I can see that. You’ve got a quick eye. When you were a little boy I’ve no doubt you liked to be helped first.”

She threw back her head with a laugh that made her chins ripple like little waves. “Ah, here’s my Ellen now!” she exclaimed, as the portières parted behind her.

Madame Olenska came forward with a smile. Her face looked vivid and happy, as if some inner lamp had been trimmed; she held out her hand gaily to Archer while she stooped to her grandmother’s kiss.

“I was just saying to him, my dear: ‘Now, why didn’t you marry my little Ellen?’”

Madame Olenska looked at Archer, still smiling. “And what did he answer?”

“Oh, my darling, I leave you to find that out! He’s been down to Florida to see his sweetheart.”

“Yes, I know.” She still looked at him, steadily, as if listening for the undertone beneath his words. “I went to see your mother, to ask where you’d gone. I sent a note that you never answered, and I was afraid you were ill.”

He muttered something about leaving unexpectedly, in a great hurry, and having intended to write to her from St. Augustine.

“And of course once you were there you never thought of me again!” She beamed on him with a gaiety so bright it seemed almost a shield—a deliberate brightness, like a dining-room lit too early to hide a foggy afternoon.

If she still needs me, she’s determined not to let me see it, he thought, stung by her manner. He wanted to thank her for going to his mother, for crossing that threshold and facing that gentle scrutiny on his behalf; but under the ancestress’s malicious eye his gratitude stuck in his throat like dry bread.

“Look at him—in such hot haste to get married that he took French leave and rushed down to implore the silly girl on his knees!” cried the old lady. “That’s something like a lover—that’s the way handsome Bob Spicer carried off my poor mother; and then got tired of her before I was weaned—though they only had to wait eight months for me! But there—you’re not a Spicer, young man; luckily for you and for May. It’s only my poor Ellen that has kept any of their wicked blood; the rest of them are all model Mingotts.”

Archer was aware that Madame Olenska, who had seated herself at her grandmother’s side, was still thoughtfully scrutinising him. The gaiety had faded from her eyes; her voice, when she spoke, had the gentleness of one laying a cloth smoothly over a troubled surface.

“Surely, Granny, we can persuade them between us to do as he wishes.”

Newland rose to go, and as his hand met hers he felt—distinctly—that she was waiting for him to speak of her letter, to give it its due place between them. The silence was another kind of power, and he hated that he obeyed it.

“When can I see you?” he asked, as she walked with him to the door of the room.

“Whenever you like; but it must be soon if you want to see the little house again. I am moving next week.”

A pang shot through him at the memory of those lamplit hours in the low-studded drawing-room, where even the air had seemed different—smelling faintly of tea and old books and whatever foreign, unnamed thing she carried with her. Few as they had been, they were thick with memories.

“Tomorrow evening?”

She nodded. “Tomorrow; yes; but early. I’m going out.”

The next day was Sunday, and if she were going out on a Sunday evening it could only be to Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s. The idea pricked him—less because she went where she pleased (he liked that stubborn liberty in her, in spite of the van der Luydens), than because it was the kind of house where Beaufort would be, and she must have known it—might even have counted on it.

“Very well; tomorrow evening,” he repeated, inwardly resolved he would not go early. He would reach her door late enough either to keep her at home or to miss her altogether; and either course, he told himself, would be simpler.

Yet when the hour came he could not bear to wait. A singular restlessness drove him through the streets—the city’s Sunday hush broken by the occasional rattle of a carriage and the distant, thin tolling of a church bell that seemed to measure not devotion but constraint. It was only half-past eight when he rang the bell under her wisteria—not as late as he had intended by half an hour.

He reflected, as he stood in the narrow hall, that Mrs. Struthers’s Sunday evenings were not like a ball; people went early, as if punctuality could absolve them. But he had not counted on finding hats and overcoats there.

Why had she bidden him to come early if she was having people to dine?

As Nastasia took his coat, his resentment gave way to curiosity. The garments laid on the chair were the very strangest he had ever seen under a polite roof; and a glance assured him neither belonged to Julius Beaufort. One was a shaggy yellow ulster of “reach-me-down” cut; the other a very old, rusty cloak with a cape—something like what the French called a Macfarlane. It seemed made for a person of prodigious size, and its greenish-black folds gave out a moist, sawdusty smell suggestive of long sessions against bar-room walls. On it lay a ragged grey scarf and an odd felt hat of semiclerical shape.

Archer lifted his brows at Nastasia. She lifted hers in return with a fatalistic “Gia!” and flung open the drawing-room door.

His hostess was not there.

Instead he saw another lady standing by the fire, and beside her, in a cloud of cigar-smoke, two men in morning clothes they had evidently not taken off since morning. The room itself had the familiar scent of Madame Olenska’s house—flowers, beeswax, a hint of old leather and tea—but it was overlaid now by tobacco, thick and masculine, clinging to curtains and upholstery like an intruder who means to stay.

The lady was long, lean, and loosely put together. Her raiment was intricately looped and fringed, with plaids and stripes and bands of plain colour disposed in a design whose clue seemed missing. Her hair, which had tried to turn white and only succeeded in fading, was surmounted by a Spanish comb and a black lace scarf; silk mittens, visibly darned, covered her rheumatic hands.

One of the men Archer recognised, to his surprise, as Ned Winsett. The other, older and unknown, had the gigantic frame that must have filled the Macfarlane; his head was feebly leonine, his crumpled grey hair unkempt, and he moved his arms with large pawing gestures, as though distributing lay blessings to a kneeling multitude.

All three stood on the hearth-rug, their eyes fixed on an extraordinarily large bouquet of crimson roses, with a knot of purple pansies at their base, lying on the sofa where Madame Olenska usually sat.

“What they must have cost at this season—though of course it’s the sentiment one cares about!” the lady was saying in a sighing staccato as Archer came in.

They turned at his entrance. The lady advanced at once, holding out her hand.

“Dear Mr. Archer—almost my cousin Newland!” she said. “I am the Marchioness Manson.”

Archer bowed, and she continued with a fluttering importance: “My Ellen has taken me in for a few days. I came from Cuba, where I have been spending the winter with Spanish friends—such delightful distinguished people: the highest nobility of old Castile—how I wish you could know them! But I was called away by our dear great friend here, Dr. Carver. You don’t know Dr. Agathon Carver, founder of the Valley of Love Community?”

Dr. Carver inclined his leonine head.

“Ah, New York—New York—how little the life of the spirit has reached it!” the Marchioness went on, as if the city’s noises were proof of its moral defect. “But I see you do know Mr. Winsett.”

“Oh, yes—I reached him some time ago; but not by that route,” Winsett said, with his dry smile.

The Marchioness shook her head reprovingly. “How do you know, Mr. Winsett? The spirit bloweth where it listeth.”

“List—oh, list!” interjected Dr. Carver in a stentorian murmur, as if even words should be commanded into obedience.

“But do sit down, Mr. Archer,” the Marchioness urged. “We four have been having a delightful little dinner together, and my child has gone up to dress. She expects you; she will be down in a moment. We were just admiring these marvellous flowers, which will surprise her when she reappears.”

Winsett remained on his feet. “I’m afraid I must be off. Please tell Madame Olenska that we shall all feel lost when she abandons our street. This house has been an oasis.”

“Ah, but she won’t abandon you. Poetry and art are the breath of life to her. It is poetry you write, Mr. Winsett?”

“Well, no; but I sometimes read it,” Winsett said; and with a general nod to the group he slipped out of the room, as quietly as if he were escaping an argument rather than a drawing-room.

“A caustic spirit—un peu sauvage,” the Marchioness murmured. “But so witty; Dr. Carver, you do think him witty?”

“I never think of wit,” said Dr. Carver severely.

“Ah—ah—you never think of wit! How merciless he is to us weak mortals, Mr. Archer!” She sighed as if the sigh were a form of prayer. “But he lives only in the life of the spirit; and tonight he is mentally preparing the lecture he is to deliver presently at Mrs. Blenker’s. Dr. Carver, would there be time, before you start for the Blenkers’ to explain to Mr. Archer your illuminating discovery of the Direct Contact? But no; I see it is nearly nine o’clock, and we have no right to detain you while so many are waiting for your message.”

Dr. Carver looked slightly disappointed, but, having compared his ponderous gold time-piece with Madame Olenska’s little travelling-clock, he reluctantly gathered up his mighty limbs.

“I shall see you later, dear friend?” he suggested to the Marchioness.

“As soon as Ellen’s carriage comes I will join you,” she replied with a smile. “I do hope the lecture won’t have begun.”

Dr. Carver looked thoughtfully at Archer. “Perhaps, if this young gentleman is interested in my experiences, Mrs. Blenker might allow you to bring him with you?”

“Oh, dear friend, if it were possible—I am sure she would be too happy. But I fear my Ellen counts on Mr. Archer herself.”

“That,” said Dr. Carver, “is unfortunate—but here is my card.”

He handed it to Archer. In Gothic characters it read:

Agathon Carver  
The Valley of Love  
Kittasquattamy, N. Y.

Dr. Carver bowed himself out. The Marchioness, with a sigh that might have meant either regret or relief, waved Archer to a seat again.

“Ellen will be down in a moment; and before she comes, I am so glad of this quiet moment with you.”

Archer murmured something civil. The air in the room seemed thick with competing scents: the cigar-smoke still hanging in the curtains, the roses’ extravagant sweetness rising in warm waves, and beneath it all the faint, domestic odor of supper recently eaten—soup, roast, bread warmed and broken, the kitchen’s invisible labor absorbed into the drawing-room as politely as possible. It struck him with sudden bitterness how easily other people’s hands could handle the food, the flowers, even the story of a life—portion it, season it, rename it.

“I know everything, dear Mr. Archer,” the Marchioness continued. “My child has told me all you have done for her. Your wise advice: your courageous firmness—thank heaven it was not too late!”

Embarrassment reddened his mind. Was there anyone to whom Ellen had not proclaimed his intervention? He heard, in the Marchioness’s voice, the relish of repetition—how a tale grows glossy in the telling, like a sauce stirred too long.

“Madame Olenska exaggerates,” he said. “I simply gave her a legal opinion, as she asked me to.”

“Ah, but in doing it—in doing it you were the unconscious instrument of—of—what word have we moderns for Providence, Mr. Archer?” She tilted her head and drooped her lids mysteriously, as if searching her memory for a fashionable term. “Little did you know that at that very moment I was being appealed to: being approached, in fact—from the other side of the Atlantic!”

She glanced over her shoulder, as though fearful of being overheard, then drew her chair closer and raised a tiny ivory fan to her lips. Behind it she breathed: “By the Count himself—my poor, mad, foolish Olenski; who asks only to take her back on her own terms.”

“Good God!” Archer exclaimed, springing up. The words tore out of him before he could hush them, and the room seemed suddenly too small for the sound.

“You are horrified? Yes, of course; I understand,” the Marchioness said, unruffled. “I don’t defend poor Stanislas, though he has always called me his best friend. He does not defend himself—he casts himself at her feet: in my person.” She tapped her emaciated bosom. “I have his letter here.”

“A letter? Has Madame Olenska seen it?” Archer stammered, his thoughts whirling so fast he could hardly hold them.

The Marchioness shook her head softly. “Time—time; I must have time. I know my Ellen—haughty, intractable; shall I say, just a shade unforgiving?”

“But, good heavens, to forgive is one thing; to go back into that hell—”

“Ah, yes,” the Marchioness acquiesced. “So she describes it—my sensitive child! But on the material side, Mr. Archer, if one may stoop to consider such things; do you know what she is giving up?”

Her hand drifted toward the roses on the sofa, as though their fragrance were an argument. “Those roses there—acres like them, under glass and in the open, in his matchless terraced gardens at Nice! Jewels—historic pearls: the Sobieski emeralds—sables—but she cares nothing for all these! Art and beauty, those she does care for, she lives for, as I always have; and those also surrounded her. Pictures, priceless furniture, music, brilliant conversation—ah, that, my dear young man, if you’ll excuse me, is what you’ve no conception of here! And she had it all; and the homage of the greatest. She tells me she is not thought handsome in New York—good heavens! Her portrait has been painted nine times; the greatest artists in Europe have begged for the privilege. Are these things nothing? And the remorse of an adoring husband?”

As the Marchioness rose to her climax her face took on an expression of ecstatic retrospection, as if she were tasting a vanished feast and finding it richer than anything set before her now. Archer might have laughed at the absurdity, if he had not been numb with amazement. It was as if a whole foreign vocabulary had been poured into this little room—titles, pearls, emeralds, Nice—words meant to outweigh Ellen’s own, to translate her suffering into commodities. He felt, with sudden clarity, the power of such a lexicon: how it could rename a prison “a palace,” and call endurance “a wife’s duty.”

“She knows nothing yet—of all this?” he asked abruptly.

Mrs. Manson laid a purple finger on her lips. “Nothing directly—but does she suspect? Who can tell? The truth is, Mr. Archer, I have been waiting to see you. From the moment I heard of the firm stand you had taken, and of your influence over her, I hoped it might be possible to count on your support—to convince you…”

“That she ought to go back? I would rather see her dead!” Archer cried, the violence in him breaking loose like a pot boiling over.

“Ah,” the Marchioness murmured, without visible resentment.

For a while she sat opening and shutting the absurd ivory fan between her mittened fingers. The fan made a small, repetitive whisper—paper against air—like a private language of impatience. Then she lifted her head and listened.

“Here she comes,” she said in a rapid whisper; and then, pointing to the bouquet on the sofa, she added softly, insistently, as if pressing the last spoonful of a bitter remedy: “Am I to understand that you prefer that, Mr. Archer? After all, marriage is marriage… and my niece is still a wife…”

“What are you two plotting together, aunt Medora?” Madame Olenska called as she swept into the room.

She had dressed as if for a ball—yet not in the dutiful New York way of being prettified into obedience. Her gown seemed spun from the palest flame: candlelight caught in silk and lace, gliding as she moved, a soft glitter that turned the drawing-room air warmer, scented faintly of orris and the crushed green bite of violets. Her hair was heaped into a chignon that looked both disciplined and about to break free, and she carried herself with the lifted chin of a woman accustomed to entering rooms where envy had teeth.

Mrs. Manson Mingott—Medora to her intimates, and never quite intimate with anyone because she was always in love with some new idea—rose with theatrical satisfaction and pointed toward the flowers on a table.

“We were saying, my dear, that here was something beautiful to surprise you with.”

Madame Olenska halted.

Newland Archer, standing a little apart near the hearth, saw the change pass over her not as color but as a whitening, a bright, cold flash—anger like summer lightning that lights the whole landscape without warming it. She looked at the bouquet as if it were a thing that had been set down alive.

“Ah,” she cried, in a sharp voice he had never heard from her—too high, too naked. “Who is ridiculous enough to send me a bouquet? Why a bouquet? And why tonight of all nights? I am not going to a ball; I am not a girl engaged to be married. But some people are always ridiculous.”

The room, which had been quiet with the domestic hush of thick carpets and banked coals, suddenly seemed full of sound: the faint ticking of a clock, the little crackle of fire, the distant scrape of wheels out in the street. Even her words had a different ring—metallic, ringing off the mirrors. Newland, who had grown up in houses where anger was muffled like a cough behind a napkin, felt the shock of it as a kind of illicit vividness.

She turned, opened the door, and called: “Nastasia!”

The handmaiden appeared at once, as if the house had been listening for her name. Madame Olenska spoke in Italian—slowly, deliberately, with that precision that made the language feel like something you could taste: clean vowels, a blade’s edge to the consonants, chosen so that Newland would understand.

“Here—throw this into the dustbin!”

Nastasia stared, protesting with her eyes; the bouquet’s scent—rose and hyacinth and something spicy—had already begun to fill the room more boldly, as if insisting on its own innocence.

“But no—it’s not the fault of the poor flowers,” Madame Olenska added at once, with a quick recoil from her own violence. “Tell the boy to carry them to the house three doors away, the house of Mr. Winsett, the dark gentleman who dined here. His wife is ill—they may give her pleasure… The boy is out, you say? Then, my dear one, run yourself; here, put my cloak over you and fly. I want the thing out of the house immediately! And, as you live, don’t say they come from me!”

She flung her velvet opera cloak over Nastasia’s shoulders—velvet that held the faint warmth of her own body and the perfume that clung to her skin like a secret—and shut the drawing-room door sharply, sealing the flowers out as if their very fragrance could betray her.

For a moment her breathing rose under the lace at her throat, and Newland thought she might cry; instead she laughed—one of those laughs that was not merriment but release—and looked from the Marchioness to him with a sudden, challenging light.

“And you two—have you made friends!”

“It’s for Mr. Archer to say, darling,” Mrs. Manson returned, pleased with herself and with the scene. “He has waited patiently while you were dressing.”

“Yes—I gave you time enough: my hair wouldn’t go,” Madame Olenska said, lifting her hand to the heavy curls as if they had been a living creature resisting her. Then, briskly: “But that reminds me: I see Dr. Carver is gone, and you’ll be late at the Blenkers’. Mr. Archer, will you put my aunt in the carriage?”

Newland found himself in the hall amid the heap of overshoes, shawls, and tippets—a clutter of wool and fur, smelling of damp and cold air and camphor, the ordinary armor New York women wore against weather and comment. He helped Mrs. Manson into it all while Madame Olenska, already impatient for the next movement, stood on the threshold and called after them:

“Mind, the carriage is to be back for me at ten!”

Then she returned to the drawing-room, and when Newland came back he found her at the mantel-glass, looking at herself with an unembarrassed scrutiny. It was not usual in New York to hear a lady call her servant “my dear one,” or to send her out wrapped in her own opera cloak. The speed with which feeling became action here—no smoothing, no waiting for propriety to cool it—gave Newland a startled pleasure, as if he had stepped into a climate where the air itself carried more oxygen.

She did not turn when he approached. Their eyes met in the mirror for the briefest second—two reflections held in the same frame—then she went to the sofa and dropped into her corner, exhaling as if she had come in from a run.

“There’s time for a cigarette.”

He handed her the box. The little ritual—paper, tobacco, flame—had its own domestic intimacy, like cutting bread at a table. He struck a spill and held it; as the flame flared and warmed her face, the room filled with that first sweet-bitter smell that seemed to lay a veil over everything.

She glanced at him through the first ring of smoke and smiled, her temper already transmuted into wit.

“What do you think of me in a temper?”

He hesitated only a moment. The clock ticked; the fire shifted; somewhere outside, a cab rattled over the cobbles and then faded, leaving the street quiet again.

“It makes me understand what your aunt has been saying about you.”

“I knew she’d been talking about me. Well?”

“She said you were used to all kinds of things—splendours and amusements and excitements—that we could never hope to give you here.”

Madame Olenska smiled faintly, smoke curling at her lips like a soft guard.

“Medora is incorrigibly romantic. It has made up to her for so many things!”

Newland, feeling the edge of time already—this being their last evening here by this fire, this room with its muffled city-silence outside—pushed further.

“Is your aunt’s romanticism always consistent with accuracy?”

“You mean: does she speak the truth?” She considered, as if weighing spices by scent alone. “Well, I’ll tell you: in almost everything she says, there’s something true and something untrue. But why do you ask? What has she been telling you?”

He looked into the coals. The heat breathed; the room’s faint odors—wax, old upholstery, tobacco—seemed suddenly precious, unrepeatable.

“She says—she pretends that Count Olenski has asked her to persuade you to go back to him.”

Madame Olenska did not start. He had noticed before her strange lack of surprise, as if life had ceased long ago to astonish her and only continued to wound or delight.

“You knew, then?” he burst out.

She was silent so long the ash fell from her cigarette. She brushed it to the floor with an absent movement.

“She has hinted about a letter: poor darling! Medora’s hints—”

“Is it at your husband’s request that she has arrived here suddenly?”

Again that pause, that inward listening.

“There again: one can’t tell. She told me she had had a ‘spiritual summons,’ whatever that is, from Dr. Carver. I’m afraid she’s going to marry Dr. Carver… poor Medora, there’s always some one she wants to marry. But perhaps the people in Cuba just got tired of her! I think she was with them as a sort of paid companion. Really, I don’t know why she came.”

“But you do believe she has a letter from your husband?”

Madame Olenska sat with her gaze lowered, as if she were reading not the present but a script long familiar.

“After all, it was to be expected.”

Newland rose and leaned against the chimney-piece, restless. The street outside seemed too quiet, as though it were holding its breath to hear what would be decided in this room. He imagined the sudden jingle of harness, the return of her carriage, time coming back for her.

“You know that your aunt believes you will go back?”

Madame Olenska lifted her head quickly. A deep blush rose over her face and down her neck and shoulders—painful, rare, as if it burned her.

“Many cruel things have been believed of me,” she said.

“Oh, Ellen—forgive me; I’m a fool and a brute!”

She smiled a little, gentler now, and, with the instinct that made her turn away from her own pain, she shifted the subject as if moving a lamp to soften a harsh corner.

“You are horribly nervous; you have your own troubles. I know you think the Wellands are unreasonable about your marriage, and of course I agree with you. In Europe people don’t understand our long American engagements; I suppose they are not as calm as we are.”

She gave the “we” a faint emphasis—ironic, almost tender in its irony. He felt it and dared not touch it.

“Yes,” he said abruptly, following her lead because he could not bear to lose her in a fog of politeness. “I went south to ask May to marry me after Easter. There’s no reason why we shouldn’t be married then.”

“And May adores you—and yet you couldn’t convince her? I thought her too intelligent to be the slave of such absurd superstitions.”

“She is too intelligent—she’s not their slave.”

Madame Olenska looked at him steadily.

“Well, then—I don’t understand.”

He reddened and went on, the words crowding like people at a door.

“We had a frank talk—almost the first. She thinks my impatience a bad sign.”

“Merciful heavens—a bad sign?”

“She thinks it means that I can’t trust myself to go on caring for her. She thinks, in short, I want to marry her at once to get away from some one that I—care for more.”

Madame Olenska’s face held curiosity, not jealousy. It was one of the ways she was unlike the women he knew—she could look at feeling without instantly turning it into a weapon.

“But if she thinks that—why isn’t she in a hurry too?”

“Because she’s not like that: she’s so much nobler. She insists all the more on the long engagement, to give me time—”

“Time to give her up for the other woman?”

“If I want to.”

Madame Olenska leaned toward the fire and stared into it. Outside, Newland heard at last the approaching trot—hoofbeats on frozen ground, the faint chiming of harness bells, sound traveling through the night like fate drawing nearer.

“That is noble,” she said, and her voice broke slightly.

“Yes. But it’s ridiculous.”

“Ridiculous? Because you don’t care for any one else?”

“Because I don’t mean to marry any one else.”

“Ah.”

Silence. The horses came nearer, then slowed.

At length she looked up and asked, almost clinically: “This other woman—does she love you?”

“Oh, there’s no other woman; I mean, the person that May was thinking of is—was never—”

“Then, why, after all, are you in such haste?”

“There’s your carriage,” he said.

She half rose and looked about, absently. Her fan and gloves lay beside her on the sofa; she picked them up as if they belonged to another woman and must be carried out for the sake of decorum.

“Yes; I suppose I must be going.”

“You’re going to Mrs. Struthers’s?”

“Yes.” She smiled. “I must go where I am invited, or I should be too lonely. Why not come with me?”

He could not let her go—not yet, not like this, with everything still unsaid and the house already filling with departure. Ignoring her question, he stood rigidly, eyes fixed on the hand that held her gloves and fan, as if he might will her fingers to open.

“May guessed the truth,” he said. “There is another woman—but not the one she thinks.”

Madame Olenska did not move. The fan’s sticks were motionless between her fingers.

He sat down beside her, and, taking her hand, unclasped it softly. Gloves and fan dropped to the sofa between them with a small, decisive sound.

She started up, pulling away, crossing to the other side of the hearth as if the fire itself were a border.

“Ah, don’t make love to me! Too many people have done that,” she said, frowning.

He changed color. It was the bitterest thing she could have said, because it pushed him into the common crowd of men who had used her beauty as a door to their own appetites.

“I have never made love to you,” he said, “and I never shall. But you are the woman I would have married if it had been possible for either of us.”

“Possible for either of us?” She stared, honestly astonished. “And you say that—when it’s you who’ve made it impossible?”

He stared back as if the room had suddenly gone dark.

“I’ve made it impossible—?”

“You, you, you!” she cried, her lip trembling like a child’s at the edge of tears. “Isn’t it you who made me give up divorcing—give it up because you showed me how selfish and wicked it was, how one must sacrifice one’s self to preserve the dignity of marriage… and to spare one’s family the publicity, the scandal? And because my family was going to be your family—for May’s sake and for yours—I did what you told me, what you proved to me that I ought to do. Ah,” she broke off with a sudden laugh, “I’ve made no secret of having done it for you!”

She sank back into the sofa, crouching among the festive folds of her dress like a masquerader struck down mid-dance. Newland stood by the fireplace, unable to move, as if the words had nailed him to the spot.

“Good God,” he groaned. “When I thought—”

“You thought?” The blush rose again, scorching her skin. She sat upright, rigid with a dignity that looked like armor hammered on while still hot. “I do ask you.”

“Well, then: there were things in that letter you asked me to read—”

“My husband’s letter?”

“Yes.”

“I had nothing to fear from that letter: absolutely nothing! All I feared was to bring notoriety, scandal, on the family—on you and May.”

He bowed his face in his hands. The silence that followed was heavy, final; it pressed on him like a stone lid. In his mind the future stretched out and not one day in it seemed capable of lifting that weight.

“At least I loved you—” he forced out.

From the other side of the hearth he heard a faint, stifled crying, small and helpless as a child’s. The sound—so unguarded in a house where even grief was usually upholstered—went through him like a knife.

“Ellen!” He crossed to her. “What madness! Why are you crying? Nothing’s done that can’t be undone. I’m still free, and you’re going to be.”

He took her in his arms. Her face was wet against his lips like a flower after rain—alive, fragile, real. In that closeness every argument shriveled instantly, as if words were only ghosts and touch the sunrise that destroyed them. She returned his kiss wholly; and for a moment it seemed to him absurd that they had stood arguing across the room, as though distance itself could be maintained by reason.

Then he felt her stiffen. She put her hands against him—not harshly, but with a firmness that made him understand she was drawing on some reservoir deeper than passion.

“Ah, my poor Newland—I suppose this had to be. But it doesn’t in the least alter things,” she said, looking down at him from the hearth as if she were already removed to a higher, colder place.

“It alters the whole of life for me.”

“No, no—it mustn’t, it can’t. You’re engaged to May Welland; and I’m married.”

He stood too, flushed, suddenly reckless with resolve.

“Nonsense! It’s too late for that sort of thing. We’ve no right to lie to other people or to ourselves. We won’t talk of your marriage; but do you see me marrying May after this?”

She leaned her thin elbows on the mantelpiece. Her profile was doubled in the glass behind her, as if the room were making a second, quieter Ellen who watched and remembered. One lock had loosened from the chignon and lay along her neck; the effect was not pretty but human, and it made her look haggard, almost old.

“I don’t see you,” she said at last, “putting that question to May. Do you?”

He shrugged, trying to make courage out of defiance.

“It’s too late to do anything else.”

“You say that because it’s the easiest thing to say at this moment—not because it’s true. In reality it’s too late to do anything but what we’d both decided on.”

“Ah, I don’t understand you!”

She smiled, pitifully; it pinched instead of smoothing her face.

“You don’t understand because you haven’t yet guessed how you’ve changed things for me: oh, from the first—long before I knew all you’d done.”

“All I’d done?”

“Yes. I was perfectly unconscious at first that people here were shy of me—that they thought I was a dreadful sort of person. It seems they had even refused to meet me at dinner. I found that out afterward; and how you’d made your mother go with you to the van der Luydens’; and how you’d insisted on announcing your engagement at the Beaufort ball, so that I might have two families to stand by me instead of one—”

At that, despite everything, he broke into a laugh—short, incredulous, almost angry with the scale of what she called small.

“Just imagine,” she went on, “how stupid and unobservant I was! I knew nothing of all this till Granny blurted it out one day. New York simply meant peace and freedom to me: it was coming home. And I was so happy at being among my own people that every one I met seemed kind and good, and glad to see me. But from the very beginning,” she continued, in a low even voice, “I felt there was no one as kind as you; no one who gave me reasons that I understood for doing what at first seemed so hard and—unnecessary. The very good people didn’t convince me; I felt they’d never been tempted. But you knew; you understood; you had felt the world outside tugging at one with all its golden hands—and yet you hated the things it asks of one; you hated happiness bought by disloyalty and cruelty and indifference. That was what I’d never known before—and it’s better than anything I’ve known.”
