Each word fell into him like something molten—burning not because it was cruel but because it was true. He sat bowed, staring at the hearth rug, at the small tip of satin shoe that showed beneath her hem as if that detail were all he could bear to look at.

Suddenly he knelt and kissed the shoe.

She bent over him, laid her hands on his shoulders, and looked down with eyes so deep he could not move under their gaze.

“Ah, don’t let us undo what you’ve done!” she cried. “I can’t go back now to that other way of thinking. I can’t love you unless I give you up.”

His arms strained toward her, but she drew away. They faced each other, divided now by the distance her words created—distance not of feet but of a moral language he could hear and yet could not speak.

Abruptly his anger surged up, wild and ugly, seeking something it could strike.

“And Beaufort? Is he to replace me?”

He expected her anger in return—expected it and would have welcomed it, for it would have been something simpler than this quiet firmness. But she only went a shade paler. Her arms hung down; her head bent slightly, as it did when she pondered something, listening inwardly.

“He’s waiting for you now at Mrs. Struthers’s; why don’t you go to him?” Archer sneered.

She turned and rang the bell.

“I shall not go out this evening; tell the carriage to go and fetch the Signora Marchesa,” she said to the maid when she came.

When the door closed again he still looked at her with bitter eyes, ashamed and unable to stop.

“Why this sacrifice? Since you tell me that you’re lonely I’ve no right to keep you from your friends.”

She smiled faintly through wet lashes.

“I shan’t be lonely now. I was lonely; I was afraid. But the emptiness and the darkness are gone; when I turn back into myself now I’m like a child going at night into a room where there’s always a light.”

Her tone wrapped her again in that soft inaccessibility, as if she lived in a country whose dialect he could almost understand and yet could never speak without an accent that betrayed him.

“I don’t understand you!” he groaned.

“Yet you understand May!”

He reddened at the hit and kept his eyes fixed on her, as if only looking could hold her there.

“May is ready to give me up.”

“What! Three days after you’ve entreated her on your knees to hasten your marriage?”

“She’s refused; that gives me the right—”

“Ah, you’ve taught me what an ugly word that is,” she said quietly.

He turned away, weariness flooding him. He felt as if he had been climbing for hours up a sheer face of rock, hands bleeding, breath torn; and now, just as he reached what should have been the top, his hold had broken and he was falling back into darkness. If he could only have taken her again—if he could have drowned her reasons in warmth and closeness—perhaps he might have swept them away. But something in her look and bearing held him off, and his own awe of her sincerity enforced the distance.

At last he began to plead again, stumbling over his desperation.

“If we do this now it will be worse afterward—worse for every one—”

“No—no—no!” she cried, almost screaming, as if his insistence frightened her.

At that moment the bell rang—long, insistent, the sound traveling through the house with a shiver. No carriage had stopped at the door. They stood motionless, staring at each other, startled by the intrusion of that sharp mechanical summons into their strained, breathing silence.

Nastasia crossed the hall; the outer door opened; then she came in with a telegram and handed it to Madame Olenska.

“The lady was very happy at the flowers,” Nastasia said, smoothing her apron with the placid satisfaction of someone reporting that a dish had been well received. “She thought it was her signor marito who had sent them, and she cried a little and said it was a folly.”

Madame Olenska smiled, took the yellow envelope, and went to the lamp. The paper crackled as she tore it open. When Nastasia had withdrawn, she held the telegram out to Newland.

It was dated from St. Augustine and addressed to the Countess Olenska. He read:

“Granny’s telegram successful. Papa and Mamma agree marriage after Easter. Am telegraphing Newland. Am too happy for words and love you dearly. Your grateful May.”

Half an hour later Newland unlocked his own front door. The house smelled of extinguished lamps, of closed rooms and furniture wax—his mother’s world, muffled and sure. On the hall-table, among notes and letters, lay a similar yellow envelope.

Inside was May’s message:

“Parents consent wedding Tuesday after Easter at twelve Grace Church eight bridesmaids please see Rector so happy love May.”

Newland crumpled the telegram as if the violence of his hand could erase the words. Then he pulled out a small pocket diary, turned the pages with trembling fingers, searching for dates as though time were an enemy he might outmaneuver. He did not find at once what he wanted. He jammed the telegram into his pocket and went upstairs.

A light showed under the door of the little hall-room that served Janey as dressing-room and boudoir. He rapped impatiently.

The door opened. His sister stood there in her immemorial purple flannel dressing-gown, her hair “on pins,” her face pale with the hope of bad news—as if misfortune would justify her vigilance.

“Newland! I hope there’s no bad news in that telegram? I waited on purpose, in case—”

He did not answer. Nothing of his correspondence was safe from Janey’s anxious devotion.

“Look here—what day is Easter this year?”

She stared, shocked at such unchristian ignorance.

“Easter? Newland! Why, of course, the first week in April. Why?”

“The first week?” He bent again over his diary, calculating rapidly under his breath. “The first week, did you say?”

He threw back his head and laughed—long, uncontrollable, as if the sound tore itself out of him because silence would have killed him.

“For mercy’s sake what’s the matter?” Janey whispered, frightened now. “Do hush, or you’ll wake Mamma.”

“Nothing’s the matter,” he said, still laughing. “Nothing’s the matter, except that I’m going to be married in a month.”

Janey flung herself upon his neck and pressed him to her purple flannel breast.

“Oh Newland, how wonderful! I’m so glad! But, dearest—why do you keep on laughing?”

The day had broken with that particular New York spring that felt like a hand turned briskly inside a glove—fresh, impatient, and full of dust. The wind worried the budding trees along Broadway and sent grit skating over carriage steps; it sifted into the very folds of the ladies’ furs as if determined to remind them that winter’s mothballs were not yet a memory.

Inside Grace Church the air was thickened by those same memories. All the old ladies in both families had unearthed their faded sables and yellowing ermines; the camphor rose from the front pews in medicinal waves and lay heavy over the faint, clean breath of lilies banked about the altar. It was a strange mingling of scents: the bruised sweetness of lily pollen and the sharp, closed-chest smell of fur stored too long with sprigs of repellent—spring pressing in at the doors, the past refusing to make room.

At a signal from the sexton, Newland Archer stepped out of the vestry and took his place on the chancel step with his best man. The signal meant that the brougham bearing the bride and her father had been sighted; it also meant, as everyone knew without saying, that there would be an interval—adjustments in the lobby, consultations over the last drape of a veil, a bouquet held too high or too low, eight bridesmaids hovering like pale blossoms, each corrected into symmetry.

During that inevitable lapse the bridegroom, as proof of eagerness, was expected to stand exposed and solitary to the gaze of the assembled company. Archer submitted to it as he had submitted to everything else that made a nineteenth-century New York wedding feel like a rite from the dawn of history—so prescribed in each movement that even a heartbeat seemed an impropriety. Everything was equally easy—or equally painful—along the path he had committed himself to tread; and he obeyed the flurried whispers of his best man as piously as other bridegrooms had once obeyed him, when he had shepherded them through the same old labyrinth.

So far he was reasonably sure he had done all that was demanded. The eight bouquets of white lilac and lilies-of-the-valley had been dispatched at the proper moment; the gold-and-sapphire sleeve-links for the eight ushers and the cat’s-eye scarf-pin for the best man had been chosen and sent; he had sat up half the night varying the wording of his thanks for the last batch of gifts from men friends and ex–lady-loves—tact, gratitude, and a faintly stale gallantry carefully measured, like ingredients that must not curdle. The fees for Bishop and Rector were safely in his best man’s pocket. His luggage was already at Mrs. Manson Mingott’s, where the wedding-breakfast would take place; the travelling clothes into which he would change were laid out there too. A private compartment had been secured for the train that would carry the young couple to their unknown destination—the hiding of the first night’s lodging being one of the most sacred taboos of this prehistoric ritual, as if secrecy itself were a kind of seasoning to matrimony.

“Got the ring all right?” young van der Luyden Newland whispered. He was inexperienced in these duties and awed by their weight, as though he had been trusted with a relic.

Archer made the gesture he had seen so many men make in his place: with his ungloved right hand he felt in the pocket of his dark grey waistcoat and assured himself that the small gold circlet—engraved inside, _Newland to May, April ——, 187—_—was there. Then he resumed his attitude, tall hat and pearl-grey gloves with black stitching held in his left hand, and looked toward the church door.

Overhead Handel’s March swelled through the imitation stone vaulting, pompous and steady, as if the organ itself had learned the city’s way of insisting that what had always been done must always be done. The music carried on its waves the faded drift of the many weddings at which Archer, with cheerful indifference, had stood on this same chancel step watching other brides float up the nave toward other bridegrooms. The sound filled the church like a well-known recipe made again: rich, orderly, impossible to taste newly.

“How like a first night at the Opera,” he thought, recognizing all the same faces in the same boxes—no, pews. He wondered, with a sudden macabre clarity, if when the Last Trump sounded Mrs. Selfridge Merry would still be there with the same towering ostrich feathers and Mrs. Beaufort with the same diamond earrings and the same smile—and whether suitable proscenium seats were already prepared for them in another world.

There was time still to review the familiar countenances in the first rows. The women’s faces were sharp with curiosity and excited appraisal; the men’s were sulky with the obligation of having put on frock-coats before luncheon, and with the foreknowledge of having to fight their way to food at the wedding-breakfast. Archer could almost hear Reggie Chivers’s complaint, as distinct as if it had been spoken aloud: “Too bad the breakfast is at old Catherine’s.” And then, as naturally, Sillerton Jackson’s authoritative correction: “My dear fellow, haven’t you heard? It’s to be served at small tables, in the new English fashion.”

At the thought Archer’s mind leapt, incongruously, to Mrs. Manson Mingott’s table: the old Baltimore silver, the massive trays that made every dish look like an offering, the hush that could fall at the exact moment the covers were lifted and steam rose—an unveiling as ceremonious as any benediction. He had grown up among people who spoke of food as “good” or “bad” in the tone they used for weather; and yet here, even in church, men were already measuring the day’s success by whether one could “get at” the cooking. It was the closest anyone came to honest anticipation.

His eyes lingered on the left-hand pew. His mother, who had entered on Mr. Henry van der Luyden’s arm, sat weeping softly under her Chantilly veil, her hands folded on her grandmother’s ermine muff. Across the aisle his sister Janey twisted herself as far as decorum allowed. “Poor Janey,” he thought; “even by screwing her head around she can see only the people in the few front pews—and they’re mostly dowdy Newlands and Dagonets.”

On the hither side of the white ribbon dividing off the seats reserved for the families, Beaufort sat tall and red-faced, scrutinising the women with his arrogant stare. Beside him his wife glittered, all silvery chinchilla and violets; and on the far side of the ribbon Lawrence Lefferts’s sleekly brushed head seemed to mount guard over the invisible deity of Good Form who presided over the ceremony. Archer wondered how many flaws Lefferts’s keen eyes would discover in the ritual of his divinity—and then, with a start as sharp as tasting something unexpectedly bitter, he remembered that he too had once thought such questions important.

The things that had filled his days now seemed like a nursery parody of life, or like the wrangles of mediaeval schoolmen over metaphysical terms nobody had ever understood. Only yesterday the last hours before the wedding had been darkened by a stormy discussion of whether the wedding presents should be “shown.” It seemed inconceivable to him now that grown people could work themselves into agitation over such trifles, and that the question should have been settled—negatively—by Mrs. Welland’s indignant tears and declaration: “I should as soon turn the reporters loose in my house.” The horror of publicity in her voice had been as real as if she were defending a child from contagion.

Yet there had been a time when Archer had held definite and rather aggressive opinions on all such problems; when everything concerning the manners and customs of his little tribe had seemed to him freighted with world-wide significance. “And all the while, I suppose,” he thought, “real people were living somewhere, and real things happening to them…”

“There they come!” breathed the best man, excited.

But Archer knew better. The cautious opening of the church door meant only that Mr. Brown of the livery-stable, gowned in black in his intermittent character of sexton, was taking a preliminary survey before marshalling his forces. The door was softly shut again. Then, after another interval, it swung majestically open and a murmur ran through the church: “The family!”

Mrs. Welland came first on the arm of her eldest son. Her large pink face was appropriately solemn, and her plum-coloured satin with pale blue side-panels, and blue ostrich plumes in a small satin bonnet, met with general approval. But before she had settled herself with a stately rustle in the pew opposite Mrs. Archer, the spectators were craning their necks to see who followed.

Wild rumours had been abroad the day before that Mrs. Manson Mingott, in spite of her physical disabilities, meant to be present. The idea suited her sporting character so well that bets had run high at the clubs on whether she could walk up the nave and squeeze into a seat. It was known she had sent her own carpenter to consider whether the end panel of the front pew could be taken down and the space between the seat and the front measured. The result had discouraged the plan. For one anxious day her family had watched her dally with the idea of being wheeled up the nave in her enormous Bath chair and sitting enthroned at the foot of the chancel. The very thought of that exposure was so painful to her relations that they would have covered with gold the ingenious person who discovered, suddenly and providentially, that the chair was too wide to pass between the iron uprights of the awning that ran from the church door to the curb.

To do away with the awning—and thus reveal the bride to the mob of dressmakers and newspaper reporters outside, fighting to get near the joints of the canvas—exceeded even old Catherine’s courage, though for a moment she had weighed it. “Why, they might take a photograph of my child and _put it in the papers_!” Mrs. Welland had exclaimed when her mother’s last plan was hinted, and from that unthinkable indecency the clan recoiled as one.

The ancestress had been forced to give in. Her concession, however, was bought by the promise that the wedding-breakfast would take place under her roof—though, as the Washington Square connection said, with the Wellands’ house in easy reach it was hard to have to bargain with Brown for a special drive to the other end of nowhere.

Though these transactions had been widely reported by the Jacksons, a sporting minority still clung to the belief that old Catherine would appear in church, and there was a distinct lowering of the temperature when she proved to have been replaced by her daughter-in-law.

Mrs. Lovell Mingott entered with the high colour and glassy stare induced in ladies of her age and habit by the effort of getting into a new dress. Once the disappointment of old Catherine’s absence had subsided, it was agreed that the black Chantilly over lilac satin, with a bonnet of Parma violets, formed the happiest possible contrast to Mrs. Welland’s blue and plum. Far different was the impression produced by the gaunt, mincing lady who followed on Mr. Mingott’s arm, in a wild dishevelment of stripes and fringes and floating scarves.

As this last apparition glided into view Archer’s heart contracted and stopped.

He had taken it for granted that the Marchioness Manson was still in Washington, where she had gone some four weeks earlier with her niece, Madame Olenska. It was generally understood that their abrupt departure was due to Madame Olenska’s desire to remove her aunt from the baleful eloquence of Dr. Agathon Carver, who had nearly succeeded in enlisting her as a recruit for the Valley of Love. In the circumstances no one had expected either lady to return for the wedding.

For a moment Archer stood with his eyes fixed on Medora’s fantastic figure, straining to see who came behind her. But the little procession was at an end. The lesser members of the family had taken their seats, and the eight tall ushers—gathering together like birds preparing for a migratory manoeuvre—were already slipping through the side doors into the lobby.

“Newland—I say: _she’s here!_” the best man whispered.

Archer roused himself with a start. A long time seemed to have passed since his heart had stopped, for the white and rosy procession was already halfway up the nave. The Bishop, the Rector, and two white-winged assistants hovered about the flower-banked altar. The first chords of the Spohr symphony fell like pale petals before the bride.

Archer opened his eyes—had they really been shut?—and felt his heart resume its usual task, as if it had been absent on some other errand. The music, the scent of the lilies on the altar, the vision of tulle and orange-blossoms drifting nearer, the sudden convulsion of happy sobs on Mrs. Archer’s face, the low benedictory murmur of the Rector’s voice, the ordered evolutions of eight pink bridesmaids and eight black ushers: all these sights and sounds and sensations—so familiar, and yet so unutterably strange and meaningless in his new relation to them—were confusedly mingled in his brain.

“My God,” he thought, “_have_ I got the ring?” and again he made the bridegroom’s convulsive gesture.

Then May was beside him. Such radiance streamed from her that it sent a faint warmth through his numbness. He straightened himself and smiled into her eyes.

“Dearly beloved, we are gathered together here,” the Rector began.

The ring was on her hand. The Bishop’s benediction had been given. The bridesmaids poised to resume their places. The organ showed preliminary symptoms of breaking out into the Mendelssohn March, without which no newly-wedded couple had ever emerged upon New York.

“Your arm—I say, give her your arm!” young Newland hissed.

Once more Archer became aware of having been adrift far off in the unknown. What had sent him there? Perhaps the glimpse among the anonymous spectators in the transept of a dark coil of hair beneath a hat which, a moment later, revealed itself to belong to an unknown lady with a long nose, so laughably unlike the person whose image it had evoked that he asked himself if he were becoming subject to hallucinations.

Now he and his wife paced slowly down the nave, borne forward on the light ripples of Mendelssohn, the spring day beckoning through the wide-open doors. At the far end of the canvas tunnel Mrs. Welland’s chestnuts, with big white favours on their frontlets, curvetted and showed off. The footman—who wore a still bigger white favour on his lapel—wrapped May’s white cloak about her, and Archer jumped into the brougham at her side.

She turned to him with a triumphant smile, and their hands clasped under her veil.

“Darling!” Archer said—and suddenly the same black abyss yawned before him. He felt himself sinking deeper and deeper, while his voice rambled on smoothly and cheerfully, as if it belonged to someone else: “Yes, of course I thought I’d lost the ring; no wedding would be complete if the poor devil of a bridegroom didn’t go through that. But you _did_ keep me waiting, you know! I had time to think of every horror that might possibly happen.”

She surprised him by turning, in full Fifth Avenue, and flinging her arms about his neck. Under the veil her breath was warm and young; he caught the faintest trace of orange-blossom and violets, and something else—clean linen, perhaps, the smell of a girl’s gloves new from tissue-paper.

“But none ever can happen now, can it, Newland, as long as we two are together?”

Every detail of the day had been so carefully thought out that, after the wedding-breakfast, the young couple had ample time to put on their travelling clothes, descend the wide Mingott stairs between laughing bridesmaids and weeping parents, and get into the brougham under the traditional shower of rice and satin slippers. There was still half an hour left to drive to the station, buy the last weeklies at the bookstall with the air of seasoned travellers, and settle in the reserved compartment where May’s maid had already placed her dove-coloured travelling cloak and the glaringly new London dressing-bag.

The old du Lac aunts at Rhinebeck had put their house at the disposal of the bridal couple, with a readiness inspired by the prospect of spending a week in New York with Mrs. Archer. Archer, glad to escape the usual “bridal suite” in a Philadelphia or Baltimore hotel—rooms that smelled of gas and new varnish and someone else’s privacy—accepted with equal alacrity. May was enchanted by the idea of going to the country, and childishly amused by the vain efforts of the eight bridesmaids to discover where their mysterious retreat lay. It was thought “very English” to have a country-house lent; the fact gave a last touch of distinction to what was generally conceded to be the most brilliant wedding of the year. But where the house was no one was permitted to know, except the parents of bride and groom; and when taxed with the knowledge they pursed their lips and said mysteriously: “Ah, they didn’t tell us”—which was manifestly true, since there was no need to.

Once settled in the compartment, and once the train, shaking off the endless wooden suburbs, pushed out into the pale spring landscape, talk became easier than Archer had expected. May was still, in look and tone, the simple girl of yesterday. She was eager to compare notes with him about the wedding’s incidents and discussed them as impartially as a bridesmaid reciting them to an usher. At first Archer imagined the detachment a disguise for inward tremor; but her clear eyes showed only tranquil unawareness. Alone for the first time with her husband, she seemed to feel only that she was alone at last with her charming comrade. There was no one she liked as much, no one she trusted so completely; and the culminating lark of this whole delightful adventure of engagement and marriage was to be off with him on a journey like a grown-up person—like a “married woman,” in fact.

It was wonderful that—as he had learned in the Mission garden at St. Augustine—such depths of feeling could coexist with such absence of imagination. He remembered how, even then, she had surprised him by dropping back into inexpressive girlishness as soon as her conscience had been eased of its burden. He saw she would probably go through life dealing as best she could with each experience as it came, never anticipating any by so much as a stolen glance.

Perhaps that faculty of unawareness was what gave her eyes their transparency, and her face the look of representing a type rather than a person—chosen to pose for a civic virtue or a Greek goddess. The blood that ran so near her fair skin might have been a preserving fluid rather than a ravaging element; and yet the look of indestructible youth made her seem neither hard nor dull, only primitive and pure.

In the thick of this meditation he found himself looking at her with the startled gaze of a stranger. To hide it he plunged into reminiscences of the wedding-breakfast and of Granny Mingott’s immense triumphant pervasion of it. May’s face brightened; she settled down to frank enjoyment of the subject, as if it were a menu recited afterward with pleasure.

“I was surprised, though—weren’t you?—that aunt Medora came after all,” she said. “Ellen wrote that they were neither of them well enough to take the journey; I do wish it had been she who had recovered! Did you see the exquisite old lace she sent me?”

He had known the moment must come sooner or later; yet he had half imagined that by sheer force of will he could hold it at bay.

“Yes—I—no: yes, it was beautiful,” he said, looking at her blindly, and wondering if, whenever he heard those two syllables, his carefully built world would tumble like a house of cards.

“Aren’t you tired? It will be good to have some tea when we arrive—I’m sure the aunts have got everything beautifully ready,” he rattled on, taking her hand.

Her mind flew instantly, with sincere delight, to the Baltimore silver service the Beauforts had sent—tea and coffee pieces that glittered with newness—how it “went” so perfectly with uncle Lovell Mingott’s trays and side-dishes. In her voice there was the tidy pleasure of things matching, of a table set correctly, of an evening predicted by the right objects. Archer listened, and somewhere in him a quieter sense noted how their world translated feeling into arrangement: affection into gifts, approval into service, intimacy into the correct height of a teapot.

In the spring twilight the train stopped at Rhinebeck. They walked along the platform to the waiting carriage.

“Ah, how awfully kind of the van der Luydens—they’ve sent their man over from Skuytercliff to meet us,” Archer exclaimed, as a sedate person out of livery approached and relieved the maid of her bags.

“I’m extremely sorry, sir,” the emissary began, with the apologetic gravity of someone bearing news of a death in the pantry. “A little accident has occurred at the Miss du Lacs’: a leak in the water-tank. It happened yesterday, and Mr. van der Luyden, who heard of it this morning, sent a housemaid up by the early train to get the Patroon’s house ready. It will be quite comfortable, I think you’ll find, sir; and the Miss du Lacs have sent their cook over, so that it will be exactly the same as if you’d been at Rhinebeck.”

Archer stared so blankly the man repeated, even more apologetically: “It’ll be exactly the same, sir, I do assure you—”

May’s eager voice broke in, covering the embarrassed silence. “The same as Rhinebeck? The Patroon’s house? But it will be a hundred thousand times better—won’t it, Newland? It’s too dear and kind of Mr. van der Luyden to have thought of it.”

And as they drove off, the maid perched beside the coachman and the shining bridal bags set before the young couple like trophies, she went on, excited, the words tumbling as if the very carriage wheels had quickened her blood.

“Only fancy—I’ve never been inside it—have you? The van der Luydens show it to so few people. But they opened it for Ellen, it seems, and she told me what a darling little place it was: she says it’s the only house she’s seen in America that she could imagine being perfectly happy in.”

“Well—that’s what we’re going to be, isn’t it?” Archer cried gaily.

She answered with her boyish smile, trusting as one trusts a familiar hymn: “Ah, it’s just our luck beginning—the wonderful luck we’re always going to have together!”

“Of course we must dine with Mrs. Carfry, dearest,” Archer said; and May, seated opposite him, answered with an anxious crease of her brows over the monumental Britannia-ware breakfast-service of their lodging-house table.

Outside, London made its usual autumnal sound: rain whisking in narrow streets, wheels sifting through wet grit, a distant iron clatter that seemed to come from everywhere at once. Inside, the dining-room had its own small acoustics—the rasp of a knife on toast, the soft collapse of a muffin when it was split, the thin music of a spoon against a teacup—domestic noises that, for May, were almost as reassuring as food. Yet even with hot tea breathing steam and the butter beginning to melt into the crannies of their bread, the invitation lay between them like something indigestible.

In all the rainy desert of London there were only two people whom the Newland Archers knew; and these two, by old New York instinct, they had tried not to know. There was a tradition—sturdy as a family recipe, and just as unexamined—that it was not “dignified” to force oneself upon acquaintances abroad. Mrs. Archer and Janey had practised that rule on earlier travels with the same careful zeal with which they packed their Wardian cases and their home pharmacy: hermetically. Friendly advances had been met with a reserve so polished it might have passed for virtue. Foreigners, as far as their record showed, existed chiefly to run hotels and sell railway tickets; and even compatriots—unless properly accredited by the right names—were treated with a colder disdain, as though American familiarity outside Fifth Avenue were a form of bad table-manners.

But precautions, like sauces, do not always hold their intended texture. One night at Botzen, an English lady from the room across the passage—whose name, dress, habits and implied social rank were already known to Janey with the precision of a food historian indexing a menu—knocked to ask for a bottle of liniment. Her sister, Mrs. Carfry, had been taken with bronchitis. Mrs. Archer, who travelled with remedies as other women travelled with jewels, produced what was needed; and because Mrs. Carfry was genuinely ill, and the sisters were alone, gratitude set to work with a stubbornness that ignored all the Archers’ notions of dignity. The Archer ladies supplied comforts with the ingenuity of people who know how to make do—hot-water bottles warmed and refilled, a tray arranged so that nourishment could be taken without effort, a brothy little soup coaxed from hotel odds and ends, its smell of pepper and simmered bone somehow more intimate than any speech. Their efficient maid helped nurse the invalid back into breath.

When the Archers left Botzen they imagined the tie would evaporate with the mountain air. To Mrs. Archer, nothing would have been more undignified than to seek out, in another country, a “foreigner” upon whom one had accidentally bestowed a service. But Mrs. Carfry and Miss Harle, to whom this whole code was as incomprehensible as a recipe in a dead dialect, felt themselves linked by eternal obligation to the “delightful Americans.” With touching fidelity—and a kind of supernatural acuteness for timetables—they seized every chance of meeting Mrs. Archer and Janey again on the Continent, and found out, with uncanny promptness, when they would pass through London. The intimacy became indissoluble. Whenever the Archer ladies alighted at Brown’s Hotel, two affectionate friends awaited them—friends who cultivated ferns in Wardian cases, made macramé lace, read the Baroness Bunsen’s memoirs, and had views about the occupants of leading London pulpits.

As Mrs. Archer said, it made “another thing of London” to know Mrs. Carfry and Miss Harle. By the time Newland became engaged, the families’ tie was so firmly established that it was thought only right to send a wedding invitation; and the English ladies replied with a pretty bouquet of pressed Alpine flowers under glass, a little preserved landscape of colour—petals flattened into permanent memory.

And on the dock, when Newland and May sailed for England, Mrs. Archer’s last injunction had been: “You must take May to see Mrs. Carfry.”

Newland and his wife had had no real intention of obeying. But Mrs. Carfry, in the way she had of making gratitude active, had run them down and sent an invitation to dine; and it was over that invitation that May was wrinkling her brows, her spoon paused above her tea.

“It’s all very well for you, Newland; you know them. But I shall feel so shy among a lot of people I’ve never met. And what shall I wear?”

He leaned back, smiling at her. She looked handsomer and more Diana-like than ever. The moist English air had deepened the bloom of her cheeks, softened the slight hardness of her virginal lines; or perhaps it was only the inner glow of content shining through, like light under ice. Even at breakfast she had a kind of clean radiance, as if happiness were something you could smell faintly—like linen warmed at a fire, or milk just brought in from the cold.

“Wear, dearest? I thought a trunkful of things had come from Paris last week.”

“Yes, of course. I meant—I shan’t know which to wear.” She pouted a little, as she had when a ribbon would not sit right. “I’ve never dined out in London; and I don’t want to be ridiculous.”

He tried to enter into her perplexity. “But don’t Englishwomen dress just like everybody else in the evening?”

“Newland! How can you ask such funny questions? When they go to the theatre in old ball-dresses and bare heads.”

“Well—perhaps they wear new ball-dresses at home; but at any rate Mrs. Carfry and Miss Harle won’t. They’ll wear caps like my mother’s—and shawls; very soft shawls.”

“Yes; but how will the other women be dressed?”

“Not as well as you, dear,” he said, struck by this sudden resemblance to Janey’s worried vigilance over clothes. May pushed back her chair with a sigh; the legs scraped faintly on the floor, a small protest in the room’s quiet.

“That’s dear of you, Newland; but it doesn’t help me much.”

He had an inspiration. “Why not wear your wedding-dress? That can’t be wrong, can it?”

“Oh, dearest! If I only had it here! But it’s gone to Paris to be made over for next winter, and Worth hasn’t sent it back.”

“Oh, well—” Archer rose, and glanced toward the window where the grey light seemed to be thinning. “Look here—the fog’s lifting. If we made a dash for the National Gallery we might manage to catch a glimpse of the pictures.”

They were on their way home after a three months’ wedding-tour which May, in letters to her girl friends, had vaguely summarised as “blissful.” They had not gone to the Italian Lakes; Archer, on reflection, could not picture his wife in that particular setting, where ruins and cypresses seemed to demand a more restless imagination than May owned. Her own inclinations—after a month with the Paris dressmakers—had been for mountaineering in July and swimming in August. This plan they punctually fulfilled, spending July at Interlaken and Grindelwald, and August at a little place called Étretat on the Normandy coast, recommended as quaint and quiet. There, the sea’s roar and the gulls’ cries entered their rooms like additional guests, and May—salt on her lips, hair damp from bathing—had been happiest in the blunt animal pleasure of motion, appetite, sleep. Once or twice in the mountains Archer had pointed southward and said, “There’s Italy”; and May, her feet sunk in a gentian-bed, had smiled cheerfully and replied: “It would be lovely to go there next winter, if only you didn’t have to be in New York.”

But in reality travelling interested her even less than he had expected. She regarded it—once her clothes were ordered—as merely an enlarged opportunity for walking, riding, swimming, and trying her hand at the fascinating new game of lawn tennis. When they finally got back to London, where they were to spend a fortnight while he ordered his clothes, she no longer concealed her eagerness to sail. In London nothing interested her but the shops and the theatres; and the theatres were less exciting than the Paris cafés chantants where, under blossoming horse-chestnuts on the Champs Élysées, she had had the novel experience of looking down from a restaurant terrace on an audience of cocottes, while her husband interpreted for her as much of the songs as he thought suitable for bridal ears. Even there, the music and laughter had seemed to Archer muffled by a sort of commercial clang, as if amusement itself were being hammered into a product; and he caught himself longing for quieter, older kinds of sound—voices unforced, a room where talk moved like a shared dish passed from hand to hand.

Archer had reverted to all his old inherited ideas about marriage. It was less trouble to conform to tradition and treat May exactly as all his friends treated their wives than to attempt the freer theories with which his untrammelled bachelorhood had once toyed. There was no use in trying to emancipate a wife who had not the dimmest notion that she was not free; and he had long since discovered that May’s only use of the liberty she supposed herself to possess would be to lay it on the altar of her wifely adoration. Her innate dignity would always keep her from making the gift abjectly; and a day might even come—as it once had—when she would find strength to take it back altogether if she thought she were doing it for his own good. But with a conception of marriage so uncomplicated and incurious as hers, such a crisis could be brought about only by something visibly outrageous in his conduct; and the fineness of her feeling for him made that unthinkable.

Whatever happened, he knew, she would always be loyal, gallant, unresentful—and that pledged him to the practice of the same virtues. All this drew him back into his old habits of mind. If her simplicity had been petty, he would have chafed; but since the lines of her character, though few, were on the same fine mould as her face, she became the tutelary divinity of his traditions and reverences. Such qualities were scarcely of a kind to enliven foreign travel—though they made her so easy, so pleasant a companion; but he saw at once how they would fall into place in their proper setting. He had no fear of being oppressed by them. His artistic and intellectual life would go on, as it always had, outside the domestic circle; and within it there would be nothing small and stifling. Coming back to May would never be like entering a stuffy room after a tramp in the open. And when they had children, the vacant corners in both their lives would be filled.

These things went through his mind during their long slow drive from Mayfair to South Kensington, where Mrs. Carfry and Miss Harle lived. Archer, too, would have preferred to escape his friends’ hospitality. In conformity with family tradition, he had always travelled as a sight-seer and looker-on, affecting a haughty unconsciousness of the presence of his fellow-beings. Once only, just after Harvard, he had spent a few gay weeks at Florence with a band of queer Europeanised Americans—dancing all night with titled ladies in palaces, gambling half the day with rakes and dandies at a fashionable club—but it had all seemed to him, though the greatest fun, as unreal as carnival. Those cosmopolitan women, steeped in complicated love-affairs they felt compelled to retail to every stranger, and the magnificent young officers and elderly dyed wits who were the recipients or subjects of those confidences, were too different from the people Archer had grown up among—too much like expensive, over-perfumed hot-house exotics, rich in scent but faintly malodorous, to detain his imagination.

To introduce his wife into such a society was out of the question; and in the course of his travels no other had shown marked eagerness for his company. Not long after their arrival in London he had run across the Duke of St. Austrey, who had instantly and cordially recognised him and said: “Look me up, won’t you?”—but no proper-spirited American would have considered that suggestion actionable, and the encounter had ended without sequel.

They had even contrived to avoid May’s English aunt, the banker’s wife in Yorkshire. Indeed they had postponed coming to London until autumn so that their arrival during the season might not appear pushing and snobbish to these unknown relatives. In the hansom May sat at his side spotlessly splendid in a sky-blue cloak edged with swansdown—so pure a piece of softness that it seemed wicked to expose it to London’s grime and coal breath.

“Probably there’ll be nobody at Mrs. Carfry’s—London’s a desert at this season, and you’ve made yourself much too beautiful,” Archer said, trying to lighten her apprehension.

“I don’t want them to think that we dress like savages,” she replied, with a scorn that Pocahontas might have resented; and again he was struck by the religious reverence of even the most unworldly American women for the social advantages of dress.

It’s their armour, he thought: their defence against the unknown—and their defiance of it. And he understood for the first time the earnestness with which May—who was incapable of tying a ribbon in her hair to charm him—had gone through the solemn rite of selecting and ordering her extensive wardrobe, as though garments were a dialect one must speak flawlessly when words failed.

He had been right in expecting the party to be small. Besides their hostess and her sister, in the long chilly drawing-room they found only another shawled lady, a genial Vicar who was her husband, a silent lad whom Mrs. Carfry named as her nephew, and a small dark gentleman with lively eyes whom she introduced as the nephew’s tutor, pronouncing a French name as she did so.

The room’s cold had a particular smell: damp wool, extinguished coal, potpourri that had long since lost its roses, and the faint metallic taint of London air that seeped in even through heavy curtains. The sounds, too, were muffled—voices absorbed by draperies, footsteps swallowed by carpet—so that every cough and the tick of the clock seemed unduly intimate.

Into this dimly lit group May floated like a swan with the sunset on her. She seemed larger, fairer, more voluminously rustling than Archer had ever seen her; and he perceived that the rosiness and rustlingness were tokens of extreme and infantile shyness. Her helpless eyes implored him—What on earth will they expect me to talk about?—at the very moment that her dazzling apparition was calling forth the same anxiety in their own bosoms.

But beauty, even distrustful of itself, awakens confidence in manly hearts; and the Vicar and the French-named tutor were soon manifesting to May their desire to put her at her ease. In spite of their best efforts, however, dinner languished. Archer noticed that May’s way of showing herself at ease with foreigners was to become more uncompromisingly local in her references, as though she could hold herself steady only by pressing her feet hard on the familiar ground of names and places that meant nothing to her listeners. The Vicar soon abandoned the struggle; but the tutor, who spoke fluent, accomplished English, gallantly continued to pour it out to her, as patiently as one bastes a roast that refuses to brown, until the ladies, to the manifest relief of all concerned, went up to the drawing-room.

The dining-room, once the women had left, seemed to exhale. Port was poured. The Vicar, after a glass, was obliged to hurry away to a meeting; the shy nephew, who appeared to be an invalid, was packed off to bed. The house fell into a quieter register, and Archer, left with the tutor, felt the air change in his lungs as if a window had opened on a different climate.

They continued to sit over their wine, and suddenly Archer found himself talking as he had not done since his last symposium with Ned Winsett. The Carfry nephew, it turned out, had been threatened with consumption and sent to Switzerland, where he had spent two years by Lake Leman. Being bookish, he had been entrusted to M. Rivière, who had brought him back to England and was to remain until the youth went up to Oxford in the spring. M. Rivière added, with a simplicity that made his poverty more naked than complaint, that he should then have to look for another job.

It seemed impossible to Archer that he should be long without one. He was about thirty, with a thin ugly face—May would certainly have called him common-looking—yet his ideas played over it with such intensity that the features became expressive, lit from within. There was nothing cheap in his animation. His father, dead young, had held a small diplomatic post; the son had been intended for the same career, but an insatiable taste for letters had thrown him into journalism, then into authorship (apparently unsuccessful), and at length—after other experiments and vicissitudes, which he spared his listener—into tutoring English youths in Switzerland.

Before that, however, he had lived much in Paris: frequented the Goncourts’ grenier, been advised by Maupassant not to attempt to write—an honour that dazzled Archer—and had often talked with Mérimée in his mother’s house. He had been desperately poor and anxious, with a mother and unmarried sister to provide for; his literary ambitions had failed. Materially his situation seemed no more brilliant than Winsett’s. But he had lived in a world where, as he said, no one who loved ideas need hunger mentally. Since it was precisely that love that poor Winsett was starving of, Archer looked with a sort of vicarious envy at this eager impecunious man who had fared so richly in his poverty.

“You see, Monsieur, it’s worth everything, isn’t it,” M. Rivière was saying, “to keep one’s intellectual liberty—not to enslave one’s powers of appreciation, one’s critical independence? It was because of that I abandoned journalism, and took to so much duller work: tutoring, private secretaryship. There is drudgery, of course; but one preserves one’s moral freedom—what we call in French one’s quant à soi. And when one hears good talk one can join in it without compromising any opinions but one’s own; or one can listen, and answer inwardly.” He paused to light another cigarette; the match flared, then died, leaving a thread of smoke that smelled sharply of sulphur and paper. “Ah, good conversation—there’s nothing like it, is there? The air of ideas is the only air worth breathing. And so I have never regretted giving up either diplomacy or journalism—two different forms of the same self-abdication.”

He fixed his vivid eyes on Archer. “Voyez-vous, Monsieur—to be able to look life in the face: that’s worth living in a garret for, isn’t it? But, after all, one must earn enough to pay for the garret. And I confess that to grow old as a private tutor—or a ‘private’ anything—is almost as chilling to the imagination as a second secretaryship at Bucharest.” He leaned forward, the question coming out as if it had been simmering for a long time and had at last boiled over. “Sometimes I feel I must make a plunge—an immense plunge. Do you suppose, for instance, there would be any opening for me in America—in New York?”

Archer looked at him, startled. New York—for a young man who had frequented the Goncourts and Flaubert, and who spoke of ideas as if they were bread and air. He stared, perplexed, wondering how to tell him that his very superiorities would be the surest hindrance to success.

“New York—New York—but must it be especially New York?” he stammered, utterly unable to imagine what lucrative opening his native city could offer to a man to whom good conversation seemed the only necessity.

A flush rose under M. Rivière’s sallow skin. “I—I thought it your metropolis: is not the intellectual life more active there?” Then, as if fearing he had asked a favour, he went on quickly: “One throws out random suggestions—more to oneself than to others. In reality, I see no immediate prospect—” He rose without constraint. “But Mrs. Carfry will think that I ought to be taking you upstairs.”

During the homeward drive Archer pondered. London’s night noises pressed in around the hansom—hooves striking wet stone, the distant, ceaseless grind of wheels, the occasional shout that ricocheted off brick and vanished. His hour with M. Rivière had put new air into his lungs, and his first impulse had been to invite him to dine the next day; but he was beginning to understand why married men did not always immediately yield to their first impulses.

“That young tutor is an interesting fellow,” he threw out tentatively. “We had some awfully good talk after dinner—about books and things.”

May roused herself from one of the dreamy silences into which he had once read so many meanings, before six months of marriage had given him the key to them.

“The little Frenchman? Wasn’t he dreadfully common?” she asked coldly; and Archer guessed she nursed a secret disappointment at having been invited out in London only to meet a clergyman and a French tutor.

The disappointment was not what is ordinarily called snobbishness. It was old New York’s sense of what was due to it when it risked its dignity abroad. If May’s parents had entertained the Carfrys in Fifth Avenue, they would have offered them something more substantial than a parson and a schoolmaster.

Archer, on edge, took her up. “Common—common where?”

“Why, I should say anywhere but in his school-room. Those people are always awkward in society.” Then, disarmingly: “But then I suppose I shouldn’t have known if he was clever.”

Archer disliked her use of “clever” almost as much as “common”; but he was beginning to fear his tendency to dwell on what he disliked in her. After all, her point of view had always been the same: that of all the people he had grown up among. He had always regarded it as necessary but negligible. Until a few months ago he had never known a “nice” woman who looked at life differently; and if a man married, it must be among the nice.

“Ah—then I won’t ask him to dine!” he concluded with a laugh; and May echoed, bewildered: “Goodness—ask the Carfrys’ tutor?”

“Well, not on the same day with the Carfrys, if you prefer I shouldn’t. But I did rather want another talk with him. He’s looking for a job in New York.”

Her surprise increased with her indifference; he almost fancied she suspected him of being tainted with foreignness.

“A job in New York? What sort of a job? People don’t have French tutors: what does he want to do?”

“Chiefly to enjoy good conversation, I understand,” her husband retorted perversely; and she broke into an appreciative laugh.

“Oh, Newland, how funny! Isn’t that French?”

On the whole, he was glad to have the matter settled by her refusing to take seriously his wish to invite M. Rivière. Another after-dinner talk would have made it difficult to avoid the question of New York; and the more Archer considered it the less he could fit M. Rivière into any conceivable picture of New York as he knew it—a city whose dominant lexicon could label a man “common” and be done with him, as though a single word were a verdict and not merely an accent.

He perceived, with a flash of chilling insight, that in future many problems would be thus negatively solved for him; but as he paid the hansom and followed his wife’s long train into the house, he took refuge in the comforting platitude that the first six months were always the most difficult in marriage. After that, he told himself, they would have finished rubbing off each other’s angles—only the worst of it was that May’s pressure was already bearing on the very angles whose sharpness he most wanted to keep.

The small bright lawn ran like a green ribbon to the big bright sea.

It had been clipped and rolled until it looked edible—like some sugared confection laid flat for a fête—its edges stitched with scarlet geranium and dark coleus. Along the curving path that dipped toward the cliff, cast-iron vases—painted a glossy chocolate-brown—lifted looping garlands of petunia and ivy geranium. Even the gravel underfoot had been combed into obedient lines, as if a fastidious cook had dragged a fork through a tray of flour to make it ready for dough.

Halfway between the cliff’s edge and the square wooden house (also chocolate-coloured, with a verandah roof striped in yellow and brown to mimic an awning) two large targets stood against a thick screen of shrubs. Opposite them, facing the targets, a real tent had been pitched; benches and garden-seats were scattered around it like place-settings at an outdoor table. Ladies in summer dresses and gentlemen in grey frock-coats and tall hats stood about or sat and leaned in murmured conversation; and every now and then a slender girl in starched muslin would step out from the tent, bow in hand, and send her arrow singing toward the painted rings. Talk would pause, as it always does when a spoon hovers over a boiling pot; there would be that small, sharpened silence; then the soft sound of approval or disappointment would ripple across the lawn.

Newland Archer stood on the Beaufort verandah and looked down as if he had never seen such a scene before. On either side of the shiny painted steps, large blue china flower-pots perched on bright yellow stands; each pot held a spiky green plant arranged with the stiff emphasis of a centrepiece. Below the verandah ran a wide border of blue hydrangeas edged again with geraniums, as though the whole place were garnished in red to make the blue seem bluer. Behind him, through the French windows he had crossed, he caught glimpses—between swaying lace curtains—of parquet floors polished to a hard, glassy sheen; chintz poufs and dwarf armchairs sat like carefully placed dishes, and velvet tables were crowded with silver trifles whose only use was to glitter.

The Newport Archery Club always held its August meeting at the Beauforts’. The bow and arrow, once rivalled only by croquet, was beginning to be displaced by lawn-tennis; but tennis was still judged too rough for occasions meant to display delicate dresses and disciplined attitudes. For a social afternoon, the clean, controlled arc of an arrow—its brief whirr, its neat arrival—was preferable to the slap and scuffle of rackets.

Archer watched and wondered at the familiarity of it all. What unsettled him was not the spectacle but the fact that it continued unchanged while his own sense of it had altered so profoundly. Newport was where he felt the change most sharply. In New York, last winter, after he and May had settled into the new greenish-yellow house with the bow-window and Pompeian vestibule, he had dropped back into the routine of the office with an almost physical relief, as if the rhythm of his days had returned his body to its old posture. There had been the pleasing absorption of choosing a showy grey stepper for May’s brougham (the Wellands had given the carriage), and the steady satisfaction of arranging his new library as he had dreamed—dark embossed paper, Eastlake bookcases, and those “sincere” armchairs and tables which his family viewed with doubt. At the Century he had found Winsett again; at the Knickerbocker, the fashionable young men of his set; and between the hours given to law and those spent dining out or entertaining at home—with now and then an evening at the Opera or the theatre—his life had seemed, if not full, at least inevitable.

Newport, however, was an escape from duty into a holiday atmosphere so unmitigated that it left no corner for refuge. He had tried to persuade May to spend the summer on a remote island off the Maine coast—Mount Desert—where a few hardy Bostonians and Philadelphians were camped in “native” cottages, and where reports spoke of enchanting scenery and a wild, almost trapper-like existence amid woods and waters. He imagined quiet there: the true sounds of place—wind in firs, oars dipping, the hush after sunset—rather than the endless churn of wheels on gravel, the constant calling of names, the restless social din that Newport produced as naturally as a kitchen produces clatter when too many hands insist on stirring the same pot.

But the Wellands always went to Newport. They owned one of the square boxes on the cliffs, and their son-in-law could find no respectable reason to refuse joining them. Mrs. Welland, with her mild tartness, pointed out that it was hardly worth while for May to have worn herself out trying on summer clothes in Paris if she was not to be allowed to wear them; and Archer had not yet learned how to answer that kind of argument.

May herself could not understand his reluctance. She reminded him that he had always liked Newport as a bachelor. Since that was true, he could only reply that he was sure he would like it better than ever now that they were there together. Yet as he stood on the Beaufort verandah and looked over the brightly peopled lawn, it came home to him with a shiver that he was not going to like it at all.

It was not May’s fault. If, during their travels, they had once or twice fallen slightly out of step, harmony returned as soon as they were back among the conditions she understood. He had always foreseen that she would not disappoint him, and she had not. He had married—as most young men did—because he had met a perfectly charming girl at the moment when a series of rather aimless sentimental adventures were curdling into early disgust; and she had represented peace, stability, comradeship, and the steadying pressure of an unescapable duty.

He could not say he had been mistaken. It was gratifying to be the husband of one of the handsomest and most popular young married women in New York, when she was also one of the sweetest-tempered and most reasonable of wives; he was not insensible to those advantages. And that moment of madness on the eve of his marriage—he had trained himself to think of it as the last of his abandoned experiments. The idea that he could ever, in his senses, have dreamed of marrying the Countess Olenska had become almost unthinkable. In memory she had turned into the most plaintive and poignant of a line of ghosts.

But these neat eliminations left his mind emptier than he had expected, hollowed out by all the things it refused to name. Perhaps that was why the busy, animated people on the Beaufort lawn shocked him as if they were children playing in a graveyard, their laughter and murmurs rising and falling like the rattle of spoons on china when there was no hunger left.

A murmur of skirts brushed his side, and the Marchioness Manson fluttered out through the drawing-room window. As usual she was extravagantly festooned—her limp Leghorn hat anchored with many windings of faded gauze, and a small black velvet parasol with a carved ivory handle absurdly balanced above the larger brim.

“My dear Newland, I had no idea you and May had arrived! You yourself came only yesterday, you say? Ah, business—business—professional duties… I understand. Many husbands, I know, find it impossible to join their wives here except for the week-end.” She cocked her head and languished at him through screwed-up eyes. “But marriage is one long sacrifice, as I used often to remind my Ellen—”

Archer’s heart gave the queer jerk it had given once before, as if a door had slammed between him and the outer world; yet the break must have been no more than a blink, for he found himself listening to Medora answering a question he had apparently asked.

“No, I am not staying here, but with the Blenkers, in their delicious solitude at Portsmouth. Beaufort was kind enough to send his famous trotters for me this morning, so that I might have at least a glimpse of one of Regina’s garden-parties; but this evening I go back to rural life. The Blenkers, dear original beings, have hired a primitive old farm-house at Portsmouth where they gather about them representative people…” She drooped a little beneath her brim, and added with a faint blush: “This week Dr. Agathon Carver is holding a series of Inner Thought meetings there. A contrast indeed to this gay scene of worldly pleasure—but then I have always lived on contrasts! To me the only death is monotony. I always say to Ellen: Beware of monotony; it’s the mother of all the deadly sins. But my poor child is going through a phase of exaltation, of abhorrence of the world. You know, I suppose, that she has declined all invitations to stay at Newport, even with her grandmother Mingott? I could hardly persuade her to come with me to the Blenkers’, if you will believe it! The life she leads is morbid, unnatural. Ah, if she had only listened to me when it was still possible… When the door was still open…” She lifted her parasol an inch, as if to usher in a new subject. “But shall we go down and watch this absorbing match? I hear your May is one of the competitors.”

Beaufort himself came striding toward them from the tent—tall, heavy, too tightly buttoned into a London frock-coat, with one of his own orchids in his buttonhole. Archer, who had not seen him for two or three months, was struck by the change in him. In the hard summer light Beaufort’s floridness looked thick and bloated; but for his erect square-shouldered walk, he might have passed for an over-fed, over-dressed old man.

Rumours clung to Beaufort like the rich scent of his hothouse flowers. In spring he had gone on a long cruise to the West Indies in his new steam-yacht, and it was reported that at various ports a lady resembling Miss Fanny Ring had been seen in his company. The steam-yacht—built in the Clyde, fitted with tiled bathrooms and unheard-of luxuries—was said to have cost half a million; and the pearl necklace he brought back for his wife had the splendour of such expiatory gifts. His fortune was substantial enough, and yet the uneasy whispers persisted in Fifth Avenue drawing-rooms and in Wall Street offices. Some said he had speculated badly in railways; others that he was being bled by one of the most insatiable members of her profession. To every hint of threatened insolvency he answered with a fresh extravagance: new orchid-houses, a new string of racehorses, another Meissonnier or Cabanel for his gallery.

He greeted them with his usual half-sneer. “Hullo, Medora! Did the trotters do their business? Forty minutes, eh? … Well, that’s not so bad, considering your nerves had to be spared.” He shook hands with Archer and then, turning back with them, placed himself on Mrs. Manson’s other side and said a few low words which Medora did not catch.

She replied with one of her foreign jerks and a “_Que voulez-vous?_” that deepened Beaufort’s frown; but he produced a congratulatory smile as he glanced at Archer. “You know May’s going to carry off the first prize.”

“Ah, then it remains in the family,” Medora rippled; and at that moment they reached the tent, and Mrs. Beaufort met them in a girlish cloud of mauve muslin and floating veils.

May Welland was just coming out of the tent. In her white dress, with a pale green ribbon around her waist and a wreath of ivy on her hat, she had the same Diana-like aloofness Archer remembered from the Beaufort ballroom the night of their engagement. In the interval, it seemed, not a thought had passed behind her eyes nor a feeling through her heart; and though Archer knew she was capable of both, he marvelled again at the way experience slid off her, leaving no mark—as water slips from a waxed fruit.

She held bow and arrow, and stepping to the chalk-mark on the turf, lifted the bow to her shoulder and took aim. The attitude had such classic grace that a murmur of appreciation followed her. Archer felt again that warm glow of proprietorship that so often tricked him into momentary content. Her rivals—Mrs. Reggie Chivers, the Merry girls, and various rosy Thorleys, Dagonets, and Mingotts—stood behind in a lovely anxious cluster, brown heads and golden bent above the scores, pale muslins and flower-wreathed hats mingling like a soft-coloured salad of summer. All were young and pretty, all steeped in the season; but none had May’s nymph-like ease when, muscles tense and brow happily knit, she put her whole being into the small feat before her.

“Gad,” Archer heard Lawrence Lefferts say, “not one of the lot holds the bow as she does”; and Beaufort retorted: “Yes; but that’s the only kind of target she’ll ever hit.”

Archer felt irrationally angry. The contempt in Beaufort’s praise—his coarse tribute to May’s “niceness”—was precisely the sort of remark a husband might be expected to welcome. That such a man found her unalluring ought to have been proof of her quality. Yet the words sent a faint shiver through Archer. What if “niceness,” carried to the supreme degree, were only a negation—a curtain dropped before emptiness? As he watched May return, flushed and calm from her final bull’s-eye, he had the old, chilling sense that he had never once lifted that curtain.

She accepted congratulations with the simplicity that was her crowning grace. No one could be jealous of her triumphs, because she gave the impression she would have been as serene had she missed. But when her eyes met Archer’s, her face brightened with the pleasure she saw reflected there.

Mrs. Welland’s basket-work pony-carriage waited, and they drove off among dispersing vehicles—May taking the reins, Archer at her side.

The afternoon light still lingered over lawns and shrubberies; up and down Bellevue Avenue rolled a double line of victorias, dog-carts, landaus and “vis-à-vis,” carrying well-dressed people away from the Beaufort garden-party or home from their daily turn along the Ocean Drive. Newport’s soundscape was its own kind of tide: wheels crunching, hooves striking, voices calling, laughter lifted and dropped—an incessant social surf that left no room for the quieter notes Archer had once imagined he needed to hear himself think.

“Shall we go to see Granny?” May proposed suddenly. “I should like to tell her myself that I’ve won the prize. There’s lots of time before dinner.”

Archer agreed, and May turned the ponies down Narragansett Avenue, crossed Spring Street, and drove out toward the rocky moorland beyond. In this unfashionable region Catherine the Great—indifferent to precedent and thrifty of purse—had long ago built herself a many-peaked, cross-beamed _cottage-orné_ on cheap land overlooking the bay. In a thicket of stunted oaks, her verandahs spread above island-dotted waters. A winding drive rose between iron stags and blue glass balls embedded in mounds of geraniums to a front door of highly varnished walnut beneath a striped verandah-roof. Inside ran a narrow hall with a black-and-yellow star-patterned parquet floor. Four small square rooms opened off it, their heavy flock-papers pressing close under ceilings where an Italian painter had lavished all the divinities of Olympus.

One room had been made into a bedroom when the burden of flesh descended on Mrs. Mingott, and in the adjoining room she spent her days enthroned in a large armchair between door and window, perpetually waving a palm-leaf fan. Her prodigious bosom held the fan so far from the rest of her person that the air it made stirred only the fringe of the anti-macassars on the chair-arms, leaving the rest of the room to the warm, slow breaths of late summer and the faint saltiness that drifted up from the bay.

Since she had been the means of hastening his marriage, old Catherine had shown Archer the cordiality that a service rendered excites toward the person served. Convinced that irrepressible passion had driven his impatience, and being an ardent admirer of impulsiveness when it did not involve spending money, she always received him with a genial twinkle and a complicity of allusion to which May seemed, fortunately, impervious.

She examined the diamond-tipped arrow pinned on May’s bosom—the prize from the match—turning it with appreciative fingers. In her day, she remarked, a filigree brooch would have been thought enough; but there was no denying Beaufort did things handsomely.

“Quite an heirloom, in fact, my dear,” she chuckled. “You must leave it in fee to your eldest girl.” She pinched May’s white arm and watched the colour rise. “Well, well—what have I said to make you shake out the red flag? Ain’t there going to be any daughters—only boys, eh? Good gracious—look at her blushing again all over her blushes! What—can’t I say that either? Mercy me—when my children beg me to have all those gods and goddesses painted out overhead I always say I’m too thankful to have somebody about me that _nothing_ can shock!”

Archer laughed outright, and May echoed him, crimson to the eyes.

“Well, now tell me all about the party, please, my dears, for I shall never get a straight word about it out of that silly Medora,” the old lady continued; and when May said: “Cousin Medora? But I thought she was going back to Portsmouth?” Mrs. Mingott answered placidly: “So she is—but she’s got to come here first to pick up Ellen. Ah—you didn’t know Ellen had come to spend the day with me? Such fol-de-rol, her not coming for the summer; but I gave up arguing with young people about fifty years ago. Ellen—_Ellen_!” she called in her shrill old voice, trying to bend forward enough to glimpse the lawn beyond the verandah.

No answer came. Mrs. Mingott rapped her stick impatiently on the shiny floor. A mulatto maid in a bright turban appeared and told her mistress she had seen “Miss Ellen” going down the path to the shore.

Mrs. Mingott turned to Archer. “Run down and fetch her, like a good grandson; this pretty lady will describe the party to me,” she said; and Archer stood up as if in a dream.

He had heard the Countess Olenska’s name often enough in the year and a half since they last met; he knew the main incidents too. She had spent the previous summer at Newport and seemed to have gone a great deal into society; then in autumn she had abruptly sublet the “perfect house” Beaufort had found for her and decided to establish herself in Washington. During the winter Archer had heard of her—as one always heard of a pretty woman in Washington—as shining in the “brilliant diplomatic society” meant to compensate for the Administration’s social shortcomings. He had listened to these reports, and to contradictory accounts of her appearance, her conversation, her point of view and her choice of friends, with the detachment one gives to stories about someone long dead. Not until Medora spoke her name at the archery match had Ellen become living again. The Marchioness’s foolish lisp called up, with cruel precision, the small fire-lit drawing-room and the sound of carriage-wheels returning down a deserted street.

He thought of a story he had read—peasant children in Tuscany lighting a bunch of straw in a wayside cavern and revealing, on tomb walls, old painted faces that had waited in silence. So a name, spoken carelessly in the midst of a party, could flare up and show him what he had tried not to see.

The path to the shore descended from the bank where the cottage perched to a walk above the water planted with weeping willows. Through their veils Archer caught the glint of Lime Rock, with its whitewashed turret and the tiny house where the heroic lighthouse keeper Ida Lewis lived out her venerable years. Beyond lay the flat reaches and ugly government chimneys of Goat Island; the bay spread northward in a shimmer of gold to Prudence Island with its low growth of oaks, and the shores of Conanicut lay faint in sunset haze.

From the willow walk a slight wooden pier projected, ending in a pagoda-like summerhouse. In the pagoda a lady stood leaning against the rail, her back to the shore. Archer stopped as though waking from sleep. That vision of the past, he told himself, belonged to a dream; reality was the house on the bank above—Mrs. Welland’s pony-carriage circling the oval at the door; May sitting under shameless Olympians, glowing with secret hopes; the Welland villa at the far end of Bellevue Avenue; Mr. Welland already dressed for dinner, pacing the drawing-room with watch in hand and dyspeptic impatience. In such houses one always knew exactly what was happening at a given hour—each sound and movement measured like a recipe repeated until no one remembered it had ever been invented.

“What am I? A son-in-law—” Archer thought.

The figure at the end of the pier did not move. He stood halfway down the bank, staring at the bay furrowed by the coming and going of sailboats, yacht-launches, fishing-craft, and the trailing black coal-barges hauled by noisy tugs. The tugboats’ engines chopped the air and water into an industrial harshness that seemed to saw at his nerves; their noise made the gentler sounds—ripples, wind in willow leaves—retreat as if they were shy creatures driven back into cover. The lady in the summerhouse appeared held by the same sight, motionless as a fixed point in a shifting field.

Beyond the grey bastions of Fort Adams, a long-drawn sunset splintered into a thousand fires; the radiance caught the sail of a catboat as it beat out through the channel between Lime Rock and the shore. Watching it, Archer remembered a scene in _The Shaughraun_, Montague lifting Ada Dyas’s ribbon to his lips while she did not know he was in the room.

“She doesn’t know—she hasn’t guessed. Shouldn’t I know if she came up behind me, I wonder?” he thought; and suddenly he said to himself: “If she doesn’t turn before that sail crosses the Lime Rock light I’ll go back.”

The catboat slid on the receding tide. It passed before Lime Rock, blotted out Ida Lewis’s little house, and crossed the turret where the light was hung. Archer waited until a wide space of water sparkled between the last reef and the stern of the boat; still the figure in the summerhouse did not turn.

He turned and walked up the hill.

“I’m sorry you didn’t find Ellen—I should have liked to see her again,” May said as they drove home through the dusk. “But perhaps she wouldn’t have cared—she seems so changed.”

“Changed?” Archer repeated in a colourless voice, eyes fixed on the ponies’ twitching ears.

“So indifferent to her friends, I mean; giving up New York and her house, and spending her time with such queer people. Fancy how hideously uncomfortable she must be at the Blenkers’! She says she does it to keep cousin Medora out of mischief: to prevent her marrying dreadful people. But I sometimes think we’ve always bored her.”

Archer said nothing. The carriage wheels made a steady rustle over the road; somewhere a late cricket began its thin, persistent note, the one sincere sound in a world of polite noise.

May went on, and there was a tinge of hardness in her frank fresh voice that he had never noticed before. “After all, I wonder if she wouldn’t be happier with her husband.”

Archer burst into a laugh. “_Sancta simplicitas!_” he exclaimed; and when she turned a puzzled frown on him, he added: “I don’t think I ever heard you say a cruel thing before.”

“Cruel?”

“Well—watching the contortions of the damned is supposed to be a favourite sport of the angels; but I believe even they don’t think people happier in hell.”

“It’s a pity she ever married abroad then,” May said in the placid tone with which her mother met Mr. Welland’s vagaries; and Archer felt himself gently, firmly, put back among the unreasonable husbands.

They drove down Bellevue Avenue and turned in between the chamfered wooden gate-posts topped with cast-iron lamps that marked the approach to the Welland villa. Lights already shone through the windows, and as the carriage stopped Archer caught a glimpse of his father-in-law exactly as he had pictured him—pacing the drawing-room floor, watch in hand, wearing the pained expression he had long since found to be far more efficacious than anger.

Mr. Welland laid down his knife and fork as if the silver had suddenly turned too heavy for his hands. Across the luncheon-table his wife, having adjusted her gold eye-glasses with a practised click, held up a card and read it aloud in the tone she reserved for the social grotesque.

“Professor and Mrs. Emerson Sillerton request the pleasure of Mr. and Mrs. Welland’s company at the meeting of the Wednesday Afternoon Club on August 25th at 3 o’clock punctually. To meet Mrs. and the Misses Blenker. Red Gables, Catherine Street. R.S.V.P.”

“Good gracious—” Mr. Welland breathed, as though the words were a draught too sharp for his throat.

“Poor Amy Sillerton,” Mrs. Welland sighed, her voice smoothing itself into pity that had long ago become a habit of the table, like passing the bread. “You never can tell what her husband will do next. I suppose he’s just discovered the Blenkers.”

Emerson Sillerton had always been spoken of in Newport as one speaks of a persistent burr caught in good cloth: not dangerous, only irritating—and impossible to remove because the fabric itself was venerable. Everyone agreed he had had “every advantage.” Wealth, position, the correct alliances, the right names on both sides—there was nothing, as Mrs. Welland was fond of observing, that obliged him to become an archaeologist, or indeed a Professor of any sort, or to live in Newport in winter, or to fill a house with long-haired men and short-haired women whose voices carried, untempered, through open windows. And if he chose to affront tradition, why—why must he have done it to poor Amy Dagonet, who had a right to expect something different, and money enough for her own carriage and the soothing clip-clop of familiar horses in the drive?

Newport’s old families endured the Sillerton-Pennilow-Dagonet connection as they endured certain winds: by drawing lots and sending one reluctant representative to the Sillertons’ annual garden-party, to stand among untidy flower-beds and hear conversation that did not pause politely at the edge of acceptable subjects. Mrs. Welland, who liked her afternoons to proceed as evenly as a well-made sauce, remembered every affront by date.

“It’s a wonder,” she remarked, “that they didn’t choose Cup Race day. Do you remember, two years ago, their giving a party for a black man on the day of Julia Mingott’s thé dansant? Luckily this time there’s nothing else going on that I know of—for of course some of us will have to go.”

Mr. Welland’s fingers worried the edge of his napkin. “ ‘Some of us,’ my dear—more than one? Three o’clock is such a very awkward hour. I have to be here at half-past three to take my drops. It’s really no use trying to follow Bencomb’s new treatment if I don’t do it systematically; and if I join you later, of course I shall miss my drive.”

The thought flushed his finely wrinkled cheek with the anxious pink of a man whose body had become, in its small requirements, an exacting household god.
