“There’s no reason why you should go at all,” Mrs. Welland returned, cheerfulness snapping into place like a napkin-ring. “I have some cards to leave at the other end of Bellevue Avenue, and I’ll drop in at about half-past three and stay long enough to make poor Amy feel she hasn’t been slighted.” Her eyes moved, after a slight hesitation, to her daughter. “And if Newland’s afternoon is provided for perhaps May can drive you out with the ponies, and try their new russet harness.”

To be “provided for” was, in the Welland household, as necessary as having the lamb properly carved or the coffee poured at the right minute. Mrs. Welland dreaded unfilled hours as she dreaded bad drainage: something unhealthy, something that produced disorders in the mind. Yet she also believed, with the same firmness, that parents should not visibly interfere with the plans of their married children. Her ingenuity, therefore, was spent in arranging days like trays of carefully separated sandwiches—each element in its place, no jostling, no empty gaps.

“Of course I’ll drive with Papa,” May said, with the gentle firmness that reminded her husband that he had not spoken. “I’m sure Newland will find something to do.”

It had distressed Mrs. Welland, during the fortnight of his stay, to discover that Newland Archer did not look ahead. When asked how he meant to spend an afternoon he answered with that perverse lightness she never knew how to meet: “Oh, I think for a change I’ll just save it instead of spending it—” And once, when she and May had been obliged to make a long-postponed round of calls, he had confessed to having lain all afternoon under a rock on the beach below the house, as if time were something one could cool on stone and salt air.

May had defended him with her serene logic. “No; but you see it doesn’t matter, because when there’s nothing particular to do he reads a book.”

“Ah, yes—like his father!” Mrs. Welland had agreed, consoling herself with heredity, as one excuses an odd flavour in a familiar dish by remembering the cook’s native province. After that, the subject was dropped; but as the day of the Sillerton reception approached, May’s conscience began to prickle at the thought of deserting him to an unprovided-for afternoon, and she proposed tennis at the Chiverses’, or a sail on Julius Beaufort’s cutter—small compensations offered with a wife’s desire to keep the air around her marriage clear of dissonance.

“I shall be back by six, you know, dear: Papa never drives later than that—” she reminded him, and seemed not fully reassured till Archer announced, with a show of foresight, that he thought of hiring a runabout and driving up the island to a stud-farm to look at a second horse for her brougham.

They had been looking for this horse for some time. The idea pleased May so much that she glanced at her mother as if to say: You see—he can plan out his time as well as any of us.

But Archer’s plan had germinated the very day he had heard of the Sillertons’ invitation, and he had kept it secret with the odd caution of a man carrying something contraband in his pocket. He had even engaged in advance a runabout with a pair of old livery-stable trotters—stout, seasoned creatures that could still do their eighteen miles on level roads if not pressed too hard. At two o’clock he hastily deserted the luncheon-table and sprang into the light carriage.

The day itself had that clean brightness which makes every object seem newly washed. A north breeze drove little puffs of white cloud across an ultramarine sky; below it the sea ran in bright ridges, as if it had been combed. Bellevue Avenue lay empty at that hour. After dropping the stable-lad at the corner of Mill Street, Archer turned down the Old Beach Road and drove across Eastman’s Beach, the wheels running with a soft crunch where sand had strayed onto the track. He felt the old, schoolboy excitement of a half-holiday—time unlatched, opening into the unknown.

He took his pair at an easy gait. He meant to reach the stud-farm beyond Paradise Rocks before three; then, after looking over the horse and perhaps trying him, he would still have four hours—four bright coins of time—to spend as he chose.

When he had first heard of the Sillertons’ party he had instantly said to himself that the Marchioness Manson would certainly come to Newport with the Blenkers, and that Madame Olenska might take the opportunity of spending the day with her grandmother. In that case the Blenkers’ house would likely be deserted, and he could, without indiscretion, satisfy a curiosity that had become, without his willing it, a kind of hunger.

He was not sure he wanted to see the Countess again. Yet ever since he had looked at her from the path above the bay he had wanted—irrationally, insistently—to see the place she lived in, to know the exact arrangement of earth and sky and water that held her, to be able to follow, in imagination, the movements of her figure in its daily setting. It was not so much her voice he craved—he told himself he did not even wish to speak to her—as the sense of her existence made tangible: the chair she might sit in, the window she might look from, the air that touched her hair. The longing was like the sick man’s sudden whim for a particular taste once known: something remembered by the body more than by the mind.

At the stud-farm a glance told him the horse was not what he wanted. Nevertheless he took a turn behind it, as if to prove to himself that he was not in a hurry, that he could be deliberate, normal. At three he shook out the reins over his trotters and turned into the by-roads leading to Portsmouth.

The wind had dropped. A faint haze lay on the horizon, the first sign of a fog waiting to creep up the Saconnet with the turn of tide. All around him the fields and woods were steeped in golden light. He drove past grey-shingled farm-houses sunk in orchards where late apples reddened, past hay-fields giving off the sun-warmed smell of cut grass, past oak-groves where the leaves made a low, steady whisper like a careful stirring with a wooden spoon. In the villages he passed, white steeples rose sharply into the fading blue, and from open doorways came the muted clang of pans, the far-off thud of a chopping block, domestic sounds that seemed to hold the day together better than any sermon.

At last, after stopping to ask the way of men at work in a field—voices carried on the even air, unhurried, as if the land itself set the tempo—he turned down a lane between high banks of goldenrod and brambles. At the end of the lane was the blue glimmer of river. To the left, in front of a clump of oaks and maples, stood a long tumble-down house, white paint peeling from its clapboards like dried icing flaking from an old cake.

Facing the gateway on the roadside stood one of the open sheds in which the New Englander shelters farming implements and where visitors hitch their teams. Archer jumped down, led his pair into the shade of it, tied them to a post, and turned toward the house.

The patch of lawn before it had relapsed into a hay-field. But to the left an overgrown box-garden, edged with the stubborn green of old hedges, held dahlias too large and heavy for their stems and rose-bushes gone rusty and wild. In it, like a relic of a forgotten fête, stood a ghostly summer-house of trellis-work that had once been white. Above it was a wooden Cupid who had lost his bow and arrow but still leaned forward in eternal, ineffectual aim.

Archer leaned against the gate a while. No one was in sight. No sound came from the open windows: no laughter, no footfall, no clink of china—only the soft insect-saw of late summer and, farther off, the river’s thin lap against reeds. A grizzled Newfoundland dozed before the door, as ineffective a guardian as the arrowless Cupid. It was strange to think this silence and decay belonged to the turbulent Blenkers, yet Archer was sure he had found the place.

For a long time he stood there, content to take in the scene, falling gradually under its drowsy spell; and then he roused himself to the sense of time slipping through his fingers. Should he look his fill and drive away? Suddenly he wanted to see inside, to fix the interior in his mind—to be able to say: this is the room she sits in. There was nothing to prevent his walking up to the door and ringing. If she was away, as he supposed, he could give his name and ask leave to go into a sitting-room to write a message.

Instead, he crossed the rough grass and turned toward the box-garden. As he entered it he caught sight of something bright-coloured in the summer-house, and a moment later made it out to be a pink parasol.

It drew him as if by a physical pull. He was sure it was hers. He went into the summer-house and sat down on the rickety seat. He picked up the silken thing and examined the carved handle, made of some rare wood that, when warmed in his hand, gave out a faint aromatic scent, like spice stored long in a closed tin. The smell rose intimately, as if it belonged to skin and hair, to drawers of folded linen, to a woman’s private world.

He lifted the handle to his lips.

A rustle of skirts brushed the box hedge. Archer sat motionless, leaning on the parasol handle with his clasped hands, letting the sound come nearer without raising his eyes. He had always known that this must happen—

“Oh, Mr. Archer!” exclaimed a loud young voice.

He looked up and saw before him the youngest and largest of the Blenker girls, blonde and blowsy, in bedraggled muslin. A red blotch on one cheek showed where it had lately been pressed against a pillow; her eyes, half-awakened, stared at him with a hospitable confusion.

“Gracious—where did you drop from? I must have been sound asleep in the hammock. Everybody else has gone to Newport. Did you ring?” she asked incoherently, as if he might have walked straight through the trellis like a character in one of the plays her parents admired.

Archer’s confusion was greater than hers. “I—no—that is, I was just going to. I had to come up the island to see about a horse, and I drove over on a chance of finding Mrs. Blenker and your visitors. But the house seemed empty—so I sat down to wait.”

Miss Blenker, shaking off sleep, looked at him with increasing interest. “The house is empty. Mother’s not here, or the Marchioness—or anybody but me.” Her glance took on a faint reproach. “Didn’t you know Professor and Mrs. Sillerton are giving a garden-party for mother and all of us this afternoon? It was too unlucky I couldn’t go; but I’ve had a sore throat, and mother was afraid of the drive home this evening. Did you ever know anything so disappointing? Of course,” she added gaily, “I shouldn’t have minded half as much if I’d known you were coming.”

A lumbering coquetry he could not return began to show itself in her pose, and Archer, feeling trapped by the pink silk in his hand, found strength to break in: “But Madame Olenska—has she gone to Newport too?”

Miss Blenker stared. “Madame Olenska—didn’t you know she’d been called away?”

“Called away?—”

“Oh, my best parasol!” she cried suddenly, as if the object itself had only now come into focus. “I lent it to that goose of a Katie, because it matched her ribbons, and the careless thing must have dropped it here. We Blenkers are all like that… real Bohemians!”

She recovered the sunshade with a powerful hand, unfurled it, and lifted its rosy dome above her head, the silk catching the light and turning the shade into a small, moving piece of sunset.

“Yes—Ellen was called away yesterday,” she continued, already wandering off into pleasure at her own news. “She lets us call her Ellen, you know. A telegram came from Boston: she said she might be gone for two days. I do love the way she does her hair, don’t you?”

Archer continued to stare through her as though she were transparent. All he saw was the trumpery parasol arched in pink above her giggling head, and all he heard was the absence where another voice ought to have been. In an instant his mind seemed to unroll a long strip of future—day after day laid out like a table set without variation—and on it he saw himself dwindling, moving farther and farther away, the man to whom nothing was ever to happen.

He glanced about at the unpruned garden, the tumble-down house, the oak-grove where dusk was thickening. It had seemed exactly the place where he ought to have found Madame Olenska. And she was far away. Even the pink parasol was not hers.

He frowned, hesitated, and then said, as if testing the words on his tongue: “You don’t know, I suppose—I shall be in Boston tomorrow. If I could manage to see her—” It was half an appeal, half a confession, and he hated that he had to make it to this girl with sleep still clinging to her face.

Miss Blenker’s interest wavered, though her smile persisted with good nature. “Oh, of course; how lovely of you! She’s staying at the Parker House; it must be horrible there in this weather.”

After that Archer was only intermittently aware of what passed between them. He remembered, as through a mist, stoutly resisting her entreaty that he should wait for the returning family and have high tea with them before driving home. The word “tea” brought before him for an instant the smell of toast and weak China tea, the clatter of cups, voices in a room already tired with the day; it was unbearable. At last, with his hostess still lingering at his side, he walked out of range of the wooden Cupid, unfastened his horses, and drove off.

At the turn of the lane he looked back. Miss Blenker stood at the gate waving the pink parasol, its bright curve bobbing against the dimming green like a signal no one had meant to send. The river glimmered beyond her; behind her the old house kept its silence, and the only sound Archer carried away was the steady, familiar beat of hooves on hardening road, as though the island itself were counting out, in measured strokes, the hours he could not keep from slipping.

The next morning, when Archer stepped down from the Fall River train, Boston rose up around him like a kettle left too long to simmer: the air thick, the light white and hard, the whole city sweating through its clothes. Near the station the smells struck first—beer yeasting sour in open-door saloons, coffee burned and re-brewed, fruit gone soft in barrels and carts, the sweet rot of bruised skins. Men in shirtsleeves moved with the unselfconscious sprawl of people who share hallways and wash-basins; the clatter of wheels on stones and the metallic cough of a distant streetcar seemed to scrape at his temples. Even the sound had heat in it—flat, relentless, without the softening hush of trees.

He took a cab to the Somerset Club and ordered breakfast as if order itself could keep his mind from running ahead. He began with melon—cool, faintly musky, the knife passing through it with a wet whisper—then waited for toast and scrambled eggs, letting the paper lie open before him while his attention kept darting to the door, to the clock, to the way the waiters’ shoes slapped the floor. In the fashionable streets too there was an untidy, domestic air: caretakers in calico stationed on rich doorsteps, the Common looking like the aftermath of a picnic where the music had stopped but the trampled grass still remembered it. It was the kind of city where sound didn’t echo so much as sprawl—voices, harness-rings, a tinny barrel-organ somewhere forcing a tune into the heat.

To imagine Ellen here, in this slackened Boston, felt like trying to set a bright, sharp spice into boiled water: the flavor wouldn’t take. Yet a new energy had been in him since the night before, since he had told May—almost lightly—that he had “business in Boston” and would go on to New York the following evening. He had let the office letter, conveniently placed where fate could wink at him, do the work of explanation. The ease of it made him briefly ashamed; it had the glibness of other men’s contrivances. But he had no patience for looking too closely at himself. He smoked a cigarette, scanned the Commercial Advertiser, exchanged the usual greetings with men he knew, and felt, with a strange lucidity, that he was still inside the same world while somehow having slipped through the net of its hours.

At half-past nine he went to the writing-room and wrote a few lines. The pen scratched, the paper rasped under his hand; the familiar instruments of communication looked suddenly crude, like tools meant for coarser work than what he was trying to do. He ordered a messenger to take a cab to the Parker House and wait for the answer, then sat with another newspaper spread before him and began to calculate—absurdly—how long a cab would take.

“The lady was out, sir,” a waiter said at his elbow.

“Out?” Archer repeated, and the word tasted foreign in his mouth, as though he had never learned its meaning in the dialect of his own life.

He rose, went into the hall, found hat and stick, and walked out as if pushed. It could not be true—out at that hour? Why had he not sent the note the moment he arrived? The city, a minute ago familiar, became vast and empty, a place with too much air and not enough direction. He started across the Common toward the Parker House, and the noises thinned under the trees into a softer weave: leaves ticking in a faint stir, a distant hoofbeat, a few voices muffled by shade.

On the first bench under a tree she was sitting.

A grey silk sunshade made a small, disciplined shadow above her head—grey, not pink; he could not imagine now how he had ever dressed her in any brighter color. She sat as if she had nothing else to do, her profile drooping with the heat, her hair knotted low at the nape under her dark hat. The glove on the hand that held the parasol was long and faintly wrinkled, as though the day itself had creased it. The whole attitude was listless, yet contained, like a candle that has learned to burn without flicker.

He came nearer. She turned and looked at him.

“Oh,” she said; and for a second there was a startled sharpness in her face, as if some long-expected sound had been heard at the wrong time. Then it softened into a slow smile of wonder and content, as though she had stepped into a remembered room.

“Oh,” she murmured again, and without rising made a place for him on the bench.

“I’m here on business—just got here,” Archer heard himself explain, and, not knowing why, he began to pretend astonishment at finding her. “But what on earth are you doing in this wilderness?”

The words sounded foolish the moment they were spoken, as if flung across distances too great for speech. He had the sensation that if he did not keep talking she would vanish again into the glare.

“I? Oh, I’m here on business too.” She turned so that they were face to face.

The words hardly reached him as meaning; what reached him was her voice. He had not remembered it at all—low-pitched, with a faint roughness on certain consonants, like the grain in a wooden spoon when it strikes a bowl. Nothing of it had remained with him, as if memory, for all its tyranny, could not translate that particular sound into anything it could keep.

“You do your hair differently,” he said; and his heart thudded as though he had crossed some line.

“Differently? No—it’s only that I do it as best I can when I’m without Nastasia.”

“Nastasia; but isn’t she with you?”

“No; I’m alone. For two days it was not worth while to bring her.”

“You’re alone—at the Parker House?”

A flash of her old malice lit her eyes. “Does it strike you as dangerous?”

“No; not dangerous—”

“But unconventional? I see; I suppose it is.” She considered a moment, the parasol’s shadow shifting on her cheek. “I hadn’t thought of it, because I’ve just done something so much more unconventional.”

The irony lingered faintly, like a spice tasted after the mouth has swallowed.

“I’ve just refused to take back a sum of money—that belonged to me.”

Archer sprang up and moved away a step or two, as if the bench had burned him. She had furled her parasol and sat absently drawing little patterns in the gravel with its tip. The small rasping sound made him think of a knife on a plate.

He came back and stood before her.

“Some one—has come here to meet you?”

“Yes.”

“With this offer?”

She nodded.

“And you refused—because of the conditions?”

“I refused,” she said after a moment.

He sat down again beside her, as if sitting were the only way to keep his mind from swaying.

“What were the conditions?”

“Oh, they were not onerous: just to sit at the head of his table now and then.”

Silence fell. It had weight; it pressed his ribs in. His heart shut itself in that queer, obstinate way it had, and he groped vainly for words, as if the language he had inherited did not have a vocabulary for the reality before him.

“He wants you back—at any price?”

“Well—a considerable price. At least the sum is considerable for me.”

He paused, circling the question he could not avoid.

“It was to meet him here that you came?”

She stared, then laughed—a quick release. “Meet him—my husband? Here? At this season he’s always at Cowes or Baden.”

“He sent some one?”

“Yes.”

“With a letter?”

She shook her head. “No; just a message. He never writes. I don’t think I’ve had more than one letter from him.”

The mention brought color to her cheek, and Archer felt his own face grow hot, as if the air had thickened again.

“Why does he never write?”

“Why should he? What does one have secretaries for?”

She said it as if it were an innocent word, emptied of its history. Archer felt the old story—known, unspoken—rise like bitterness at the back of his throat. For a moment the question was on his tongue, but the memory of that one letter Count Olenski had written was too present; he swallowed the impulse and asked instead, carefully:

“And the person—”

“The emissary? The emissary,” she repeated, still smiling, “might, for all I care, have left already; but he has insisted on waiting till this evening… in case… on the chance…”

“And you came out here to think the chance over?”

“I came out to get a breath of air. The hotel’s too stifling. I’m taking the afternoon train back to Portsmouth.”

They sat looking straight ahead at the passing figures. The Common’s sounds went on—the far-off creak of a cart, a child’s high call, the dry percussion of shoes—yet around them there was a pocket of hush, as if the leaves above had decided to shelter this particular conversation.

Finally she turned her eyes to his face. “You’re not changed.”

He wanted to answer: I was, till I saw you again. Instead he stood abruptly and looked about at the sweltering park, at the trampled grass and the hot shimmer beyond the trees.

“This is horrible,” he said. “Why shouldn’t we go out a little on the bay? There’s a breeze; it will be cooler. We might take the steamboat down to Point Arley.”

She glanced up at him, hesitating, and he hurried on, as if speed could keep the world from catching them. “On a Monday morning there won’t be anybody on the boat. My train doesn’t leave till evening—I’m going back to New York. Why shouldn’t we?” He leaned toward her, the words breaking out of him. “Haven’t we done all we could?”

“Oh,” she murmured again—on the same note of reluctant yielding.

She rose, opened her sunshade, and looked around as if to consult the scene and confirm the impossibility of staying in it. Then her gaze returned to him.

“You mustn’t say things like that to me.”

“I’ll say anything you like—or nothing. I won’t open my mouth unless you tell me to. What harm can it do to anybody? All I want is to listen to you,” he stammered, hating the clumsiness of his own tongue.

She drew out a little gold-faced watch on an enamelled chain.

“Oh, don’t calculate,” he burst out; “give me the day! I want to get you away from that man. At what time was he coming?”

Her color rose again. “At eleven.”

“Then you must come at once.”

“You needn’t be afraid—if I don’t come.”

“Nor you either—if you do. I swear I only want to hear about you, to know what you’ve been doing. It’s a hundred years since we’ve met—it may be another hundred before we meet again.”

She wavered, her anxious eyes searching his. “Why didn’t you come down to the beach to fetch me, the day I was at Granny’s?” she asked.

“Because you didn’t look round—because you didn’t know I was there. I swore I wouldn’t unless you looked round.” The confession sounded childish as soon as he said it, and he laughed.

“But I didn’t look round on purpose.”

“On purpose?”

“I knew you were there; when you drove in I recognised the ponies. So I went down to the beach.”

“To get away from me as far as you could?”

She repeated, low and exact, as if quoting from the only language she trusted: “To get away from you as far as I could.”

He laughed again, this time with boyish triumph. “Well, you see it’s no use. I may as well tell you,” he added, “that the business I came here for was just to find you. But, look here, we must start or we shall miss our boat.”

“Our boat?” She frowned, puzzled, then smiled. “Oh, but I must go back to the hotel first: I must leave a note—”

“As many notes as you please. You can write here.” He drew out a note-case and one of the new stylographic pens, proud, absurdly, of its readiness. “I’ve even got an envelope—you see how everything’s predestined! There—steady the thing on your knee, and I’ll get the pen going in a second. They have to be humoured; wait—”

He banged the hand holding the pen against the back of the bench, like a cook knocking a stubborn bottle to start the pour. “It’s like jerking down the mercury in a thermometer—just a trick. Now try—”

She laughed, bending over the paper laid on the note-case. Archer walked a few steps away, staring with radiant, unseeing eyes at passersby who slowed to stare in their turn at the sight of a fashionably dressed lady writing a note on her knee in the Common. The scene had the innocent absurdity of something out of place, like a fine porcelain dish set down on a park bench.

She slipped the sheet into the envelope, wrote a name on it, and put it into her pocket. Then she stood up.

They walked back toward Beacon Street. Near the club Archer caught sight of the plush-lined herdic that had carried his note to the Parker House. Its driver, having performed this exertion, was bathing his brow at a hydrant as if the city itself had wrung him out.

“I told you everything was predestined! Here’s a cab for us. You see!”

They laughed at the small miracle of finding a public conveyance at that hour in that spot, in a city where cab-stands still seemed an imported notion. Archer glanced at his watch and saw there was time to drive to the Parker House before going to the steamboat landing.

They rattled through the hot streets and drew up at the hotel’s door. The building exhaled its own stale breath—polish, damp stone, and the faint sweet of iced drinks meant to pretend at coolness.

Archer held out his hand. “Shall I take it in?” he asked, meaning the letter.

Madame Olenska shook her head, sprang out, and vanished through the glazed doors.

It was barely half-past ten. Yet Archer’s mind filled the hotel at once with the emissary, seated among travellers with sweating glasses at their elbows, waiting for her with the patience of someone used to being obeyed. Archer began to pace before the herdic, the cobbles jolting the soles of his shoes at each turn. A Sicilian boy with eyes like Nastasia’s offered to shine his boots; an Irish matron thrust peaches toward him, their skins warm and faintly furry, the fruit-scent rising when she shifted the basket. Every few moments the doors opened and hot men in straw hats came out, glancing at him as they passed. It astonished him how often the door swung, and how alike the faces were—lank and weary, round and startled, lantern-jawed and mild—repeating themselves as if stamped from one mould by the day’s oppressive heat and the country’s habit of moving in crowds.

Then, in the stream, a different face flashed—pale too, dulled by heat or worry, but quicker, more conscious, more various, as if several expressions lived in it at once. Archer’s memory caught for an instant on a thin thread and then lost it; the face was gone, doubly foreign in that setting, and the thread snapped and drifted away.

He resumed his patrol, unwilling to stand watch in hand within view of the doors. His sense of time, unaided, became a torment. If she stayed so long, it could only be because the emissary had found her and was holding her with words, with pressure, with whatever vocabulary power uses when it means to be obeyed.

“If she doesn’t come soon I’ll go in and find her,” he said aloud.

The doors swung, and she was suddenly at his side.

They got into the herdic. As it jolted forward Archer pulled out his watch and saw she had been gone three minutes.

The windows clattered loose in their frames, making talk impossible; the city’s hard noises battered at them until the wharf came into view and the water lay beyond like a promise. On the half-empty boat they sat side by side on a bench and found they had almost nothing to say—or rather, what they had to say went better unspoken, passing between them in the relief of being away from walls, away from eyes, away from the thick, listening air of rooms.

As the paddle-wheels began to turn and the wharves and shipping slid backward through the veil of heat, Archer felt the familiar world of habit receding too, as if all the old arrangements were being gently unmoored. He longed to ask her if she felt it: that they were setting out on a voyage from which they might never return. But he was afraid of jarring something delicate in her trust. In truth he had no wish to betray it. There had been nights when the memory of their kiss had burned on his lips; even the day before, driving to Portsmouth, the thought of her had run through him like fire. Now that she sat beside him and the boat bore them out into an unknown stretch of day, the nearness had changed: it was deeper, quieter, like a heat held under a lid. A touch might break it.

The boat turned seaward and a breeze began to move around them. The bay, at first an oily undulation, broke into ripples tipped with spray. Behind them Boston remained under its sultry fog, but ahead lay ruffled water, distant promontories, a lighthouse bright in the sun. The soundscape changed with the air: the steady slap and churn of the paddles, the hiss of water, the rigging’s faint clink, the wind’s thin singing in the rails. Archer felt his mind ease under it, as if the right kind of sound could rinse the day clean.

Madame Olenska leaned back against the rail and drank in the coolness between parted lips. A long veil was wound about her hat, but her face was uncovered, and he was struck by the tranquil gaiety in her expression. She seemed to accept their adventure as a matter of course—neither frightened of unexpected encounters nor, worse, feverishly exalted by the possibility of them. That composure—so unforced, so unadvertised—made the world’s rules seem, for the moment, like a dialect one could step out of without apology.

At the inn where Archer had hoped they would be alone, the dining-room was filled with a strident party of innocent-looking young men and women. Their laughter rang too loudly against the bare boards; forks clinked, chairs scraped, voices rose and fell like a bad band practicing the same refrain. The landlord, proud of his information, told Archer they were school-teachers on a holiday.

Archer’s heart sank. “This is hopeless—I’ll ask for a private room,” he said.

Madame Olenska waited without objection, as if she had long ago learned how to move through the world without wasting strength on what could not be changed.

The room he found opened on a long wooden verandah, with the sea coming in at the windows. It was bare and cool. A coarse checkered cloth covered the table; a bottle of pickles stood like a greenish sentinel; and under a little cage a blueberry pie waited, its dark fruit showing through the crust’s cracks, the faint, homely perfume of baked sugar and berries rising even in that spare place. No more guileless-looking cabinet particulier had ever offered shelter to a clandestine couple. Archer fancied he saw Madame Olenska register the reassurance of its simplicity in the faintly amused smile with which she sat opposite him.

A woman who had run away from her husband—and reputedly with another man—was likely to have mastered the art of taking things for granted. Yet the quiet, unsurprised simplicity of her manner blunted his irony. By being so calm, so unastonished, she brushed away the conventions; and he felt, with a sudden tenderness, that to seek to be alone might be the most natural thing in the world for two old friends who had so much to say—if only there existed a language in which it could safely be said.

They let the luncheon draw itself out as if time, too, had been set on the table between them—passed, tasted, and set down again. The room smelled of hot metal and salt blown in from the beach, of coffee that had stood too long on the stove and taken on a faint bitterness, and of fish—fresh enough to speak of the morning’s catch, yet already glossed with the heavy, sweet butter of a provincial cook’s generosity. Outside, the sea kept its own counsel, laying down its long, even phrases of sound; the hush between the waves seemed to seep into the pauses between their words, so that talk and silence alternated like courses.

Archer had not meant to avoid his own affairs; it happened of itself, as the hand reaches again and again for what is rare on a plate. He leaned forward, listening as one listens to a recipe passed down in a dialect that will not survive the next generation unless it is held fast in the ear. Ellen sat with her chin on her clasped hands, her eyes on him and yet, at moments, turned inward—as if she were tasting in memory what she described.

She told him, in plain, unexaggerated phrases, of the year and a half since they had last met. She had grown tired of what people called “society.” New York, she admitted, had been kind—almost oppressively so, like a table pressed on a guest until the guest cannot tell appetite from obligation. She would never forget how warmly it had welcomed her back; but after the first flush of novelty she had found herself, as she put it, too “different” to care for the things it cared about. And so she had decided to try Washington, where one was supposed to meet more varieties of people and of opinion.

“On the whole,” she said, “I shall probably settle down there—and make a home for poor Medora.” Her voice softened on the name, as if it were a homely ingredient that needed careful handling. Medora, it seemed, had worn out the patience of all her other relations at the very moment when she most needed watching over, and protecting—above all from matrimonial perils.

“But Dr. Carver—aren’t you afraid of Dr. Carver?” Archer asked. He had heard the man’s name buzzing about the Blenkers’ like a fly that returns to the sugar no matter how often it is waved away.

Ellen’s mouth curved—not quite amusement, not quite contempt. “Oh, the Carver danger is over. Dr. Carver is very clever. He wants a rich wife to finance his plans, and Medora is simply a good advertisement as a convert.”

“A convert to what?”

“To all sorts of new and crazy social schemes.” She hesitated a moment, then went on with the quiet obstinacy that startled him more than any heat would have done. “But they interest me more than the blind conformity to tradition—somebody else’s tradition—that I see among our friends. It seems stupid to have discovered America only to make it into a copy of another country.”

She smiled across the table, and the smile, light as it was, made Archer’s skin tighten as if a knife had gone too near the bone. “Do you suppose Christopher Columbus would have taken all that trouble just to go to the Opera with the Selfridge Merrys?”

The words struck him with the sharpness of vinegar. He felt the blood rise. “And Beaufort—do you say these things to Beaufort?” he demanded, abruptly, as if the name were a spice he could not bear.

“I haven’t seen him for a long time,” she answered, without flinching. “But I used to; and he understands.”

“Ah—it’s what I’ve always told you: you don’t like us. And you like Beaufort because he’s so unlike us.” His glance ran, restless, around the bare little room, and out beyond it to the bare beach and the row of stark white houses strung along the shore, each one looking as if it had been scrubbed into respectability. The whole place, with its thin smells and plain furniture, seemed to him suddenly like a parable of their world—washed, bleached, and airless. “We’re damnably dull. We’ve no character, no colour, no variety. I wonder—” His voice broke, and it was as if the sea outside had pushed its long, indifferent breath through him. “Why don’t you go back?”

Her eyes darkened, and he braced himself for anger. Instead she fell silent. She did not answer at once; she seemed to weigh his words the way a cook weighs salt—knowing how little it takes to ruin what might have been nourishing. The pause lengthened until the sounds outside grew unnaturally clear: the steady wash of water against the shore, the remote creak of wood, a far-off call and answering call along the beach. Archer’s fear rose—not of her indignation, but of her agreement.

At last she said: “I believe it’s because of you.”

She said it without flourish, without any inviting softness. The confession came, oddly, with the flatness of truth, as if it had been spoken first to herself and only now brought out into the open air. Archer reddened to the temples. He did not move, scarcely breathed. Her words seemed to him like some fragile winged thing that might lift and vanish at the least stir—yet if he kept perfectly still it might settle and multiply, filling the room with its presence.

“At least,” she continued, “it was you who made me understand that under the dullness there are things so fine and sensitive and delicate that even those I most cared for in my other life look cheap in comparison.” She drew her brows together, troubled, as though she were trying to find a word that existed in no language she had ever spoken. “I don’t know how to explain myself—but it seems as if I’d never before understood with how much that is hard and shabby and base the most exquisite pleasures may be paid.”

Exquisite pleasures—he felt the retort flare up in him, quick and bitter: it was something to have had them. Yet he could not utter it. The look in her eyes—appealing, steadfast—held him as firmly as a hand on the wrist.

“I want,” she went on, “to be perfectly honest with you—and with myself. For a long time I’ve hoped this chance would come: that I might tell you how you’ve helped me, what you’ve made of me—”

He stared at her under frowning brows, then broke in with a laugh that sounded to his own ear like a plate slipping on a stone floor. “And what do you make out that you’ve made of me?”

She paled a little, as if the air had suddenly cooled. “Of you?”

“Yes. For I’m of your making much more than you ever were of mine. I’m the man who married one woman because another one told him to.”

A fugitive flush rose, then ebbed. “I thought—you promised—you were not to say such things today.”

“Ah—how like a woman! None of you will ever see a bad business through!”

She lowered her voice, and the softening of it did not lessen its force. “Is it a bad business—for May?”

He had crossed to the window and stood drumming on the raised sash, his eyes fixed on the sea as if it might offer him some new vocabulary. He felt, in every fibre, the wistful tenderness with which she had said her cousin’s name; it stung him more than accusation could have done.

“For that’s the thing we’ve always got to think of—haven’t we—by your own showing?” she persisted, as if she were holding to a rule of conduct the way one holds to a household measure when everything else in the kitchen has been lost.

“My own showing?” he echoed, blankly, still staring outward.

“Or if not—” she went on, following her thought with a painful exactness, “if it’s not worth while to have given up, to have missed things, so that others may be saved from disillusionment and misery—then everything I came home for, everything that made my other life seem by contrast so bare and so poor because no one there took account of them—all these things are a sham or a dream—”

He turned his head without leaving his place. “And in that case there’s no reason on earth why you shouldn’t go back,” he finished for her, savagely glad to force the conclusion into words.

Her eyes clung to him. “Oh—is there no reason?”

“Not if you staked your all on the success of my marriage. My marriage,” he said, and the words tasted of iron, “isn’t going to be a sight to keep you here.”

She did not answer.

“What’s the use?” he burst out. “You gave me my first glimpse of a real life, and at the same moment you asked me to go on with a sham one. It’s beyond human enduring—that’s all.”

“Oh, don’t say that,” she cried, and the cry came from so deep that it seemed to shake the stillness in the room. “When I’m enduring it!”

Her arms had slipped down along the table, her hands open, helpless; and she sat with her face abandoned to his gaze as if, in the recklessness of some desperate peril, she could no longer afford concealment. The sight of her—so unguarded—overwhelmed him. It was as if, in that instant, the years of her silence had found their only possible utterance: not in any dominant lexicon, not in any tidy phrase, but in the plain, untranslatable evidence of tears.

“You too—oh, all this time, you too?” he stammered.

She let the tears gather and overflow, sliding slowly downward. They were not dramatic, only inevitable—like the slow yielding of a fruit pressed too long.

Half the width of the room still lay between them, and neither made any show of moving. Archer was aware, with a strange detachment, of her bodily nearness, as if his senses had been drawn away from touch into some more inward faculty of hearing and memory. Yet his eyes were caught—as they had been once before, in the little house on Twenty-third Street—by one of her hands flung out on the table. He fixed on it as if it were the only steady thing in the whirl of his thoughts. His imagination spun around that hand like water around the rim of a basin; and still he did not advance.

He had known the love that feeds on caresses and lives by them. This was not that. This passion was closer than his bones; it could not be soothed by any hurried reaching out. His terror was to do anything that might blur the sound and impression of what she had said; his one fierce comfort was the thought that he would never again be quite alone.

Then, like the aftertaste of smoke that comes when sweetness has been too sudden, the sense of waste and ruin rose in him. Here they were, shut in, safe, close; and yet chained to their separate destinies as firmly as if a continent lay between them.

“What’s the use—when you will go back?” he cried. Beneath the words another cry struggled up, raw and helpless: How on earth can I keep you?

She sat motionless, her lids lowered, as if listening not to him but to some inner sound she could not silence. “Oh—I shan’t go yet!”

“Not yet? Some time, then? Some time that you already foresee?”

At that she raised her eyes, and they were suddenly clear—as if, for a moment, all the muddied meanings and borrowed phrases of their world had fallen away. “I promise you: not as long as you hold out. Not as long as we can look straight at each other like this.”

He dropped back into his chair. What her answer truly said was: If you lift a finger, you’ll drive me back—back to all the abominations you know of, and all the temptations you half guess. The unspoken sentence sounded in him as plainly as if she had spoken it aloud, and it kept him on his side of the table in a moved, almost sacred submission, as if this restraint were their only protection against a greater violence.

“What a life for you—” he groaned.

“Oh—as long as it’s a part of yours.”

“And mine a part of yours?”

She nodded.

“And that’s to be all—for either of us?”

“Well; it is all, isn’t it?”

At that he sprang up, forgetting everything but the sweetness and aching candour of her face. She rose too—not to meet him, not to flee, but quietly, as though the worst of the task were already done and she had only to wait. So quietly that, when he came close, the hands she held out did not stop him; they guided him. Her fingers fell into his, and her arms, extended but not rigid, kept him far enough away to let her surrendered face say what words could not.

They stood so—how long he could not have told. Time, like the tide outside, had lost its markings. It was long enough for her silence to communicate all she had to say, and for him to understand that only one thing mattered: he must do nothing to make this meeting their last. He must leave their future in her care, asking only that she keep fast hold of it for both of them.

“Don’t—don’t be unhappy,” she said, and her voice broke as she drew her hands away.

“You won’t go back—you won’t go back?” he pleaded, as if that were the one prospect he could not survive.

“I won’t go back,” she answered.

Then she turned, opened the door, and led the way into the public dining-room.

The strident school-teachers were gathering up their possessions, their voices sharp and metallic in the common air, like cheap cutlery scraped against tin. The sudden clatter seemed to strike at the nerves after the room’s charged quiet, and Archer felt it as an assault—proof of how sound can bruise the mind when it has been living on whispers. Through the windows the beach lay bleached in sunlight; the white steam-boat waited at the pier, its presence a blunt, practical promise of departure. And over the bright waters Boston loomed in a line of haze, distant as a memory already being rewritten.

Back on the boat, with other people’s voices skimming over the water like thrown pebbles, Archer found a calmness settling in him—an unasked-for tranquillity that both steadied and shamed him. By any measure his day had ended in absurd defeat: he had not pressed his lips to Madame Olenska’s hand, had not drawn from her a single promise of “another time.” And yet—sick as he was with longing, and leaving for an indefinite while the one face that made the rest of life look like a dimly lit room—he felt almost comforted.

It was the way she had held the scales. Not by cleverness, not by the practiced poise of a woman playing to an audience, but by something raw and exacting in her sincerity—her tears and her broken phrases proving how little it was calculated. She had kept, without boasting of it, a balance between loyalty to others and truth to themselves, and now that the danger of some irrevocable word had passed, the very restraint filled him with a kind of tender awe. He found himself grateful—grateful, even, that no vanity had prompted him to “play” the lover in a scene where witnesses might later retail the performance. What remained of their meeting felt, strangely, like a saving and not a loss.

Even after they had clasped hands at Fall River and he had turned away alone, that conviction walked beside him. He wandered back to the club and sat in the deserted library, turning the hours over and over, as one worries a remembered taste on the tongue—trying to locate where sweetness ended and bitterness began, and why the mingling still sustained.

The more he examined it, the clearer it grew: if she chose at last to return to Europe—return to her husband—it would not be because that old life tempted her, even with new terms offered. She would go only if she felt herself becoming a temptation to him: an invitation to let go of the very standard they had, with such pain, set up between them. Her choice, he saw, would be to remain near him as long as he did not ask her to come nearer; and it depended on him to keep her there—safe, and kept apart.

Those thoughts followed him into the train, enclosing him in a kind of golden haze through which the faces of other travellers looked remote, as if heard through a closed door. The rhythm of wheels and rails made its own dull, iron music—a hard, ceaseless industrial chant that swallowed up the softer human sounds. Archer, who in his recent hours with Ellen had begun to measure comfort by the quality of the silence between two voices, felt the brutality of that noise as a violation; and yet even it could not break the spell he carried.

The next morning, the spell thinned in the stifling reality of New York’s September heat. The station poured out its crowd of heat-withered faces, and Archer still looked at them through his lingering blur—until one face detached itself with an insistence that cut clean through his abstraction.

It was the young man he had noticed the day before in Boston, passing out of the Parker House: a face that did not “belong” to the American hotel world, that had about it the disciplined fatigue of Europe and the wary quickness of someone forever reading a room. Now the same foreignness struck Archer again; with it came a dim stir of association, like the half-heard refrain of a song in a dialect one has nearly forgotten.

The young man stood, valise in hand, blinking a little at the harshness of American travel, and then advanced toward Archer, lifted his hat, and said in careful English: “Surely, Monsieur, we met in London?”

“Ah—of course. In London!” Archer seized his hand, curiosity and sympathy rising together. “So you did get here, after all?” He looked with renewed wonder at the astute, haggard little countenance of young Carfry’s French tutor.

“Oh, I got here—yes.” M. Rivière smiled, but his lips seemed drawn over something tight. “But not for long. I return the day after tomorrow.” He stood holding his light valise in one neatly gloved hand, and his eyes had the anxious, perplexed look of a man who has been dropped into a place where even the simplest questions are answered with indifference.

“I wonder, Monsieur,” he began, “since I have had the good luck to run across you, if I might—”

Archer cut in, almost eagerly, as though grasping at the human scale of ordinary kindness after the strained delicacy of the day before. “I was just going to suggest it. Come to luncheon, won’t you? Down town—if you’ll look me up at my office I’ll take you to a very decent place in that quarter.”

M. Rivière looked genuinely touched, and surprised. “You are too kind. But I was only going to ask if you would tell me how to reach some sort of conveyance. There are no porters, and no one here seems to listen—”

“I know. Our stations must astonish you. When you ask for a porter they offer you chewing-gum.” Archer’s irritation at the scene around them sharpened his humor. “Come along—I’ll extricate you. And you really must lunch with me.”

The young man hesitated—so slightly Archer would hardly have noticed if he had not been watching the play of feeling beneath the Frenchman’s composure—then replied, with profuse thanks and a tone that did not quite convince, that he was already engaged. But once they had reached the comparative reassurance of the street, M. Rivière asked if he might call that afternoon.

Archer, with the midsummer leisure of the office before him, fixed an hour and scribbled his address. M. Rivière pocketed it with reiterated thanks and a wide flourish of his hat. A horse-car received him, its wheels making a gentler, more tolerable clatter than the iron uproar of the station, and Archer walked away.

At the hour named, M. Rivière appeared—shaved, smoothed, the careful surface restored—yet still unmistakably drawn and serious, as if some inner hunger could not be fed by mere grooming. Archer was alone in his office. Before accepting the chair Archer offered, the young man began abruptly:

“I believe I saw you, sir, yesterday in Boston.”

Archer was about to assent, but something in the visitor’s insistent gaze stopped him—the look of someone who has come to a door with a message that burns in his hands.

“It is extraordinary—very extraordinary,” M. Rivière went on, “that we should have met in the circumstances in which I find myself.”

“What circumstances?” Archer asked, crudely wondering if the man needed money.

M. Rivière studied him, as if testing where to place the weight of what he must say. “I have come, not to look for employment, as I spoke of doing when we last met, but on a special mission—”

“Ah—!” The two meetings clicked together in Archer’s mind, like the closing of a clasp. He paused, taking in the sudden light, while M. Rivière waited, as if aware he had said enough.

“A special mission,” Archer repeated at last.

M. Rivière opened his palms slightly, then let them fall, and the two men looked at each other across the desk until Archer roused himself. “Do sit down.”

M. Rivière bowed, chose a distant chair, and waited again with a restraint that felt less like timidity than discipline.

“It was about this mission that you wanted to consult me?” Archer asked, hearing his own voice for its steadiness and distrusting it.

M. Rivière bent his head. “Not in my own behalf. On that score I—I have fully dealt with myself. I should like—if I may—to speak to you about the Countess Olenska.”

Archer had known the words were coming; but when they came, his blood surged to his temples, as if a branch had whipped back and struck him in the face. He forced the next question out through the rush.

“And on whose behalf,” he said, “do you wish to do this?”

M. Rivière met the question sturdily. “Well—I might say hers, if it did not sound like a liberty. Shall I say instead: on behalf of abstract justice?”

Archer looked at him with an irony he did not feel. “In other words, you are Count Olenski’s messenger.”

A blush darkened on M. Rivière’s sallow face. “Not to you, Monsieur. If I come to you, it is on quite other grounds.”

“What right have you, in the circumstances, to be on any other ground?” Archer snapped. “If you’re an emissary, you’re an emissary.”

M. Rivière considered, turning his hat slowly in his gloved hands, as though reading his own fate in its worn lining. “My mission is over. As far as the Countess Olenska goes, it has failed.”

“I can’t help that.”

“No. But you can help—” He paused, looked down, then back at Archer with an appeal that did not beg so much as insist on being understood. “You can help, Monsieur, I am convinced, to make it equally a failure with her family.”

Archer pushed his chair back and rose. “Well—and by God I will!” he burst out. His hands went into his pockets; he stood staring down at the smaller man, wrath giving him an instant’s false clarity.

“Why the devil,” he went on, “should you have thought—since I suppose you’re appealing to me on the ground of my relationship to Madame Olenska—that I should take a view contrary to the rest of her family?”

M. Rivière’s expression changed as though the words had struck him physically. Timidity gave way to a distress so naked it disarmed Archer more than any argument could have done.

“Oh, Monsieur—”

“I can’t imagine,” Archer pressed, “why you should have come to me when there are others so much nearer to the Countess; still less why you thought I should be more accessible to the arguments I suppose you were sent over with.”

M. Rivière took the onslaught with a humility that seemed to come from some older world of formal obedience. “The arguments I want to present to you, Monsieur, are my own, and not those I was sent over with.”

“Then I see still less reason for listening to them.”

Again M. Rivière glanced into his hat, as if deciding whether Archer’s last words were dismissal enough. Then he spoke with sudden quiet decision.

“Monsieur—will you tell me one thing? Is it my right to be here that you question? Or do you perhaps believe the whole matter to be already closed?”

The steadiness of the question made Archer feel the coarseness of his bluster, as out of place as shouting in a room where bread is being kneaded. He reddened slightly, sat down again, and signed to the young man to do the same.

“I beg your pardon. But why isn’t the matter closed?”

M. Rivière’s gaze held anguish. “You do, then, agree with the rest of the family that, in face of the new proposals I have brought, it is hardly possible for Madame Olenska not to return to her husband?”

“Good God!” Archer exclaimed.

The murmur that left M. Rivière’s throat confirmed everything Archer had not known he feared. “Before seeing her, I saw—at Count Olenski’s request—Mr. Lovell Mingott, with whom I had several talks before going to Boston. I understand that he represents his mother’s view; and that Mrs. Manson Mingott’s influence is great throughout her family.”

Archer sat silent, feeling as though he clung to the edge of something sliding. Surprise—sharp, humiliating—came first: that he had been excluded not only from the family’s decisions but even from the knowledge that negotiations were going on. And then, with the surprise, recognition: the family had ceased to consult him because some deep tribal instinct warned them he was no longer on their side.

May’s voice came back with sudden clarity—an offhand remark during their drive home from Mrs. Manson Mingott’s on the day of the Archery Meeting: “Perhaps, after all, Ellen would be happier with her husband.” At the time he had protested with indignation; since then May had never named Ellen to him again. He saw now how that careless-sounding sentence had been a thin straw held up to learn the wind. His answer had been carried home, and after that he had been silently omitted from counsel.

He could even admire the discipline that made May bow to the decision. She would not have done so, he knew, had her conscience rebelled; but she probably shared the family’s conviction that a woman was “better off” as an unhappy wife than as a separated one. And there was no use, in their view, discussing such matters with Newland Archer, who had an awkward way of failing to take the most fundamental things for granted—as though he had begun to hear, beneath their polished speech, the harsher vocabulary of power it concealed.

He looked up and met M. Rivière’s anxious eyes.

“Don’t you know, Monsieur—is it possible you don’t know—that the family begin to doubt if they have the right to advise the Countess to refuse her husband’s last proposals?”

“The proposals you brought?”

“The proposals I brought.”

It was on Archer’s lips to say that whatever he knew or did not know was no concern of this young man. But something in M. Rivière’s humble tenacity—the courage of someone who had chosen to speak plainly in a language that could never quite carry what he meant—made Archer answer him with a question.

“What is your object in speaking to me of this?”

The reply came at once, as if it had been beating against M. Rivière’s teeth from the first moment.

“To beg you, Monsieur—to beg you with all the force I’m capable of—not to let her go back. Oh, don’t let her!”

Archer stared, astonishment cutting through his anger. There was no mistaking the sincerity of the distress, or the decision beneath it: the man had resolved to sacrifice everything but the necessity of this appeal.

“May I ask,” Archer said at length, “if this is the line you took with the Countess Olenska?”

M. Rivière reddened, but did not look away. “No, Monsieur. I accepted my mission in good faith. I really believed—for reasons I need not trouble you with—that it would be better for Madame Olenska to recover her situation, her fortune, the social consideration that her husband’s standing gives her.”

“So I supposed. You could hardly have accepted such a mission otherwise.”

“I should not have accepted it.”

“Well, then—?” Archer waited, and their eyes held each other in another long scrutiny.

“Ah, Monsieur—after I had seen her, after I had listened to her, I knew she was better off here.”

“You knew—?”

“Monsieur, I discharged my mission faithfully. I put the Count’s arguments, I stated his offers, without adding any comment of my own. The Countess was good enough to listen patiently; she carried her goodness so far as to see me twice; she considered impartially all I had come to say. And it was in the course of these two talks that I changed my mind—that I came to see things differently.”

“May I ask what led to this change?”

“Simply seeing the change in her,” M. Rivière replied.

“The change in her? Then you knew her before?”

Again that rising color. “I used to see her in her husband’s house. I have known Count Olenski for many years. You can imagine he would not have sent a stranger on such a mission.”

Archer’s gaze drifted past the young man to the blank walls of his office, and rested on the hanging calendar, with the rugged features of the President of the United States above its neat squares. That such a conversation should be going on anywhere within the millions of square miles under that stern gaze seemed as strange as any fiction. Outside, the city’s din pressed in—hoofbeats, wheels, shouted calls—each sound competing, each insisting, a marketplace of noise where even grief and love must raise their voices to be heard. Archer thought, with sudden bitterness, how their world’s language worked the same way: the dominant terms—duty, propriety, family—drowned out the smaller, truer words until one could hardly pronounce them without feeling indecent.

“The change—what sort of a change?” he asked.

“Ah, Monsieur, if I could tell you!” M. Rivière’s hands opened and closed on his hat as if he were trying to shape the unshapable. “Tenez—the discovery, I suppose, of what I’d never thought of before: that she’s an American. And that if you’re an American of her kind—of your kind—things that are accepted in certain other societies, or at least put up with as part of a general convenient give-and-take—become unthinkable. Simply unthinkable.” He paused, then continued with a gravity that felt like a condemnation. “If Madame Olenska’s relations understood what these things were, their opposition to her returning would no doubt be as unconditional as her own; but they seem to regard her husband’s wish to have her back as proof of an irresistible longing for domestic life. Whereas it’s far from being as simple as that.”

Archer looked again at the President’s face, then down at the papers on his desk—the ordinary detritus of an ordinary day. For a few seconds he could not trust himself to speak. In that silence he heard M. Rivière’s chair scrape back; the young man had risen.

When Archer looked up, he saw that M. Rivière was as moved as himself, his careful composure strained thin.

“Thank you,” Archer said at last, simply.

“There is nothing to thank me for, Monsieur. It is I, rather—” M. Rivière broke off, as if his throat refused the rest. Then he steadied his voice. “I should like, though, to add one thing. You asked me if I was in Count Olenski’s employ. I am, at this moment. I returned to him a few months ago for reasons of private necessity—such as may happen to anyone who has persons, ill and older persons, dependent on him. But from the moment I have taken the step of coming here to say these things to you, I consider myself discharged; and I shall tell him so on my return, and give him the reasons. That is all, Monsieur.”

He bowed and drew back a step.

“Thank you,” Archer said again.

Their hands met—Archer’s warm with a feeling he could not yet name, M. Rivière’s enclosed in its glove like something protected and already half-withdrawn—and the young man turned to go, leaving Archer alone with the city’s noise at his window and the taste of a truth that no family phrase could properly translate.

Every year, as surely as the fifteenth of October came round, Fifth Avenue performed its quiet rites of awakening. Shutters were flung back like eyelids after a long sleep; carpets, rolled all summer like stored dough, were uncurled and laid down smooth; heavy curtains—three layers, as if propriety required padding—were rehung so that lamplight might be softened into something becoming. By the first of November the work of reopening had passed from servants’ hands into habit, and by the fifteenth society—having warmed itself, having checked its reflection—began to move in earnest: the Opera, theatres, dinners, dances, and all the carefully-timed collisions that made up the season’s music.

It was at about this point that Mrs. Archer always said New York was very much changed.

From the elevated quiet of a non-participant she listened for discord the way an old farmer listens for a wrong note in weather: not the storm itself but the tremor that foretells it. With Mr. Sillerton Jackson and Miss Sophy to assist her, she would identify each new fissure in the old surface and each weed pushing up between the ordered rows of social vegetables. Newland Archer, as a boy, had waited for this pronouncement the way children wait for the first carving at table—half amused, half hungry. He used to smile and let the lamentations wash over him, as if they were merely the yearly sauce served with familiar dishes.

But this was the winter of the second year of his marriage, and even he—who had once believed the town’s manners as fixed as the stone lintels above its doors—could feel the house settling, ever so slightly, with a creak in the beams.

The topic had risen, as usual, over Mrs. Archer’s Thanksgiving dinner—an occasion on which, officially directed to gratitude, she took instead a mournful inventory of blessings and found them scarce. If there was anything, she seemed to imply, to call down Biblical thunder upon, it was the state of society; and everyone present knew exactly what the Reverend Dr. Ashmore meant when he had chosen his text from Jeremiah. Dr. Ashmore, the new rector of St. Matthew’s, was thought “advanced.” His sermons, like fashionable sauces, were praised for their novelty; his language had a tang to it, and when he denounced the world he called its movement a “trend.” To Mrs. Archer it was terrifying—and irresistibly absorbing—to feel herself part of a community that could be said to be trending, as if the word named an actual crack you might put a finger into.

“There’s no doubt Dr. Ashmore is right,” she said. “There is a marked trend.”

“It was odd, though, to preach about it on Thanksgiving,” Miss Jackson observed.

“Oh, he means us to give thanks for what’s left,” Mrs. Archer answered, dry as a well-kept biscuit.

Archer, listening, found that the “trend” could be tasted now, like metal on the tongue in certain kinds of air. The talk went, as it always did, from general doom to particulars.

“The extravagance in dress—” Miss Jackson began, leaning forward with the energy of someone describing a spoiled preserve. “Sillerton took me to the first night of the Opera, and I can only tell you that Jane Merry’s dress was the only one I recognized from last year; and even that had had the front panel changed. Yet I know she got it out from Worth only two years ago, because my seamstress always goes in to make over her Paris dresses before she wears them.”

“Ah, Jane Merry is one of us,” Mrs. Archer sighed—almost as if membership in that “us” were now a kind of poverty.

“Yes; she’s one of the few. In my youth it was considered vulgar to dress in the newest fashions,” Miss Jackson persisted, the way her generation persisted over a recipe that younger hands kept ruining. “Amy Sillerton has always told me that in Boston the rule was to put away one’s Paris dresses for two years. Old Mrs. Baxter Pennilow—who did everything handsomely—used to import twelve a year: two velvet, two satin, two silk, and the other six of poplin and the finest cashmere. It was a standing order. And as she was ill for two years before she died they found forty-eight Worth dresses that had never been taken out of tissue paper; and when the girls left off their mourning they were able to wear the first lot at the Symphony concerts without looking in advance of the fashion.”

“Boston is more conservative than New York,” Mrs. Archer conceded, as if admitting that another soil produced sturdier roots. “But I always think it’s a safe rule for a lady to lay aside her French dresses for one season. It was Beaufort who started the new fashion by making his wife clap her new clothes on her back as soon as they arrived. I must say at times it takes all Regina’s distinction not to look like… like…”

Miss Jackson’s glance slid round the table, encountered Janey’s bulging interest, and she saved herself in an unintelligible murmur.

“Like her rivals,” Mr. Sillerton Jackson supplied, with the small satisfaction of a man placing the final garnish.

The ladies murmured; and Mrs. Archer, partly to distract her daughter, said: “Poor Regina. Her Thanksgiving hasn’t been a very cheerful one, I’m afraid. Have you heard the rumours about Beaufort’s speculations, Sillerton?”

Mr. Jackson nodded without eagerness. Everybody had heard. In their world, tales did not need retelling to have weight; they floated like smoke in parlours and were breathed in with one’s tea.

A gloom settled, heavy as a thick tablecloth. No one liked Beaufort, and his private hypocrisy would have been a permissible secret pleasure; but dishonour in business was another matter—an unclean flavour that spoiled every dish at once. New York might accept lies in love as part of the social seasoning; but in money it required an unclouded, almost ceremonial honesty. The memory of the last public failure—years ago—still had the force of a warning taste. If Beaufort fell, the Beauforts would fall with him, whatever power he had bought and whatever popularity Regina had earned. Not even the Dallas connection could preserve her if the unlawful rumours were true.

They escaped, as people do, into safer complaints, but each subject led back to the same sense of loosened stitching.

“Of course, Newland, I know you let dear May go to Mrs. Struthers’s Sunday evenings—” Mrs. Archer began.

“Oh, you know everybody goes to Mrs. Struthers’s now,” May put in gaily; “and she was invited to Granny’s last reception.”

Archer, watching the ease with which the younger voice spoke it, thought again how New York managed its transitions: it pretended not to see them until they were complete, and then it remembered—sincerely—that they had always existed. Somewhere there was always a traitor in the citadel; and after the keys had been handed over, what was the use of swearing the gate had never opened? Once people had tasted the convenience of Mrs. Struthers’s Sunday hospitality they were unlikely to sit at home recalling what that hospitality had once been said to be made of.

“I know, dear, I know,” Mrs. Archer sighed. “Such things have to be, I suppose, as long as amusement is what people go out for. But I’ve never quite forgiven your cousin Madame Olenska for being the first person to countenance Mrs. Struthers.”

A sudden blush rose in May’s face—so quick, so hot, that it startled Archer more than it did the rest. She lowered her eyes as if some word had been dropped like a stain.

“Oh, Ellen—” she murmured, with that peculiar note the family had learned to use on Ellen’s name: accusing and yet pleading, as if the person spoken of had embarrassed them by existing.

The mention of Ellen always altered the air, the way the opening of a door lets in a different soundscape—street-noise, the echo of other lives. Mrs. Archer, with less than her usual tact for atmosphere, continued: “I’ve always thought that people like the Countess Olenska, who have lived in aristocratic societies, ought to help us keep up our social distinctions, instead of ignoring them.”

May’s blush deepened and stayed, as though the colour meant more than mere family disapproval.

“I’ve no doubt we all seem alike to foreigners,” Miss Jackson said tartly.

“I don’t think Ellen cares for society,” May went on, as if reaching for a safe phrase and finding none. “But nobody knows exactly what she does care for.”

“Ah, well—” Mrs. Archer sighed again, and the sigh carried with it all New York’s conclusion.

Everyone knew Ellen was no longer in her family’s good graces. Even Mrs. Manson Mingott, who had once defended her with the ferocity of a matriarch defending a wayward calf, could not openly praise Ellen’s refusal to return to her husband. The Mingotts had not proclaimed disapproval; their solidarity was too strong for that. They had simply, as Mrs. Welland said, “let poor Ellen find her own level”—and that level, mortifyingly, was in the dim depths where the Blenkers ruled and “people who wrote” gathered, conducting untidy rites that did not fit into the old vocabulary. It was incredible—and yet true—that Ellen, with all her opportunities, had become simply “Bohemian.”

The fact was used, like a moral, to prove she had made her fatal mistake in not returning to Count Olenski. A young woman belonged, people said, under her husband’s roof—especially when she had left it in circumstances that… well… if one cared to look into them…

“Madame Olenska is a great favourite with the gentlemen,” Miss Sophy said, with her conciliating air that always seemed to flick a concealed barb.

“Ah, that’s the danger a young woman like Madame Olenska is always exposed to,” Mrs. Archer agreed, mournful as a doctor describing a hereditary illness.

With that, the ladies gathered their skirts and went toward the drawing-room, toward the steady glow of carcel lamps and the softened, domesticated hush that old houses perfected. Archer and Mr. Sillerton Jackson withdrew to the Gothic library, where the fire made its own dry commentary and the room held the muffled acoustics of leather and books.

Before the grate, consoling himself for what he considered the dinner’s inadequacy with the undeniable perfection of his cigar, Mr. Jackson grew portentous.

“If the Beaufort smash comes,” he announced, “there are going to be disclosures.”

Archer lifted his head. Beaufort’s name always called up a sharp image: the heavy figure, the expensive fur, the confident tread through snow at Skuytercliff, as if the world had been built to receive him.

“There’s bound to be,” Mr. Jackson continued. “The nastiest kind of a cleaning up. He hasn’t spent all his money on Regina.”

“Oh, well—that’s discounted, isn’t it? My belief is he’ll pull out yet,” Archer said, wanting to change the subject, wanting not to have the bitter taste of scandal on his tongue any longer.

“Perhaps—perhaps.” Mr. Jackson spoke as if measuring a doubtful ingredient. “I know he was to see some of the influential people today. Of course,” he reluctantly conceded, “it’s to be hoped they can tide him over—this time anyhow. I shouldn’t like to think of poor Regina spending the rest of her life in some shabby foreign watering-place for bankrupts.”

Archer said nothing. It seemed to him so natural—however tragic—that money ill-gotten should be expiated. His mind hardly lingered on Regina’s possible exile; instead it went, with the instinct of pain, toward something nearer.

May’s blush. The way Ellen’s name had brought it.

Four months had passed since the midsummer day he and Ellen had spent together, and since then he had not seen her. He knew she had returned to Washington, to the small house she shared with Medora Manson. He had written once—only a few words, asking when they were to meet again. She had replied more briefly still: Not yet.

After that there had been nothing, and in the silence he had built a sanctuary inside himself, a private room where Ellen sat among his secret thoughts and longings. Little by little it became the scene of his real life. Into it he carried the books he read, the ideas that nourished him, the judgments and visions that made the day bearable. Outside it—in the visible rooms of his existence—he moved with a growing sense of unreality, blundering against familiar prejudices and inherited points of view the way an absent-minded man knocks into the furniture in his own darkened house. Absent: that was what he was. So absent from everything most solid and immediate that it sometimes startled him that others still believed he was present.

Mr. Jackson cleared his throat, preparing another course.

“I don’t know, of course, how far your wife’s family are aware of what people say about—well, about Madame Olenska’s refusal to accept her husband’s latest offer.”

Archer was silent.

“It’s a pity—certainly a pity—that she refused it,” Mr. Jackson went on.

“A pity? In God’s name, why?” Archer said, and felt his own voice sharpen.

Mr. Jackson looked down his leg to the neat join of unwrinkled sock and glossy pump, as if the smoothness reassured him.

“Well—to put it on the lowest ground—what’s she going to live on now?”

“Now—?” Archer echoed.

“If Beaufort—”

Archer sprang up, his fist coming down on the black walnut edge of the writing-table so hard that the brass double inkstand jumped, the wells dancing like startled eyes.

“What the devil do you mean, sir?”

Mr. Jackson shifted slightly and turned his calm, hard look full on Archer’s face, as if he were observing a young man’s temper the way one observes a flame that might run along spilled spirits.

“Well—I have it on pretty good authority—in fact, on old Catherine’s herself—that the family reduced Countess Olenska’s allowance considerably when she definitely refused to go back to her husband; and as, by this refusal, she also forfeits the money settled on her when she married—which Olenski was ready to make over to her if she returned—why, what the devil do you mean, my dear boy, by asking me what I mean?”

Archer moved to the mantel and bent to tap his ashes into the grate. The fire gave off a faint resinous smell, and the sound of falling ash seemed too loud in the thick room.

“I don’t know anything of Madame Olenska’s private affairs,” he said, forcing each word, “but I don’t need to, to be certain that what you insinuate—”

“Oh, I don’t: it’s Lefferts, for one,” Mr. Jackson cut in.

“Lefferts—who made love to her and got snubbed for it!” Archer flung back, contempt trembling in his voice.

“Ah—did he?” Mr. Jackson snapped, and Archer felt again that quick sense of stepping into a trap whose spring he had not seen. The old man sat turned sideways to the fire, but his gaze held Archer’s face with the cold grip of steel.

“Well, well: it’s a pity she didn’t go back before Beaufort’s cropper,” Mr. Jackson repeated. “If she goes now, and if he fails, it will only confirm the general impression. Which isn’t by any means peculiar to Lefferts, by the way.”

“Oh, she won’t go back now—less than ever!” Archer said, and even as he spoke he knew he had given Mr. Jackson precisely the certainty he wanted.

The old gentleman considered him, as if tasting the statement and finding it significant.

“That’s your opinion, eh? Well, no doubt you know. But everybody will tell you that the few pennies Medora Manson has left are all in Beaufort’s hands; and how the two women are to keep their heads above water unless he does, I can’t imagine. Of course, Madame Olenska may still soften old Catherine—who’s been the most inexorably opposed to her staying; and old Catherine could make her any allowance she chooses. But we all know she hates parting with good money; and the rest of the family have no particular interest in keeping Madame Olenska here.”

Archer burned with anger that could do nothing.
