He was in that state where a man feels himself about to act foolishly, and cannot stop, though he recognizes the folly as clearly as a cook recognizes that a sauce has split and will not come back. He saw, too, that Mr. Jackson had instantly noticed his ignorance of Ellen’s quarrels with her grandmother and the others, and had drawn his own conclusions about why Archer was not included in family councils. The knowledge warned Archer to be cautious; but the mention of Beaufort made him reckless. Still, some old training held: Mr. Jackson was under his mother’s roof. Old New York did not permit open quarrels with guests.

“Shall we go up and join my mother?” Archer suggested curtly.

On the drive home May was oddly silent. The city’s night sound—wheels, distant shouts, the muffled beat of hooves—seemed to press against the carriage like a tide. Through the darkness Archer still felt May’s blush about her, as if it were a warmth that had not cooled. He could not guess what the menace of it meant, but he felt warned by the simple fact that Ellen’s name had called it forth.

At home they went upstairs. Archer turned into the library; May usually followed him, but he heard her pass down the passage toward their bedroom.

“May!” he called, impatient without knowing why.

She came back, a slight surprise in her glance at his tone.

“This lamp is smoking again,” he said, seizing on the first domestic annoyance as if it could steady him. “I should think the servants might see that it’s kept properly trimmed.”

“I’m so sorry; it shan’t happen again,” she answered, firm and bright in the manner she had learned from her mother.

The competent cheerfulness exasperated him; it made him feel as though he were already becoming, in her eyes, something to be managed—a younger Mr. Welland with moods that required soothing. She bent to the lamp, lowered the wick with careful fingers. The light struck up on her white shoulders, on the clean curves of her face, and a sudden thought stabbed him with its simplicity: How young she is. For what endless years this life will have to go on.

He felt, with a kind of horror, his own youth, his blood bounding, the strength in him that had nowhere truthful to go.

“Look here,” he said suddenly, “I may have to go to Washington for a few days—soon. Next week perhaps.”

Her hand stayed on the key of the lamp as she turned slowly. The heat of the flame had brought colour back to her cheeks, but it faded as she looked up.

“On business?” she asked, in a tone that implied no other reason could exist, and that she had only supplied the expected phrase to complete his.

“On business, naturally. There’s a patent case coming up before the Supreme Court—” He named the inventor and went on, furnishing details with an ease that surprised him by reminding him of Lawrence Lefferts’s practiced glibness. May listened attentively, saying at intervals: “Yes, I see.”

When he finished she said, simply: “The change will do you good.”

Then, with her cloudless smile, looking him straight in the eyes, she added: “And you must be sure to go and see Ellen.”

The words were ordinary. The tone was the tone she might have used in reminding him not to neglect some irksome family obligation. But the message behind it arrived in him without sound, like a note passed in a crowded room: that she knew what people were saying; that she knew the family’s pressure on Ellen; that she knew, for reasons he had not told her, he had advised Ellen against returning; that he had helped Ellen hold out, and that he must now understand what such encouragement exposed Ellen to—and what it exposed him to. She was telling him she approved his visit, explicitly, and that he was to use it as a tool: to deliver the family’s meaning to Ellen.

Her hand was still on the lamp as the last of this silent speech settled between them. She turned the wick down, lifted off the globe, and breathed on the sulky flame.

“They smell less if one blows them out,” she explained, with her bright housekeeping air, as if the whole question were only a matter of keeping the air sweet.

On the threshold she turned and paused, waiting for his kiss.

Wall Street, the next day, breathed easier about Beaufort.

Nothing definite—only that vague, well-fed confidence New York reserved for men who knew whom to send for when the ledgers turned ugly. The rumor went round that he had “tided over,” and in the evening, when Mrs. Julius Beaufort appeared in her opera box with her practiced smile set like icing and a new emerald necklace catching the gaslight in cold green sparks, society let out the breath it had been holding.

New York could be merciless about money, about the clean, unspoken code that was as strict as any catechism; but it could also be selfishly relieved. The Beauforts, whatever else they were, had been useful—an easy center of heat and motion in the winter season, the best ballroom, the most obliging hospitality. Their fall would be painful; it would also be inconvenient.

Archer, meanwhile, had made up his mind to go to Washington. He had been waiting for the opening of the lawsuit he had mentioned to May, so he might braid the dates together and give his journey the proper professional look; but on Tuesday Mr. Letterblair told him the case might be postponed several weeks. Archer went home that afternoon with a decision that tasted like iron at the back of his throat: postponed or not, he would leave the next evening. May knew so little of his work, and cared so little for its minutiæ, that the postponement would pass by her like a dish carried through a room she was not hungry to enter. And he could no longer endure the delay of seeing Madame Olenska. There were too many things in him, unsaid, pressing upward like heat under a lid.

But on Wednesday morning, as soon as Archer reached his office, Mr. Letterblair met him with a face gone slack and gray, as if all the color had been wrung out of it.

Beaufort, after all, had not managed to tide over.

The “reassuring reports” had been the cleverest, most poisonous seasoning—sprinkled to keep depositors calm while money poured in. For a day the bank had seemed to fatten; then, the previous evening, the air had changed. Disturbing whispers began to outnumber hopeful ones. When the doors opened, a run began. Before the day was done, the doors would likely close for good.

Now the ugliest words were being spoken of Beaufort’s maneuver—words that stuck to the palate and made one want to spit. His failure promised to be among the most discreditable in Wall Street history, and the sheer breadth of the calamity left Mr. Letterblair shaken, almost helpless.

“I’ve seen bad things in my time,” he said, and his voice sounded like paper dragged over a rasp. “But nothing as bad as this. Everybody we know will be hit, one way or another. And what will be done about Mrs. Beaufort? What can be done about her? I pity Mrs. Manson Mingott as much as anybody—coming at her age, there’s no knowing what effect this affair may have. She always believed in Beaufort—she made a friend of him! And there’s the whole Dallas connection: poor Mrs. Beaufort is related to every one of you. Her only chance would be to leave her husband—yet how can any one tell her so? Her duty is at his side; and luckily she seems always to have been blind to his private weaknesses.”

There was a knock. Letterblair turned sharply, as if sound itself were an insult. “What is it? I can’t be disturbed.”

A clerk brought in a letter for Archer and withdrew.

Archer knew the hand at once. May’s writing, with its round, young confidence, always looked to him like something piped onto a cake—pretty, decided, not to be argued with. He broke the seal and read:

“Won’t you please come up town as early as you can? Granny had a slight stroke last night. In some mysterious way she found out before any one else this awful news about the bank. Uncle Lovell is away shooting, and the idea of the disgrace has made poor Papa so nervous that he has a temperature and can’t leave his room. Mamma needs you dreadfully, and I do hope you can get away at once and go straight to Granny’s.”

He handed the note to Mr. Letterblair. A few minutes later he was crawling northward in a crowded horse-car, the wheels grinding over frozen ruts with a jarring rhythm that climbed into his bones. At Fourteenth Street he climbed up to one of the high, swaying omnibuses of the Fifth Avenue line. The city’s midday clatter—hooves, iron rims, calls, the harsh squeal of harness—seemed louder than usual, as if panic in the financial district had sharpened every edge of sound and carried it uptown.

It was after twelve when the lumbering vehicle dropped him at old Catherine’s.

The ground-floor sitting-room window where Mrs. Mingott usually sat enthroned was occupied by the inadequate figure of her daughter, Mrs. Welland, who made him a haggard sign of welcome the moment she saw him. May met him in the doorway.

The hall had that unnatural look well-kept houses take on when illness breaks in—like a spotless kitchen suddenly turned into a sickroom. Wraps and furs lay in heaps on chairs as if flung down without thought; a doctor’s bag and overcoat rested on the table; beside them, cards and letters already began to pile up, ignored like cooling dishes no one had appetite for. Even the silence had a different quality here: not peace, but a strained quiet in which every distant footfall and door-click carried too far.

May looked pale but smiling; Dr. Bencomb, who had just come for the second time, took a more hopeful view, and Mrs. Mingott’s bulldog will to live and get well was already beginning to steady her household.

May led Archer into the old lady’s sitting-room. The sliding doors that opened into the bedroom had been drawn shut and covered by heavy yellow damask portières that held the light back as though it were too loud. There, in horrified undertones, Mrs. Welland gave him the details.

Something dreadful and mysterious, it seemed, had happened the night before.

At about eight o’clock, just after Mrs. Mingott had finished the game of solitaire she always played after dinner—an old, domestic ritual as fixed as the pouring of tea—the door-bell rang. A lady, so thickly veiled the servants did not at first recognize her, asked to be received. The butler, catching a familiar voice beneath the veil, flung open the sitting-room door and announced, with the solemnity of an ill-omened ingredient added to a broth: “Mrs. Julius Beaufort”—and closed the door again on the two women.

They were together, he thought, about an hour.

When Mrs. Mingott’s bell rang, Mrs. Beaufort had already slipped away unseen, like a scent that vanishes as soon as you turn to name it. And there the old lady sat—white, vast, terrible—in her great chair, and signed to be helped into her room. She had seemed, at that time, though plainly distressed, in full command of her mind and body. The mulatto maid put her to bed, brought her her usual cup of tea—tea brewed with the same measured care as always, because households like this clung to routine the way hands clung to a rail in a storm—straightened everything, and went away.

But at three in the morning the bell rang again. The summons was so unusual that both servants hastened in at once, for old Catherine ordinarily slept “like a baby.” They found their mistress sitting up against her pillows, a crooked smile on her face, one small hand hanging limp from its huge arm.

The stroke had clearly been slight; she could articulate and make her wishes known, and after the doctor’s first visit she began to regain control of her face. But the terror had been real; and when it was pieced together, from her thickened fragments of speech, why Mrs. Beaufort had come, indignation rose in the room like steam.

Regina Beaufort had come to ask her—effrontery beyond belief—to back up her husband, to “see them through,” not to “desert” them, as she called it. In short: to induce the whole family to cover and condone their monstrous dishonour.

“I said to her: ‘Honour’s always been honour, and honesty honesty, in Manson Mingott’s house, and will be till I’m carried out of it feet first,’” the old woman had stammered into her daughter’s ear, her words thick, her breath coming with effort.

“And when she said: ‘But my name, Auntie—my name’s Regina Dallas,’ I said: ‘It was Beaufort when he covered you with jewels, and it’s got to stay Beaufort now that he’s covered you with shame.’”

So much, with tears and gasps, Mrs. Welland delivered, blanched and demolished by the obligation—so unfamiliar to her—of having to look directly at what was ugly, discreditable, and loud in its consequences.

“If only I could keep it from your father-in-law,” she wailed, hands trembling as if she could not hold her own thoughts steady. “He always says: ‘Augusta, for pity’s sake, don’t destroy my last illusions’—and how am I to prevent his knowing these horrors?”

“After all, Mamma, he won’t have seen them,” May suggested, trying to set her voice like a cup carefully back on a saucer.

Mrs. Welland sighed. “Ah, no; thank heaven he’s safe in bed. And Dr. Bencomb has promised to keep him there till poor Mamma is better, and Regina has been got away somewhere.”

Archer had sat down near the window and was looking out at the deserted street. Even the avenue’s usual music seemed hushed at that hour, as if the very traffic kept its distance from illness and scandal. He could feel that he had been summoned less for anything practical he could do than to stand there and lend, by sheer presence, a kind of masculine steadiness to the frightened women.

Mr. Lovell Mingott had been telegraphed for; messengers were out with notes and telegrams to the family members in New York. Meanwhile there was nothing to do but whisper about consequences—about Beaufort’s dishonour, about Mrs. Beaufort’s unjustifiable visit.

Mrs. Lovell Mingott, who had been writing notes in another room, came back and joined the discussion. In their day, the elder ladies agreed, when a man had done something disgraceful in business the wife had but one idea: to efface herself, to disappear with him.

“There was the case of poor Grandmamma Spicer; your great-grandmother, May,” Mrs. Welland said, eager, as always, to wrap the present in the gauze of precedent. “Of course,” she added hastily, “your great-grandfather’s money difficulties were private—losses at cards, or signing a note for somebody—I never quite knew, because Mamma would never speak of it. But she was brought up in the country because her mother had to leave New York after the disgrace, whatever it was: they lived up the Hudson alone, winter and summer, till Mamma was sixteen. It would never have occurred to Grandmamma Spicer to ask the family to ‘countenance’ her, as I understand Regina calls it; though a private disgrace is nothing compared to the scandal of ruining hundreds of innocent people.”

“Yes,” Mrs. Lovell Mingott agreed, “it would be more becoming in Regina to hide her own countenance than to talk about other people’s. I understand that the emerald necklace she wore at the Opera last Friday had been sent on approval from Ball and Black’s in the afternoon. I wonder if they’ll ever get it back?”

Archer listened, unmoved by the relentless chorus. The law of financial probity—clean accounts, clean hands—was in him like a native tongue: not learned, but inherited, spoken without translation. No sentimental pity could make a Beaufort excusable in that dialect. An adventurer like Lemuel Struthers might make his Shoe Polish millions on any number of questionable tricks; but old New York, for all its hypocrisies, still expected its own kind of honesty from the men who claimed to belong to it.

Nor did Mrs. Beaufort’s fate greatly stir him. He felt more sorry for her than did her outraged relatives; yet it seemed to him that the tie between husband and wife, even if society quietly treated it as breakable in prosperity, should be indissoluble in misfortune. A wife’s place was at her husband’s side when he was in trouble. But society’s place was not at his side; and Mrs. Beaufort’s cool assumption that it should be—her attempt to enlist the Family as a shield against the one thing it could not forgive—made her seem almost an accomplice.

The mulatto maid called Mrs. Lovell Mingott into the hall. She returned in a moment, frowning.

“She wants me to telegraph for Ellen Olenska,” she said. “I had written to Ellen, of course, and to Medora; but now it seems that’s not enough. I’m to telegraph immediately, and tell her she’s to come alone.”

Silence received this, a silence that had its own sound—like the pause in a room when a kettle stops singing and the ear strains, waiting. Mrs. Welland sighed as if surrendering to the inevitable. May rose and began gathering up newspapers scattered on the floor, smoothing them as though tidiness could press down calamity.

“I suppose it must be done,” Mrs. Lovell Mingott continued, almost hoping someone would contradict her.

“Of course it must be done,” May said, and the name she spoke had a peculiar distinctness, as if she had tapped two small silver bells to make sure everyone heard. “Granny knows what she wants, and we must carry out all her wishes. Shall I write the telegram for you, Auntie? If it goes at once Ellen can probably catch tomorrow morning’s train.”

“Well, it can’t go at once. Jasper and the pantry-boy are both out with notes and telegrams.”

May turned to Archer with a smile that was both dutiful and bright. “But here’s Newland, ready to do anything. Will you take the telegram, Newland? There’ll be just time before luncheon.”

He rose with a murmur of readiness. She seated herself at old Catherine’s rosewood bonheur du jour and wrote out the message in her large, immature hand. When it was finished she blotted it carefully and handed it to him.

“What a pity,” she said, “that you and Ellen will cross each other on the way!—Newland,” she added, turning toward her mother and aunt, “is obliged to go to Washington about a patent law-suit that is coming up before the Supreme Court. I suppose Uncle Lovell will be back by tomorrow night, and with Granny improving so much it doesn’t seem right to ask Newland to give up an important engagement for the firm—does it?”

She paused as if waiting for the correct response to be served to her. Mrs. Welland, prompt with approval, declared: “Oh, of course not, darling. Your Granny would be the last person to wish it.”

As Archer left the room with the telegram in his hand, he heard his mother-in-law add—presumably to Mrs. Lovell Mingott—“But why on earth she should make you telegraph for Ellen Olenska—” and May’s clear voice answer at once: “Perhaps it’s to urge on her again that after all her duty is with her husband.”

The outer door closed behind Archer. He walked quickly toward the telegraph office, the message in his pocket feeling heavier than paper, and the city’s noon noises rising again around him, rough as a tide he had to push through.

“Ol—ol—how d’you spell it, anyhow?” the sharp young woman behind the brass-grilled window asked, her pencil poised as if she were about to score a careless cook for muddling a recipe.

Newland Archer pushed his wife’s telegram nearer. The office smelled of damp wool, metal dust, and the faint, singed sweetness of the electric wire—an acrid modern odor that seemed to live on its own thin hiss. Outside, Broadway’s wheels and hoofbeats kept up their restless, urban percussion, a racket that made his temples throb.

“Olenska—O-len-ska,” he repeated, drawing the message back and printing the syllables carefully above May’s slanting hand, as though spelling were a way of keeping the name from being mishandled.

“It’s an unlikely name for a New York telegraph office—at least in this quarter,” an unexpected voice remarked close to his shoulder.

Archer turned and found Lawrence Lefferts beside him, fingers smoothing his moustache with the same practiced care one might use to skim froth from a consommé—showing skill while pretending it was effortless. Lefferts’s eyes, deliberately polite, affected not to glance at the telegram.

“Hallo, Newland. Thought I’d catch you here. I’ve just heard of old Mrs. Mingott’s stroke; and as I was on my way to the house I saw you turning down this street and nipped after you. I suppose you’ve come from there?”

Archer nodded and slid the telegram under the lattice.

“Very bad, eh?” Lefferts went on. “Wiring to the family, I suppose. I gather it is bad, if you’re including Countess Olenska.”

Something hot and bitter rose in Archer—like the first scorch when butter is left too long in the pan. His lips hardened; for an instant he wanted, with an almost primitive clarity, to strike the long, vain, handsome face beside him.

“Why?” he asked.

Lefferts, who prided himself on never being caught in open debate, lifted his brows in an ironic grimace that reminded Archer, without words, of the girl behind the grill and her listening pencil. Nothing could be worse form than temper on public display. Archer had never felt less in awe of form; but the impulse to do Lefferts bodily harm passed as quickly as it came. To handle Ellen Olenska’s name with Lefferts—here, now, at the very lip of Catherine Mingott’s sick-room—was unthinkable, like letting a vulgar street tune blare through a house where someone lay in pain.

He paid for the telegram. The two men went out together into the street, where the city’s clangor pressed on them—iron-rimmed wheels over cobbles, a vendor’s cry, the harsh bell of a passing car—each sound jostling for dominance as if noise itself were the new social order.

Archer, regaining control as one regains a knife’s edge after it slips, said evenly: “Mrs. Mingott is much better. The doctor feels no anxiety whatever.”

Lefferts, instantly relieved, overflowed with the proper phrases; then, as if the conversation must have its fashionable sauce, asked if Archer had heard the beastly bad rumors again about Beaufort.

That afternoon the announcement of Beaufort’s failure was in all the papers. It fell on New York like soot—dulling everything it touched, even the earlier report of Mrs. Manson Mingott’s stroke. Only the few who knew the obscure thread that bound the two events wondered if old Catherine’s seizure had been something more than age and flesh rebelling at last.

The tale of Beaufort’s dishonor darkened drawing-rooms and club-corners alike. There had never, Mr. Letterblair said, been a worse case in his memory—nor, for that matter, in the memory of the far-off Letterblair who had given his name to the firm. The bank had continued to take in money for a whole day after failure was inevitable; and since so many of its clients belonged to one or another of the ruling clans, Beaufort’s duplicity tasted doubly rancid—like finding stale meat hidden beneath fresh herbs.

If Mrs. Beaufort had not struck the note that such misfortunes (the word was her own) were “the test of friendship,” compassion might have softened the general indignation against her husband. As it was—especially after the object of her nocturnal visit to Mrs. Manson Mingott had become known—her cynicism was judged to exceed his. And she had not the excuse, nor her detractors the satisfaction, of pleading that she was “a foreigner.”

It comforted those whose securities were safe to remind themselves that Beaufort was foreign; but when a Dallas of South Carolina took Beaufort’s view and glibly spoke of his soon being “on his feet again,” the argument lost its savor, and nothing remained but to accept this dreadful proof of the indissolubility of marriage. Society must manage without the Beauforts; that was all—except for the hapless victims of the disaster: Medora Manson, the poor old Miss Lannings, and certain other misguided ladies of good family who might have been spared if only they had listened to Mr. Henry van der Luyden.

“The best thing the Beauforts can do,” Mrs. Archer pronounced, as if diagnosing a fever and prescribing broth, “is to go and live at Regina’s little place in North Carolina. Beaufort has always kept a racing stable, and he had better breed trotting horses. I should say he had all the qualities of a successful horsedealer.”

Everyone agreed, with that easy unanimity by which New York seasoned its judgments, but no one stooped to inquire what the Beauforts meant to do. They were already being scraped from the social platter.

The next day Mrs. Manson Mingott was much better. Her voice returned enough for her to order that no one should mention the Beauforts to her again, and when Dr. Bencomb appeared she demanded to know what her family meant by making such a fuss.

“If people of my age will eat chicken-salad in the evening what are they to expect?” she asked.

It was said with her usual imperious humor, but there was something revealing in the very domesticity of it: as though the grand dramas of money and disgrace could be reduced, in the end, to the stubborn facts of appetite and digestion. Bencomb, seizing the opening like a cook who knows how to rescue a dish, modified her diet, and the stroke was transformed—conveniently, almost miraculously—into an attack of indigestion.

Yet in spite of her firmness old Catherine did not wholly recover her former attitude toward life. The remoteness of age, though it had not diminished her curiosity, had blunted a compassion never very lively; and she had no difficulty in letting Beaufort’s ruin slide out of her mind. But for the first time she grew absorbed in her own symptoms, and began to take a sentimental interest in family members she had formerly dismissed.

Mr. Welland, in particular, became—by a strange turn of fortune—the object of her attention. Of her sons-in-law he was the one she had most consistently ignored; and all his wife’s efforts to paint him as forceful and clever “if he had only chosen” had been met with Catherine’s derisive chuckle. Now, however, his eminence as a valetudinarian made him precious to her, and she issued an imperial summons that he should come and compare diets as soon as his temperature permitted—for old Catherine was suddenly the first to recognize that one could not be too careful about temperatures.

Twenty-four hours after Madame Olenska’s summons a telegram announced that she would arrive from Washington on the evening of the following day.

At the Wellands’—where the Newland Archers happened to be lunching—the question of who should meet her at Jersey City sprang up at once, sharpened by the Welland household’s continual struggle with material difficulties, as if it were a frontier post improvising meals from scant stores. Servants, horses, carriages, appointments—everything had to be portioned out like rations.

It was decided that Mrs. Welland could not possibly go, because she was to accompany her husband to old Catherine’s that afternoon, and the brougham could not be spared: if Mr. Welland were “upset” by seeing his mother-in-law for the first time after her attack, he might have to be taken home at a moment’s notice. The Welland sons would, of course, be “down town”; Mr. Lovell Mingott would be hurrying back from shooting, and the Mingott carriage would be engaged to meet him; and one could not ask May, at the close of a winter afternoon, to go alone across the ferry to Jersey City—even in her own carriage.

And yet it might appear inhospitable—contrary to old Catherine’s express wishes—if Madame Olenska were allowed to arrive with no one at the station. Mrs. Welland’s tired voice implied that it was just like Ellen to place the family in such a dilemma.

“It’s always one thing after another,” the poor lady grieved, in one of her rare little revolts; “the only thing that makes me think Mamma must be less well than Dr. Bencomb will admit is this morbid desire to have Ellen come at once, however inconvenient it is to meet her.”

The words were thoughtless, as impatience so often is; and Mr. Welland pounced on them instantly.

“Augusta,” he said, turning pale and setting down his fork with the gravity of a man laying down a surgical instrument, “have you any other reason for thinking that Bencomb is less to be relied on than he was? Have you noticed that he has been less conscientious than usual in following up my case or your mother’s?”

Mrs. Welland, going pale in her turn as the consequences of her blunder unrolled before her, managed to laugh, and even helped herself to a second portion of scalloped oysters, as if butter and breadcrumb crust could shore up her cheerfulness. Then, struggling back into her old armor, she said: “My dear, how could you imagine such a thing? I only meant that, after the decided stand Mamma took about its being Ellen’s duty to go back to her husband, it seems strange that she should be seized with this sudden whim to see her, when there are half a dozen other grandchildren she might have asked for. But we must never forget that Mamma, in spite of her wonderful vitality, is a very old woman.”

Mr. Welland’s brow stayed clouded; his anxious imagination had latched on to that last phrase as a tooth catches on gristle.

“Yes—your mother’s a very old woman; and for all we know Bencomb may not be as successful with very old people. As you say, my dear, it’s always one thing after another; and in another ten or fifteen years I suppose I shall have the pleasing duty of looking about for a new doctor. It’s always better to make such a change before it’s absolutely necessary.”

Having arrived at this Spartan decision, he firmly took up his fork again.

“But all the while,” Mrs. Welland began, as she rose from table and led the way into the wilderness of purple satin and malachite called the back drawing-room, “I don’t see how Ellen’s to be got here tomorrow evening; and I do like to have things settled for at least twenty-four hours ahead.”

Archer, who had been staring with an odd fascination at a small painting of two cardinals carousing—its reds like spilled wine—set in octagonal ebony with onyx medallions, turned.

“Shall I fetch her?” he proposed. “I can easily get away from the office in time to meet the brougham at the ferry, if May will send it there.”

His heart beat with a sudden, illicit liveliness, as though the very prospect of seeing Ellen had thrown open a window in him. Mrs. Welland sighed with gratitude; May, who had moved to the window, turned and beamed approval.

“So you see, Mamma, everything will be settled twenty-four hours in advance,” May said, bending to kiss her mother’s troubled forehead.

May’s brougham waited at the door, and she was to drive Archer to Union Square so he could catch a Broadway car to the office. As she settled into her corner, she said, carefully: “I didn’t want to worry Mamma by raising fresh obstacles; but how can you meet Ellen tomorrow, and bring her back to New York, when you’re going to Washington?”

“Oh, I’m not going,” Archer answered.

“Not going? Why—what’s happened?” Her voice was clear as a bell, full of the bright correctness of wifely concern.

“The case is off—postponed.”

“Postponed? How odd! I saw a note this morning from Mr. Letterblair to Mamma saying that he was going to Washington tomorrow for the big patent case that he was to argue before the Supreme Court. You said it was a patent case, didn’t you?”

“Well—that’s it: the whole office can’t go. Letterblair decided to go this morning.”

“Then it’s not postponed?” she persisted—an insistence so unlike her that Archer felt the heat rise to his face, as if he were blushing for her lapse from the old, trained delicacies. It wasn’t the question that hurt; it was the sight of her making believe she hadn’t caught him.

“No; but my going is,” he said, cursing the needless particulars he had offered when he first spoke of Washington, and remembering how he had once read that clever liars give details, but the cleverest do not.

It stung him less to lie to May than to watch her struggle to keep the lie from naming itself between them. He took refuge, meanly, in sarcasm.

“I’m not going till later on—luckily for the convenience of your family.”

As he spoke he felt her looking at him. He forced himself to meet her eyes, not wishing to seem to evade them. Their glances touched, and in that instant something passed—some knowledge, sharp as the chime of a bell heard too near—that perhaps neither of them wanted.

“Yes; it is awfully convenient,” May answered brightly, “that you should be able to meet Ellen after all; you saw how much Mamma appreciated your offering to do it.”

“Oh, I’m delighted to do it.”

The carriage stopped. As he sprang down, she leaned forward and laid her hand on his.

“Good-bye, dearest,” she said; and her eyes were so intensely blue that afterward he wondered if they had shone through tears.

He turned away and hurried across Union Square, where the city’s din broke against him in hard waves, and repeated to himself in an inward chant—measuring time as a cook measures heat, as a lover measures possibility: “It’s all of two hours from Jersey City to old Catherine’s. It’s all of two hours—and it may be more.”

His wife’s dark blue brougham—so new that the varnish still had that faint, resinous bite when the door shut—was waiting for Archer at the ferry. It carried him with a kind of cushioned propriety to the Pennsylvania terminus in Jersey City, where the afternoon had turned sombre and snow-laden, and the station’s iron ribs threw back every sound: bootheels worrying the planks, porters calling out, wheels grinding, the hiss of steam like a creature taking breath.

Gas-lamps were already lit, their flames fretting in glass globes and casting a buttery light on the crowd. He paced the platform with the impatience of a man who has measured desire in minutes. In the big reverberating space he remembered, with sudden irritation, that there were people who believed the day would come when a tunnel would be bored under the Hudson and the trains would slide straight into New York—as if the river were only an inconvenience to be drilled through, not a widening of the world, a pause, a crossing.

They were the same sort who predicted ships that would eat up the Atlantic in five days, machines that would fly, light conjured from wires, voices carried without any wire at all—Arabian Nights made mechanical. Archer thought, almost childishly: let all the other marvels arrive if they must, but let the tunnel wait. He wanted the interval, the old ritual of separation and approach—the long minute when the carriage would stand quiet on the ferry, and the city would drift away as if it were the world itself rolling to the other side of the sun.

In his mind he kept setting the scene like a table: Madame Olenska stepping down, the sight of her from far off among meaningless faces, her hand finding his arm as naturally as a spoon finds the mouth; then the slow crawl toward the wharf through slipping horses and carts heavy with coal, with the hoarse cries of teamsters and the clatter of iron rims on frozen ruts; and then, astonishingly, the ferry’s hush, where snow muffled everything and the water’s low slap against the timbers sounded like breath in sleep. They would sit side by side in the motionless brougham under the falling flakes, and he would tell her—everything. The words arranged themselves with absurd elegance on his tongue, as if he had been rehearsing for months.

The train came in with a clanging groan, staggering to rest like a monster dragging its weight home. Archer pressed forward, shouldering through damp wool and fur, staring into window after window. Then, suddenly, she was there—so close that her face filled the glass: pale, surprised, alive. And with the shock of seeing her, he had again that mortified sensation of having forgotten what she looked like, as though memory were always too coarse a language for her features.

They reached each other; their hands met; he drew her arm through his.

“This way—I have the carriage,” he said.

After that it happened almost as he had imagined. He helped her into the brougham with her bags. He remembered, later, in a blur, that he had said the proper soothing things about her grandmother and had given her the briefest summary of the Beaufort situation. He saw, in her immediate softness, how easily pity rose in her—“Poor Regina!” she murmured, as if the phrase had a taste she could not spit out.

Meanwhile the brougham worked itself out of the snarl of cabs and wagons, and they crawled down the slippery incline to the wharf. Coal-carts swayed past like dark cliffs; horses’ flanks steamed; an express-wagon jolted by, dishevelled with rope ends; and then—an empty hearse, black as a shut mouth. At the sight of it she shut her eyes and clutched Archer’s hand, the leather of her glove stiff and cold.

“If only it doesn’t mean—poor Granny!”

“Oh, no, no—she’s much better—she’s all right, really. There—we’ve passed it!” he exclaimed, as though the mere act of passing could change an omen’s meaning.

Her hand stayed in his. As the carriage lurched onto the gang-plank and the ferry took them, he bent over, worked at the tight brown glove as carefully as if he were untying some small, sacred parcel, and kissed her palm with the reverence of a man kissing a relic. She drew her hand back with a faint smile.

“You didn’t expect me today?” he asked.

“Oh, no.”

“I meant to go to Washington to see you. I’d made all my arrangements—I very nearly crossed you in the train.”

“Oh—” she breathed, as if frightened by the narrowness of their escape.

Then she said, turning her eyes on him with a candour that unsettled him: “Do you know—I hardly remembered you?”

“Hardly remembered me?”

“I mean—how shall I explain? I—it’s always so. Each time you happen to me all over again.”

Something in him answered with a force that was almost painful. “Oh, yes: I know! I know!”

“Does it—do I too: to you?” he insisted.

She nodded, looking out of the window. Outside, snow streaked the dusk; beyond the glass the world was rubbed into shadow, and the ferry’s motion made it seem as though the earth itself were sliding away.

“Ellen—Ellen—Ellen!” he said, on a breath.

She did not answer. He sat in silence, watching her profile blur against the whitening air. Four months: what had she done with them? Where had her days been laid away? How near they were, shoulder and sleeve almost touching, and how helplessly remote; and in that darkness of the carriage, where they could not fully see each other’s faces, the nearness and remoteness felt like the very shape of their fate.

“What a pretty carriage! Is it May’s?” she asked suddenly, turning from the window.

“Yes.”

“It was May who sent you to fetch me, then? How kind of her!”

The words struck him like the clean ring of a spoon against china—domestic, innocent, and sharp. He was silent too long; then something in him flared, ugly with jealousy and the wish to retaliate.

“Your husband’s secretary came to see me the day after we met in Boston.”

In the brief letter he had sent her he had not mentioned M. Rivière. He had meant to bury the incident as one buries a bone in the body: out of sight, still felt. But the reminder that they sat in his wife’s brougham pulled the bitter thing up.

As on other occasions when he had expected to startle her from composure, she showed no surprise at all. And at once he concluded—irrationally, violently—that there must be correspondence.

“M. Rivière went to see you?” she said.

“Yes: didn’t you know?”

“No,” she answered simply.

“And you’re not surprised?”

She hesitated. “Why should I be? He told me in Boston that he knew you; that he’d met you in England, I think.”

“Ellen—I must ask you one thing.”

“Yes.”

“I wanted to ask it after I saw him, but I couldn’t put it in a letter.” His heart knocked against his ribs, thick and suffocating. “It was Rivière who helped you to get away—when you left your husband?”

“Yes: I owe him a great debt,” she answered, in the same quiet voice, without a tremor.

Her naturalness, almost an indifferent clarity, calmed him in spite of himself. Once again her sheer simplicity made him feel absurdly conventional at the very moment he believed he was tearing convention down.

“I think you’re the most honest woman I ever met!” he burst out.

“Oh, no—but probably one of the least fussy,” she returned, and there was a smile in her voice, like warmth passing briefly over cold hands.

“Call it what you like: you look at things as they are.”

“Ah—I’ve had to. I’ve had to look at the Gorgon.”

“Well—it hasn’t blinded you! You’ve seen that she’s just an old bogey like all the others.”

“She doesn’t blind one,” Ellen said slowly; “but she dries up one’s tears.”

The answer checked the plea rising to his mouth. It came from somewhere in her experience he could not reach, as if she spoke in a dialect he could hear but not speak, a language of endurance whose meanings wouldn’t translate into the words he had learned at his mother’s table.

The ferry’s slow advance ceased; the bows bumped the piles with a jarring violence that made the brougham stagger, throwing them together. Archer, trembling, felt the pressure of her shoulder, and his arm went around her as naturally as hunger to bread.

“If you’re not blind, then, you must see that this can’t last.”

“What can’t?”

“Our being together—and not together.”

“No. You ought not to have come today,” she said, and her voice changed—strained, urgent.

Then she turned, flung her arms about him, and pressed her lips to his.

At that same moment the carriage began to move, and a gas-lamp at the head of the slip flashed its hard light through the window, bleaching their faces, exposing them to the world’s gaze even in that enclosed dark. She drew away at once. They sat silent and motionless while the brougham struggled through the congestion of carriages at the landing, the whole place loud again with wheels and shouted orders, the wet smack of the river replaced by the city’s incessant grinding.

As they gained the street Archer began to speak quickly, as if speed could outrun consequence.

“Don’t be afraid of me: you needn’t squeeze yourself back into your corner like that. A stolen kiss isn’t what I want.” He forced his hands to remain where they were, as if he were proving something. “Look: I’m not even trying to touch the sleeve of your jacket. Don’t suppose that I don’t understand your reasons for not wanting to let this feeling between us dwindle into an ordinary hole-and-corner love-affair. I couldn’t have spoken like this yesterday, because when we’ve been apart, and I’m looking forward to seeing you, every thought is burnt up in a great flame. But then you come; and you’re so much more than I remembered, and what I want of you is so much more than an hour or two every now and then, with wastes of thirsty waiting between, that I can sit perfectly still beside you, like this, with that other vision in my mind, just quietly trusting to it to come true.”

For a moment she did not answer. Then she asked, barely above a whisper: “What do you mean by trusting to it to come true?”

“Why—you know it will, don’t you?”

“Your vision of you and me together?” She gave a sudden, hard laugh. “You choose your place well to put it to me!”

“Do you mean because we’re in my wife’s brougham? Shall we get out and walk, then? I don’t suppose you mind a little snow?”

She laughed again, softer, but there was no mirth in it. “No; I shan’t get out and walk, because my business is to get to Granny’s as quickly as I can. And you’ll sit beside me, and we’ll look, not at visions, but at realities.”

“I don’t know what you mean by realities. The only reality to me is this.”

She let the words hang. The carriage rolled down an obscure side-street, then turned into the searching illumination of Fifth Avenue, where lamps and shopfronts made every flake glitter like ground salt. The city’s sound changed too: less wharf-chaos, more the controlled roar of money—carriages, sleigh-bells, the hard rhythm of hooves on packed snow, the occasional sharp crack like a whip.

“Is it your idea, then,” she asked at last, “that I should live with you as your mistress—since I can’t be your wife?”

The word startled him by its nakedness. Women of his world dodged it as they dodged certain smells in a kitchen—pretending they did not exist because acknowledging them would admit the body’s truth. But Ellen pronounced it as if it belonged to speech, as if she had had to learn it in a place where euphemisms were a luxury.

He floundered. “I want—I want somehow to get away with you into a world where words like that—categories like that—won’t exist. Where we shall be simply two human beings who love each other, who are the whole of life to each other; and nothing else on earth will matter.”

She drew a deep sigh that ended in another laugh. “Oh, my dear—where is that country? Have you ever been there?”

He had no answer ready; the silence between them thickened with the carriage’s muffled motion.

“I know so many who’ve tried to find it,” she went on; “and, believe me, they all got out by mistake at wayside stations: at places like Boulogne, or Pisa, or Monte Carlo—and it wasn’t at all different from the old world they’d left, but only rather smaller and dingier and more promiscuous.”

He had never heard her speak like this—so stripped of hope, so sharpened by knowledge. He remembered her phrase.

“Yes, the Gorgon has dried your tears,” he said.

“Well, she opened my eyes too; it’s a delusion to say that she blinds people. What she does is just the contrary—she fastens their eyelids open, so that they’re never again in the blessed darkness. Isn’t there a Chinese torture like that? There ought to be. Ah, believe me, it’s a miserable little country!”

They had crossed Forty-second Street. The brougham-horse—May’s sturdy animal—pulled them northward with the steady confidence of a creature trained to obey and not to question, as if he could outrun, by sheer muscle, whatever waited at the end.

Archer choked on the sense of wasted minutes, on words that seemed to die as soon as they were spoken, lost in the carriage’s small, upholstered darkness and the city’s relentless noise outside.

“Then what, exactly, is your plan for us?” he asked.

“For us? But there’s no us in that sense!” Her voice steadied, not with cruelty but with a kind of grim tenderness. “We’re near each other only if we stay far from each other. Then we can be ourselves. Otherwise we’re only Newland Archer, the husband of Ellen Olenska’s cousin, and Ellen Olenska, the cousin of Newland Archer’s wife, trying to be happy behind the backs of the people who trust them.”

“Ah, I’m beyond that,” he groaned.

“No, you’re not! You’ve never been beyond. And I have,” she said, and there was something in her tone like a door closing, “and I know what it looks like there.”

He sat silent, dazed with pain he could not put into the language of his life. He groped along the side of the carriage for the little bell that signalled orders to the coachman. He remembered May rang twice when she wished to stop. He pressed the bell, and the carriage drew up beside the curbstone.

“Why are we stopping? This is not Granny’s,” Ellen exclaimed.

“No: I shall get out here,” he stammered, opening the door and jumping down into the raw air.

By the light of a street-lamp he saw her startled face and the instinctive motion she made to detain him—her hand half-lifted, as if touch could pull him back from his own decision. He closed the door and leaned a moment into the window.

“You’re right: I ought not to have come today,” he said, lowering his voice so the coachman would not hear.

She bent forward, as though to speak, but he had already called out the order to drive on. The brougham rolled away, the wheels hissing over packed snow, carrying with it the faint smell of leather and varnish and the warmth of her presence, as if those too belonged to his wife’s household and could not be kept.

He stood on the corner. The snow had stopped, and a tingling wind rose, clean and cruel, whipping his face. In that sudden, sharpened cold he felt something stiff on his lashes and understood, with a bewildered shame, that he had been crying and the wind had frozen the tears where they lay.

He thrust his hands deep into his pockets and walked fast down Fifth Avenue to his own house, through the city’s clattering, indifferent music.

That evening, when Archer came down before dinner, the drawing-room waited for him like a covered dish gone cold—orderly, polished, and mute, with not so much as a whisper of fire in it. Since Mrs. Manson Mingott had been struck down, the family’s dinners and visits had been postponed with the same solemnity with which a cook shutters a kitchen in mourning; and Archer and May, left to dine alone, moved through their quiet house as if through a well-run pantry where every jar was labeled and nothing unexpected ever fermented.

May was usually first—punctual as the dinner-bell itself—so his surprise sharpened into a small, persistent uneasiness. Upstairs, while he dressed, he had heard her footsteps and the soft drag of drawers, the faint metallic click of hairpins; proof she was at home, only delayed. He found himself clinging to such little proofs the way a man in a storm clings to the sound of a lighthouse bell, counting each pulse to keep his mind from drifting. Perhaps, he thought with a sudden sour sympathy, that was what had kept Mr. Welland so devoted to minor household details all these years: a careful husbandry of trifles to keep wilder visions from invading the domestic table.

When May finally entered, Archer saw at once that something in her had dimmed. She wore the low-necked, tightly-laced dinner dress that the Mingott ceremonial demanded even on the most “informal” evenings—an absurd insistence on trussing youth like poultry for a family feast. Her fair hair had been wound into its familiar accumulated coils with the patience of a hand that had done the same task in the same mirror for generations; but her face, in contrast, was wan, almost faded, as if the daylight had scalded it and left it pale. Yet she turned on him her practiced tenderness, and her eyes still held the blue dazzle of the day before.

“What became of you, dear?” she asked. “I was waiting at Granny’s, and Ellen came alone, and said she had dropped you on the way because you had to rush off on business. There’s nothing wrong?”

“Only some letters I’d forgotten,” he said, “and wanted to get off before dinner.”

“Ah—” May’s voice, after that little catch, steadied itself again. A moment later she added, too evenly: “I’m sorry you didn’t come to Granny’s—unless the letters were urgent.”

“They were,” Archer returned, surprised at the insistence, and suddenly irritated by the way she held to a small point as if it were a bone to be picked clean. “Besides, I don’t see why I should have gone to your grandmother’s. I didn’t know you were there.”

She turned away to the looking-glass above the mantel. Standing there, lifting her long arm to fasten a puff that had slipped in the intricate architecture of her hair, she looked to him oddly languid, as if the springiness had gone out of her, as if even her obedience to convention had begun to tire her. He wondered, with an uncomfortable pinch of conscience, whether the dead level of their days had laid its weight on her too.

And then he remembered: that morning, as he had gone out, she had called down from the landing that she would meet him at her grandmother’s so they might drive home together. He had answered lightly—too lightly—“Yes!” and then, his mind already elsewhere, had forgotten. The omission was small enough to be swallowed whole by any generous appetite; yet now it lay between them like an unacknowledged bitterness in a sauce.

Compunction pricked him, but irritation rose with it. Nearly two years of marriage, and still a trifling lapse could be stored up and served back at dinner like a cold leftover. He was weary of living in a perpetual tepid honeymoon—no heat of passion, yet all the obligations, all the careful attendance, all the expectation that he would be endlessly attentive to the minute cravings of a life arranged to exclude hunger.

To cover his annoyance, he asked after Mrs. Mingott. May said her grandmother was improving, but had been disturbed by the latest news about the Beauforts.

“What news?”

“They’re going to stay in New York. I believe he’s going into an insurance business, or something. They’re looking about for a small house.”

It was too preposterous for discussion—Beaufort reduced to hawking security like a peddler, the Beaufort name being packed into a “small house” like furniture in a cart. The absurdity sat on Archer’s tongue and would not be spoken, and they went in to dinner.

The meal was served with the Welland perfection—courses arriving as reliably as the chiming of a clock. There was soup with the faint, safe perfume of long-simmered stock; a fish course whose butter sauce shone without daring; game carved with reverence and no appetite; and the familiar sweets that ended the ritual as inevitably as a hymn. Yet even the most carefully managed table has its silences, and Archer noticed May made no allusion to Madame Olenska—neither to her presence at old Catherine’s nor to how she had been received.

He was grateful, and yet the omission seemed ominous, like a bird refusing to sing in a garden where it always sang before.

After dinner they went up to the library for coffee. Archer lit a cigar and took down Michelet, choosing history as he had taken to doing in the evenings—less from any devotion to France than from a wish to avoid poetry. If May saw him with verse she had lately begun to ask him to read aloud, and then to offer her own comments—opinions she had once borrowed obediently from him, but now produced with a conscientious freshness that spoiled for him what he loved. History, at least, could be held at arm’s length, and its dead voices could not be made to blush under her judgments.

May fetched her workbasket and drew up an armchair to the green-shaded student lamp. She uncovered a cushion she was embroidering for his sofa, stabbing at the canvas with slow diligence. Her hands—large, capable, meant for oars and reins and the clean resistance of outdoor life—were not made for such finicking labor, but since other wives embroidered cushions for their husbands, she would not omit this final stitch in the tapestry of devotion.

She sat so that, by merely lifting his eyes, Archer could see her bent over the frame; the ruffled elbow sleeves slipping back from firm round arms; the betrothal sapphire shining above the broad gold wedding ring; the right hand driving the needle down and drawing it up again, as if trying to force softness out of stubborn threads. The lamplight lay full on her clear brow, and with a secret dismay he told himself that he would always know the thoughts behind it. Never—through all the years ahead—would she startle him by an unexpected mood, a new idea, a weakness, a cruelty, an emotion. She had spent her romance in their brief courting as one spends the rarest spices: lavishly, and then—when the dish is served—there is no more to add. Now she was ripening into a copy of her mother, and by some obscure domestic alchemy trying to turn him into a Mr. Welland.

He laid down his book and stood up abruptly; at once she raised her head.

“What’s the matter?”

“The room is stifling. I want a little air.”

He had insisted that the library curtains should run on a rod, so they could actually be drawn, not nailed in place and looped over lace like a parlor’s dead drapery. He pulled them back and pushed up the sash, leaning out into the icy night.

The cold struck his face like a hard truth. Down the street the city made its winter music: the thin crunch of a late carriage wheel, the far-off iron ring of hoof on frozen ground, a muffled shout dissolving into distance. Above it all, the wind worried at cornices and chimneys with a steady hiss, a cleaner sound than the thick, upholstered hush indoors. The mere fact of not looking at May—seated beside his table, under his lamp, in the circle of domestic light—was a relief. Other houses rose in darkness, other roofs and chimneys, other lives behind other windows; and beyond them, other cities beyond New York, and a whole world beyond his world. The sense of that larger breathing space cleared his brain.

After a few minutes he heard May’s voice, drawn a little tight: “Newland! Do shut the window. You’ll catch your death.”

He pulled the sash down and turned back. “Catch my death!” he echoed, and felt the savage impulse to add: But I’ve caught it already. I am dead—I’ve been dead for months.

And then the word—death—leaped up in him, not as a metaphor but as a wild practical suggestion, as if a door he had never allowed himself to see had swung open on its hinges.

What if it were she who was dead?

If May were to die—soon—leave him free—

He stood there in the warm familiar room and looked at her and wished her dead; and the strangeness of the wish, the intoxicating release it promised, held him so completely that its horror did not strike him at once. He felt only that chance had offered him a new possibility to which his sick soul might cling. People did die: young people, healthy people. May might die, and set him free.

She glanced up; and he saw by the widening of her eyes that something in his own face had betrayed him.

“Newland! Are you ill?”

He shook his head quickly and turned toward his chair. She bent over her work again, and as he passed he laid his hand on her hair.

“Poor May,” he said.

“Poor? Why poor?” she echoed, with a strained little laugh.

“Because I shall never be able to open a window without worrying you,” he answered, and laughed too.

For a moment she was silent; then, very low, her head bowed over the canvas, she said: “I shall never worry if you’re happy.”

“Ah, my dear; and I shall never be happy unless I can open the windows.”

“In this weather?” she protested; and with a sigh he buried himself in Michelet, as if the dead past could drown out the living present.

Six or seven days passed. Archer heard nothing from Madame Olenska, and became aware that her name would not be spoken in his presence by any member of the family—as if it were a pungent spice banned from the household lest it awaken appetites. He did not try to see her; while she watched at old Catherine’s guarded bedside it would have been almost impossible. In the uncertainty he let himself drift, conscious somewhere below the surface of a resolve that had come to him at the open window. That resolve—dark, steady—made it easy to wait and make no sign.

Then one day May told him that Mrs. Manson Mingott had asked to see him.

There was nothing surprising in the request. The old lady was steadily recovering, and she had always declared openly that she preferred Archer to any other of her grandsons-in-law. May delivered the message with evident pleasure; she was proud of her grandmother’s approval of her husband, as if it were another course served up in honor of their marriage.

A pause, and then Archer said, as duty required: “All right. Shall we go together this afternoon?”

May’s face brightened at once, then she answered quickly: “Oh, you’d much better go alone. It bores Granny to see the same people too often.”

His heart began to beat violently as he rang old Mrs. Mingott’s bell. He had wanted above all things to go alone, for he felt sure the visit would give him a chance to speak a word in private to the Countess Olenska. He meant to wait until the chance presented itself naturally. Here it was; here he stood on the doorstep.

Behind the door, behind the curtains of the yellow damask room next to the hall, she was surely waiting. In another moment he should see her, speak to her before she led him to the sick-room. He wanted only to ask one question. What was the date of her return to Washington? She could hardly refuse to answer that.

But in the yellow sitting-room it was the mulatto maid who waited, her white teeth shining like a keyboard as she pushed back the sliding doors and ushered him in.

Old Catherine sat in a vast, throne-like armchair near her bed. Beside her stood a mahogany table bearing a cast bronze lamp with an engraved globe, topped by a green paper shade like a little canopy. There was no book within reach, no newspaper, no basket of knitting, no feminine pretense of busy fingers: conversation had always been Mrs. Mingott’s sole occupation, and she would have scorned to garnish it with fancywork. Archer saw no trace of the distortion left by her stroke. She looked paler, the shadows deeper in the folds and recesses of her obesity; and in her fluted mob-cap, tied with a starched bow between her first two chins, and the muslin kerchief crossed over her billowing purple dressing-gown, she had the air of some shrewd, kindly ancestress—one of those formidable farm-grandmothers who have always known where the best provisions are kept and who has eaten well enough to forgive most human folly.

She held out one of the little hands that nestled in her enormous lap like a pampered animal and called to the maid: “Don’t let in any one else. If my daughters call, say I’m asleep.”

When the maid vanished, the old woman turned her bright attention to Archer.

“My dear, am I perfectly hideous?” she asked gaily, shooting out a hand toward the inaccessible folds of muslin on her bosom. “My daughters tell me it doesn’t matter at my age—as if hideousness didn’t matter all the more the harder it gets to conceal!”

“My dear, you’re handsomer than ever,” Archer returned in her own tone; and she threw back her head and laughed.

“Ah, but not as handsome as Ellen!” she snapped, twinkling maliciously; and before he could answer she added: “Was she so awfully handsome the day you drove her up from the ferry?”

He laughed in spite of himself.

“Was it because you told her so that she had to put you out on the way? In my youth young men didn’t desert pretty women unless they were made to!”

She chuckled again, then cut it short to say almost querulously: “It’s a pity she didn’t marry you; I always told her so. It would have spared me all this worry. But who ever thinks of sparing their grandmother worry?”

For an instant Archer wondered if illness had blurred her discretion. But she broke out at once, brisk and resolute: “Well, it’s settled, anyhow. She’s going to stay with me, whatever the rest of the family say! She hadn’t been here five minutes before I’d have gone down on my knees to keep her—if only, for the last twenty years, I’d been able to see where the floor was!”

He listened, breath quickening, while she went on, her voice gaining force like a kettle coming to boil.

“They’d talked me over—you know that, no doubt. Persuaded me—Lovell and Letterblair and Augusta Welland and all the rest—that I must hold out and cut off her allowance till she was made to see it was her duty to go back to Olenski. They thought they’d convinced me when the secretary, or whatever he was, came out with the last proposals. Handsome proposals, I confess. After all, marriage is marriage, and money’s money—both useful things in their way—and I didn’t know what to answer—”

She paused to draw breath, and Archer heard in that pause the labor of her body, the heavy machinery of a great life refusing to stop.

“But the minute I laid eyes on her,” she resumed, “I said: ‘You sweet bird, you! Shut you up in that cage again? Never!’ And now it’s settled that she’s to stay here and nurse her Granny as long as there’s a Granny to nurse. It’s not a gay prospect, but she doesn’t mind; and of course I’ve told Letterblair she’s to be given her proper allowance.”

The news rushed through Archer with a heat he could not name—joy or pain, relief or fresh torment. He had so firmly decided on a course that for a moment he could not readjust his thoughts. Then, gradually, a delicious sense of difficulties deferred stole over him, of opportunities miraculously provided. If Ellen had consented to live with her grandmother, surely it was because she had recognized she could not give him up. It was her answer to his final appeal: if she would not take the extreme step he had urged, she had at last yielded to the half-measure that still kept her near.

He sank back into that thought with the involuntary relief of a man ready to gamble everything who suddenly tastes the dangerous sweetness of security.

“She couldn’t have gone back—it was impossible!” he exclaimed.

“Ah, my dear, I always knew you were on her side,” Mrs. Mingott said with satisfaction; “and that’s why I sent for you today, and why I said to your pretty wife, when she proposed to come with you: ‘No, my dear, I’m pining to see Newland, and I don’t want anybody to share our transports.’ For you see, my dear—” She drew her head back as far as her tethering chins allowed and fixed him with a look hard and keen as a cook’s carving-knife—“you see, we shall have a fight yet. The family don’t want her here. They’ll say it’s because I’ve been ill, because I’m a weak old woman, that she’s persuaded me. I’m not well enough yet to fight them one by one, and you’ve got to do it for me.”

“I?” Archer stammered.

“You. Why not?” Her round eyes sharpened further, and her hand fluttered from the chair-arm and lit on his with a clutch of little pale nails, bird-claws gripping. “Why not?” she repeated, probing.

Under her gaze Archer recovered himself. “Oh, I don’t count—I’m too insignificant.”

“Well, you’re Letterblair’s partner, ain’t you? You’ve got to get at them through Letterblair. Unless you’ve got a reason,” she insisted, and the word “reason” fell with a weight that made his skin prickle.

He met it with a steadiness he had learned from years of family dinners where the smallest false note was heard. “Oh, my dear, I back you to hold your own against them all without my help; but you shall have it if you need it,” he said.

“Then we’re safe!” she sighed; and smiling on him with all her ancient cunning, she added, settling her head among the cushions: “I always knew you’d back us up, because they never quote you when they talk about its being her duty to go home.”

He winced at her terrifying perspicacity and had a sudden, sharp desire to ask: And May—do they quote her? But he judged it safer to turn the question away from his wife’s mouth.

“And Madame Olenska? When am I to see her?” he asked.

The old lady chuckled, crumpled her lids, and performed archness like an actor who knows the audience will laugh. “Not today. One at a time, please. Madame Olenska’s gone out.”

He flushed with disappointment, and she went on, timing her effect as carefully as any hostess arranging courses.

“She’s gone out, my child—gone in my carriage to see Regina Beaufort.” She paused, letting the name settle, bitter as burnt sugar. “That’s what she’s reduced me to already. The day after she got here she put on her best bonnet and told me, cool as a cucumber, she was going to call on Regina Beaufort. ‘I don’t know her; who is she?’ says I. ‘She’s your grand-niece, and a most unhappy woman,’ she says. ‘She’s the wife of a scoundrel,’ I answered. ‘Well,’ she says, ‘and so am I, and yet all my family want me to go back to him.’ Well, that floored me, and I let her go. And finally one day she said it was raining too hard to go out on foot, and she wanted me to lend her my carriage. ‘What for?’ I asked. And she said: ‘To go and see cousin Regina’—cousin! Now, my dear, I looked out of the window and saw it wasn’t raining a drop; but I understood her, and I let her have the carriage. After all, Regina’s a brave woman, and so is she; and I’ve always liked courage above everything.”

Archer bent and pressed his lips to the little hand still resting on his, as if to seal his vow to her plot against the family.

“Eh—eh—eh!” Mrs. Mingott snapped out with her mocking cackle. “Whose hand did you think you were kissing, young man—your wife’s, I hope?”

As he rose to go she called after him, brisk again, dismissing him as if sending a messenger from her kitchen: “Give her her Granny’s love; but you’d better not say anything about our talk.”

Archer had been stunned by old Catherine’s news. It was only natural that Ellen should have come rushing back from Washington at her grandmother’s summons; but that she should mean to stay under that roof—now, too, when Mrs. Mingott had almost regained her formidable health—was harder to understand, and harder still to endure.

Not that Archer believed Ellen’s decision had been coaxed by money. He knew too exactly the paltry sum Count Olenski had allowed her after their separation, knew how little it amounted to in the Mingott arithmetic of comfort. Without her grandmother’s purse it was scarcely a “living” by any standard the family would have named with approval; and now that poor Medora Manson—who drifted in Ellen’s wake like an ineffectual gull—had been ruined, the same few dollars would barely keep the two women fed, clothed, and warm. Yet Ellen had never had the prudence of the needy. She was capable of lavishness from habit and of renunciation from disdain; she could do without the things her relatives called necessities, and Mrs. Lovell Mingott and Mrs. Welland had long lamented that any one who had known the cosmopolitan plenty of the Olenski establishments should care so little for “how things were done.”

Besides, months had passed since Ellen’s allowance had been cut off; and in all that interval she had made no effort to placate her grandmother. If she had shifted her course now, it was not to win back money. Archer did not have far to seek the reason.

On the way from the ferry she had told him they must remain apart—yet she had said it with her head on his breast. There had been no calculated coquetry in it; she was fighting her fate as he fought his, clinging with desperate fingers to the resolve that they should not betray the people who trusted them. But during the ten days since her return she had perhaps read something in his silence, and in his making no attempt to see her: that he was brooding over a decisive step, a step from which there would be no return. At that thought, fear of her own weakness might have seized her; and she might have felt that, after all, it was safer to accept the compromise society kept ready for such cases—to take the line of least resistance, and let custom do the dirty work.

An hour earlier, when he had rung Mrs. Mingott’s bell, Archer had fancied his path lay straight before him. He meant to have a word alone with Ellen; failing that, to learn from her grandmother on what day and by which train she returned to Washington. That train he intended to take with her, to Washington or farther—Japan, perhaps, the word had flashed through him like a bright strange flavour on the tongue. Wherever she went, he would go: she would understand that at once. And for May he meant to leave a note that would cut off every other alternative.

He had fancied himself not only nerved for the plunge but eager. Yet his first sensation, on hearing the course of events had changed, was relief. And now, walking home from Mrs. Mingott’s, he became aware of a growing distaste for what lay before him. There was nothing unknown in the path he was presumably to tread; he had trodden it before as a free man, accountable to no one, able to lend himself with amused detachment to the game of precautions and prevarications, concealments and compliances, that the part required.

It was called “protecting a woman’s honour.” The novels he had read and the after-dinner talk of his elders had long since initiated him into every detail of the code. But now he saw it differently, and his part in it seemed singularly diminished. It was, in fact, the very role he had watched Mrs. Thorley Rushworth play toward her fond and unperceiving husband: a smiling, bantering, humouring, watchful, incessant lie. A lie by day, a lie by night; a lie in every touch and every look; in every caress and every quarrel; in every word and every silence.

It was easier, and less dastardly, for a wife to play such a part. A woman’s standard of truthfulness was tacitly held to be lower: she was the subject creature, versed in the arts of the enslaved. She could plead nerves and moods, and the right not to be held too strictly to account; and even in the most strait-laced societies the laugh was always against the husband.

But in Archer’s world no one laughed at a wife deceived, and a measure of contempt attached to men who continued their philandering after marriage. In the rotation of crops there was a season for wild oats; they were not to be sown twice. Archer had always shared the view. In his heart he found Lefferts despicable.

Yet to love Ellen Olenska was not to become like Lefferts. For the first time Archer found himself face to face with the dread argument of the individual case. Ellen was like no other woman; he was like no other man; their situation resembled no one else’s; they were answerable to no tribunal but their own judgment.

Yes—but in ten minutes he would be mounting his own doorstep; and there were May, and habit, and honour, and all the old decencies he and his people had believed in. At his corner he hesitated, then walked on down Fifth Avenue.

Ahead of him, in the winter night, loomed a big unlit house. As he drew near he thought how often he had seen it blazing with light, its steps awninged and carpeted, carriages waiting in double line to draw up at the curb. It was in the conservatory, along the side street where the house stretched its dead-black bulk, that he had taken his first kiss from May; it was under the myriad candles of the ballroom that he had seen her appear, tall and silver-shining as a young Diana.

Now the house was as dark as the grave, except for a faint flare of gas in the basement and a light in one upstairs room where the blind had not been lowered. As Archer reached the corner he saw that the carriage at the door was Mrs. Manson Mingott’s. What an opportunity for Sillerton Jackson, if he should happen by.

Old Catherine’s account of Ellen’s attitude toward Mrs. Beaufort had moved Archer; it made New York’s righteous reprobation look like a passing-by on the other side. But he knew well enough what construction the clubs and drawing-rooms would put on Ellen Olenska’s visits to her cousin. He paused and looked up at the lighted window. No doubt the two women were sitting in that room together; Beaufort had likely sought consolation elsewhere. There were rumours he had left New York with Fanny Ring, though Mrs. Beaufort’s posture made the tale seem improbable.

The night gave Archer a Fifth Avenue almost to himself. At that hour most people were indoors, dressing for dinner; and he was secretly glad that Ellen’s exit was likely to be unobserved. As the thought crossed him the door opened, and she came out. Behind her was a faint light, as though some one had carried a lamp down the stairs to show her the way. She turned to say a word to someone; then the door shut and she came down the steps.

“Ellen,” he said in a low voice as she reached the pavement.

She stopped with a slight start—and at that moment he saw two young men in fashionable cut approaching. There was a familiar air in their overcoats, in the way their smart silk mufflers were folded over white ties; and he wondered how youths of their quality happened to be dining out so early. Then he remembered that the Reggie Chiverses, a few doors above, were taking a large party that evening to see Adelaide Neilson in Romeo and Juliet, and guessed the two were among them. They passed under a lamp, and he recognised Lawrence Lefferts and a young Chivers.

A mean desire not to have Ellen seen at the Beauforts’ door vanished as he felt the penetrating warmth of her hand in his.

“I shall see you now—we shall be together,” he broke out, hardly knowing what he said.

“Ah,” she answered, “Granny has told you?”

While he watched her he saw that Lefferts and Chivers, on reaching the far side of the corner, had discreetly struck away across Fifth Avenue. It was the kind of masculine solidarity he himself had often practised; now he sickened at their connivance. Did Ellen truly imagine they could live like this? And if not—what did she imagine?

“Tomorrow I must see you—somewhere we can be alone,” he said, in a voice that sounded almost angry to himself.

She wavered and moved toward the carriage. “But I shall be at Granny’s—for the present, that is,” she added, as if conscious her change of plans required some explanation.

“Somewhere we can be alone,” he insisted.

She gave a faint laugh that grated on him. “In New York? But there are no churches… no monuments.”

“There’s the Art Museum—in the Park,” he said, as she looked puzzled. “At half-past two. I’ll be at the door…”

She turned away without answering and got quickly into the carriage. As it drove off she leaned forward, and he thought she waved her hand in the obscurity.

He stared after her in a turmoil of contradictory feelings. It seemed he had been speaking not to the woman he loved but to another—a woman he was indebted to for pleasures already wearied of. It was hateful to find himself trapped in this hackneyed vocabulary.

“She’ll come,” he said to himself, almost contemptuously.

Avoiding the popular “Wolfe collection,” whose anecdotic canvases filled one of the main galleries of the queer wilderness of cast iron and encaustic tiles known as the Metropolitan Museum, they wandered down a passage to the room where the “Cesnola antiquities” mouldered in unvisited loneliness. They had this melancholy retreat to themselves, and seated on the divan that enclosed the central steam radiator, they stared silently at the glass cabinets in ebonised wood containing the recovered fragments of Ilium.

“It’s odd,” Ellen said at last. “I never came here before.”

“Ah—well. Some day, I suppose, it will be a great Museum.”

“Yes,” she assented absently, as though the word “great” had no nourishment in it.

She rose and wandered across the room. Archer, remaining seated, watched the light movements of her figure—so girlish even under heavy furs—the cleverly planted heron wing in her fur cap, the way a dark curl lay like a flattened vine spiral on each cheek above the ear. His mind, as always when they met, was absorbed in the delicious particulars that made her herself and no other, as though her very presence had a scent and texture the world had lacked before.

Presently he rose and approached the case before which she stood. Its glass shelves were crowded with small broken objects—hardly recognisable domestic utensils, ornaments, and personal trifles—made of glass, clay, discoloured bronze, and other time-blurred substances.

“It seems cruel,” she said, “that after a while nothing matters… any more than these little things, that used to be necessary and important to forgotten people, and now have to be guessed at under a magnifying glass and labelled: ‘Use unknown.’”

“Yes; but meanwhile—”

“Ah, meanwhile—”

As she stood there in her long sealskin coat, hands thrust into a small round muff, veil drawn down like a transparent mask to the tip of her nose, the bunch of violets he had brought her stirring with her quickly taken breath, it seemed incredible that such harmony of line and colour should ever be altered by the stupid law of change.

“Meanwhile everything matters—that concerns you,” he said.
