She looked at him thoughtfully, then turned back to the divan. He sat down beside her and waited; but suddenly he heard a step echoing far off down the empty rooms and felt the pressure of the minutes.

“What is it you wanted to tell me?” she asked, as if she too had heard the warning.

“What I wanted to tell you?” he rejoined. “Why—that I believe you came to New York because you were afraid.”

“Afraid?”

“Of my coming to Washington.”

She looked down at her muff, and he saw her hands stir in it uneasily.

“Well—?”

“Well—yes,” she said.

“You were afraid? You knew—?”

“Yes. I knew…”

“Well, then?” he insisted.

“Well, then: this is better, isn’t it?” she returned, with a long questioning sigh.

“Better—?”

“We shall hurt others less. Isn’t it, after all, what you always wanted?”

“To have you here, you mean—in reach and yet out of reach? To meet you in this way, on the sly? It’s the very reverse of what I want. I told you the other day what I wanted.”

She hesitated. “And you still think this—worse?”

“A thousand times!” He paused. “It would be easy to lie to you; but the truth is I think it detestable.”

“Oh, so do I!” she cried, drawing a deep breath of relief.

He sprang up impatiently. “Well, then—it’s my turn to ask: what is it, in God’s name, that you think better?”

She hung her head and continued to clasp and unclasp her hands in her muff. The step drew nearer, and a guardian in a braided cap walked listlessly through the room like a ghost in a necropolis. They fixed their eyes simultaneously on the case opposite; and when the official figure had vanished down a vista of mummies and sarcophagi Archer spoke again.

“What do you think better?”

Instead of answering she murmured: “I promised Granny to stay with her because it seemed to me that here I should be safer.”

“From me?”

She bent her head slightly without looking at him.

“Safer from loving me?”

Her profile did not stir, but he saw a tear overflow on her lashes and hang in the mesh of her veil.

“Safer from doing irreparable harm. Don’t let us be like all the others!” she protested.

“What others? I don’t profess to be different from my kind. I’m consumed by the same wants and the same longings.”

She glanced at him with a kind of terror, and a faint colour crept into her cheeks.

“Shall I—once come to you; and then go home?” she suddenly hazarded in a low clear voice.

The blood rushed to Archer’s forehead.

“Dearest,” he said, without moving. It was as if he held his heart in his hands like a full cup, and the least motion might spill it. Then her last phrase struck his ear and his face clouded.

“Go home? What do you mean by going home?”

“Home to my husband.”

“And you expect me to say yes to that?”

She raised troubled eyes to his. “What else is there? I can’t stay here and lie to the people who’ve been good to me.”

“But that’s the very reason why I ask you to come away!”

“And destroy their lives, when they’ve helped me to remake mine?”

He stood looking down on her in inarticulate despair. It would have been easy to say: Yes—come; come once. He knew what power she would put into his hands if she consented; he could then persuade her not to go back. But something silenced the words. There was in her a passionate honesty that made it inconceivable he should lure her into the familiar trap.

“If I were to let her come,” he said to himself, “I should have to let her go again.” And that could not be imagined.

Yet he saw the shadow of her lashes on her wet cheek and wavered.

“After all,” he began again, “we have lives of our own… There’s no use attempting the impossible. You’re so unprejudiced about some things, so used, as you say, to looking at the Gorgon, that I don’t know why you’re afraid to face our case and see it as it really is—unless you think the sacrifice is not worth making.”

She stood up too, her lips tightening under a rapid frown. “Call it that, then—I must go,” she said, drawing her little watch from her bosom.

She turned away. He followed and caught her by the wrist.

“Well, then: come to me once,” he said, his head turning suddenly at the thought of losing her. For a second or two they looked at each other almost like enemies. “When?” he insisted. “Tomorrow?”

She hesitated. “The day after.”

“Dearest—!” he said again.

She had disengaged her wrist; but they continued for a moment to hold each other’s eyes, and he saw that her face, which had grown very pale, was flooded with a deep inner radiance. His heart beat with awe: he felt he had never before beheld love made visible.

“Oh, I shall be late—good-bye. No, don’t come any farther than this,” she cried, walking hurriedly away down the long room, as if the reflected radiance in his eyes had frightened her. When she reached the door she turned a moment to wave a quick farewell.

Archer walked home alone.

Darkness was falling when he let himself into his house. He looked about at the familiar objects in the hall as though he viewed them from the other side of the grave. The parlour-maid, hearing his step, ran upstairs to light the gas on the upper landing.

“Is Mrs. Archer in?”

“No, sir; Mrs. Archer went out in the carriage after luncheon, and hasn’t come back.”

With a sense of relief he entered the library and flung himself into his armchair. The parlour-maid followed with the student lamp and shook fresh coals onto the dying fire. When she left he sat motionless, elbows on knees, chin on clasped hands, eyes fixed on the red grate.

He sat without conscious thought, without sense of time, in a deep grave amazement that seemed to suspend life rather than quicken it.

“This was what had to be, then… this was what had to be,” he kept repeating, as if he hung in the clutch of doom. What he had dreamed of had been so different that there was a mortal chill in his rapture.

The door opened and May came in.

“I’m dreadfully late—you weren’t worried, were you?” she asked, laying her hand on his shoulder with one of her rare caresses.

He looked up astonished. “Is it late?”

“After seven. I believe you’ve been asleep!” She laughed, and drawing out her hatpins tossed her velvet hat on the sofa. She looked paler than usual, but sparkling with unwonted animation. “I went to see Granny, and just as I was going away Ellen came in from a walk; so I stayed and had a long talk with her. It was ages since we’d had a real talk…”

She had dropped into her usual armchair facing his, running her fingers through her rumpled hair. He fancied she expected him to speak.

“A really good talk,” she went on, smiling with what seemed to Archer an unnatural vividness. “She was so dear—just like the old Ellen. I’m afraid I haven’t been fair to her lately. I’ve sometimes thought—”

Archer stood and leaned against the mantelpiece, out of the lamp’s radius.

“Yes—you’ve thought—?” he echoed as she paused.

“Well, perhaps I haven’t judged her fairly. She’s so different—at least on the surface. She takes up such odd people—she seems to like to make herself conspicuous. I suppose it’s the life she’s led in that fast European society; no doubt we seem dreadfully dull to her. But I don’t want to judge her unfairly.”

She paused again, a little breathless with the unwonted length of her speech, lips slightly parted, a deep blush on her cheeks. As Archer looked at her he was reminded of the glow that had suffused her face in the Mission Garden at St. Augustine. He became aware of the same obscure effort in her, the same reaching out toward something beyond the usual range of her vision.

“She hates Ellen,” he thought, “and she’s trying to overcome the feeling, and to get me to help her overcome it.”

The thought moved him. For a moment he was on the point of breaking the silence between them, of throwing himself on her mercy. But she went on:

“You understand, don’t you, why the family have sometimes been annoyed? We all did what we could for her at first; but she never seemed to understand. And now this idea of going to see Mrs. Beaufort, of going there in Granny’s carriage! I’m afraid she’s quite alienated the van der Luydens…”

“Ah,” said Archer, with an impatient laugh. The open door had closed between them again.

“It’s time to dress; we’re dining out, aren’t we?” he asked, moving from the fire.

She rose also, but lingered near the hearth. As he walked past, she moved forward impulsively, as though to detain him. Their eyes met, and he saw hers were of the same swimming blue as when he had left her to drive to Jersey City. She flung her arms about his neck and pressed her cheek to his.

“You haven’t kissed me today,” she whispered; and he felt her tremble in his arms.

“At the court of the Tuileries,” said Mr. Sillerton Jackson, and the phrase came out of him the way a well-worn spice comes out of a tin—faintly sweet, faintly stale, and always ready—“such things were pretty openly tolerated.”

They were in the van der Luydens’ black-walnut dining-room on Madison Avenue, the air held in a costly stillness by heavy curtains, thick carpets, and the discipline of servants trained to move without a syllable. The long table shone with massive plate and a row of Skuytercliff carnations that looked as if they had been cut with a jeweller’s care—stems trimmed to equal length, blooms opened to precisely the same degree, their clove-like scent pressed down beneath the odor of hot wax and old wood.

Mr. and Mrs. van der Luyden had come in from Skuytercliff for a few days, drawn back to town as if by a rope thrown across the river of scandal. Beaufort’s failure had shaken New York’s social house like a sudden draft down the chimney: doors banged, ashes rose, and every one ran to see who would pretend not to notice the smell of smoke. It had been represented to the van der Luydens—as to a pair of ancestral trees whose shade was thought indispensable—that their presence was more necessary than ever. As Mrs. Archer had explained, with the pious irritation of a woman who knew the weight of such obligations, they “owed it to society” to go to the Opera, and even to open their own doors.

“It will never do, my dear Louisa,” Mrs. Archer had urged, in the tone with which an old farmer’s wife might speak of blight creeping into a treasured orchard, “to let people like Mrs. Lemuel Struthers think they can step into Regina’s shoes. It is just at such times that new people push in and get a footing. It was owing to the epidemic of chicken-pox in New York the winter Mrs. Struthers first appeared that the married men slipped away to her house while their wives were in the nursery. You and dear Henry, Louisa, must stand in the breach as you always have.”

And so—reluctantly, heroically—the van der Luydens had returned, unmuffled the house, and sent out invitations for two dinners and an evening reception, as if reopening shutters to show the street that the old hearth still burned.

This evening’s party was small: Sillerton Jackson; Mrs. Archer; Newland Archer and May. They were to go afterward to the Opera, where Faust was being sung for the first time that winter. Nothing under the van der Luyden roof was done without ceremony; and though there were only four guests besides the hosts, dinner began at seven punctually so that the proper procession of courses could be served without haste before the men settled with their cigars. Even the meal’s quiet sounds—spoons laid down, the soft slide of plates, the muffled pouring of wine—were part of the house’s acoustic order, a carefully kept silence that protected the mind from the rougher noises outside, from the street’s iron wheels and hawkers, from the city’s growing roar.

Archer had not seen May since the night before. He had left early for the office, and in the afternoon a senior partner had taken him unexpectedly; he reached home so late that May had already driven to the van der Luydens’ and sent back the carriage. Now, across the carnations and the bright moat of plate, she struck him as pale and languid; yet her eyes shone with a kind of excited light, and her talk had an exaggerated animation that made him think of a hand that keeps beating egg whites past the right moment—airier, brighter, and suddenly fragile.

It was their hostess who brought up the subject that had tempted Sillerton Jackson to his favorite reminiscence. The Beaufort failure—more precisely, the Beaufort attitude since the failure—was still rich game for drawing-room moralists; and after it had been hunted, worried, and condemned, Mrs. van der Luyden turned her scrupulous gaze on May.

“Is it possible, dear, that what I hear is true? I was told your grandmother Mingott’s carriage was seen standing at Mrs. Beaufort’s door.”

It was noticeable that she no longer called the offending lady by her Christian name, as if even the syllables of “Regina” had become contaminated.

May’s color rose; and Mrs. Archer interposed quickly: “If it was, I’m convinced it was there without Mrs. Mingott’s knowledge.”

“Ah—you think—?” Mrs. van der Luyden paused, sighed, and glanced at her husband. In the pause, the dining-room’s hush made the faintest domestic noises loom: a servant’s careful breath, the distant clink of a dish in the pantry, the soft tick of time in a house built to resist change.

“I’m afraid,” Mr. van der Luyden said, “that Madame Olenska’s kind heart may have led her into the imprudence of calling on Mrs. Beaufort.”

“Or her taste for peculiar people,” Mrs. Archer added dryly; and though her eyes rested with an innocent steadiness on her son’s, the words had the bite of something sharp slipped into a sweet sauce.

“I’m sorry to think it of Madame Olenska,” Mrs. van der Luyden said; and Mrs. Archer murmured, with a sigh that had in it both reproach and proprietary hurt: “Ah, my dear—and after you’d had her twice at Skuytercliff!”

It was then that Jackson, feeling the gaze of the company turn toward him as toward a dish expected at its proper moment, placed his familiar offering on the table.

“At the Tuileries,” he repeated, smiling as if he were uncorking a bottle of old talk, “the standard was excessively lax in some respects; and if you’d asked where Morny’s money came from—or who paid the debts of some of the Court beauties—”

“I hope, dear Sillerton,” Mrs. Archer said, “you are not suggesting that we should adopt such standards?”

“I never suggest,” Jackson returned imperturbably. “But Madame Olenska’s foreign bringing-up may make her less particular—”

“Ah,” the two elder ladies sighed together, as if the single sound covered a whole vocabulary of disapproval that politeness forbade them to articulate. There are judgments in New York that are never spoken in full; they are conveyed by a pause, a look, an “ah”—a dominant lexicon in which a woman’s fate can be decided by what is not said.

“Still, to have kept her grandmother’s carriage at a defaulter’s door!” Mr. van der Luyden protested; and Archer guessed that he was remembering—and resenting—the hampers of carnations he had sent to the little house in Twenty-third Street, as if flowers given in good faith had been made to decorate a betrayal.

“Of course I’ve always said that she looks at things quite differently,” Mrs. Archer summed up.

A flush rose to May’s forehead. She looked straight at Archer and said, too quickly: “I’m sure Ellen meant it kindly.”

“Imprudent people are often kind,” Mrs. Archer returned, as if kindness, when it strayed beyond prescribed boundaries, ceased to be a virtue and became merely a weakness. Mrs. van der Luyden murmured: “If only she had consulted some one—”

“Ah, that she never did!” Mrs. Archer said, with the satisfaction of an accusation that fits too well.

Then Mr. van der Luyden glanced at his wife; she bent her head slightly toward Mrs. Archer; and the glimmering trains of the three ladies swept out while the men remained, as if some rite had been completed and the sexes separated into their appointed chambers.

On Opera nights Mr. van der Luyden supplied short cigars; but they were so good that they made his guests deplore his inexorable punctuality. The first draw was full and mellow, and the smoke lay on the tongue like a dark sweetness. Archer, however, tasted little. He felt the evening’s discourse like a layer of dust over everything—over the carnations’ perfume, over the food’s careful richness, over the smooth ritual of a world that could condemn a woman with a few phrases while still insisting on the correct order of courses.

After the first act he detached himself from the party and made his way to the back of the club box. From there he watched, over various Chivers, Mingott, and Rushworth shoulders, the same familiar scene he had seen two years before, on the night of his first meeting with Ellen Olenska. The house itself seemed to echo that earlier night: the rustle of silk, the muffled coughs held back by good breeding, the orchestra’s disciplined swell, and, above it, the city’s far-off hum blocked at the doors as if the Opera were a sanctuary against modern noise.

He had half-expected Ellen to appear again in old Mrs. Mingott’s box, but it remained empty. He sat motionless, his eyes fixed on the vacancy as on an unanswered letter, until suddenly Madame Nilsson’s pure soprano broke out into “M’ama, non m’ama...”

He turned to the stage where, in the familiar setting of giant roses and pen-wiper pansies, the same large blonde victim was succumbing to the same small brown seducer. The painted flowers looked too large, too perfect—like the society bouquets that smelled of nothing once you leaned close. His eyes wandered again, to the point of the horseshoe where May sat between two older ladies, just as, on that former evening, she had sat between Mrs. Lovell Mingott and her newly arrived “foreign” cousin.

As on that evening May was all in white; and Archer, who had not noticed what she wore, recognized the blue-white satin and old lace of her wedding dress. It was the old New York custom for brides to appear in the costly garment during the first year or two of marriage: his mother kept hers in tissue paper, cherishing a hope that Janey might someday wear it—though poor Janey was nearing the age when pearl-grey poplin and no bridesmaids would be thought more “appropriate.”

It struck Archer that since their return from Europe May had seldom worn her bridal satin; and the surprise of seeing her in it made him compare her with the girl he had watched two years earlier, his heart full of anticipations as fixed and glossy as the satin itself. Her outline was slightly heavier, as her goddesslike build had foretold; but the athletic erectness of her carriage and the girlish transparency of her expression remained unchanged. But for the slight languor he had lately noticed, she would have been the exact image of that girl playing with her lilies-of-the-valley bouquet on her betrothal evening.

The sight appealed to his pity with a sharpness that was almost physical. Such innocence moved him like a child’s trusting clasp. And then he remembered the passionate generosity that could flare under that incurious calm: May’s understanding glance when he had urged that their engagement be announced at the Beaufort ball; the voice in the Mission garden saying: “I couldn’t have my happiness made out of a wrong—a wrong to some one else.” An uncontrollable longing seized him to tell her the truth, to throw himself on her generosity, and ask for the freedom he had once refused—freedom not only from marriage, but from the whole vocabulary in which his life had been spelled out for him.

Newland Archer was quiet and self-controlled. Conformity to the discipline of a small society had become almost a second nature. It was deeply distasteful to him to do anything melodramatic and conspicuous—anything Mr. van der Luyden would have deprecated, and the club box condemned as bad form. Yet suddenly he was unconscious of the club box, of Mr. van der Luyden, of all that had enclosed him so long in the warm shelter of habit. He walked along the semicircular passage at the back of the house and opened the door of Mrs. van der Luyden’s box as if it were a gate into the unknown.

“M’ama!” thrilled out Marguerite; and the occupants of the box looked up in surprise. He had already broken one of the rules of his world, which forbade entering a box during a solo. Slipping between Mr. van der Luyden and Sillerton Jackson, he leaned over his wife.

“I’ve got a beastly headache; don’t tell any one, but come home, won’t you?” he whispered.

May gave him a glance of comprehension. He saw her whisper to his mother, who nodded sympathetically; then May murmured an excuse to Mrs. van der Luyden and rose from her seat just as Marguerite fell into Faust’s arms.

As Archer helped her with her Opera cloak, he noticed the exchange of a significant smile between the older ladies—a smile as smooth and finished as icing laid over a cake to hide whatever cracks might be underneath.

In the carriage May laid her hand shyly on his.

“I’m so sorry you don’t feel well. I’m afraid they’ve been overworking you again at the office.”

“No—it’s not that: do you mind if I open the window?” he returned, confused, letting down the pane. Cold air rushed in, full of street-sounds—hooves, wheels, the distant shout of a driver—noisy, alive, uncontained. He stared out, keeping his eyes fixed on the passing houses as if the moving darkness could give him words. Beside him he felt May’s presence like a silent, watchful interrogation, gentle but steady.

At their door she caught her skirt in the carriage step and fell against him.

“Did you hurt yourself?” he asked, steadying her with his arm.

“No; but my poor dress—see how I’ve torn it!” she exclaimed.

She bent to gather up a mud-stained breadth, the bridal satin taking on the city’s grime as if the outside world had at last reached it. They went up the steps into the hall. The servants had not expected them so early; only a glimmer of gas burned on the upper landing. Archer mounted the stairs, turned up the light, and put matches to the brackets on each side of the library mantelpiece.

The curtains were drawn, and the warm, friendly aspect of the room smote him like that of a familiar face met during an unavowable errand. The fire’s smell—coal and a faint dusty sweetness from the logs’ char—rose to meet him; the upholstered chairs held the remembered shapes of evenings; even the clock’s tick had the intimate confidence of a household sound. Against that domestic comfort, his purpose felt like a knife drawn at the table.

May was very pale. He asked if he should get her some brandy.

“Oh, no,” she exclaimed, flushing for an instant as she took off her cloak. “But hadn’t you better go to bed at once?” she added as he opened a silver box on the table and took out a cigarette.

He threw down the cigarette and walked to his usual place by the fire.

“No; my head is not as bad as that.” He paused, feeling the words bunch and stick in his throat like dough refusing to rise. “And there’s something I want to say; something important—that I must tell you at once.”

She had dropped into an armchair and raised her head. “Yes, dear?” she replied—so gently that he wondered at the lack of surprise with which she received his preamble.

“May—” he began, standing a few feet from her chair and looking over at her as if the small space between them were an unbridgeable abyss.

His voice echoed uncannily through the homelike hush, and he repeated: “There is something I’ve got to tell you… about myself…”

She sat silent, without a movement or even a tremor of the lashes. She was still extremely pale, but her face had a curious tranquillity, as if drawn from some secret inner storehouse where she kept her strength, as women in old houses keep preserves—quietly, in the dark, against the time when they are needed.

He checked the conventional phrases of self-accusal crowding to his lips. He was determined to put the case baldly, without recrimination or excuse.

“Madame Olenska—” he said.

At the name May raised her hand as if to silence him. As she did so, the gaslight struck on the gold of her wedding ring.

“Oh, why should we talk about Ellen tonight?” she asked, with a slight pout of impatience.

“Because I ought to have spoken before.”

Her face remained calm. “Is it really worth while, dear? I know I’ve been unfair to her at times—perhaps we all have. You’ve understood her, no doubt, better than we did: you’ve always been kind to her. But what does it matter, now it’s all over?”

He looked at her blankly. The sense of unreality in which he felt himself imprisoned—had it spread to her too, turning everything into a dream where nothing had weight?

“All over—what do you mean?” he asked, in an indistinct stammer.

May still looked at him with transparent eyes. “Why—since she’s going back to Europe so soon; since Granny approves and understands, and has arranged to make her independent of her husband—” She broke off.

Archer grasped the corner of the mantelpiece in one convulsed hand, as if holding to it could steady the room’s spinning. He made a vain effort to extend the same control to his reeling thoughts.

“I supposed,” he heard May’s even voice go on, “that you had been kept at the office this evening about the business arrangements. It was settled this morning, I believe.”

She lowered her eyes under his unseeing stare, and another fugitive flush passed over her face. He understood that his own eyes must be unbearable. Turning away, he rested his elbows on the mantel-shelf and covered his face. Something drummed and clanged furiously in his ears; he could not tell if it was the blood in his veins or the tick of the clock, loud now in the closed room as if the house were counting out the last measures of some old tune.

May sat without moving or speaking while the clock slowly measured out five minutes. A lump of coal fell forward in the grate; she rose to push it back, and the small, domestic sound of the poker against iron struck him like an answer in a language he could not translate—plain, practical, and final.

He turned and faced her.

“It’s impossible,” he exclaimed.

“Impossible—?” Her voice did not rise; she only waited, as if he were the one who had come late to what had already been decided.

“How do you know—what you’ve just told me?”

“I saw Ellen yesterday—I told you I’d seen her at Granny’s.”

“It wasn’t then that she told you?”

“No; I had a note from her this afternoon.—Do you want to see it?”

He could not find his voice. May went out of the room and came back almost immediately.

“I thought you knew,” she said simply.

She laid a sheet of paper on the table; Archer put out his hand and took it up.

The letter contained only a few lines:

“May dear, I have at last made Granny understand that my visit to her could be no more than a visit; and she has been as kind and generous as ever. She sees now that if I return to Europe I must live by myself, or rather with poor Aunt Medora, who is coming with me. I am hurrying back to Washington to pack up, and we sail next week. You must be very good to Granny when I’m gone—as good as you’ve always been to me. Ellen.

“If any of my friends wish to urge me to change my mind, please tell them it would be utterly useless.”

Archer read the letter two or three times, as if repetition could alter its meaning, as if the same words might yield a different taste on the tongue—salt becoming sugar by sheer will. Then he flung it down and burst out laughing.

The sound startled him. It recalled Janey’s midnight fright when she had caught him rocking with incomprehensible mirth over May’s telegram announcing that the date of their marriage had been advanced. This laugh too had no lightness in it; it was the noise of something breaking inside a man who had long prided himself on not making a noise.

“Why did she write this?” he asked, checking himself with a supreme effort.

May met the question with unshaken candour. “I suppose because we talked things over yesterday—”

“What things?”

“I told her I was afraid I hadn’t been fair to her—hadn’t always understood how hard it must have been for her here, alone among so many people who were relations and yet strangers; who felt the right to criticise, and yet didn’t always know the circumstances.”

She paused, and in the pause Archer heard again the house’s sounds: the clock, the fire, the distant, well-trained quiet of servants. Outside, somewhere beyond the drawn curtains, the city kept grinding and rattling, but here the old order held its breath.

“I knew you’d been the one friend she could always count on,” May went on, “and I wanted her to know that you and I were the same—in all our feelings.”

She hesitated, as if waiting for him to speak, and then added slowly: “She understood my wishing to tell her this. I think she understands everything.”

She came up to Archer and took one of his cold hands, pressing it quickly against her cheek. The touch was innocent, intimate, and strangely irrevocable—like a seal set on a document in a language he had been raised to obey.

“My head aches too; good-night, dear,” she said.

She turned to the door, her torn and muddy wedding dress dragging after her across the room, satin and lace whispering over the carpet with a soft, persistent sound, as if the past itself were being drawn away—still beautiful, still heavy, and stained now where it had touched the street.

It was, as Mrs. Archer said with her smile that never lost its edge, a great event for a young couple to give their first big dinner.

Since the Newland Archers had set up their house there had been, as the phrase went, “a good deal of company”—three or four friends dropped in to dine, a familiar clink of glasses, a joint carved without ceremony, May’s bright readiness set like a napkin across the whole table. Archer liked such evenings. They had the homely mercies of routine: the servants moved with less self-consciousness, the silver seemed less on parade, and the talk, if it did not become truer, at least grew less rehearsed. Yet even in those small gatherings he had felt how little of May’s impulse belonged to herself, and how much to the pattern pressed on her by mothers, aunts, traditions—by the old New York conviction that marriage was a social duty performed in public, like attending church or sending one’s card on New Year’s Day.

But a big dinner was another matter. A hired chef meant a stranger in the kitchen giving orders in a tongue the household did not speak; two borrowed footmen meant borrowed hands carrying borrowed importance; Roman punch meant the whole ceremonial unrolled like a carpet—canvas-backs or terrapin, two soups in procession, a hot sweet and a cold, shoulders bared under gaslight, and guests of that rank which required you to be careful not only what you served, but what you implied.

It was always curious to watch a young pair launch their first invitations in the third person, their names printed as if they were an institution. The summons was seldom refused. Still, it was triumph enough that the van der Luydens, at May’s request, should have stayed over to be present at her farewell dinner for the Countess Olenska.

The two mothers-in-law sat in May’s drawing-room on the afternoon of the great day, as if presiding over a rite older than either family. Mrs. Archer wrote out menus on Tiffany’s thickest gilt-edged bristol, her sharp gold pen scratching with the dry certainty of one who knew exactly how many syllables a dish might have without seeming pretentious. Mrs. Welland superintended palms and standard lamps, moving furniture a fraction of an inch as though a corner wrongly created might shift the moral balance of the evening.

Archer, coming late from the office, found them still at it. Mrs. Archer had turned from the menus to the name-cards; Mrs. Welland was considering whether the large gilt sofa ought to be brought forward to make a new “corner” between piano and window.

May, they told him, was in the dining-room inspecting the mound of Jacqueminot roses and maidenhair in the centre of the long table, and the placing of the Maillard bonbons in openwork silver baskets between the candelabra. On the piano stood a large basket of orchids Mr. van der Luyden had sent from Skuytercliff—blooms whose perfume had the thick, expensive languor of a conservatory in winter. Everything was, in short, as it should be when a household meant to show that it knew how to do what must be done.

Mrs. Archer ran thoughtfully over the list, checking off each name as if she were ticking the beats in a score.

“Henry van der Luyden—Louisa—the Lovell Mingotts—the Reggie Chiverses—Lawrence Lefferts and Gertrude—(yes, I suppose May was right to have them)—the Selfridge Merrys, Sillerton Jackson, Van Newland and his wife. How time passes! It seems only yesterday he was your best man, Newland—and Countess Olenska—yes, I think that’s all.”

Mrs. Welland surveyed her son-in-law with that affectionate approval which made admiration feel like supervision. “No one can say, Newland, that you and May are not giving Ellen a handsome send-off.”

“Ah, well,” said Mrs. Archer, “I understand May’s wanting her cousin to tell people abroad that we’re not quite barbarians.”

“I’m sure Ellen will appreciate it. She was to arrive this morning, I believe. It will make a most charming last impression. The evening before sailing is usually so dreary,” Mrs. Welland continued, as if dreariness were a thing one could banish with hot soup and well-timed orchids.

Archer turned toward the door. “Do go in and have a peep at the table. And don’t let May tire herself too much,” his mother-in-law called after him. He affected not to hear. He sprang upstairs to his library.

The room looked back at him like an alien face arranged into polite features. It had been ruthlessly tidied, prepared by a judicious distribution of ash-trays and cedar-wood boxes for the gentlemen to smoke in later. Even the air had been scoured of any trace of private life, as if the day had demanded not a dinner but a small surrender.

“Ah, well,” he thought, “it’s not for long—” and went on to his dressing-room.

Ten days had passed since Madame Olenska’s departure from New York. In those ten days there had been no sign from her except the return of a key, wrapped in tissue paper, sent to his office in a sealed envelope addressed in her hand. It might have been, in another story, a move in a familiar game; Archer chose to read it as something else. She was still fighting, he told himself, not against him but against what claimed her. She was going to Europe, and she was not returning to her husband. Nothing, therefore, would prevent his following her; and once he had taken the irrevocable step—once she had seen that it could not be undone—he believed she would not send him away.

That confidence steadied him. It kept him from writing to her, from betraying by any sign his misery and mortification. He felt, absurdly, that in the deadly silent game between them the trumps were still in his hands, and he waited.

Yet there had been moments he had had to pass through as one walks through a room in which everyone has stopped talking. The day after Madame Olenska’s departure, Mr. Letterblair had sent for him to go over the details of the trust Mrs. Manson Mingott wished to create for her granddaughter. For two hours Archer examined the terms of the deed, hearing the lawyer’s voice as if it came from behind a screen, and all the while feeling that he had been summoned for something other than cousinship, and that the real purpose would appear at the end.

“Well, the lady can’t deny that it’s a handsome arrangement,” Mr. Letterblair had summed up at last, mumbling over the settlement. “In fact I’m bound to say she’s been treated pretty handsomely all round.”

“All round?” Archer echoed, with a touch of derision. “Do you refer to her husband’s proposal to give her back her own money?”

Mr. Letterblair’s bushy eyebrows rose a fraction. “My dear sir, the law’s the law; and your wife’s cousin was married under the French law. It’s to be presumed she knew what that meant.”

“Even if she did—what happened subsequently—” Archer began; but he paused. The lawyer had laid his pen-handle against his big corrugated nose and looked down it with the expression virtuous elderly gentlemen put on when they wish their youngers to understand that virtue is not synonymous with ignorance.

“My dear sir, I’ve no wish to extenuate the Count’s transgressions; but—but on the other side… I wouldn’t put my hand in the fire… well, that there hadn’t been tit for tat… with the young champion…” Mr. Letterblair unlocked a drawer and pushed a folded paper toward Archer. “This report, the result of discreet enquiries…”

Archer made no effort to glance at it, no effort to deny. The lawyer, a little flatly, continued: “I don’t say it’s conclusive, you observe; far from it. But straws show… and on the whole it’s eminently satisfactory for all parties that this dignified solution has been reached.”

“Oh, eminently,” Archer had said, pushing back the paper.

A day or two later, when Mrs. Manson Mingott sent for him, the ordeal had been more trying because it was dressed in affection.

He found the old lady depressed and querulous, sunk in her chair as in a featherbed of grievances.

“You know she’s deserted me?” she began at once; and without waiting for his reply: “Oh, don’t ask me why! She gave so many reasons I’ve forgotten them all. My private belief is that she couldn’t face the boredom. At any rate that’s what Augusta and my daughters-in-law think. And I don’t know that I altogether blame her. Olenski’s a finished scoundrel; but life with him must have been a good deal gayer than it is in Fifth Avenue. Not that the family would admit that: they think Fifth Avenue is Heaven with the rue de la Paix thrown in. And poor Ellen, of course, has no idea of going back to her husband. She held out as firmly as ever against that. So she’s to settle down in Paris with that fool Medora… Well, Paris is Paris; and you can keep a carriage there on next to nothing. But she was as gay as a bird, and I shall miss her.”

Two tears, the parched tears of the old, rolled down her puffy cheeks and vanished in the abysses of her bosom.

“All I ask is,” she concluded, “that they shouldn’t bother me any more. I must really be allowed to digest my gruel…” She twinkled a little wistfully at Archer, as if even gruel, properly digested, were a kind of dignity.

It was that evening, on his return home, that May announced her intention of giving a farewell dinner to her cousin.

Madame Olenska’s name had not been spoken between them since the night of her flight to Washington. Archer looked at his wife with surprise, as if a drawer had suddenly slid open in a room where all the furniture had been counted.

“A dinner—why?” he asked.

Her colour rose. “But you like Ellen—I thought you’d be pleased.”

“It’s awfully nice—your putting it in that way. But I really don’t see—”

“I mean to do it, Newland,” she said quietly, rising and going to her desk. “Here are the invitations all written. Mother helped me—she agrees that we ought to.”

She paused, embarrassed and yet smiling; and in that instant Archer saw, more clearly than he had ever seen it, the embodied image of the Family—its collective will dressed in a girl’s gentleness.

“Oh, all right,” he said, staring with unseeing eyes at the list of guests she put into his hand.

When he entered the drawing-room before dinner May was stooping over the fire, coaxing the logs to burn in their unaccustomed setting of immaculate tiles. The sound of the poker on clean brick struck him as loud as a hammer in a church: the house, for all its velvet and gilt, had an acoustic of restraint, every noise sharpened by the expectation of silence.

The tall lamps were all lit. The rosy shades gave the room a tropical glare, and the palms made soft, faint rustlings—leaf against leaf—whenever a door opened and the draught moved through. Mr. van der Luyden’s orchids had been conspicuously disposed in modern porcelain and knobby silver. Mrs. Newland Archer’s drawing-room was generally thought a great success: the gilt bamboo jardinière crowded with primulas and cinerarias punctually renewed, the pale brocade sofas grouped about little plush tables densely covered with silver toys, porcelain animals, and efflorescent photograph frames. It was a room that smelled not of people but of flowers trained to bloom on command; and even their perfume seemed disciplined.

“I don’t think Ellen has ever seen this room lighted up,” May said, rising flushed from her struggle, sending about her a glance of pardonable pride.

The brass tongs she had propped against the side of the chimney fell with a crash that drowned her husband’s answer. The sound rang out—an honest clang in a room built for soft footfalls—and then, before he could restore them, Mr. and Mrs. van der Luyden were announced.

The other guests quickly followed, for it was known the van der Luydens liked to dine punctually. Voices blended, then separated; silk made its small, dry murmurs; the servants’ movements had the measured hush of trained steps. Archer found himself showing Mrs. Selfridge Merry a small, highly-varnished Verbeckhoven “Study of Sheep,” which Mr. Welland had given May for Christmas, when he became aware that Madame Olenska stood at his side.

She was excessively pale, and her pallor made her dark hair seem denser and heavier than ever. Perhaps it was that, or the fact that she had wound several rows of amber beads around her neck—amber with a resinous, forest smell if one warmed it in one’s palm—that reminded him suddenly of the little Ellen Mingott he had danced with at children’s parties when Medora Manson first brought her to New York.

The amber was trying to her complexion, or her dress was unbecoming: her face looked lustreless, almost ugly, and he had never loved it as he did at that minute. Their hands met. He thought he heard her say, “Yes, we’re sailing tomorrow in the Russia—”; then came the unmeaning noise of opening doors, and after an interval May’s voice:

“Newland! Dinner’s been announced. Won’t you please take Ellen in?”

Madame Olenska put her hand on his arm. He noticed the hand was ungloved, and remembered how his eyes had kept coming back to it in the little Twenty-third Street drawing-room. All the beauty that had forsaken her face seemed to have taken refuge in the long pale fingers and faintly dimpled knuckles resting on his sleeve; and he said to himself, with a kind of physical ache: If it were only to see her hand again I should have to follow her.

It was only at an entertainment ostensibly offered to a “foreign visitor” that Mrs. van der Luyden could suffer the diminution of being placed on her host’s left. The fact of Madame Olenska’s foreignness could hardly have been more adroitly emphasized than by this farewell tribute; and Mrs. van der Luyden accepted her displacement with an affability that left no doubt as to her approval. There were things that had to be done, and if done at all, done handsomely and thoroughly; and one of these, in the old New York code, was the tribal rally around a kinswoman about to be eliminated from the tribe.

There was nothing on earth the Wellands and Mingotts would not have done to proclaim their unalterable affection for the Countess Olenska now that her passage for Europe was engaged; and Archer, at the head of his table, marveled at the silent, untiring activity with which her popularity had been retrieved, grievances silenced, her past countenanced, her present irradiated by family approval. Mrs. van der Luyden shone on her with the dim benevolence that was her nearest approach to cordiality, and Mr. van der Luyden, from his seat at May’s right, cast down the table glances plainly intended to justify all the carnations he had sent from Skuytercliff.

The meal itself moved like a practiced procession. The soup arrived with its faint steam and its careful garnish; the fish followed, then the canvas-backs, their rich, dark meat giving off that unmistakable savor of wild feeding—marsh and grain and distance—lifted and intensified by the sharpness of currant jelly. The Roman punch came in with its cold sting and its sweetened fire, a frosted promise of release that dissolved on the tongue and left only a clearer awareness of the room’s heat and of the crowd’s controlled breathing. Archer could hear the thin chiming of forks on china, the muffled slide of chairs on carpet, the murmur of voices trained never to rise too high; and beneath it all, like a second, secret music, the pulse of implication—what was not said but was heard, what was not looked at but was seen.

He felt himself assisting at the scene in a state of odd imponderability, as if he floated somewhere between chandelier and ceiling. As his glance traveled from one placid well-fed face to another he saw all the harmless-looking people engaged upon May’s canvas-backs as a band of dumb conspirators, and himself and the pale woman on his right as the center of their conspiracy. And then it came over him in a vast flash made up of many broken gleams: to all of them he and Madame Olenska were lovers—lovers in the extreme sense peculiar to foreign vocabularies.

He guessed himself to have been for months the center of countless silently observing eyes and patiently listening ears. He understood that by means unknown to him the separation between himself and the partner of his guilt had been achieved; and that now the whole tribe had rallied about his wife on the tacit assumption that nobody knew anything—or had ever imagined anything—and that the evening’s entertainment was simply May Archer’s natural desire to take an affectionate leave of her friend and cousin.

It was the old New York way of taking life without effusion of blood: the way of people who dreaded scandal more than disease, who placed decency above courage, and who considered that nothing was more ill-bred than scenes, except the behavior of those who gave rise to them.

As these thoughts succeeded each other Archer felt like a prisoner in the center of an armed camp. Over the Florida asparagus—pale spears smelling faintly of earth and forced warmth—they were dealing with Beaufort and his wife.

“It’s to show me,” he thought, “what would happen to me—” and a deathly sense of the superiority of implication and analogy over direct action, and of silence over rash words, closed in on him like the doors of the family vault.

He laughed, abruptly, and met Mrs. van der Luyden’s startled eyes.

“You think it laughable?” she said with a pinched smile. “Of course poor Regina’s idea of remaining in New York has its ridiculous side, I suppose;” and Archer muttered: “Of course.”

At that moment he became conscious that Madame Olenska’s other neighbor had been engaged for some time, and that the lady on his right had been left without the expected civilities. He saw May, serenely enthroned between Mr. van der Luyden and Mr. Selfridge Merry, cast a quick glance down the table—a glance that was not reproachful, only arranging. It was evident that the host and the lady on his right could not sit through the whole meal in silence.

He turned to Madame Olenska. Her pale smile met him, and in it he read a kind of weary courage: Oh, do let’s see it through.

“Did you find the journey tiring?” he asked; and his own voice surprised him by its naturalness, as if the words had been ladled out by habit like soup.

“On the contrary,” she answered, “I’ve seldom traveled with fewer discomforts. Except—the dreadful heat in the train.”

He remarked that she would not suffer from that particular hardship in the country she was going to.

“I never,” he declared with intensity, “was more nearly frozen than once, in April, in the train between Calais and Paris.”

She said she did not wonder, but remarked that one could always carry an extra rug, and that every form of travel had its hardships. He returned abruptly that he thought them all of no account compared with the blessedness of getting away.

She changed color. He heard his own voice rise as if the room’s enforced hush made every sharp note louder.

“I mean to do a lot of traveling myself before long.”

A tremor crossed her face. He leaned over to Reggie Chivers and cried out—too loudly, as if the desire for distance had to be shouted above the walls:

“I say, Reggie, what do you say to a trip round the world: now, next month, I mean? I’m game if you are—”

Mrs. Reggie piped up that she could not think of letting Reggie go till after the Martha Washington Ball she was getting up for the Blind Asylum in Easter week; and Reggie, placidly, observed that by that time he would have to be practicing for the International Polo match.

Mr. Selfridge Merry caught the phrase “round the world,” and, having once circled the globe in his steam-yacht, seized the chance to send down the table several striking items concerning the shallowness of Mediterranean ports. It didn’t matter, he added, for when you’d seen Athens and Smyrna and Constantinople, what else was there? Mrs. Merry said she could never be too grateful to Dr. Bencomb for making them promise not to go to Naples on account of the fever.

“But you must have three weeks to do India properly,” her husband conceded, anxious to be understood as no frivolous globe-trotter.

At that point the ladies went up to the drawing-room, the drift of skirts and the soft closing of the door altering the room’s sound at once: fewer voices, more smoke, and the relieved scrape of chairs.

In the library, in spite of weightier presences, Lawrence Lefferts predominated. The men settled into their places, glasses touched, cigars were cut; the air thickened with toasted leaf and the sweet, heavy scent of Madeira. Archer could hear the faint crackle of the fire downstairs, as if the house itself were eating slowly.

The talk, as usual, veered around to the Beauforts; and even Mr. van der Luyden and Mr. Selfridge Merry, installed in the honorary arm-chairs reserved for them, paused to listen to Lefferts’s philippic.

Never had Lefferts so abounded in the sentiments that adorn Christian manhood and exalt the sanctity of the home. Indignation lent him a scathing eloquence. It was clear that if others had followed his example and acted as he talked, society would never have been weak enough to receive a foreign upstart like Beaufort—no, sir, not even if he’d married a van der Luyden or a Lanning instead of a Dallas. And what chance would there have been, Lefferts wrathfully questioned, of his marrying into such a family as the Dallases if he had not already wormed his way into certain houses, as people like Mrs. Lemuel Struthers had managed to worm theirs in his wake? If society chose to open its doors to vulgar women the harm was not great, though the gain was doubtful; but once it got in the way of tolerating men of obscure origin and tainted wealth, the end was total disintegration—and at no distant date.

“If things go on at this pace,” Lefferts thundered, looking like a young prophet dressed by Poole and not yet stoned, “we shall see our children fighting for invitations to swindlers’ houses, and marrying Beaufort’s bastards.”

“Oh, I say—draw it mild!” Reggie Chivers and young Newland protested. Mr. Selfridge Merry looked genuinely alarmed, and pain and disgust settled on Mr. van der Luyden’s sensitive face.

“Has he got any?” cried Mr. Sillerton Jackson, pricking up his ears; and while Lefferts tried to turn the question with a laugh, the old gentleman twittered into Archer’s ear: “Queer, those fellows who are always wanting to set things right. The people who have the worst cooks are always telling you they’re poisoned when they dine out. But I hear there are pressing reasons for our friend Lawrence’s diatribe:—typewriter this time, I understand….”

The talk swept past Archer like some senseless river running and running because it did not know enough to stop. He saw on the faces about him expressions of interest, amusement, even mirth. He listened to the younger men’s laughter, and to the praise of the Archer Madeira which Mr. van der Luyden and Mr. Merry were thoughtfully celebrating, as if the wine’s age were a moral guarantee.

Through it all he was dimly aware of a general attitude of friendliness toward himself, as if the guard of the prisoner he felt himself to be were trying to soften his captivity; and that perception increased his passionate determination to be free.

When they joined the ladies again he met May’s triumphant eyes and read in them the conviction that everything had “gone off” beautifully. She rose from Madame Olenska’s side, and immediately Mrs. van der Luyden beckoned the latter to a seat on the gilt sofa where she throned. Mrs. Selfridge Merry bore across the room to join them, and it became clear to Archer that here also a conspiracy of rehabilitation and obliteration was going on.

The same silent organization that held his world together was determined to put itself on record as never having questioned for a moment the propriety of Madame Olenska’s conduct, or the completeness of Archer’s domestic felicity. All these amiable and inexorable people were resolutely engaged in pretending to one another that they had never heard of, suspected, or even conceived possible, the least hint to the contrary; and from this tissue of elaborate mutual dissimulation Archer once more disengaged the fact that New York believed him to be Madame Olenska’s lover.

He caught the glitter of victory in his wife’s eyes and for the first time understood that she shared the belief. The discovery woke a laughter of inner devils that reverberated through all his efforts to discuss the Martha Washington ball with Mrs. Reggie Chivers and little Mrs. Newland; and so the evening swept on, running and running like a senseless river that did not know how to stop.

At length he saw that Madame Olenska had risen and was saying good-bye. He understood that in a moment she would be gone, and tried to remember what he had said to her at dinner; but he could not recall a single word, as if the night’s talk had been swallowed by the room’s thick upholstery and disciplined air.

She went up to May, and the rest of the company made a circle about her as she advanced. The two young women clasped hands; then May bent forward and kissed her cousin.

“Certainly our hostess is much the handsomer of the two,” Archer heard Reggie Chivers say in an undertone to young Mrs. Newland; and he remembered Beaufort’s coarse sneer at May’s ineffectual beauty, and the way the world rewarded one kind of face and punished another without ever admitting it did either.

A moment later he was in the hall, putting Madame Olenska’s cloak about her shoulders. The hall smelled of cold air and fur and the faint metallic scent of wet carriage-steps. Through all his confusion he held fast to the resolve to say nothing that might startle or disturb her. Convinced that no power could now turn him from his purpose, he had found strength to let events shape themselves as they would.

But as he followed her out he felt a sudden hunger to be alone with her for one instant at the door of her carriage—one instant with only the night sounds outside, the honest jingle of harness, the hoof-beats, the driver’s muffled call—sounds not trained to politeness.

“Is your carriage here?” he asked.

At that moment Mrs. van der Luyden, who was being majestically inserted into her sables, said gently: “We are driving dear Ellen home.”

Archer’s heart gave a jerk. Madame Olenska, clasping her cloak and fan with one hand, held out the other to him. Her fingers were cold, and for an instant he felt as if all the heat of the dinner, all the clamor of unsaid words, had been condensed into that chill touch.

“Good-bye,” she said.

“Good-bye—but I shall see you soon in Paris,” he answered aloud—it seemed to him that he had shouted it in the guarded hush of the house.

“Oh,” she murmured, and her voice was so low it was like a breath against his ear, “if you and May could come—!”

Newland Archer sat at the writing-table in his library on East Thirty-ninth Street, the house quiet at last in that after-dinner lull when even the city seemed to chew more slowly.

He had come straight back from the official reception for the opening of the new Metropolitan Museum galleries. The evening had been all marble and velvet, officials and benefactors flowing like a well-trained sauce around the great rooms. The air had smelt of fresh varnish, damp wool, crushed violets, and that faint metallic tang that clings to newly lit electric lamps. Yet it was not the glitter of the new that had stayed with him. It was the way a remark, tossed off as carelessly as one salts a dish, had struck a buried place in him.

“Why, this used to be one of the old Cesnola rooms,” he had heard someone say; and instantly the present fell away. He was no longer under lofty ceilings with catalogued treasures arranged like provisions in a well-ordered pantry. He was sitting alone on a hard leather divan against a radiator that clicked and sighed, while a slight figure in a long sealskin cloak moved away down the meagrely furnished vista of the old Museum—Ellen, retreating as if she were the last warm loaf carried out of reach and into winter.

The soundscape of the reception—the scraping of chairs, the bright clink of glasses, the hiss of laughter that rose and broke against stone—had, for a moment, turned into other sounds: the radiator’s tick, the museum’s hollow hush, the soft sweep of her steps. It was curious how the ear remembered what the mind tried to file away under “finished.”

Now, in his own library, he looked around as though he had been absent for years and had come home to find the familiar room subtly seasoned by time. For over thirty years it had been the stage of his private life. Here the family’s great announcements had been made with the ritual caution of old New York—nothing flung down raw, everything served with garnish and circumlocution, as if plain speech were a coarse ingredient.

Here, nearly twenty-six years ago, May had told him—blushing, circling the fact as carefully as she would have folded a napkin—that she was to have a child. Here Dallas, too delicate to be taken to church in midwinter, had been christened by their old friend the Bishop of New York, broad and glowing, a man whose voice filled a room the way a cathedral organ fills the chest: a sound you lean into without noticing you’ve done so. Here Dallas had first tottered across the floor shouting “Dad,” while May and the nurse laughed behind the door—laughter like the quick rattle of dried peas in a tin. Here their second child, Mary—so like her mother—had announced her engagement to the dullest and most reliable of Reggie Chivers’s many sons; and here Archer had kissed Mary through her wedding veil before they went down to the motor that would carry them to Grace Church. The world had shifted under everyone’s feet, but the “Grace Church wedding” endured like some old recipe no one dared alter, however much the market changed.

In this room he and May had mapped out the children’s future as methodically as menus: the studies of Dallas and his younger brother Bill, Mary’s incurable indifference to “accomplishments,” her appetite for sport and philanthropy, and Dallas’s vague leanings toward “art,” which had ended with him in the office of a rising New York architect. The young men, Archer had noticed, were leaving law and business as if those trades were heavy, overcooked things their fathers insisted were nourishing. They ran after politics, reform, archaeology, architecture, landscape-engineering—everything that sounded like rebuilding rather than counting. They argued about Georgian types, corrected one another’s vocabulary, and protested at the meaningless use of the word “Colonial.” Nobody, they said, had “Colonial” houses now except the millionaire grocers of the suburbs.

Yet if he searched for the moment when he had felt most intensely that his life had been asked to matter, it was not May’s news, nor a child’s first step, but an evening in this same room when the Governor of New York, down from Albany to dine and stay the night, had turned on him suddenly, pounding the table and gnashing his eye-glasses as if the words themselves were hard to digest.

“Hang the professional politician! You’re the kind of man the country wants, Archer. If the stable’s ever to be cleaned out, men like you have got to lend a hand in the cleaning.”

Men like you. He could still taste the phrase—sharp as mustard, warming as brandy. It had made him rise at once, eager, as if called into a kitchen where there was urgent work to be done. It echoed Ned Winsett’s old appeal to roll up his sleeves and get down into the muck; but this time it came from a man whose example made refusal feel like cowardice.

Looking back, Archer was not sure men like himself had been what the country needed in that active service Theodore Roosevelt had pointed toward. There was reason to think it did not: after a year in the State Assembly he had not been re-elected, and had slipped back, with a kind of relief, into obscure municipal work—useful, modest—and then into writing occasional articles for one of the reforming weeklies trying to shake the country out of apathy. It was little enough to show, perhaps. But when he remembered what the young men of his generation and set had expected—money-making, sport, society, a life eaten cold and unexamined—then even his small contribution seemed to count, as each brick counts in a well-built wall.

He had done little in public life. He had always been by nature contemplative, a dilettante. But he had had high things to contemplate, great things to delight in; and one great man’s friendship to steady him. People were beginning to call him “a good citizen.” In New York, every philanthropic, municipal, or artistic movement wanted his opinion and his name. “Ask Archer,” they said, when there was talk of the first school for crippled children, reorganising the Museum of Art, founding the Grolier Club, inaugurating the new Library, or getting up a society of chamber music. His days were full, decently filled. He supposed it was as much as a man ought to ask.

Something he knew he had missed: the flower of life. He had come to think of it as unattainable and improbable, like a first prize in a lottery where the tickets ran into the hundreds of millions and the prize was one. The odds had been against him so completely that to repine would have been like lamenting that he had not inherited a kingdom. Ellen Olenska had become, in his mind, not so much a person as the composite vision of all that had been denied him: a perfume trapped in a stopped bottle, faint but unmistakable.

That vision—thin as it had grown—had kept him from thinking of other women. He had been what was called a faithful husband; and when May had suddenly died—carried off by the infectious pneumonia through which she had nursed their youngest—he had honestly mourned her. Long years had shown him that it did not matter so much if marriage was a dull duty, so long as it kept the dignity of a duty. Once you let duty spoil into appetite, the whole thing curdled. Looking about him, he honoured his own past and mourned it. After all, there was good in the old ways.

His gaze made the familiar round of the room. Dallas had “done it over” with English mezzotints, Chippendale cabinets, bits of chosen blue-and-white, and pleasantly shaded electric lamps—light softened like milk in tea. But Archer’s eye always came back to the old Eastlake writing-table he had refused to banish, and to his first photograph of May beside the inkstand.

There she was: tall, round-bosomed, willowy, in starched muslin and a flapping Leghorn hat, as he had first seen her under the orange-trees in the Mission garden. As he had seen her that day, so she had remained—never quite at the same height, perhaps, but never far below it: generous, faithful, unwearied. Yet she had lacked imagination, been incapable of growth. The world of her youth had fallen apart and rebuilt itself without her ever feeling the tremor. She carried a hard bright blindness like a lantern held close: it lit what was near and made the distance disappear. Her incapacity to recognise change made her children conceal their views from her as Archer had concealed his. From the first there had been a joint pretence of sameness, a kind of innocent family hypocrisy in which father and children had collaborated without speaking of it.

She had died thinking the world a good place, full of loving households like her own. She had gone resigned because she believed, firmly, that whatever happened Newland would continue to inculcate in Dallas the same principles and prejudices that had shaped his parents’ lives, and that Dallas in turn would transmit the sacred trust to little Bill. Of Mary she was as sure as of her own self. So, having snatched little Bill from the grave and given her life in the effort, she had gone contentedly to her place in the Archer vault in St. Mark’s, where Mrs. Archer already lay safe from the terrifying “trend” her daughter-in-law had never even become aware of.

Opposite May’s photograph stood one of their daughter. Mary Chivers was as tall and fair as her mother, but large-waisted, flat-chested, slightly slouching, as fashion now required. Mary’s mighty feats of athleticism could not have been performed with the twenty-inch waist May’s azure sash had so easily spanned. The difference seemed symbolic. The mother’s life had been as closely girt as her figure. Mary, no less conventional and no more intelligent, yet led a larger life and held more tolerant views. There was good in the new order too.

The telephone clicked. Archer turned, unhooked the receiver at his elbow, and listened as the modern world announced itself with its dry little sound. How far it was from the days when the brass-buttoned messenger boy’s legs had been New York’s only quick communication—quick, and yet human, with footfall and breath and street-noise clinging to the message.

“Chicago wants you.”

It must be Dallas. The firm always sent Dallas on errands, and he was in Chicago to talk over plans for a Lakeside palace for a young millionaire with ideas.

“Hallo, Dad—Yes: Dallas. I say—how do you feel about sailing on Wednesday? Mauretania: yes, next Wednesday as ever is. Our client wants me to look at some Italian gardens before we settle anything, and has asked me to nip over on the next boat. I’ve got to be back on the first of June—” The voice broke into a joyful, conscious laugh—“so we must look alive. I say, Dad, I want your help: do come.”

It startled Archer—less the request than the laugh. That laugh, travelling over all those miles—forest, river, mountain, prairie, roaring cities, indifferent millions—arrived in his library as plainly as if the boy were lounging by the fire. The laugh said without saying: Of course I must get back on the first, because Fanny Beaufort and I are to be married on the fifth.

Dallas began again, brisk and affectionate. “Think it over? No, sir: not a minute. You’ve got to say yes now. Why not, I’d like to know? If you can allege a single reason—No; I knew it. Then it’s a go, eh? Because I count on you to ring up the Cunard office first thing tomorrow; and you’d better book a return on a boat from Marseilles. I say, Dad; it’ll be our last time together, in this kind of way—. Oh, good! I knew you would.”

Chicago rang off. Archer stood a moment, holding the dead receiver, hearing the faint after-noise in his ear as one hears the sea after a door has shut. Then he rose and began to pace the room.

It would be their last time together in that particular way; the boy was right. There would be other “times,” no doubt: the two were born comrades, and Fanny Beaufort—whatever one thought of her—did not seem likely to interfere with their intimacy. Archer suspected she would be folded into it naturally, as some new spice is folded into an old dish until you cannot imagine it without. Still, change was change. He felt the temptation to seize this last chance of being alone with his son.

There was no reason not to, except the profound one that he had lost the habit of travel. May had disliked moving except for valid reasons—taking the children to the sea or the mountains. She could imagine no other motive for leaving Thirty-ninth Street or their comfortable quarters at the Wellands’ in Newport. After Dallas took his degree she had decided it was her duty to travel for six months; and the whole family had made the old-fashioned tour through England, Switzerland, and Italy. Their time being limited (no one knew why) they had omitted France. Archer remembered Dallas’s wrath at being asked to contemplate Mont Blanc instead of Rheims and Chartres. But Mary and Bill wanted mountain-climbing and had already yawned their way in Dallas’s wake through English cathedrals; and May, always fair, had insisted on balancing athletic and artistic proclivities. She had even proposed that Archer should go to Paris for a fortnight and join them later on the Italian lakes. He had declined.

“We’ll stick together,” he had said; and May’s face had brightened, as though he had set their son a moral example as simple as eating what one is served.

Since her death two years before, there had been no reason to keep the same routine. The children urged him to travel. Mary was sure it would do him good to go abroad and “see the galleries,” as though art were a tonic one swallowed in measured doses. The mysteriousness of such a cure made her only the more certain of it. But Archer had found himself held fast by habit and memories and a startled shrinking from new things.

Now, reviewing his past, he saw how deep a rut he had sunk into. The worst of doing one’s duty was that it seemed to unfit one for doing anything else. At least that was the view the men of his generation had taken. The divisions between right and wrong, honest and dishonest, respectable and the reverse, had been so trenchant that there was little room for the unforeseen. And then came moments when a man’s imagination, so easily subdued to what it lives in, rose abruptly above daily level and looked down the long windings of destiny. Archer hung there and wondered.

What was left of the little world he had grown up in, with its standards that bent and bound him? He remembered a sneering prophecy poor Lawrence Lefferts had uttered years ago in this very room: “If things go on at this rate, our children will be marrying Beaufort’s bastards.”

It was exactly what his eldest son, the pride of his life, was doing—and nobody wondered, nobody reproved. Even Aunt Janey, who still looked as she had in her elderly youth, had taken her mother’s emeralds and seed-pearls out of their pink cotton-wool and carried them with her own twitching hands to the future bride. And Fanny Beaufort, instead of looking disappointed at not receiving a Paris “set,” had cried out at their old-fashioned beauty, declaring that when she wore them she would feel like an Isabey miniature.

Fanny Beaufort had appeared in New York at eighteen after her parents’ death and won its heart much as Ellen Olenska had won it thirty years earlier—only New York did not distrust her, did not draw back from her. It took her joyfully for granted. She was pretty, amusing, accomplished: what more did anyone want? Nobody was narrow-minded enough to rake up the half-forgotten facts of her father’s past or her own origin. Only older people remembered Beaufort’s failure, or the quiet marriage, after his wife’s death, to the notorious Fanny Ring, and the way he had left the country with his new wife and a little girl who inherited her beauty. He had been heard of in Constantinople, then Russia, and later American travellers spoke of being handsomely entertained by him in Buenos Ayres, where he represented a large insurance agency. He and his wife had died there in the odour of prosperity. Then one day their orphaned daughter had appeared in New York in charge of May Archer’s sister-in-law, Mrs. Jack Welland, whose husband had been appointed the girl’s guardian. The fact threw her into an almost cousinly relationship with the Archer children. Nobody was surprised when Dallas’s engagement was announced.

Nothing could more clearly measure the distance the world had travelled. People were too busy—busy with reforms and “movements,” with fads and fetishes and frivolities—to bother much about their neighbours. In the huge kaleidoscope where social atoms spun on the same plane, what account was anyone’s past?

And now Archer stood in Paris.

He looked out of his hotel window at the stately gaiety of the streets and felt his heart beat with the confusion and eagerness of youth, as if some old clock inside him had been wound too tight. It was long since it had plunged and reared under his widening waistcoat, leaving him a moment later with an empty breast and hot temples. He wondered if it was like that for Dallas with Fanny Beaufort—and decided it was not.

“It functions as actively, no doubt,” he reflected, “but the rhythm is different.”

The difference, perhaps, was that young people now assumed they would get what they wanted, while his generation had almost always assumed they would not. But then—could a heart ever beat as wildly for what one is certain of in advance?

It was the day after their arrival. Spring sunshine held Archer at his open window above the wide silvery prospect of the Place Vendôme. Paris moved below him like an orchestration: wheels on stone, voices braided in many pitches, the occasional cry of a street-seller rising and falling, a distant hammering softened by distance into something almost like percussion. The city sounded alive in a way New York, with its newer clamours and harsher engines, did not. Here, even noise seemed to have a history, a vocabulary that had not been entirely drowned by industry’s blunt consonants.

When he had agreed to come abroad with Dallas, he had stipulated—almost the only thing—that in Paris he would not be taken to one of the newfangled “palaces.”

“Oh, all right—of course,” Dallas had agreed. “I’ll take you to some jolly old-fashioned place—the Bristol, say—” leaving Archer speechless at the thought that the century-long home of kings and emperors had become an “old-fashioned” inn visited for quaint inconveniences and lingering local colour, as one might seek out a country kitchen for the taste of wood-smoke and slow-cooked broth.

In the early impatient years Archer had often pictured his return to Paris. Then the personal vision had faded, and he had tried simply to see the city as the setting of Ellen’s life. At night in his library, after the household slept, he had evoked spring down the avenues of horse-chestnuts, flowers and statues in public gardens, lilac-scented flower-carts, the river’s majestic roll under great bridges, and the life of art and study and pleasure that filled each artery of the city to bursting. Now it was all before him, and he felt shy, old-fashioned, inadequate: a grey speck compared with the ruthless magnificent fellow he had once dreamed of being.

Dallas’s hand came down cheerfully on his shoulder. “Hullo, father: this is something like, isn’t it?” They stood a while looking out in silence. Then Dallas added, as lightly as if noting the hour of a train: “By the way, I’ve got a message for you: the Countess Olenska expects us both at half-past five.”

He said it carelessly, almost teasingly. Archer looked at him and thought he caught, in the young man’s gay eyes, a gleam of old Catherine Mingott’s malice.

“Oh, didn’t I tell you?” Dallas pursued. “Fanny made me swear to do three things while I was in Paris: get her the score of the last Debussy songs, go to the Grand-Guignol, and see Madame Olenska. You know she was awfully good to Fanny when Mr. Beaufort sent her over from Buenos Ayres to the Assomption. Fanny hadn’t any friends in Paris, and Madame Olenska used to be kind to her and trot her about on holidays. I believe she was a great friend of the first Mrs. Beaufort’s. And she’s our cousin, of course. So I rang her up this morning, before I went out, and told her you and I were here for two days and wanted to see her.”

Archer continued to stare at him. “You told her I was here?”

“Of course—why not?” Dallas’s eyebrows went up whimsically; and then, getting no answer, he slipped his arm through his father’s with a confidential pressure. “I say, father: what was she like?”

Archer felt colour rise under his son’s unabashed gaze. “Come, own up: you and she were great pals, weren’t you? Wasn’t she most awfully lovely?”

“Lovely? I don’t know. She was different.”

“Ah—there you have it! That’s what it always comes to, doesn’t it? When she comes, she’s different—and one doesn’t know why. It’s exactly what I feel about Fanny.”

Archer drew back a step, releasing his arm. “About Fanny? But, my dear fellow—I should hope so! Only I don’t see—”

“Dash it, Dad, don’t be prehistoric! Wasn’t she—once—your Fanny?”

Dallas belonged body and soul to the new generation. He was Newland and May Archer’s first-born, and it had never been possible to instil in him even the rudiments of reserve. “What’s the use of making mysteries? It only makes people want to nose ’em out,” he always objected when urged to discretion. Yet in his banter Archer saw the filial light, affectionate and fearless.

“My Fanny?”

“Well, the woman you’d have chucked everything for—only you didn’t,” Dallas continued, watching him.

“I didn’t,” Archer echoed, and the words came out with a kind of solemnity, like a vow renewed.

“No: you date, you see, dear old boy. But mother said—”

“Your mother?”

“Yes: the day before she died. It was when she sent for me alone—you remember? She said she knew we were safe with you, and always would be, because once, when she asked you to, you’d given up the thing you most wanted.”

Archer received this in silence. His eyes fixed unseeingly on the crowded square below, where carriages and motors moved like toys in bright sun. At length he said in a low voice: “She never asked me.”

“No. I forgot. You never did ask each other anything, did you? And you never told each other anything. You just sat and watched each other, and guessed at what was going on underneath. A deaf-and-dumb asylum, in fact! Well, I back your generation for knowing more about each other’s private thoughts than we ever have time to find out about our own.—I say, Dad,” Dallas broke off, suddenly anxious, “you’re not angry with me? If you are, let’s make it up and go and lunch at Henri’s. I’ve got to rush out to Versailles afterward.”

Archer did not accompany him to Versailles. He preferred to spend the afternoon alone, walking Paris as one walks through a remembered house, touching walls, opening doors, letting dust rise and settle. He had to deal all at once with the packed regrets and stifled memories of an inarticulate lifetime.

After a little while he did not regret Dallas’s indiscretion. It loosened something like an iron band from his heart to know that, after all, someone had guessed and pitied—and that it had been May moved him indescribably. Dallas, for all his affectionate insight, would not have understood why. To the boy the episode was only a pathetic instance of frustration, wasted forces. But was it really no more? Archer sat for a long time on a bench in the Champs-Élysées while the stream of life rolled by, listening to the layered sounds—footsteps on gravel, wheels, birds threading their thin bright calls through the heavier human din—and wondered how much of a life’s meaning was made of what could not be spoken in the dominant tongue of one’s time.

A few streets away, a few hours away, Ellen Olenska waited. She had never gone back to her husband; and when he died, years before, she had made no change in her way of living. There was nothing now to keep her and Archer apart—no law, no vow, no living obstacle but the old habits of speech and silence, the inherited grammar of restraint. And that afternoon he was to see her.

He rose and walked across the Place de la Concorde, through the Tuileries gardens, to the Louvre. Ellen had once told him she often went there. He wanted, before meeting her, to spend time in a place where she might lately have been—where her eyes had rested on the same colours, her steps had sounded on the same floors.

For an hour or more he wandered from gallery to gallery through the dazzle of afternoon light. Paintings burst on him one by one in half-forgotten splendour, as if doors long closed had been flung open and air rushed in. The smell of old varnish, stone, and human warmth mixed with the faint dust of centuries. Beauty—stored and labelled and guarded—still struck him with the force of something freshly made.

After all, his life had been too starved.
